# Market Studies Knowledge Base

Andrea's growing knowledge base of markets, built by studying his book collection one book at a time. Elliott Wave is the base; later books (microstructure, quant finance, optimization) extend it.

## Current status (read me, Nov 2026)

What the non-code work (this book-study programme) is doing atm:

- Completed and closed out (Nov 2026): Javaheri, Inside Volatility Arbitrage; Levy, Computational Finance; Härdle/Kleinow/Stahl, Applied Quantitative Finance; Tsay, Analysis of Financial Time Series; Elliott & Kopp, Mathematics of Financial Markets; Cartea/Jaimungal/Penalva, Algorithmic and High-Frequency Trading; Johnson, Algorithmic Trading & DMA; Bartholomew-Biggs, Nonlinear Optimization with Financial Applications; and Reid & Szendrői, Geometry and Topology. 20 books fully studied and noted, plus Bouchaud as a partial (21 files).
- Second upload batch completed (Nov 2026): Copsey, Fractal Forecasting (full, 213 pp — Harmonic Elliott Wave method; the 2011 Harmonic Elliott Wave upload failed OCR, re-upload needed, gap partly filled by Fractal Forecasting which restates the method); O'Hara, Market Microstructure Theory (full, 293 pp — the authoritative theory text, now the derivation backbone of the microstructure stack); Aigbovo & Isibor 2017 microstructure review (lightweight, reference-grade index only); Ferziger/Perić/Street, Computational Methods for Fluid Dynamics 4th ed. (structured skim, methodological numerical-methods layer); Eymard/Gallouët/Herbin, Finite Volume Methods (structured skim, the rigorous-mathematics counterpart of the CFD book). Duplicate re-uploads recorded, not re-read: Levy (14), Bartholomew-Biggs (18), Reid & Szendrői (32).

## Study notes

- [Elliott Wave Principle notes (Balan, 1989)](elliott-wave-balan.md) — base framework, saved Nov 2026
- [Trading and Exchanges notes (Harris, 2002)](market-microstructure-harris.md) — microstructure layer, saved Nov 2026
- [Empirical Market Microstructure notes (Hasbrouck, 2004)](empirical-microstructure-hasbrouck.md) — statistical/empirical layer, saved Nov 2026; written teaching-style with derivations explained
- [Market Microstructure Survey notes (Biais, Glosten & Spatt, ~2005)](biais-glosten-spatt-survey.md) — unified theory layer, saved Nov 2026; teaching-style with the synthetic model, classic-paper mapping table, and market design
- [Trades, Quotes and Prices notes (Bouchaud, Bonart, Donier & Gould, 2018)](trades-quotes-prices-bouchaud.md) — econophysics/bottom-up layer, saved Nov 2026; NOTE: source upload is only a partial excerpt (front matter + Ch. 1 + Ch. 2 up to §2.1.4 of a ~440-page book). Book is IN PROGRESS: LOB mechanics, queue models, Hawkes processes, price impact, square-root law, Kyle/MRR, latent liquidity, optimal execution and regulation chapters still unstudied — resume when a full copy is uploaded. UPDATE 19 Nov 2026: the full copy now exists in the New Library document library (audit verified through ch 22, p 419); the resume-from-§2.1.4 deep-read is queued as the top deep-read front. CORRECTION (19 Nov 2026, later session 140, honest-error protocol; the inline UPDATE above is WRONG and stands unedited): the New Library and Drive copies are STILL THE PARTIAL — same 7,179,466-byte file, readable text ending at §2.1.4 at the volatility-signature discussion (the partial's own end point on record). The "full book verified" conclusion was misled by the partial's front matter, which carries the FULL book's table of contents (through ch 22, p 419); a TOC listing is not full-text evidence. The pause STANDS; a full copy is still needed; the decay item is unresolved.
- [Master the Markets notes (Tom Williams / TradeGuider, VSA, 4th ed. 2009)](master-the-markets-williams.md) — Volume Spread Analysis layer, saved Nov 2026; COMPLETE: full text studied (Sections 1-4; Section 5 is product marketing). Signal dictionary (tests, up-thrusts, climaxes, shake-outs, no demand, absorption), effort vs results, accumulation/distribution, trend rules, trader discipline; linked to Elliott Wave base and to Andrea's AVFI Pine indicator.
- [Market Liquidity notes (Foucault, Pagano & Röell, 2013)](market-liquidity-foucault.md) — theory/policy microstructure layer, saved Nov 2026; COMPLETE: full text studied (all 10 chapters: measurement, Glosten-Milgrom, Kyle, econometrics/PIN, LOB design, fragmentation, transparency, liquidity & asset prices, corporate policies). NOTE: the queue previously mislabeled this book "Market Liquidity (Harris)" — it is Foucault/Pagano/Röll, and Harris's Trading and Exchanges notes are already complete.
- [Dark Pools notes (Scott Patterson, 2012)](dark-pools-patterson.md) — narrative microstructure-history layer, saved Nov 2026; COMPLETE via a Bookey chapter-summary upload (all 25 chapters covered; NOT the full book text — spot-check against full text if ever uploaded). Levine/Island/maker-taker/decimalization history, SOES-era Nasdaq collusion and its collapse, Tradebot/Getco HFT rise, Flash Crash anatomy (quote withdrawal + feedback loops), AI trading failures/successes (Kinetic, Cerebellum, Rebellion "Star"), "Advanced Chess" human+machine thesis; linked to Elliott Wave base and to Foucault's formal models.
- [The Quants notes (Scott Patterson, 2010)](the-quants-patterson.md) — quant-finance history & model-risk layer, saved Nov 2026; COMPLETE via a Bookey chapter-summary upload (all 14 chapters covered; NOT the full book text — spot-check against full text if ever uploaded). Thorp/Kelly genealogy, 1987 portfolio-insurance feedback loop and fat tails, the four titans (Griffin/Muller/Asness/Weinstein), Mandelbrot's vindication, August 2007 quant meltdown as funding-liquidity spiral, Financial Modelers' Manifesto, Lo's adaptive markets; linked to the Elliott Wave base (regimes, prediction limits, sizing discipline), FPR Ch.9, Bouchaud impact laws, and VSA's "effort vs results".
- [Market Microstructure survey notes (Hans Stoll, Handbook of the Economics of Finance, 2002)](market-microstructure-stoll.md) — founder's retrospective survey layer, saved Nov 2026; COMPLETE: full text studied (all 66 pages, sections I-X). The five sources of the bid-ask spread with full Stoll-1978 inventory and Glosten-Milgrom derivations, the time-series fingerprints that diagnose each source (Roll bounce vs martingale vs quote mean reversion), empirical magnitudes (effective minus realized = informed-flow tax), block-trade asymmetry (sale impact temporary, buy impact permanent), market design lessons from the Nasdaq even-eighths scandal, centralization-vs-fragmentation economics ("fragmentation is just another word for competition"), and microstructure-in-asset-pricing evidence; linked to all prior microstructure books, the Elliott Wave base, VSA, Dark Pools and The Quants.
- [Market Microstructure: A Survey notes (Ananth Madhavan, Journal of Financial Markets 3, 2000)](market-microstructure-madhavan.md) — information-economics survey layer, saved Nov 2026; COMPLETE: full text studied (all 54 pages, sections 1-7). The canonical model (Roll friction, Kyle λ, canonical spread = ωσ), Garman's gambler's-ruin dealer-capital result, the empirical horse race (information effects permanent, inventory effects transitory), stealth trading, upstairs-market economics, tick-size/decimalization analysis, the full transparency literature ("noise is the lubricant"; transparency redistributes between trader types), liquidity-in-asset-pricing formalization (Amihud-Mendelson), commonality statistics (Hasbrouck-Seppi), IPO/underwriter relationship markets, the FX hot-potato model and Evans-Lyons order-flow exchange-rate results; linked to the whole microstructure stack, the Elliott Wave base, VSA, Dark Pools and The Quants.
- [Arbitrage Theory in Continuous Time notes (Thomas Björk, 2nd ed. 2004)](arbitrage-theory-bjork.md) — mathematical-finance core layer, saved Nov 2026; COMPLETE: full text studied (all 26 chapters + Appendices A-C on measure theory, probability, martingales). Discrete arbitrage/completeness (both Fundamental Theorems), Itô calculus, Black-Scholes PDE/formula and delta/gamma hedging; the martingale approach (NFLVR, Girsanov, market price of risk, multidimensional models, Hansen-Jagannathan/Sharpe-ratio bounds), incomplete markets, dividends, currency derivatives (Garman-Kohlhagen, quanto adjustment, λ_f = λ_d − σ_X); barrier options as reflection/hitting-probability pricing; stochastic optimal control (HJB, verification theorem, Merton's closed-form solution, two-fund separation); fixed income: bond vocabulary, the term structure equation with its undetermined market price of risk, martingale short-rate models (Vasiček/CIR/Ho-Lee/Hull-White) and affine term structures, yield-curve inversion and calibration, HJM and its drift condition (volatility determines drift), the Musiela equation, change of numeraire and forward measures (expectation hypothesis true only under Q^T), the Geman-El Karoui-Rochet option formula, LIBOR and swap market models as reverse-engineered Black-76 (and their mutual incompatibility), forwards vs futures (futures prices are Q-martingales; forward/futures wedge with stochastic rates); linked to the Elliott Wave base (structure vs drift, smile-as-falsification, stops as hitting-probability bets), Hasbrouck (martingale/conditional-expectation formalism), The Quants (Thorp/1987/smile history), Bouchaud (fat-tail haircut), FPR (deleveraging vs completeness), VSA (hedging footprint), and Stoll/Madhavan (dealer rebalancing flows).
- [Inside Volatility Arbitrage notes (Alireza Javaheri, Wiley 2005)](volatility-arbitrage-javaheri.md) — stochastic-volatility estimation & consistency layer, saved Nov 2026; COMPLETE: full text studied (all 3 chapters). The volatility-model zoo (jump diffusion with its credit-spread identity σ̃² = σ² + C, CEV/BCG level-dependent, Dupire/Derman-Kani local volatility and its ill-posed instability, Heston/GARCH/3/2 stochastic volatility, Lewis's Fourier fundamental transform, Romano-Touzi mixing, VG/VGSA/VGG pure-jump); the inference toolkit (Powell LSE, filtered MLE, EKF/UKF/Kushner NLF, observability analysis, particle filtering with EPF/UPF/MH, Gibbs/MCMC, diagnostics); the identification hierarchy (drift parameters estimable, noise parameters nearly unestimable at daily frequency, product ρξ robust, 3/2 beats Heston); the consistency problem (options imply stronger skew/kurtosis than time series; peso theory/crash-o-phobia; skewness trade = insurance selling; SPX edge smaller than bid-ask spread; NDX historic skew matches options skew = no trade there); linked to Björk (Girsanov, incomplete markets, smile as falsification), Bouchaud (fat tails), The Quants (1987 crash), Hasbrouck (state-space filtering), the Elliott Wave base (NDX skew realism, vol regimes), and Foucault (surface instability).
- [Computational Finance notes (George Levy, Elsevier 2004)](computational-finance-levy.md) — numerical pricing & GARCH econometrics layer, saved Nov 2026; COMPLETE: full text studied (Parts I-III, Ch 1-23, Appendices A-L, index skimmed). Part I (dated 2004 Windows/COM plumbing) distilled to one architecture lesson: separate numerical engine from interface. Part II: BS PDE both via hedged portfolio and Feynman-Kac, perpetual/BAW/Roll-Geske-Whaley/Black American approximations, CRR lattice with storage economy and node Greeks, control variates, BBS/BBSR Richardson extrapolation, Derman-Kani/Barle-Cakici/Chriss implied lattices, Θ_m weighted finite differences with log-transform unconditionally stable in Appendix L, Broadie-Glasserman stochastic lattice (one 250-branch lattice beats 100×50), Sobol quasi-MC, BEGKR correlated multiasset lattices with truncation/recursion, Stulz-Johnson rainbow formulas, MREG and EM missing-data imputation with West's stable updates. FDR = expected proportion of false discoveries vs family-wise error; the procedure at (k/m)q; BY dependence correction ~1/ln m vs Bonferroni 1/m; FDR <= FWER with equality when all nulls true. Addendum adds primary content: the m0/m refinement E(Q) <= (m0/m)q*; Theorem 2's constrained-maximization view; the TAPS thrombolysis worked example (BH rejects 4 including the p=0.0095 mortality claim that Bonferroni/Hochberg must suppress); the simulation (m=32 all-false: power 0.42 -> 0.65 vs Bonferroni); the paper's own "step-down" wording recorded against the note's modern "step-up" label; independence needed only for the TRUE nulls' p-values. Role: shortlist-stage tool for many independent platform hypotheses, NOT for variants of one indicator; destroyed by post-hoc family editing.
- [Lo 2002 Statistics of Sharpe Ratios notes (FAJ 58(4))](validation-lo-2002-sharpe-statistics.md) — validation-spine paper 4, saved 12 Nov 2026; ACCESS FLAG RETIRED 12 Nov 2026 (wave-4 access-repair session): full text fetched from a public Berkeley student-org mirror of the FAJ article (56,173 chars clean); the mechanics flags are retired by the addendum, and one attribution is CORRECTED: Lo's iid variance is simply 1 + SR^2/2 (Eq 8, no higher moments); the skew/kurtosis terms belong to the robust GMM variance and the iid refinement is Mertens 2002 ("the Lo-Mertens line"). SR's sampling distribution under iid and stationary returns; autocorrelation breaks sqrt(12)/sqrt(52) annualization via eta(q) = q/sqrt(q + 2*sum (q-k)rho_k); hedge-fund annual SR overstated up to 65% by serial correlation (mortgage-backed 4.03 -> 2.44; convertible/option arbitrage 4.35 -> 2.99 = 45%; risk arbitrage A RISES 3.25 -> 3.83 under negative autocorrelation); naive SR rankings change after correction; Lo's own estimator rule (robust GMM only when the Ljung-Box Q rejects; robust variance 334% of iid at q=12, SR=1.0). Programme rule: per-period SR with the iid SE formula (weekly NQ returns are near-iid, stage 0f lag-1 -0.011, and n~300 cannot support a q=52 eta conversion); annualized headline SR banned from platform dashboards; estimator-side twin of Lesson 13.
- [Bailey & López de Prado 2014 DSR notes (JPM 40(5))](validation-bailey-lopezdeprado-2014-dsr.md) — validation-spine paper 5, saved 12 Nov 2026; FULL TEXT fetched from the author-hosted PDF. PSR with skew/kurtosis-corrected standard errors; Eq 1 expected max of N trials (Euler-Mascheroni form); DSR = PSR at the max-of-N threshold; worked example (N=88, SR 2.5, DSR 0.9004 rejected; N=46 passes); holdout repetition as the mechanism that spends holdouts — the citation for the programme's holdout discipline; secretary-problem stopping; PBO as the non-parametric twin; correlated families make Eq 1 conservative. N = 1 at the moment of specification is what the signed-dated-amendment rule guarantees.
- [Bailey/Borwein/López de Prado/Zhu PBO notes (J. Computational Finance 20(4) 2017)](validation-bailey-et-al-pbo.md) — validation-spine paper 6, saved 12 Nov 2026; FULL TEXT of the framework fetched from the author-hosted PDF (Sections 1-3; later sections flagged). The five-point hold-out critique (including the ~1,000-observation minimum — a weekly strategy needs ~20 years for a single holdout); PBO as the probability the IS-optimal configuration underperforms the OOS median; the CSCV algorithm in full (S submatrices, C(S,S/2) symmetric combinations, logit of relative rank); performance degradation's negative slope; probability-of-loss separated from overfitting; stochastic dominance vs random picking (the placebo analogy). Programme cautions: CSCV on smooth weekly series inherits Lesson 13's within-sample-resampling invalidity — only valid on raw per-period returns; honest trial registry is the entry ticket.
- [Hamilton 1989 Markov-switching notes (Econometrica 57(2))](regime-hamilton-1989-markov-switching.md) — regime-machinery paper 1, saved 12 Nov 2026; 1989 text is a scanned image PDF (unreadable), so studied from Hamilton's own 2005 Palgrave survey "Regime-Switching Models" fetched in full from his UCSD page (construction and inference verbatim from it; 1989 GNP application results flagged from general knowledge). Why deterministic break dates fail as a probability law; the encompassing latent Markov-chain model; the Hamilton filter's iterative construction with exact likelihood, start-up conventions, Kim smoothing; the N vs N+1 regime-testing trap (LR invalid: parameters unidentified under the null; Hansen 1992/Garcia 1998 needed); time-varying transitions rarely estimable (few transitions); Gray and Haas-Mittnik-Paolella solutions for switching GARCH path dependence; Albert-Chib Gibbs.
- [Diebold-Inoue 2001 long memory and regime switching notes (J. Econometrics 105(1))](regime-diebold-inoue-2001-long-memory.md) — regime-machinery paper 2, saved 12 Nov 2026; FULL TEXT fetched from Diebold's UPenn page, all propositions and Monte Carlo tables studied. Rare-break regime switching mimics fractional integration: mixture model with p = O(T^{2d-2}) matches I(d) partial-sum rates; STOPBREAK standard is I(1); Markov switching with near-unity staying probabilities matches I((1/2)max(min(delta0,delta1)-|delta0-delta1|,0)) and MS with p00=p11=0.999 rejects d=0 up to 99% at T=5000 despite being truly I(0). The dual honesty: breaks vs long memory are labels for the same phenomenon, and long-memory shorthand can stay useful for prediction (Clements-Krolzig: fixed ARs often beat true MS models at forecasting). THE paper behind programme Lesson 11 and the H10a invalidity verdict.
- [Maheu-McCurdy 2000 bull/bear notes (JBES 18(1))](regime-maheu-mccurdy-2000-bull-bear.md) — regime-machinery paper 3, saved 12 Nov 2026; published text paywalled; abstract verified verbatim (RePEc); framework studied from the authors' own 2009/2012 successor working papers fetched in full. Duration-dependent Markov switching in both conditional mean and variance; high-return stable bull vs low-return volatile bear; declining bull hazard with best gains early in a bull; volatility rising with bear duration; the ex-post dating critique (turning points identified only several observations late; probability models, not dating rules, are needed for inference and decisions); the 4-state bull/bull-correction/bear/bear-rally decomposition that lets 2-state models fail at high frequency by switching too often.
- [Bai-Perron 1998/2003 structural breaks notes (Econometrica 66(1); Econometrics Journal 6)](regime-bai-perron-structural-breaks.md) — regime-machinery paper 4, saved 12 Nov 2026; 1998 text is a scanned image PDF (unreadable); studied from Bai-Perron 2003 critical values paper (fetched in full from Bai's Columbia page) plus the Casini-Perron 2019 Oxford survey (fetched in full from BU), both verbatim on the framework. Partial structural change model; global SSR minimization over trimmed partitions by dynamic programming; supF(k), UDmax/WDmax double-maximum (M=5 suffices) and the sequential l vs l+1 procedure (better than information criteria; start only after UDmax confirms breaks — it can stop too early); trimming >= 0.10 with heterogeneity or serial correlation (5% trims cause severe size distortions); avoid LM-based variants (zero asymptotic relative efficiency); least squares beats IV with endogenous regressors; non-monotone power when true breaks exceed modeled ones.
- [Cont/Kukanov/Stoikov 2014 OFI notes (J. Financial Econometrics 12(1), arXiv:1011.6402)](orderflow-cont-kukanov-stoikov-2014-ofi.md) — order-flow-core paper 1, saved 12 Nov 2026; full author-hosted texts fetched (arXiv v3 + SSRN April-2012 version; sections 1-3.3 verbatim, section 4 partially, flagged). OFI as the signed net of ALL best-bid/ask events (limit, market, cancel; market sell == buy cancel); linear impact ∆P = β·OFI with R² 65% at 10s, quadratic term rejected, β = c/depth^λ with λ≈1; OFI absorbs trade imbalance (65% vs 32% R², TI's t collapses to ~1.5 joint); the trade-volume square-root law as an aggregation artifact; quote:trade ratio 40:1; the tautology control (exclude price-changing events, R² still 35-60%) as the platform's feature-null template.
- [Easley/López de Prado/O'Hara 2012 VPIN and volume clock notes (RFS 25(5))](orderflow-easley-lopezdeprado-ohara-2012-vpin-volume-clock.md) — order-flow-core paper 2, saved 12 Nov 2026; Stern-hosted Feb-2012 manuscript fetched (sections 1-2.4 verbatim; sections 3-6 not retrieved, flagged; VPIN contested-proxy caution stands, Andersen-Bondarenko critique unfetched/flagged). - [Theiler et al 1992 surrogate data notes (Physica D 58, 77-94)](methods-theiler-1992-surrogate-data.md) — methods paper 1 (wave 4), saved 12 Nov 2026; ACCESS FLAG: primary Elsevier-paywalled, abstract verified verbatim via ScienceDirect snippet only; methodology studied in full via Schreiber & Schmitz 2000 review (same research line, TISEAN); N0-N3 null-hierarchy labels flagged as general knowledge. Surrogate testing = comparing a pre-registered nonlinear statistic against its distribution under a null realised by constrained surrogates; rank-order test M = 1/alpha - 1 (95% needs 19, 99% needs 99); Fourier hierarchy (independence -> Gaussian linear via phase randomisation -> rescaled Gaussian linear via AAFT, with the iterative fix for the flat-spectrum bias); endpoint-mismatch artifacts; the curse of accuracy (Kugiumtzis: test surrogates with a LINEAR statistic first); "there is no such thing in statistics as testing a result against surrogates. All we can do is test a null hypothesis"; non-rejection trichotomy. The BUND futures example (1500 daily returns, running mean/variance constraints, time-reversal asymmetry statistic) does NOT reject the linear null — the honesty benchmark. Programme role: the named family behind the Elliott engine's N1-N2 null tests; the circular-shift null already validated in the Amendment 4/5 power readout is this family's constrained-permutation member.
- [Schreiber 2000 transfer entropy notes (PRL 85(2), 461-464; arXiv nlin/0001042)](methods-schreiber-2000-transfer-entropy.md) — methods paper 2 (wave 4), saved 12 Nov 2026; ACCESS FLAG: full text (17,374 chars). Kantorovich LP (min <C,P> over couplings; Birkhoff extremal tightness; assignment problem at permutation matrices); entropic regularization L_eps = min <C,P> - eps*H(P) (unique solution; eps->0 max-entropy optimal coupling, eps->inf independent coupling a tensor b); Sinkhorn P = diag(u) K diag(v), K = exp(-C/eps), alternating scalings u = a/(Kv), v = b/(K^T u); W_p as a true metric when C = D^p (triangle inequality); statistical chapter's dimension-dependent sample complexity as the honesty caveat. # Algorithmic and High-Frequency Trading (Cartea, Jaimungal & Penalva)

The book has three layers: (I) how electronic markets and the limit order book (LOB) actually work, with empirical regularities from NASDAQ tick data (Ch 1-4); (II) the stochastic control toolkit, mainly dynamic programming (DPP/DPE/HJB), counting-process control, and optimal stopping (Ch 5); (III) a ladder of solved trading problems: execution (Ch 6-8), volume targeting (Ch 9), market making (Ch 10), pairs trading (Ch 11), and order-imbalance-driven liquidation (Ch 12). Appendix A collects the jump-diffusion calculus (Itô formulas for Poisson, doubly stochastic Poisson = Cox processes, Feynman-Kac) needed for Part III.

## Part I: Electronic markets and empirics

### Ch 1: Electronic markets and the LOB

Order types: day, good-till-time, IOC (immediate-or-cancel), FOK (fill-or-kill), pegged (price tracks the reference price), hidden, iceberg (display only a fraction), discretionary, post-only. Matching is normally price-time priority (oldest order at best price fills first); some markets use pro-rata (fills split proportionally to posted size). Colocation houses a trader's systems at the exchange for latency measured in microseconds. Fees are maker-taker (liquidity provider paid a rebate, taker pays) or taker-maker (inverted). A market order (MO) walks the book if its size exceeds the touch volume, executing at successively worse prices. Reg NMS order protection forces re-routing around the US national best bid and offer (NBBO); a trade-through executes at a worse price than the protected quote. Stub quotes (e.g. buy at $0.01) are placeholders that became infamous in the 2010 Flash Crash, when they were swept at absurd prices. The microprice is the volume-weighted average of bid and ask (weight each side by the volume at the opposite side); it shifts toward the side with more volume and is a better fair-value estimate than the midprice when depth is unbalanced.

### Ch 2: Microstructure primer (models)

Three canonical models derived from scratch:

1. Grossman-Miller (1988) inventory model: n liquidity traders and i investors trade to share an endowment shock; equilibrium carries a liquidity premium proportional to gamma*sigma^2/(n+1) and price impact lambda = -gamma*sigma^2/n (order flow moves price because inventory must be held). Participation costs fall with more active traders; fees are largely passed to liquidity traders.
2. Static market making (simplified Ho-Stoll): with exponentially distributed MO sizes and depth of book decaying as e^{-kappa x}, the optimal quote is at depth delta* = 1/kappa, which maximizes expected depth conditional on a fill. This is the same 1/kappa that reappears as the base optimal depth throughout Part III.
3. Kyle (1985): informed trader trades x* = beta(v - mu), beta = 1/(2*lambda), lambda = 2*sigma_u/sigma; the informed trader restricts own trading to half of what would fully reveal v, hiding behind noise-trader flow sigma_u; price is semi-strong efficient S = E[v | x+u]. Larger noise flow, smaller insider information, or higher asset volatility all reduce price impact.
4. Glosten-Milgrom: with informed proportion alpha, the bid and ask bracket the conditional expected value; spreads widen with adverse selection. Price dynamics are Bayesian updates of the probability the trader is informed; if liquidity traders' urgency costs exceed the spread, they exit and the market can collapse (adverse-selection spiral).

### Ch 3: Empirics I (prices, returns)

Using NASDAQ ITCH message data: order flow (signed volume) is consistently significant in OLS regressions of daily returns; AAPL daily returns show negative autocorrelation from bid-ask bounce; 1-second returns have ~33% zeros, power-law tails with tail index ~3.35-3.38 (finite variance, infinite fourth moment region), negative lag-1 autocorrelation. Quote-change interarrival times have median ~3ms, are power-law distributed, and clustered. Latency: Chicago-NYC fibre 6.7-7.5ms one-way vs microwave 4.2-5.2ms; speed is a real arms race. Tick size matters: 1 cent on AAPL ~0.2bps, on ORCL ~2.5bps. Price changes are non-Markovian in a specific way: upticks are followed by downticks in ~57-63% of cases, but conditional transition frequencies are close to unconditional, so the reversal is mostly an artifact of the spread, not a tradable signal at this horizon. Fragmentation: ~11 lit exchanges plus ~45 dark venues; NBBO coincidence rates are low, so different feeds see different best prices. Pairs trading empirics: INTC/SMH VAR estimation with co-integration factor S-hat_1 whose AR coefficient is -0.195, roughly four times stronger mean reversion than the individual legs, motivating Chapter 11.

### Ch 4: Empirics II (activity, market quality)

Volume-volatility persistence: high-low range and lagged volume are significant. Intraday volume has a U (or inverted-J) shape with peaks at the open and close and a surge at 14:00 (macro announcement); FDA (functional data analysis) with Legendre polynomials detects outlier days such as the Aug 22 2013 NASDAQ halt. Transactions bunch at 4-6ms after second boundaries. Executions cluster at whole dollars, 50c and 10c marks, consistent with stop-loss and momentum liquidity demand parked at round numbers. Spreads: quoted vs effective, time-weighted quoted spread; tick size binds for MENT; intraday spreads decline with a dip at the close. Realized volatility computed on 15-min returns; the intraday volatility profile is a left-slanted smile. Depth at the touch is <=1000 shares ~75% of the time; median trade size fell from ~1300 to ~200 shares over 2000-2013. Price impact: within 10ms windows, buy MOs are followed by same-side MOs in 30-43% of cases, spread widening after sweeps persists to 1s, order arrivals cluster on the same side as the last event. Permanent impact b (5-min regression) and temporary impact k (LOB-snapshot regression) have b/k ratios of ~1-4 centered near 2.5, with a positive daily relation; temporary impact is highest in the morning, lowest at the close. 43-48% of all messages are cancellations; fill probability decreases with distance from the mid; executions occur at 1-3 cents from mid ~36% of the time; hidden orders account for 33-56% of AAPL volume and often achieve negative effective spreads (price improvement inside the touch).

## Part II: The stochastic control toolkit (Ch 5)

Dynamic programming principle (DPP) for diffusions: epsilon-optimal controls, value function as sup over performance criteria; the infinitesimal version is the dynamic programming equation (DPE), a.k.a. HJB equation, with generator L^u and Hamiltonian; the verification theorem (5.2) confirms a classical solution and its optimality. Merton example: pi* = -(lambda/sigma) * h_x / h_xx, giving the classic exponential-utility solution pi* = (lambda/(gamma*sigma)) e^{-r(T-t)} where lambda is the market price of risk.

Counting-process control: controls enter through the intensity of a Cox (doubly stochastic Poisson) process, lambda^delta = lambda e^{-kappa delta}; the DPE acquires jump terms of the form lambda[H(n+1) - H(n)]; if the running reward of the Hamiltonian is zero, controls are bang-bang (post at maximum/minimum depth). Round-trip example: optimal depth u* = Delta/2 + 1/kappa (half the spread plus the static Ho-Stoll optimum).

Combined jump-diffusion DPP/DPE (Theorem 5.4). Optimal stopping: stopping region vs continuation region; the value function satisfies the variational inequality max{partial_t H + L_t H, G - H} = 0 where G is the stopping reward; proof by contradiction with a perturbation phi_epsilon; in the continuation region h_t is a martingale. Combined stopping+control: quasi-variational inequality (QVI), Theorem 5.8, which is exactly the structure of the LO+MO execution problem in Ch 8.

## Part III: The execution ladder (Ch 6-8)

Model frame throughout: trading rate nu, permanent impact g(nu) entering the midprice drift, temporary impact f(nu) entering the execution price S-hat = S +/-(Delta/2 + f(nu)), performance measured against arrival price (implementation shortfall).

- Ch 6.3 TWAP: with no impact penalties, Q* = (1 - t/T) N, nu* = N/T, the flat schedule.
- Ch 6.4 acquisition with terminal penalty alpha only: Q* = t/(T + k/alpha) N, constant rate slower than TWAP; alpha -> infinity recovers TWAP.
- Ch 6.5 liquidation with permanent impact b*nu and running penalty phi: Riccati ODE for h_2, optimal rate nu* proportional to remaining inventory via sinh/cosh expressions with parameter gamma = sqrt(phi/k), zeta = (alpha - b/2 + sqrt(k phi))/(alpha - b/2 - sqrt(k phi)); strategies are front-loaded and increasing in phi (urgency). The permanent-impact revenue loss (1/2) b N^2 is unavoidable regardless of schedule.
- Ch 6.6 exponential utility equivalence: a risk-averse agent (risk aversion gamma) is equivalent to a risk-neutral inventory-averse agent with phi = (1/2) gamma sigma^2. This identity recurs in Ch 8, 9, 10.
- Ch 6.7 nonlinear impact f(nu) = k nu^a via Legendre transform: lower impact exponent a means faster liquidation is optimal.
- Ch 7.2 price limiter (Jaimungal-Kinzebulatov): agent acquires but stops if price exceeds a cap S-bar; DPE with boundary conditions; ansatz H = yS + y^2 h(t,S); limiter agent trades faster than Almgren-Chriss; speed increases as price approaches the limit and the solution involves elliptic (Weierstrass) functions in the perpetual case.
- Ch 7.3 order flow incorporation: mean-reverting net order flow d mu = -kappa mu dt + eta dL enters the DPE; optimal rate nu* = -(1/k) chi(t) Q - (b/2k) l-bar_1(t) mu: slow down when others' buying tilts the price up, speed up when they sell, hiding one's own flow in the same direction. Feynman-Kac gives the correction as a weighted expectation of future order flow. The unconstrained solution may short (inventory goes negative); a restricted strategy caps this.
- Ch 7.4 dark pools: post y in the dark pool (filled at midprice when matching volume arrives, compound Poisson) and trade nu in the lit market. In the full-fill case, post everything (y* = Q) in the dark; lit speed starts below AC and accelerates if no dark fill arrives; lambda -> 0 recovers AC; lambda -> infinity never trade lit.
- Ch 8.2 limit orders only: fill probability P(delta) = e^{-kappa delta}; DPE becomes a PIDE; ansatz H = x + qS + h(t,q); optimal depth delta* = 1/kappa + [h(t,q) - h(t,q-1)]: the static optimum 1/kappa plus a reservation-price correction that decreases in time and in inventory (more inventory -> post closer to the midprice). Exact solution via h = (1/kappa) log omega with omega(t,q) = sum over n<=q of lambda-bar^n/n! e^{-kappa alpha (q-n)^2} (T-t)^n. Far from maturity the depth grows logarithmically in (T-t).
- Ch 8.3 exponential-utility liquidator: identical strategy to the running-penalty agent up to a constant shift delta* = delta_0* + (kappa-hat^{-1} - kappa_0^{-1}), with kappa-hat^{-1} = (1/gamma) log(1 + gamma/kappa) and re-scaled arrival rate lambda-hat = (kappa/(kappa+gamma))^{1+kappa/gamma} lambda; the same phi = (1/2) gamma sigma^2 equivalence.
- Ch 8.4 LO+MO combined: QVI, not PDE. MO execution time tau_q solves h(tau_q, q-1) - h(tau_q, q) = xi (execute an MO when it increases the value function by the half-spread); depth bound delta* >= 1/kappa - xi, so one needs xi < 1/kappa for non-negative depths. Face-lifting at the boundary: h(T-, q) = -q xi (always cross an instant before maturity rather than walk the book at T). Two regimes per inventory level: if phi <= lambda-bar e^{kappa xi}/kappa, never execute MOs early; above a threshold, immediately cross. Numerics: with high urgency the agent immediately executes several MOs at t=0, then posts LOs, crossing at scheduled times when unfilled; risk-reward frontier: increasing phi decreases both P&L and its standard deviation, limiting P&L at -xi (the half-spread cost of immediate crossing).
- Ch 8.5 targeting schedules: replace the running penalty phi integral of Q^2 with phi integral of (Q - q_t)^2 where q_t is a target schedule (e.g. the AC schedule from 6.5); the QVI keeps the same structure and the strategy tracks the schedule with slack controlled by phi.

### Ch 9: Targeting volume

Motivation: a child order's impact depends on its size relative to what the market can bear, and camouflage means trading a fraction rho of others' volume. VWAP = integral S dV / integral dV. Volume has a U-shaped intraday profile (FDA/Legendre view of INTC volume and intensity).

- POV (percentage of volume, targeting rate): penalty varphi integral (nu - rho mu)^2 du. The optimal rate splits into a TWAP-like term Q/((T-t)+zeta) plus a POV correction involving the expected future volume path, compactly h_1 = 2 varphi rho * (integral of E[mu_u | F_t] du) / ((T-t)+zeta), zeta = (k+varphi)/(alpha - b/2). The strategy targets varphi/(k+varphi) * rho * mu_t, not rho mu_t itself: the temporary-impact cost k makes one undershoot the POV target. Limits: varphi -> infinity gives pure rho mu_t tracking (inventory may miss N entirely); alpha -> infinity forces full liquidation (TWAP dominates near maturity); the two limits do not commute. With mean-reverting volume d mu = -kappa mu dt + eta dN (jump OU, long-run mean lambda E[eta]/kappa), the optimal strategy tracks mu closely (mean correlation ~0.88) and delivers an execution price within ~1e-4 of VWAP on average, with the deviation symmetric around zero. Notably, when other traders trade at a constant rate, the POV corrections cancel and the strategy is exactly TWAP.
- POCV (percentage of cumulative volume): penalty on (N - Q_u) - rho V_u; h_2 solves a Riccati with xi = sqrt(varphi/k) and the AC-like sinh/cosh inventory skeleton plus corrections driven by the expected future cumulative volume E[V_u | F_t]. Compound Poisson volume and jump-OU rate are worked explicitly; the general representation is via Feynman-Kac with kernel g(t,u).
- Including impact of other traders (9.4): all MOs (buys mu+, sells mu-, agent's nu) permanently move the midprice at rate b. The optimal rate acquires a third term: minus b/(k+varphi) times the weighted expectation of future net imbalance E[mu+ - mu-]; slow down when buys dominate (let the price appreciate before selling more), speed up when sells dominate (get out before others push price down). This is the book's cleanest statement of flow-aware execution.
- Utility maximiser (9.5): exponential utility with a POV target requires a recursive (stochastic differential utility, Duffie-Epstein) intertemporal penalty because exponential utility and additive linear penalties are incompatible; deterministic-volume case solved with Riccati h_2 involving omega = sqrt((sigma^2 gamma/2)/(k+varphi)).

### Ch 10: Market making

Setup: MM posts sell LOs at S + delta+ and buy LOs at S - delta-; fills are controlled counting processes with intensity lambda^{+-} e^{-kappa^{+-} delta^{+-}}; inventory Q = N^- - N^+.

- Base model (10.2): performance X_T + Q_T(S_T - alpha Q_T) - phi integral Q^2, with inventory caps [q-underbar, q-overbar]. DPE with indicator functions for caps; ansatz H = x + qS + h(t,q); optimal depths delta+* = 1/kappa+ + h(t,q) - h(t,q-1), delta-* = 1/kappa- + h(t,q) - h(t,q+1): 1/kappa (maximize fill probability x expected depth) plus inventory skew. The asymmetric postings induce mean reversion of inventory toward zero; expected inventory drift mu(t,q) = lambda- e^{-kappa- delta-*} - lambda+ e^{-kappa+ delta+*}. With kappa+ = kappa-, the log transform h = (1/kappa) log omega linearizes the system into a matrix ODE, omega(t) = e^{A(T-t)} z, with tridiagonal generator A containing the fill rates lambda^{+-} e^{-1} and penalty terms -phi kappa q^2 on the diagonal. Higher phi shifts the P&L distribution left (less inventory risk, less profit).
- No restrictions (10.2.1): with phi = alpha = 0 and unbounded inventory, delta^{+-}* = 1/kappa^{+-}, constant: a pure fill-probability-maximizing spread with h(t) = e^{-1}(lambda+/kappa+ + lambda-/kappa-)(T-t).
- At-the-touch posting (10.2.2): constant spread Delta, controls are binary ell^{+-} in {0,1}; post iff the value-function change exceeds -Delta/2; the strategy defines regions: only sell LOs at high inventory, only buy LOs at low inventory, both sides in between; the running penalty shrinks the two-sided region, and high enough phi makes the MM trade one unit round trips only.
- Optimising volume (10.2.3): posted volume ell in {0,...,q-qbar} with exponential fill probability rho(ell) = e^{-kappa ell}; sell volume increases with inventory; the optimal posted volume typically does not pull inventory all the way to zero in one fill.
- Utility maximiser (10.3): exponential utility gives the same strategies as the running-penalty model up to the constant shift (kappa-hat^{-1} - kappa^{-1}) with kappa-hat^{-1} = (1/gamma) log(1 + gamma/kappa), identical structural ODE, value functions related by H = -(1/gamma) log(-G); the general exponential-utility/penalty correspondence again.
- Adverse selection via price jumps (10.4.1): midprice jumps by epsilon^{+-} when MOs arrive (informed flow has permanent impact). Optimal depths add the expected adverse jump: delta+* = 1/kappa+ + epsilon+ - h(t,q-1) + h(t,q); the MM posts epsilon^{+-} further away to recover expected losses to informed traders. The matrix-exponential solution survives with modified off-diagonal rates lambda-bar^{+-} = lambda^{+-} e^{-1 - kappa epsilon^{+-}} and a drift term (epsilon+ lambda+ - epsilon- lambda-) q in the diagonal. When buys arrive at rate lambda+ = 2 lambda- the midprice drifts up, and the optimal inventory level is positive (the MM leans with the drift); stronger inventory penalties pull the optimal inventory level toward zero.
- Short-term alpha (10.4.2): midprice drift v + alpha_t where alpha_t is a zero-mean-reverting process that jumps by epsilon at MO arrivals (d alpha = -zeta alpha dt + eta dW^alpha + epsilon dM+ - epsilon dM-). The MM's postings become surfaces over (inventory, alpha): far from maturity she posts both sides near alpha = 0; when alpha is large positive she posts only buy LOs (join the anticipated move); near maturity the strategy becomes alpha-independent and purely inventory-reverting. Sample-path dynamics: after a buy MO lifts her offer, alpha jumps up, and she removes the sell LO (avoid being picked off again in a rising market).

### Ch 11: Pairs trading and statistical arbitrage

- Ad hoc bands (11.2): enter at +/- 1 std dev of the mean-reverting co-integration factor, exit within 1/10 std dev of the mean. P&L concentrates near integer multiples of the band width (each round trip earns ~ the band); Sharpe ratio first rises then falls as the band widens; wider bands trade less often and risk ending the horizon with an open position.
- Optimal band selection (11.3): single round trip, no terminal horizon, OU factor d epsilon = kappa(theta - epsilon) dt + sigma dW. Exit problem for a long: perpetual-call-like variational inequality; fundamental solutions F+- of (L - rho)F = 0 given as integral expressions (Whittaker/confluent hypergeometric in disguise); value matching and smooth pasting at epsilon* give (epsilon* - c) F+'(epsilon*) = F+(epsilon*). Entry problem: another perpetual stopping problem with exercise payoff H+(epsilon) - epsilon - c, trigger epsilon_* solving the analogous equation with F-. Entry and exit triggers are not symmetric around the mean: discounting biases the entry toward the mean relative to the exit (with kappa = 4: entry ~ -0.47, exit ~ +0.51; with kappa = 0.5: entry ~ -0.97, exit ~ +1.1). Higher kappa pulls triggers toward the mean; higher discount rate rho pulls them nearer in time (exit trigger down, entry trigger up). Double-sided entry-exit: the optimal entry trigger for the long equals the optimal exit trigger of the short portfolio (table: kappa = 0.5 gives exit 1.9537, entry -0.4060 = short exit; etc.).
- Co-integrated log prices with short-term alpha (11.4): n assets with dY^k/Y^k = delta_k alpha_t dt + sigma dW, alpha_t = a_0 + sum a_i log Y^i; alpha is itself an OU process with kappa = -delta'a, theta = Tr(A Omega)/(2 delta' a) (need delta'a < 0). Exponential-utility investment in dollar positions pi: the DPE reduces, after cancellations, to a linear PDE for h(t, alpha) with solution h = E*[ (delta' Omega delta / 2 gamma) integral alpha_s^2 ds ] where, remarkably, under the risk-neutral measure (market price of risk lambda = -sigma^{-1} delta alpha), alpha is a Brownian motion with drift -Tr(A Omega)/2. Optimal investment: pi* = (1/gamma)(Omega^{-1} delta) alpha + (delta' Omega delta)[(tau/2) alpha + (Tr(A Omega)/4) tau^2] a: a Merton-like term scaling with the co-integration factor, plus a co-integration correction that decays as the horizon approaches. The value of the strategy is increasing in the expected integrated alpha^2.

### Ch 12: Order imbalance

Definition: rho_t = (V^b - V^a)/(V^b + V^a), quoted volume imbalance; the touch-only version offers the best predictive-power-to-complexity trade-off. ORCL Nov 1 2013: buy MOs are biased toward high-imbalance regimes (40.1% of buy MOs arrive in regime 4), total MO arrival is U-shaped in imbalance (MOs come when the book is one-sided); imbalance has significant autocorrelation over 200s lags and is positively correlated with subsequent price changes.

Markov chain model: 5 equally spaced regimes, MLE of the transition matrix is the empirical frequency A-hat_ij = n_ij / sum_j n_ij (Lagrange-multiplier proof); the estimated chain moves only between neighbours and the slightly bid-heavy regime is stickiest. Discrete-to-continuous conversion: B-hat = (1/Delta T) log(A-hat) (matrix logarithm), generator with outflow rates on the diagonal.

Joint modelling with MO arrivals: conditional on regime k, buy and sell MOs are independent Poisson with rates lambda_k^{+-} (doubly stochastic). Likelihood factorizes into within-epoch Poisson pieces and chain-transition pieces; MLEs: lambda-hat_i^{+-} = M-hat_i^{+-}/Delta tau_i, Lambda-hat_i = (sum_{j != i} n_ij)/Delta tau_i, P-hat_ij = n_ij/(sum_{j != i} n_ij). Estimated for ORCL: lambda+ rises from 0.037 (neutral) to 0.216 (buy-heavy); lambda- falls from 0.856 (sell-heavy) to 0.025 (buy-heavy); the day was sell-heavy overall, yet conditional on being in bid-heavy regimes, buy MOs dominate: order imbalance predicts order flow.

Price jumps: conditional distributions of 1-second midprice changes given MO side and prior imbalance regime (Table 12.1): buy MOs in buy-heavy regimes produce the most upward jumps (+0.01: 28%); sell MOs in sell-heavy regimes produce the most downward jumps (-0.01: 21%); ~60-80% of MOs still produce zero change. Neutral imbalance gives near-symmetric jumps.

Daily features: FDA view of activity mu(rho) as a random field projected on Legendre polynomials; mean curve and functional principal components describe how activity vs imbalance varies across the year.

Optimal liquidation with imbalance (12.4): three-state chain Z in {-1,0,+1} for the imbalance; midprice jumps at MO arrivals with regime-dependent jump distributions plus exogenous jumps between MOs (posts/cancellations). The DPE collapses (via ansatz x + qS + h(t,z,q)) to a coupled system with the drift mu(z) = lambda+(z)E[eps+] - lambda-(z)E[eps-] + eta+(z)E[eta+] - eta-(z)E[eta-] multiplying q, plus the standard depth optimization and chain transition terms. Optimal depth delta* = max(1/kappa - (h(t,z,q) - h(t,z,q-1)); 0). Behaviour: in the buy-heavy regime the agent posts deeper (ride the upward drift, avoid adverse selection); in the sell-heavy regime she posts closer to the midprice (capture price now before it falls). The regime state shifts the whole posting surface in real time.

## Appendix A (skim-level)

Standard stochastic calculus condensed: filtered probability space with usual conditions, cádlág versions; Brownian motion; Itô integral via left-point Riemann sums, Itô isometry, martingale property; general Itô formula and generators; Poisson and compound Poisson processes, compensated versions are martingales, Itô formulas for single jumps and jump-diffusions; doubly stochastic Poisson (Cox) processes with conditionally known intensity, stochastic-integral martingales, generator of (N, lambda); intensity models (Feller, jump-OU, jump-diffusion, Hawkes); Feynman-Kac theorem linking linear PDEs with discounted expectations of functionals of the underlying process.

## Durable lessons

1. The universal LO depth formula: delta* = 1/kappa + (reservation-price correction), where 1/kappa maximizes expected depth conditional on a fill. Every more elaborate model (utility, MOs, schedules, market making, imbalance) only modifies the correction term.
2. Exponential utility with risk aversion gamma is equivalent to a running inventory penalty phi = (1/2) gamma sigma^2; this one identity connects Merton risk aversion, execution urgency, and market-making inventory caps (proved three times: Ch 6, 8, 10).
3. Urgency phi is the single dial that trades expected P&L against P&L variance, in execution and in market making; the limiting cost of maximum urgency is the half-spread xi (cross immediately) and the permanent-impact loss (1/2) b N^2 is unavoidable by any schedule.
4. When order flow moves prices permanently, optimal execution adapts to expected future flow: slow down when others' buying pushes price up, speed up before others' selling pushes it down (Ch 9.4's imbalance term).
5. Never wait until the terminal date to cross the spread: face-lifting h(T-, q) = -q xi means MOs an instant before T always dominate walking the book at T.
6. POV targeting implies undershooting: with temporary impact k, the optimal strategy targets varphi/(k+varphi) rho mu, not rho mu; only as varphi -> infinity does it track exactly, at which point completion of N is no longer guaranteed. The two objectives (complete vs camouflage) generally do not commute.
7. A market maker's skew induces inventory mean reversion: posting asymmetric depths around the midprice is the mechanism, and the optimal resting inventory level tilts with the permanent-drift direction of the midprice (lambda+ vs lambda- asymmetry).
8. Adverse selection compensation is additive: with expected adverse jump epsilon, the optimal depth shifts by exactly epsilon; with short-term alpha, the strategy abandons the two-sided quote entirely when alpha is large, joining the move instead of providing liquidity into it.
9. Optimal pairs-trading bands are asymmetric around the mean (entry closer to the mean than exit, because discounting hits the later exit more), and the double-sided optimal entry for the long equals the optimal exit for the short.
10. Order imbalance predicts both order flow and price direction: the chain's regime shifts the arrival rates (lambda+ vs lambda-) and the jump distributions simultaneously; touch-only imbalance is the best complexity-adjusted predictor.
11. Empirically, round numbers attract executions (stops and momentum orders cluster at dollars/50c/10c), cancellations are ~half of all messages, and hidden orders are a third to a half of volume with negative effective spreads: the visible LOB understates true liquidity and activity.
12. Linear impact is a simplification: the book's own empirics give b/k ~ 2.5 with a positive cross-sectional relation, but the square-root and power-law impact evidence (Bouchaud) is a sharper description of reality; the control architecture survives, the impact functional form is the research frontier.

## Connections to prior books

- arbitrage-theory-bjork: Ch 5's DPP/DPE/verification machinery and the Merton example are the same stochastic control toolkit as Björk's optimal-control chapter, here applied to execution and market making rather than portfolio choice; the Girsanov change of measure used in Ch 11.4 (lambda = -sigma^{-1} delta alpha rendering assets martingales) is Björk's risk-neutral valuation logic applied to a statistical arbitrage strategy; the market price of risk uniqueness discussion in Ch 11 mirrors Björk's multifactor completeness conditions.
- mathematics-financial-markets-elliott-kopp: the variational inequalities and optimal stopping of Ch 11.3 are the continuous-time Snell envelope machinery of Elliott-Kopp Ch 5/8 in perpetual form; the perpetual-put smooth-pasting logic in Ch 8 of Elliott-Kopp is exactly the structure of the pairs exit problem; exponential-utility equivalence recalls Elliott-Kopp Ch 10's certainty-equivalence value-function relations.
- market-microstructure-harris, market-microstructure-stoll, market-microstructure-madhavan, biais-glosten-spatt-survey: Ch 2 re-derives Grossman-Miller, Kyle and Glosten-Milgrom from scratch; those books supply the deeper institutional and informational context; Harris's inventory and order-handling costs map onto phi and alpha here.
- market-liquidity-foucault: maker-taker fees, queue priority, and limit-order competition in Ch 1 are Foucault's ground; the fill-probability-vs-depth trade-off is the workhorse of both.
- empirical-microstructure-hasbrouck: Ch 3-4's impact regressions (permanent b vs temporary k) are Hasbrouck's vector-autoregression impact decomposition in reduced static form; the NBBO fragmentation discussion parallels Hasbrouck's price-discovery-across-venues agenda.
- trades-quotes-prices-bouchaud: the book's linear (b, k) impact is the local approximation of Bouchaud's square-root/power-law impact laws; Bouchaud's hidden-order and queue evidence explains the 33-56% hidden volume and cancellation shares found in Ch 4; both agree the LOB's visible depth understates true liquidity.
- volatility-arbitrage-javaheri: the filtered-state tricks (short-term alpha as a hidden mean-reverting factor, Markov-chain regime estimation) are the same state-space/filtering discipline Javaheri uses for stochastic volatility; Ch 12's MLE of chain generators is a discrete analog of SV likelihood estimation.
- analysis-financial-time-series-tsay: the VAR/cointegration estimation of Ch 3 and 11 (INTC/SMH) and the Markov-chain estimation of Ch 12 are direct applications of Tsay's multivariate time-series toolkit; duration/point-process modelling connects to Tsay's ACD models.
- computational-finance-levy, applied-quantitative-finance-hardle: the book's numerical layer (finite differences, Crank-Nicolson, iterative schemes for QVIs) is the same toolbox; Härdle's VaR chapters complement the risk framing.
- the-quants-patterson, dark-pools-patterson: the latency arms race (fibre vs microwave), Flash Crash stub quotes, and dark-pool crossing mechanics are the institutional backdrop those histories describe; Ch 7.4 formalizes the dark-pool venue choice the Patterson books narrate.
- elliott-wave-balan: the execution layer disciplines how weekly-chart decisions get implemented: impact-aware slicing, imbalance as a confirmation signal, and the optimal-stopping band logic of Ch 11 as the formal skeleton for entry/exit rules; Balan's stops are, formally, exercises of perpetual options, and Ch 11.3 shows how to price the band optimally rather than by convention.

## Strategy / research candidates (weekly NQ practice)

1. Impact-aware execution of weekly-chart decisions: even at small size, split NQ entries/exits across time (a mini AC schedule with urgency phi tuned to sigma) instead of crossing the spread in one clip; the 1/kappa + reservation-price logic says passive fills earn the half-spread, and the limit of maximum urgency costs exactly the half-spread.
2. Order-imbalance confirmation: compute touch-volume imbalance on the NQ book (or its proxy via delta/volume splits on the daily/weekly series); Ch 12's evidence says imbalance predicts both flow direction and next-move direction, with buy-heavy regimes tilting both arrival rates and jump distributions upward.
3. Optimal bands, not eyeballed bands: for any mean-reverting spread you trade (e.g. NQ vs ES basis, or an oscillator around a Balan wave count), use the Ch 11.3 perpetual OU machinery to set entry/exit triggers from kappa, sigma, costs c and discount rho, including the documented asymmetry (entry closer to mean than exit) instead of symmetric +/- 2 standard deviation bands.
4. Microprice as reference: when the NQ quote is one-sided, reference entries to the volume-weighted microprice rather than the mid; the round-number clustering of stops/liquidity demand (whole levels, halves) is a map of where resting liquidity and stop cascades live, useful for target placement.
5. Urgency dial as risk management: phi = (1/2) gamma sigma^2 identifies your effective risk aversion; in volatile weeks (high sigma) the equivalent urgency rises, which front-loads exits exactly as an inventory-penalty model prescribes, a coherent alternative to discretionary de-risking.
6. Flow-aware liquidation: when unwinding, slow down when the market's own buying is pushing price up and accelerate when the flow turns sell-heavy (Ch 9.4's imbalance term and Ch 12's regime-dependent depths); the stop-loss firework at round numbers is the empirical footprint of everyone else getting this wrong.
7. Adverse-selection self-check before providing liquidity: if you rest orders, demand epsilon of extra depth for the expected adverse move against you (Ch 10.4.1), and pull the far-side quote when short-term alpha jumps against it (Ch 10.4.2's regime logic).
8. Venue/fragmentation caveat: NQ executions on CME are less fragmented than US equities, but the book's NBBO lesson generalizes: the visible book is not the whole book (hidden/iceberg volume), so effective liquidity is better than displayed depth at the touch.

# Algorithmic Trading & DMA (Barry Johnson)

---
source: "Algorithmic Trading & DMA: An introduction to direct access trading strategies, Barry Johnson, 4Myeloma Press 2010, upload 5"
status: complete
