# Algorithmic Trading & DMA (Barry Johnson, 2010)

## What this book is

The practitioner's counterpart to Cartea-Jaimungal-Penalva. Where Cartea derives optimal execution schedules from stochastic control, Johnson explains how the industry actually implements them: the full stack from order types and matching mechanics, through the trading algorithm taxonomy (TWAP/VWAP/POV/IS and descendants), transaction cost analysis, execution tactics, infrastructure, portfolio and multi-asset extensions, news handling, and finally data mining and AI. The author is a software developer with 12+ years in investment bank trading systems, and the book reads like an engineering manual: every concept is tied to how an order actually flows from a portfolio manager to a matching engine.

## Chapter 1: Overview

- Execution methods ladder: manual trading → DMA (direct market access, client's own orders on the exchange) → sponsored access → algorithmic trading ("low touch", broker's engine works the order) → crossing venues.
- Agency vs principal trading. Trading types reviewed: institutional, quantitative, portfolio/basket/program, systematic, HFT, statistical arbitrage.
- Electronic trading milestones: Instinet 1969, NASDAQ 1971, POSIT 1987, GLOBEX 1992, Eurex fully electronic 1998, Reg NMS/MiFID 2007.
- Three generations of trading algorithms: (1) order slicing with schedules (TWAP, VWAP), (2) TCA-driven cost/risk balancing (implementation shortfall, the trader's dilemma), (3) liquidity-seeking and opportunistic (dark pools, smart routing, adaptive).
- Order sizes have collapsed (NYSE average ~2500 shares in the 1990s to ~300 by late 2000s); decimalisation (2001) compressed spreads; algo adoption keeps growing.

## Chapter 2: Microstructure essentials

- Informed vs liquidity traders; liquidity as depth, tightness (spread), and resiliency. Persaud: liquidity needs diversity of opinion; uniform models create liquidity black holes.
- Market types: quote-driven (dealer), order-driven (auction), hybrid; continuous vs periodic (call auctions) vs request-driven (RFQ/RFS); upstairs bilateral and dark pools.
- Matching rules: price/time priority (FIFO) vs price/pro-rata (rewards size, first-order preference) with worked examples. Tick size and pennying.
- Circuit breakers, halts, transparency (pre-trade vs post-trade), Stoll's centralisation vs fragmentation economics.
- Clearing and settlement: CCP novation, netting, T+5 to T+0 evolution.
- Transaction cost benchmarks: pre-trade (decision price, arrival, previous close, open), intraday (OHLC, TWAP, VWAP), post-trade (close). Realized spread uses a later mid (e.g. 5 minutes) to capture reversal.
- Explicit costs (commissions, fees, taxes) vs implicit (timing, delay, market impact, opportunity cost). Market impact = temporary (immediacy demand) + permanent (information content).

## Chapter 3: World markets

- Asset class survey: equities, fixed income, FX, money markets, derivatives, ADRs/GDRs/ETFs.
- Market structure evolution: dealer platforms → multi-dealer → exchanges/IDBs. Inter-dealer venues (ICAP BrokerTec, BGC eSpeed, MTS, EBS, Reuters), dealer-to-client (Tradeweb, MarketAxess, FXAll, Currenex), ECNs/MTFs (BATS, Direct Edge, Chi-X, Turquoise), dark pool ATS taxonomy (scheduled, blind, negotiated, advertised, internal crossing).
- Global trends: latency race (300ms to microseconds, co-location), transparency trade-off (Schwartz/Pagano: price discovery wants transparency, quantity discovery wants opacity), buy-side direct access, centralisation-fragmentation cycles (~40 US equity venues by late 2000s), and market size context ($208T global debt, $613T derivatives notional).

## Chapter 4: Orders

- Market vs limit orders: demand vs supply liquidity; walking the book costs the half-spread. Marketable / at / behind the market.
- Optional instructions: duration (GFD/GTD/GTC), auction variants (MOO/LOO/MOC/LOC, imbalance-only), fill conditions (IOC, FOK, AON, minimum volume), preferencing, routing (do-not-route, inter-market sweeps, flash orders), linking (OCO, OTO), short-sale ticks, odd lots.
- Hybrid orders: market-to-limit, market-with-protection (CME protection points), conditional stops (stop-limit, trailing with price latching, if-touched), hidden orders (fully hidden, iceberg with display size, pinging with IOC), discretionary (not-held, discretionary range), pegged (bid/offer/mid with offset), scale orders, routed (pass-through, NASDAQ MOPP/DOTI/SKIP/SCAN/STGY), crossing (committed/uncommitted/negotiated, IOIs), order-contingent (linked "OR" orders, contingent spread orders, implied IN/OUT orders: NYMEX 44.6% and CME Eurodollars 16% of trades via implied orders).

## Chapter 5: Trading algorithm taxonomy

- Macro/micro decomposition: the algorithm sets a target trajectory; execution tactics place the child orders.
- Impact-driven: TWAP (time slicing, randomisation, tilt factors), VWAP (historical volume profiles, target per period = u_j·X, participation within 1bp achievable), POV (dynamic participation with 1/(1−p) adjustment, chasing risk), minimal impact (dark routing, visibility parameter).
- Cost-driven: Implementation Shortfall (optimal horizon from volatility, risk aversion, size; tilted VWAP or tilted POV implementations), Adaptive Shortfall (AIM: aggressive in the money, adapt up when price is favourable; PIM: passive in the money; Kissell: AIM gets better prices but higher risk), Market Close algorithms (optimal start time, Cushing-Madhavan close sensitivity and auction imbalances).
- Opportunistic: price inline (AIM/PIM participation adjustments), liquidity-driven (virtual order book aggregation across venues, hidden liquidity detection, pinging, signalling risk controls), pair trading (spread vs ratio triggers, 2-SD bands, legging limits).
- Other: multi-leg (bond barbells, futures calendars/butterflies/condors, option spreads), volatility-driven (implied vol order books, cheapness in vol terms), GWAP (gamma-weighted average price with adjusted delta).

## Chapter 6: Transaction cost analysis

- Kissell: costs are paid by buyers but not received by sellers. Nicoll: ~$120bn/year US equity implicit costs.
- Perold implementation shortfall; Leinweber's Value Line fund: 26.2% paper vs 16.1% actual returns.
- Worked example (buy 50k XYZ): close benchmark 37bps, VWAP −23bps, decision price −263bps. RPM (Relative Performance Measure: % of volume traded at less favourable prices).
- Expanded shortfall decomposition: delay cost (Wagner-Glass), spread, temporary + permanent impact, price trend, timing risk, opportunity cost. Example total: 307bps of which impact only 30bps.
- ITG global cost tables: US large cap 35bps (2007) to 62bps (2008 crisis). Crisis conditions double costs.

## Chapter 7: Optimal trading strategies

- Wagner's difficulty factors: order size vs ADV (>20-25% of ADV is large), liquidity, volatility, momentum, urgency, horizon, time of day.
- Almgren-Chriss efficient trading frontier: minimise E(cost) + λ·V(risk); convex frontier in cost-variance space; λ is the risk aversion dial (0-3 normalised, roughly linear in risk concern, tied to trading time).
- Benchmark choice shifts the frontier: VWAP can minimise both delay cost and risk via even participation.
- The trader's dilemma: minimise cost for a given risk, or get price improvement for a given cost, or balance the trade-off.
- Mapping algorithms onto the frontier: TWAP sits off-frontier (above VWAP in cost), POV left of VWAP, IS left of POV, Market Close left of IS, AIM right of POV (cheaper but riskier), PIM left, pairs hard to place.
- Algorithm selection decision tree: urgent + liquid → IS/AS; large + liquid → VWAP/POV; illiquid → liquidity-driven. Crisis conditions: spreads doubled, displayed liquidity down 37%, optimal algo choice saves ~60bps (Mittal-Wong).
- Crossing evidence: POSIT +4bps vs dark algorithms −4bps (Domowitz et al); multiple dark pool use leaks information; performance degrades with execution duration.

## Chapter 8: Order placement

- Price formation: fair value (DCF/Gordon) vs market price; inventory models (quotes shift with position); information models (Copeland-Galai options framework, adverse selection; Taiwan: 25% of spread is valuation, 75% adverse selection).
- Order book data: Biais-Hillion-Spatt: thin book → passive orders; deep book → aggressive. Ranaldo: aggressiveness rises with own-side depth.
- Matching mechanics: price/time vs pro-rata worked examples; CME Eurodollar two-stage allocation; NYSE Priority-Parity-Yielding; mid-point matches; call auction crossing algorithm (max executable volume → min surplus → consistent pressure → reference price).
- Order aggressiveness: Biais-Hillion-Spatt 6-category classification. Market factors: spread (wider → less aggressive), book depth/height/imbalance (aggressive when own side deeper), trade flow, volatility (transient vs fundamental), price momentum (EMA ratio MOM), tick size (Harris: larger ticks → more order exposure), time of day (limit orders morning, market orders at close; hidden orders increase toward close).
- Hidden liquidity: NASDAQ ~10.4% hidden ADV, Euronext up to 44%; spotting via trade-vs-book discrepancies; iceberg display size ~10% of parent (Esser-Mönch Xetra); estimating via ITG regression models.
- Execution probability: Lo-MacKinlay-Zhang survival analysis; ITG limit order model: at bid 25% filled within 2 min, 70% within 30 min; certainty costs ~$23.15. Dark pool hit rates decline before the close (Mittal-Taur).

## Chapter 9: Execution tactics

- Algorithms are the strategy; tactics are the mini-algorithms placing child orders (seconds/minutes horizon).
- Impact-driven tactics: slicing (with randomisation), hiding (synthetic vs native iceberg: native retains price priority and participates in crossing; synthetic misses fills awaiting confirmations), display size balance.
- Price/risk-driven: layering (maintains time priority across levels, good for dense books and illiquid assets), pegging (with randomisation; momentum contribution and queue-position loss risks, Brown-Holden), catching (one-directional price latch + trigger limit to cut losses).
- Opportunistic: seeking (IOC/FOK probing for hidden liquidity, feedback into models), sniping (take less than full size to avoid disturbing the spread), routing (virtual order book decomposition into venue-specific children), pairing (legging limits, contingent emulation, least liquid asset drives).
- Algorithm-tactic matrix (Table 9-2). Factors: average spreads by venue (S&P E-mini ~2bps on CME, NASDAQ ~7bps), average trade sizes (E-mini most common trade = single lot), latency (high latency → layering/catching instead of pegging).

## Chapter 10: Enhancing trading strategies

- Short-term prediction turns reactive algorithms proactive: better prices via more passive placement.
- Price prediction: trade imbalance (ASX last-30-trades), best bid/offer size imbalance (Yang ordered probit; Maslov-Mills), order book imbalance I_j and weighted price measures (Cao-Hansch-Wang: linked to returns; effects last under 1 minute; Farmer et al LSE: 85% of price-changing market orders match best-price size, price jumps caused by book gaps, not order volume).
- Volume prediction: historical profiles (stable for liquid assets), seasonality (Hong-Yu "Gone Fishin'": Q3 volumes −7.9%, Europe −15.6%), day-of-week (Monday low, mid-week peak), holidays, volume decomposition (Lo-Wang two-factor turnover; PCA into market + idiosyncratic; Bialkowski-Darolles-Le Fol: SETAR beats ARMA beats simple average).
- Liquidity prediction: depth grows through the day (Xetra, Euronext, Spanish SIBE 3-phase pattern: low-liquidity open, improving middle, US-open surge at 15:30 CET).
- Volatility: EMA/ARMA/GARCH review (Poon-Granger: implied volatility beats them all), volatility smiles/skews (fat tails; equities skewed left, FX/commodities symmetric), VIX (October 2008: 89 record).
- Cost estimation: Almgren et al 2005 model: permanent impact power α≈0.89 (≈linear), temporary β≈0.60 (concave, 3/5 power), liquidity factor δ≈0.27, universal coefficients γ≈0.314, η≈0.142; worked IBM/DRI example. Kissell-Glanz cost allocation: 95% of impact is temporary, 5% permanent regardless of strategy; instantaneous impact functions (linear/non-linear/power variants; non-linear fits best).
- Special events: on/off-the-run Treasuries (volume drops 90% within days of going off-the-run), futures roll (lead switches at ~8 days to expiry for S&P; volume and open interest migrate; open-interest change adjusts volume data, Holmes-Rougier), witching days (opening volume up to 7-10x; excess volatility dissipates by ~10am; NASDAQ's 2004 opening call auction improved efficiency), index rebalances (S&P 500 additions +5.12% on announcement, +8.37% by effective date, permanent; deletions −8% to −14%, reverting within 60 days; option prices move up to 10 days before announcement as a leading indicator), trading halts vs price limits vs volatility auctions (halt reopening call price is a good predictor; halts preferred to price limits; volatility auctions recover fastest), corporate announcements (unanticipated news preceded by abnormal volume; late reports signal bad news: 1 penny/share below consensus per day of delay).

## Chapter 11: Infrastructure

- OMS (portfolio management, accounting, risk, allocations) vs EMS (real-time benchmarks, order book depth, DMA/algorithms, pre-trade analytics); converging.
- Order entry: fat-finger sanity checks (price vs market, size vs ADV, total value).
- FIX protocol: tag=value messages, FIXML (70% larger), TimeInForce values, OrdType values, Triggering Instructions block, discretionary/pegged/iceberg tags, algorithm specification (TargetStrategy, NoStrategyParameters, FIXatdl XML for parameters and UI), order lists and portfolio BidRequest mechanisms, session management (sequence numbers, heartbeats, PossDupFlag).
- Latency: total = order path (client→broker→venue) and price path (venue→vendor→feed handler); measure at peak load, not best case; co-location; London-NY 68ms, London-Tokyo 246ms round-trip benchmarks.
- Capacity: OPRA >1.2 million messages/second (tripled in two years); feed handlers must cope.
- Platform design: event-driven, object-oriented (trading algorithm, order manager, market data subscriptions, order book class, destination wrappers); rules coded as event handlers (onOrderUpdate, onMarketDataUpdate patterns); scripted rules (Apama Event Modeler state machines).
- Testing: unit tests per rule, replayed historical data, extreme-condition datasets, back-testing with realistic market reaction (simulators: Penn Exchange Simulator, Allied Testing SIM), live shadow testing before release. Compliance: sanity checks, session/halt awareness, kill-switch requirements.

## Chapter 12: Portfolios

- Markowitz: portfolio volatility is nonlinear in constituents; σ_p = √(w'Ωw); diversification is the only free lunch; 15+ stocks exhausts most benefit (Malkiel); correlation ρ=0.5 gives 25% risk elimination.
- Risk decomposition: systematic (beta) vs specific; Fama-French factors; factor breakdowns via Russell reconstitution (style Value/Growth factor can dominate).
- Measures: beta (Cov/Var), tracking error (std dev of active returns), marginal contribution to risk MCR_i = (Ωw)_i/σ_p = β_i·σ_p; trade high-MCR positions faster, negative-MCR positions are natural hedges, trade them slower.
- Portfolio impact is additive (no diversification discount on impact costs); timing risk adds liquidity risk to residual price risk.
- Portfolio trading goals: cash balancing, beta/market neutrality, sector/country neutrality, tracking error minimisation (net beta 1.0); worked examples with beta-weighted targets.
- Optimal single-asset risk-reduction trade: w_optimal = A·B·w (Kissell-Glanz), recompute after each trade; worked example sells 62 ABC to cut risk from $21.17 to $14.74.
- One-size-fits-all fails: Domowitz-Yegerman transition study, costs double estimates; classify orders by volatility × size/ADV into aggressive (IS), passive (VWAP), opportunistic buckets.
- Portfolio algorithms: shared risk model adjusting each constituent's schedule (Instinet Wizard PRO; CAL/AMR natural hedge traded passively all day; Credit Suisse PHD cuts tracking error vs POV and VWAP); "algorithm of algorithms" (Kissell-Freyre-Sanders-Carrie): master portfolio algorithm dynamically retunes participation rates/tilts/risk aversion of constituent algorithms via MCR.

## Chapter 13: Multi-asset trading

- Drivers: hedge funds seeking alpha across classes, sell-side desk consolidation, venue cross-listing, MiFID, prime brokerage extension.
- Strategy types: utility (FX cash legs sized to foreign settlement, automated short covering via stock lending/repos), structured (principal protected notes = zero-coupon bond + call options), hedging, arbitrage.
- Hedging: market risk with index futures: N = −β_P·V_P/(P_F·m), worked examples (short $5m S&P basket, sell 13 futures; long hedge with β=1.5, buy 19); interest rate risk with duration/DV01: N = −DV01_bond/DV01_future × S/F (sell 83 futures to hedge $10m portfolio), CTD conversion factor, convexity and basis risk caveats.
- Greeks: delta hedging (call delta Φ(d1), put Φ(d1)−1; daily rebalancing worked example; Sinclair bands: time/delta/price-triggered re-hedging, short positions hedged defensively, longs "let run"); gamma hedging with offsetting options (worked example: net gamma −10 offset with 125 cheap OTM calls, rebalance delta from 15 to 4 shares); vega/theta/rho spillover risk.
- Arbitrage: ADR vs local share (Gagnon-Karolyi: small caps and emerging markets show real gaps; administration fees cut opportunities); basis trading (B = S − F, cost of carry C = funding + storage − income, convergence to zero at expiry, normal backwardation vs contango); index arbitrage (fair value worked example: S&P SEP07 cost of carry 12.78 index points, FV 1505.67; cash-and-carry and reverse cash-and-carry worked P&L tables; SOQ settlement; >70% of positions closed early per Sofianos; index futures more often underpriced due to short-selling costs per Shalen); ETF arbitrage (creation/redemption mechanism; ETF cash leg costs 18bps vs 68bps for the full basket per Economopoulos; dividends accumulated and discounted 45 days); put-call parity (C + Xe^(−rt) = P + S; conversion/reversal worked examples with $100 locked profit); put-call-futures parity (Markose-Er; Black effect limits efficiency); dividend arbitrage (deep ITM puts lock in ex-div profit, worked examples; covered call variant risks early assignment).
- Multi-asset algorithm factors: liquidity (least liquid asset drives), latency (450ms mismatch example), transparency, consistency (compare prices net of asset-specific costs), dependency (lead/lag as prediction). MCR-based prioritisation extends to mixed lists.

## Chapter 14: News

- News classes: global/regional, macroeconomic, corporate. US macro calendar: Non-Farm Payrolls, CPI/PPI, Fed Funds Target, GDP, ISM, retail sales, etc., at fixed times (mostly 8:30 ET).
- Digitisation: screen scraping → category-coded headlines → elementised XML (Dow Jones Elementized News Feed) → XBRL/RIXML machine-readable reports; social media as early source (blog predicted Google's Q4 miss; NCI posting beat Genentech's own press release by 4 hours, 25% price jump); fraud caution (Lucent fake postings, $7bn market cap drop; pump-and-dump).
- Computerised handling: filtering, clustering, association (dependency models; Cohen-Frazzini: Callaway's 30% crash took ~1 month to propagate to supplier Coastcast, 18.6% annualised from customer-momentum strategy), sentiment (Seo-Giampapa-Sycara bigrams ~75% accuracy ceiling; Infonic/Reuters NewsScope hybrid linguistic + machine learning), interpretation (feed into asset pricing models), news mining (chaining stories to measure new information; EntreMed 330% jump on republished old news, front page vs page 28).
- Market reactions, macro: Non-Farm Payrolls dominates everything (a 1 SD surprise ≈ 89% of daily volatility for the 10-year note); rankings by price and volume (Fed Funds Target tops volume); price moves immediate and mostly complete within 5-60 minutes; volumes spike up to 400% (bills) and stay elevated 1-2.5 hours; spreads widen and recover in 30 min to 2 hours; "calm before the storm" volume lull before announcements; stronger-than-expected US news → dollar appreciation.
- Corporate: earnings announcements cluster news; good news +1.7%, bad −1% overnight, much in the opening auction; drift lasts 10+ days (Tetlock); news-attached declines keep drifting, no-news declines revert (Chan); late announcements signal bad news (44.5% vs 36.1%); liquidity effects mixed by market structure; volatility higher for bad news and lasts longer.
- Surprise quantification: (actual − consensus)/SD; procyclical indicators hurt bond prices, countercyclical help. Timeliness matters: two-month-old Trade Balance data moves prices little. Business cycle matters: news against the trend has the most impact (Veredas: negative surprises in expansion cause the largest drops); ISM >55 = cycle top, <50 = bottom.
- News in execution: news flow (volume of headlines predicts volatility spikes; Relegence heat index), sentiment aggregates (index-level sentiment correlates ~0.48 with returns and Granger-causes them, Das-Chen), surprise factors; news-adaptive algorithms (trade less aggressively on good news when selling); news-driven algorithms behave like conditional orders, essentially information-triggered stops.

## Chapter 15: Data mining and AI

- Conventional AI (top-down rules; a trading algorithm is an expert system) vs computational intelligence (bottom-up: neural nets, evolutionary computation, fuzzy logic).
- Data mining: classification/clustering, time-series mining, association rules. Precautions: association ≠ causality (lemon imports vs pedestrian fatalities, ρ = −0.965 in-sample only), Texas sharpshooter fallacy, small samples, over-fitting (Leinweber: Bangladesh butter production + sheep populations "explain" S&P 500 with R² = 0.99), out-of-sample testing on both temporal and cross-sectional held-out data.
- Relationships: intra-market (Kallberg-Pasquariello excess co-movement, only 27% explained by fundamentals, negatively related to volatility and rates; Hou: large firms lead small; supply-chain momentum upstream ≈ 2x cross-industry, downstream ≈ 1.5x; Cohen-Frazzini customer effect; Hong-Torous-Valkanov: banking, real estate lead the market by a month), inter-market (European indices correlate more at the US open; IT stocks globally responsive), cross-asset lead/lag (cost-efficient index futures/options lead cash; stocks lead single-stock options; stocks lead CDS leads bonds; FX spot leads FX futures; longer-dated commodity futures lead shorter), crises and contagion (correlation rises in down markets, Longin-Solnik; correlation increases reduce diversification exactly when needed; flight to quality; sovereign downgrades spread 12bps to other countries).
- Techniques: k-NN; neural networks (perceptrons, feed-forward vs recurrent, back propagation, black box problem, sizing rule of thumb: half to double the input count for hidden nodes); SVMs (optimal separating plane, quadratic programming gives global optimum, no local minima); genetic algorithms (fitness, crossover, mutation, immigration) and genetic programming (tree-based rule evolution).
- Evidence: ANN/SVM price predictions 65-73% directional accuracy across many markets (S&P 500, Nikkei, FX, futures, options; ANN beats Black-Scholes on option pricing with historical vol); volatility: ANN-GARCH hybrids beat GARCH, SVM comparable to best GARCH and better in some regimes; volume: ANN beats ARIMA for futures volume. Ensembles/boosting combine weak models into stronger forecasts.
- Application to execution: augment limit order models and cost models with short-term forecasts; PIM vs AIM is implicitly a bet on trend vs reversion; volume forecasts feed VWAP/POV/IS horizon choices.
- Strategy generation: GP tree rules for order placement (spread below average, own-side depth < 2x order size, volatility 10% above normal → passive limit); EDDIE system for index return prediction; arbitrage rule evolution.
- Back-testing: deterministic vs stochastic simulation, artificial markets (zero-intelligence agents with budget constraints reach ~97% allocative efficiency; ZIP traders; electronic agents beat humans by ~20% in continuous double auctions, Das et al), real-data simulators (Penn-Lehman PLAT, Allied Testing SIM) that adjust historical data in response to your test orders and model competition.
- Verdict: markets are harder than chess; incremental gains over statistical models are real but modest; forecast quality still determines usefulness.

## Epilogue

Algorithmic trading survived the 2007-09 crisis without systemic algorithm failures; adoption keeps rising across asset classes; algorithms keep evolving toward adaptivity, portfolio awareness, and cross-asset capability. Consolidation and fragmentation of venues continue in waves.

## Appendices A-F (world market reference)

- A Equities: US leads adoption (55% of institutional flow via DMA/algo/crossing/program in 2008); NYSE/NASDAQ market share and the rise of BATS/Direct Edge; Reg NMS trade-through and fair access rules; specialists → DMMs.
- B Fixed income: dealer-centric, RFQ/RFS dominated, e-trading growing (BrokerTec, eSpeed, MTS, Tradeweb, MarketAxess).
- C FX: $3.2T daily turnover (2007), >56% electronic; EBS vs Reuters split by currency pair; dealer-to-client platforms (FXAll ~$60bn/day, Currenex); ECNs (Hotspot, LavaFX); failed FXMarketSpace exchange.
- D Money markets: T-bills, Fed funds, LIBOR deposits, commercial paper, repos (GC vs special, haircut mechanics), stock lending (recall risk).
- E Derivatives: futures and options market statistics, listed vs OTC; implied orders; interest rate swaps and CDS e-trading.
- F ADRs/ETFs: depositary receipt types and conversion; ETF creation/redemption keeps price at NAV; SPY/QQQQ/IWM volume leaders; ETFs reached ~1/3 of US equity volume in late 2008.

## Durable lessons

1. Execution is a cost/risk optimisation problem, not an afterthought. The efficient trading frontier frames every trade: pay impact to reduce timing risk, or accept risk to reduce impact. Lambda (risk aversion) is the dial that sets the trade-off.
2. Market impact is mostly temporary (Kissell-Glanz: ~95%), which means slow, even participation recovers most cost; but the ~5% permanent part is information leakage and cannot be traded away.
3. Impact is concave in size (Almgren et al: temporary exponent ~0.6; square-root-ish), so doubling size raises cost by less than double; splitting over time T reduces temporary impact as (X/VT)^0.6 but extends exposure.
4. Benchmark choice determines measured success before any execution skill: decision price (implementation shortfall) is the honest measure; VWAP rewards even participation; close benchmarks create auction game theory.
5. Benchmark gaming is real: algorithms are designed to beat their benchmark (TWAP front-runs its own slices; Market Close algorithms optimise start time). Judge algorithms against your objective, not their favourite benchmark.
6. The aggressiveness decision is driven by the state of the book: spread, own-side depth, opposite-side depth, momentum, volatility, tick size, time of day (Biais-Hillion-Spatt framework).
7. Price jumps are caused by book gaps, not trade size (Farmer et al: 85% of price-changing market orders match the best-price size). Reading the gap structure beats reading volume.
8. Hidden liquidity is a large, estimable fraction of depth (10-44% by venue); iceberg display size ~10% of parent; execution probability is a function of price distance and time, and falls before the close.
9. Signalling risk: every visible order reveals intent. Tactics exist to minimise it (slicing, randomisation, hidden orders, layering, pegging with random offsets). On low-latency venues use time-priority tactics; on high-latency links pegging loses to layering/catching.
10. Portfolio context changes optimal execution: trade high-MCR (marginal contribution to risk) names faster, negative-MCR names slower; natural hedges permit passive trading of volatile pairs; the largest risk contributors are not always the riskiest single names.
11. News reactions are fast, quantifiable and mostly complete within minutes for easy-to-interpret surprises (macro numbers, earnings vs consensus); the size of the surprise, its timeliness, and the business-cycle phase all scale the impact.
12. Event calendars are tradable information: futures rolls, witching, index rebalances, on/off-the-run transitions, halt mechanics. Each has a documented, persistent volume/volatility fingerprint.
13. Data mining finds associations, not causes. Guard against over-fitting with held-out data, both temporal and cross-sectional; the more variables you mine, the more spurious R² you will find (Leinweber's butter).
14. Correlation is not constant: it rises in crises and downward moves (Longin-Solnik, Ang-Chen), destroying diversification exactly when it is needed. Any hedged or diversified structure must be stress-tested for correlation regime shifts.
15. AI methods (NN, SVM, GA/GP) provide modest but real forecasting improvements over linear/statistical models, especially for direction and volatility; their value in execution is feeding limit-order and cost models, not replacing the rule framework.
16. Back-testing without a market reaction simulator is optimistic: your own orders consume liquidity and reprice the book. Simulators that respond to test orders (PLAT-style) are the minimum standard for honest performance claims.
17. Infrastructure is strategy: latency components, message capacity, failover, and FIX semantics all bound what any algorithm can achieve. Measure latency at peak load.
18. Every arbitrage is a hedging problem with a cost model: fair value = cash + cost of carry; profit exists only when mispricing exceeds spreads, borrow costs, funding asymmetry, and assignment/regulatory risk. This is why simple arbitrages die first as electronification spreads.

## Connections to prior books

- Cartea-Jaimungal-Penalva: this is the practitioner mirror of Cartea's mathematical execution theory. The same ladder appears (TWAP/VWAP/POV/IS), but where Cartea derives optimal schedules from HJB equations with running penalty φ, Johnson presents the λ of Almgren-Chriss as that same urgency dial, plus the implementation details (tactics, venue choice, FIX) that theory abstracts away. Johnson's 95/5 temporary/permanent split is a calibration of Cartea's temporary f(ν) and permanent g(ν) impact functions.
- Harris, Stoll, Madhavan, Biais-Glosten-Spatt, Foucault: Chapters 2 and 8 are the applied digest of the microstructure canon. Biais-Hillion-Spatt's aggressiveness classification features directly; Persaud's liquidity black holes, Stoll's fragmentation economics, Madhavan's cost model requirements all reappear in operational form.
- Hasbrouck: the TCA and impact measurement chapters operationalise Hasbrouck's VAR framework; his 1991 square-root impact finding is cited as the empirical basis against linear impact.
- Bouchaud (Trades, Quotes and Prices): the concave impact functions, hidden order shares, and gap-driven price changes here are the same stylised facts; Johnson's Farmer et al citation (gaps cause jumps) is the bridge to Bouchaud's order-book empirics.
- Tsay: volume decomposition (market vs idiosyncratic via PCA), ARMA/SETAR volume forecasting, GARCH volatility review, and the VAR lead/lag studies in Chapter 15 are direct applications of the time-series toolkit.
- Bjork: the Almgren-Chriss efficient frontier is mean-variance optimisation applied to a trading path, the same machinery Bjork builds for Merton; Johnson skips the derivations and presents the solution family.
- Elliott-Kopp: covariance, correlation and the Markowitz machinery from their chapter reappear as the portfolio risk engine here, now coupled to execution scheduling.
- Javaheri: volatility estimation (implied vs realised, smiles/skews, VIX) and the delta/gamma hedging sections overlap with Javaheri's option-risk material; Sinclair's hedging bands are borrowed directly.
- Levy and Härdle: numerical implementation (finite difference, simulation) and the data-mining chapter extend their computational finance coverage.
- The Quants and Dark Pools (Patterson): the venue landscape, dark pool taxonomy and due diligence questions, crisis-era statistics, and the AI chapters are the same ecosystem seen from the practitioner's seat.
- Master-the-Markets (Williams): VSA's effort-vs-result reading maps onto the order book imbalance and trade-flow analysis of Chapter 10; Johnson supplies the quantitative instrumentation for what Williams reads off a chart.
- Elliott Wave base (Balan): execution of a weekly-chart decision is exactly where this book applies: position sizing vs ADV, exit urgency (λ), benchmark accounting (implementation shortfall on your own trades), and event calendars (roll dates, witching) around weekly bars.

## Strategy / research candidates (weekly NQ practice)

1. Implementation shortfall accounting on your own NQ trades: record the decision price (the level your weekly analysis flagged) separately from the fill price; the gap is your true execution cost. Track it per trade to separate analysis error from execution error.
2. Urgency dial for exits: adopt the Almgren-Chriss framing. When exiting a position, decide in advance the risk aversion (fast and costly vs slow and exposed). A weekly-chart trade does not need intraday execution heroics: a participation-based exit over a defined window usually beats "at market" fills by holding most of the 95% temporary-impact component.
3. Event calendar overlay on weekly bars: NQ quarterly roll (about 8 days before expiry volume migrates), quad witching Fridays (open/close volume up to 7-10x, excess volatility gone by 10am ET), FOMC and Non-Farm Payroll days (8:30/14:15 ET spikes). Avoid placing stop exits blindly into these windows; the volatility fingerprints are documented and persistent.
4. E-mini execution facts as calibration: average spread ~2bps, most common trade size a single lot. For a retail-scale NQ account, impact is negligible but the spread is not: a market order costs ~1 tick, which on a weekly horizon is noise, but it compounds across repeated adjustments. Prefer resting limit orders at reference levels; your fill probability logic is Chapter 8's (distance from touch, time of day, falling fill rates near the close).
5. Microprice/imbalance as entry confirmation: trade imbalance and best bid/offer size imbalance have sub-minute predictive value (Cao-Hansch-Wang, Hellström-Simonsen). For weekly decisions, use them only to time the execution within the day (avoid entering on a book already tilted against you), not as a signal generator.
6. Round-number awareness: executions cluster at whole numbers (Johnson Ch 10 in Cartea; here via stop-loss/momentum liquidity demand). Weekly-chart stops and targets placed at obvious levels coincide with liquidity pools; expect faster fills and sharper reversals exactly there. Offset targets by a tick or two where fill quality matters more than the round level.
7. Temporary vs permanent impact discipline: on liquid NQ, your own orders are almost entirely temporary impact. Do not mistake a self-induced move for market movement: if a working order sweeps several levels, wait for the reversion (temporary impact decays) before concluding the market has trended.
8. Correlation regime check for hedges: if you ever hedge NQ with correlated futures (ES, YM) or spread trade them, remember correlations rise in down moves (Longin-Solnik; Ang-Chen). A spread that looks low-risk in a bull phase will tighten exactly during the drawdown. Size spread positions to crisis-phase correlation, not average correlation.
9. Cost model for any scaling plan: if position sizes ever grow, apply the Almgren et al functional forms: permanent impact roughly linear in participation, temporary impact concave (~(X/VT)^0.6). Splitting a larger order over more time cuts temporary impact with diminishing returns while linear exposure risk accumulates; the optimal horizon is a genuine computation, not intuition.
10. Volatility forecasting for exit windows: use realised (15-min or daily) and implied (VXN, the NASDAQ-100 volatility index) together; implied anticipates event risk that historical vol misses (the CVTX example). Choose low-vol intraday windows for passive exits and expect limit fill rates to fall as volatility rises.
11. News-adaptive behaviour on scheduled macro releases: NFP and FOMC move index futures immediately with effects complete within minutes to an hour. Never carry resting stop orders sized for normal conditions across an 8:30 ET release; either flatten the tactical layer or widen stops to announcement-day volatility (volatility can exceed 2x normal for 30-45 minutes).
12. Sentiment/flow as context only: index-level sentiment correlates with returns and can Granger-cause them (Das-Chen), but at the individual-asset level the relationship is unreliable. For a weekly NQ practice, sentiment is background for contrarian checks at extremes, not a timing tool.

## Status

Complete. All 595 pages read (Parts I-IV, Chapters 1-15, epilogue, appendices A-F, abbreviations and references).

# Analysis of Financial Time Series (Tsay, 2nd ed. 2005) — Teaching Notes

The full econometrics backbone for everything else in this knowledge base. Where Levy gave GARCH as one chapter of a computational book and Härdle gave research papers, Tsay is the systematic textbook: returns → linear models → volatility → nonlinear models → microstructure → continuous time → VaR/EVT → multivariate systems → factor models → multivariate volatility → state space → Bayesian MCMC. Read cover to cover (all 12 chapters, appendices and index skimmed).

## Ch 1: Asset returns and distributions

Simple return R_t = (P_t + D_t − P_{t−1})/P_{t−1}; log return r_t = ln(1+R_t). Log returns are additive across time, simple returns additive across assets (portfolio return is the wealth-weighted average of simple returns). For short horizons they nearly coincide. Moments: excess kurtosis of equity returns is large (leptokurtosis), skewness typically negative; Jarque-Bera rejects normality essentially always on daily data. The stylized fact that drives the whole book: returns are approximately serially uncorrelated but their squares are strongly autocorrelated (volatility clustering).

## Ch 2: Linear time series

- Weak stationarity: mean and autocovariance constant over time, Cov(r_t, r_{t−l}) depends only on lag l. Sample ACF ρ̂_l with Bartlett approximate standard error 1/√T; Ljung-Box Q(m) tests m autocorrelations jointly, χ²_m under white noise.
- AR(p): stationarity = all characteristic roots inside the unit circle; PACF cuts off at p. MA(q): invertibility = roots outside unit circle; ACF cuts off at q. ARMA(p,q): identify by EACF, whose theoretical pattern has an "O" triangle with corner at (p,q).
- Estimation: conditional LS (condition on initial values) vs exact/maximum likelihood (treat initial a_t as parameters — matters under near-nonstationarity or overdifferencing). Order selection AIC/BIC. Forecasting recursions; ARMA forecast MSE converges to σ_a² for stationary series; the ψ-weights give forecast error variance.
- Unit roots: random walk is the canonical nonstationary model; trend-stationary vs difference-stationary must be distinguished (detrending a unit-root series creates spurious autocorrelation). Dickey-Fuller/ADF: regress Δr_t on r_{t−1} (plus lags of Δr and optionally trend/constant); nonstandard DF critical values. If a series has a unit root, work in differences or cointegration — never levels in regression (spurious regression).
- Seasonality: multiplicative seasonal models (airline model) with backshift factorization.
- Long memory: fractional differencing (1−B)^d with 0 < d < 0.5 gives autocorrelations decaying like ρ_l ≈ c l^{2d−1} instead of geometrically. ARFIMA(p,d,q). Daily equity volatilities show d ≈ 0.3-0.4 in the literature. The practical hook: this is Tsay's formal version of what Härdle's Ch 14 measured with R/S.

## Ch 3: Conditional heteroscedasticity (the volatility chapter)

- ARCH effect test: Ljung-Box on squared returns, or Engle's LM test on ε_t². If present, model the variance equation, not just the mean.
- ARCH(q): h_t = α_0 + Σ α_i a²_{t−i}. GARCH(p,q): h_t = α_0 + Σ α_i a²_{t−i} + Σ β_j h_{t−j}. GARCH(1,1) is the workhorse. The squared series follows an ARMA structure: a_t² = α_0 + Σ(α_i+β_i)a²_{t−i} + η_t − Σ β_j η_{t−j}, with η_t = a_t² − h_t a martingale difference. Persistence Σ(α+β) < 1 gives finite unconditional variance α_0/(1−α−β); = 1 is IGARCH (RiskMetrics: h_t = 0.06 a²_{t−1} + 0.94 h_{t−1}, i.e. EWMA with λ=0.94, no mean reversion).
- Fat tails persist even in GARCH: the conditional distribution can be Gaussian while the unconditional is leptokurtic (Jensen's inequality: E(a⁴) inflated by variance-of-variance). Even so, Student-t or GED conditionals fit better. Asymmetry: volatility responds asymmetrically to positive vs negative shocks (leverage). EGARCH: ln h_t = α_0 + β ln h_{t−1} + γ[|ε_{t−1}| − E|ε_{t−1}|] + θ ε_{t−1}; γ (the signed term) captures leverage. TGARCH/GJR: add γ a²_{t−1} I(a_{t−1} < 0). Skewness requires either asymmetric variance response or a skewed conditional law.
- GARCH-M: risk premium in the mean equation (return depends on volatility). Two-component/long-memory SV alternatives.
- GARCH(1,1) h-step forecast: h_{t+h} → σ² by geometric decay; the α-root-of-time rule used in VaR is only a rough approximation.
- Realized and range-based volatility: Parkinson (high-low), Garman-Klass (OHLC), and Yang-Zhang (OHLC with overnight jumps, handles drifting and opens) estimators are far more efficient than close-to-close. This is directly usable on daily NQ OHLC data to build a weekly volatility estimate without intraday data.

## Ch 4: Nonlinear models and tests

- Bilinear, TAR/SETAR (threshold autoregression, self-exciting when threshold variable is its own lag), STAR (smooth transition), Markov switching autoregression (state-dependent parameters with Markov transition probabilities), kernel regression/Nadaraya-Watson, functional AR, neural networks (feed-forward with skip layers; backpropagation).
- Nonlinearity tests: Q²(m) on squared residuals, bispectral, BDS, RESET, Keenan, Tsay's Ori-F (arranged autoregression, specific for TAR), TAR-F. Diagnostic: no consensus winner; use several.
- Forecasting nonlinear models: parametric bootstrap (simulate paths through the model). Example: US unemployment TAR beats linear models specifically in contractions — regime-dependent dynamics pay at turning points. The link to the Elliott Wave base is direct: the claim that markets behave differently in different regimes has a formal econometric home here.

## Ch 5: Market microstructure and high-frequency data

- Nonsynchronous trading (Lo-MacKinlay): stale prices induce negative lag-1 autocorrelation and lead-lag distortions in portfolio/index returns; even a martingale market shows spurious autocorrelation under nontrading.
- Roll model: observed price = efficient price + bid-ask bounce; bounces induce ρ_1 = −0.5 in price changes (for a pure bounce series) and the spread estimator s = 2√(−Cov(Δp_t, Δp_{t−1})). This is the ancestor of Hasbrouck's variance decompositions.
- Ordered probit (Hausman-Lo-MacKinlay): price changes are discretized outcomes of a latent continuous variable crossing tick boundaries, conditioned on trade size, spacing, order-book imbalance.
- Price-change/duration decomposition: trade direction d_i, price change indicator, durations between trades. The ADS decomposition found P(price change | previous price change) with reversal probability ≈ 0.904 for the data studied — a striking microstructure reversal effect at the tick level.
- Duration models (Engle-Russell): EACD (exponential), WACD (Weibull), GACD (generalized Gamma) ACD models — the "ARCH of durations". Hazard function h(x) = f(x)/S(x). IBM data: ψ persistence ≈ 0.949 — long durations cluster with long durations, exactly like volatility clustering. Threshold WACD, and the PCD joint model of price changes and durations. Financial point processes matter for execution; the summary relevance for daily/weekly trading is conceptual (market state variables live on trading time, not calendar time).

## Ch 6: Continuous-time models

Standard Brownian motion/Wiener process, generalized Wiener, Itô process; Itô's lemma (the chain rule with the +½σ²∂²f/∂x² correction term); GBM for prices. Derivation of Black-Scholes via the no-arbitrage hedged portfolio (and via risk-neutral expectation). Delta hedging; implied volatility; volatility smile as the market's falsification of the constant-σ assumption. Jump diffusion (Kou: double-exponential jump sizes, asymmetric up/down jump means, analytically tractable). Estimation of diffusion parameters: quasi-MLE, method of moments, Ait-Sahalia's approximate likelihood, MCMC (bridge to Ch 12).

## Ch 7: Value at Risk and extreme value theory

- VaR definitions and the econometric approach: VaR via conditional quantile of a fitted return model (Gaussian vs Student-t with ν≈4-5 for daily equity); RiskMetrics EWMA. Multiperiod: α-root of time only under iid; better to iterate the volatility model. Expected shortfall (conditional tail expectation) as the coherent alternative.
- Empirical quantile (historical simulation) — no distributional assumption but no conditioning either.
- EVT: block maxima follow the GEV family (Fréchet ξ>0 heavy tails, Weibull ξ<0 bounded, Gumbel ξ=0); the tail index ξ estimated by Hill/Pickands. For financial returns, the Fréchet domain. The peaks-over-threshold approach is better for VaR (uses data efficiently): exceedances over a high threshold u follow the Generalized Pareto Distribution GPD(ξ, β); VaR_{1−p} = u + (β/ξ)[(n/n_u · p)^{−ξ} − 1]. Mean-excess plot e(u) = E[x − u | x > u] linear in u signals GPD and helps pick u (≈ 2.5-3% of the sample for the examples). Two-dimensional Poisson process formulation of (times, sizes) of exceedances; inhomogeneous Poisson (with trend/GARCH-scaled threshold) to make the tail model conditional and time-varying.
- Model checking: hit sequence (indicator VaR is exceeded) must be Bernoulli with the right rate and independent; z/w statistics test both.

## Ch 8: Multivariate time series, cointegration, and threshold arbitrage

- Weak stationarity vector version; cross-correlation matrices with Tiao-Box notation; multivariate portmanteau Q_k(m).
- VAR(p): stationarity = all eigenvalues of the companion matrix < 1; multivariate Yule-Walker; order selection M(i) χ²_{k²} + AIC/BIC/HQ; OLS equation by equation (same as ML under Gaussian); forecast error covariance recursion; impulse response via Cholesky orthogonalization (ordering-dependent — an interpretive choice, not a fact).
- VARMA: conditional vs exact likelihood; identifiability requires structure; marginal models of a k-dim VARMA(p,q) are ARMA[kp, (k−1)p+q] — mixtures blur nothing.
- Cointegration: if a linear combination β'x_t of I(1) series is stationary, the series share a common trend. The error-correction form Δx_t = αβ'x_{t−1} + lags; Π = −Φ(1); rank(Π) = m = number of cointegrating vectors; rank 0 = spurious regression in levels, rank k = all series stationary, 0 < m < k = cointegration. Deterministic term specifications (unrestricted vs restricted constant). Johansen MLE: reduced-rank regression, eigenvalue problem |λS_11 − S_10 S_00^{-1} S_01| = 0, trace and max-eigenvalue statistics with nonstandard Brownian-motion critical values. Example: US TB 3m/6m rates cointegrated with vector ≈ (1, −1.012), i.e. the spread tb3m − tb6m is stationary with mean ≈ −0.225 — the term-structure spread is the classic cointegrating residual; ECM forecasts beat naive ones on the spread.
- Threshold cointegration and arbitrage (the chapter's trading apex): cost-of-carry futures-cash relation f − s = (r−q)(ℓ−t) + z*, with S&P 500 futures and cash (May 1993, 7060 one-minute observations). A three-regime multivariate threshold model with threshold variable the lagged pricing error z_{t−1}: AIC grid search selects thresholds −0.0226 and 0.0377. In the middle (no-arbitrage) regime the futures coefficient is insignificant — no adjustment; in the outer regimes the error-correction term is strongly significant — prices converge. Δf_t also shows a lag-1 bid-ask bounce effect, and futures lead cash in price discovery. This is the statistically rigorous version of pairs trading: trade the spread only outside the transaction-cost band.

## Ch 9: Factor models and principal components

- r_it = α_i + β'f_t + ε_it; Cov = βΣ_fβ' + D. Macro factor models (OLS betas; Chen-Roll-Ross: all betas negative on CPI surprise but low R² — macro factors price poorly in the time series sense); market model (13-stock example: β 0.47-1.80, R² 0.09-0.41); BARRA fundamental factors (betas are known indicators, factors estimated by WLS/GLS; factor-mimicking portfolio ω' = (β'D^{-1}β)^{-1}β'D^{-1}); Fama-French SMB/HML as hedge portfolios.
- PCA: spectral decomposition of the sample covariance; PC_i = e_i'r, Var = λ_i; proportion λ_i/Σλ_j; scree plot elbow. Five-stock example: two PCs = 72% — the first PC is the market, the second separates industries.
- Statistical factor analysis: orthogonal factor model Σ = ββ' + D; non-uniqueness → varimax rotation makes factors interpretable (Example 9.2 separates financials from techs); ML with LR(m) χ² test for the number of factors; bond-index example: two factors explain >90%, level and maturity slope. Asymptotic PCA (Connor-Korajczyk) for k > T (many assets, short panel): eigenanalyze the T×T matrix; Bai-Ng/CK criteria for the number of factors; 40 stocks × 36 months example.
- This chapter is the formal core of what Levy called PCA-GARCH and Härdle's Ch 6 did to the implied volatility surface.

## Ch 10: Multivariate volatility

- The curse: k(k+1)/2 conditional variances/covariances. EWMA with λ ≈ 0.96 (Hong Kong-Japan). Diagonal VEC/DVEC: Hadamard products; simple but no cross-dynamics and no guaranteed positive definiteness. BEKK guarantees PD: Σ_t = AA' + ΣA_i(a a')A_i' + ΣB_jΣB_j'; parameter count k²(m+s) + k(k+1)/2 — most insignificant in practice (Pfizer-Merck).
- Reparameterizations: (a) correlation models ρ_t with D_t diagonal — likelihood complicated for k ≥ 3; (b) Cholesky-based sequential regressions: Σ_t = L_t G_t L_t' where q_ij,t are regression coefficients of a_it on the previous b_jt and g_ii,t the residual variances. No PD constraints needed, |Σ| = Πg_ii, likelihood simple; b_it = a_it − Σq_ij b_jt. Ordering matters but only as a computational device (the model is exact).
- Constant correlation (Bollerslev); uncoupled vs feedback bivariate models compared by likelihood (Hong Kong-Japan). Time-varying correlation: logistic ρ = e^q/(1+e^q) with GARCH(1,1) q_t; IBM-S&P example: correlation declines over time, mean ≈ 0.612; Fisher transformation when the correlation sign is unknown. Cholesky approach with AR(1) q_21,t persistence ≈ 0.9915 gives smoother correlations. Tse-Tsui and Engle DCC: ρ_t = J_t Q_t J_t with scalar θ_1+θ_2 — same dynamics for all pairs.
- Sequential build-up for higher dimensions: univariate → augment → bivariate → augment... 3-asset example (S&P, Cisco, Intel): market volatility does not depend on the stocks, but stocks depend on the market; correlations rise in volatile periods (correlation breakdown exactly when diversification is needed most).
- Factor volatility models: PCA on a_t, GARCH the first PC (82.5% common factor), relate individual variances to the factor variance — parsimony at the cost of higher-lag adequacy.
- VaR application: VaR = √(VaR_1² + VaR_2² + 2ρVaR_1VaR_2); univariate + sample ρ vs constant-correlation bivariate vs Cholesky time-varying gave $57,117 / $58,180 / $57,648 — practically close; the time-varying machinery matters more for risk decomposition than for the scalar number on a small portfolio.

## Ch 11: State-space models and Kalman filtering

- Local trend model: y_t = μ_t + e_t with μ_{t+1} = μ_t + η_t is ARIMA(0,1,1); the MA(1) parameter solves (1+θ²)σ_a² = 2σ_e² + σ_η² and θσ_a² = σ_e². Filtering (uses data up to t), prediction, smoothing (uses all data).
- Kalman filter via multivariate normal conditioning (Theorem 11.1): K_t = Σ_{t|t−1}/V_t (Kalman gain), μ_{t+1|t} = μ_{t|t−1} + K_t v_t, Σ_{t+1|t} = Σ_{t|t−1}(1 − K_t) + σ_η². The one-step prediction errors v_t are mutually independent; the filter is the Cholesky decomposition of Cov(y). State-error recursion; steady state. Smoothing: backward recursions q_{t−1} = v_t/V_t + L_t q_t, μ_{t|T} = μ_{t|t−1} + Σ_{t|t−1} q_{t−1}; disturbance smoothing for e_t, η_t.
- Missing values: set v_t = 0, K_t = 0 — the filter skips them. Diffuse initialization for unknown initial states.
- ML via prediction-error decomposition: ln L = −½ Σ(ln V_t + v_t²/V_t). Alcoa realized volatility example: σ̂_e (measurement noise) ≫ σ̂_η — the volatility signal is slow-moving and the measurement is noisy; filter the estimate, don't trust the raw number.
- General state-space, and transformations into it: time-varying CAPM (random-walk α_t, β_t — GM example finds the fixed model adequate), ARMA ↔ state-space three ways (Akaike, Harvey, Aoki), regression with ARMA errors, structural time series (trend + seasonal + cycle). JNJ quarterly earnings: σ̂_e = 0.00143 vs σ̂_seasonal = 0.1712, σ̂_η = 0.2696 — the seasonal pattern genuinely evolves.
- This is the same machinery Härdle's Ch 13 used for house-price indices and Hasbrouck uses for efficient-price extraction; here it is derived from first principles.

## Ch 12: Markov chain Monte Carlo

- Markov chain simulation: construct a chain on parameter space Θ whose stationary distribution is the posterior P(θ|X); run long enough. Historical route: EM algorithm (E-step/M-step for missing data) → Tanner-Wong data augmentation (simulate the missing values rather than plug in expectations) → Gibbs sampler.
- Gibbs sampling: iterate draws from full conditional distributions f(θ_i | θ_{−i}, X, M); after burn-in the draws are a sample from the joint posterior. Regularity: the chain must be irreducible over the full space. Discard the first m draws (burn-in); estimate θ̂_i and σ̂_i² from the retained n − m. Test functions of parameters (e.g. θ_1 − θ_2) directly from the posterior draws — a major Bayesian convenience. When parameters are highly correlated, draw them jointly (block Gibbs) — correlation slows convergence. Check convergence by rerunning from multiple starting values; no method guarantees convergence.
- Bayesian inference: posterior ∝ likelihood × prior. Conjugate prior families: normal-mean/normal, normal-precision/gamma, Bernoulli/beta, Poisson-gamma, exponential-gamma, negative binomial/beta, normal with unknown mean and precision (normal-gamma), variance/inverted chi-squared. The precision-parameter form: posterior precision = prior precision + data precision; posterior mean = precision-weighted average of prior mean and sample mean — the prior washes out as n grows.
- Metropolis: accept candidate draw from a symmetric jumping distribution with probability min(r,1), r = posterior ratio; normalization constants cancel. Metropolis-Hastings: asymmetric proposals allowed with the corrected ratio. Griddy Gibbs: grid the conditional posterior, invert the approximate CDF — for nonlinear parameters with no closed form (GARCH/MA coefficients); check that the grid doesn't concentrate mass at the endpoints.
- Regression with time series errors: alternate draws of β (multivariate normal, from quasi-differenced regression), φ (normal, from the residual AR), σ² (inverted chi-squared, from the innovations). Weekly interest-rate example: posterior means match MLE but the posteriors of φ_1, φ_2 are skewed — finite-sample inference differs from asymptotic normality.
- Missing values and outliers: treat a missing x_h as a parameter; for AR(p) it enters p+1 equations y_{h+j} = φ_j x_h + b_{h+j} — a tiny regression per missing point; patches should be drawn jointly. Additive outliers: y_t = δ_t β_t + x_t with Bernoulli δ_t, giving posterior probabilities of each point being an outlier plus its magnitude, estimated jointly with the model. Treasury-rate example finds t=323 (May 1994, −0.304 magnitude) and t=201 with probabilities 0.83/0.58. Joint estimation beats the traditional detect-after-estimation when multiple outliers are present.
- Stochastic volatility (univariate): r_t = x'β + √h_t ε_t, ln h_t = α_0 + α_1 ln h_{t−1} + v_t. The likelihood mixes over the n-dimensional H; under Bayesian augmentation H becomes parameters and Gibbs handles it: β from a variance-weighted regression; each h_t from a lognormal-normal product kernel (Griddy Gibbs or Metropolis); (α_0, α_1) normal from the AR(1) on ln h; σ_v² inverted chi-squared. S&P monthly example: α_1 posterior ≈ 0.685, strong persistence; fitted volatilities match GARCH(1,1)'s pattern.
- Multivariate SV via Cholesky: separate log-AR(1) for each g_ii,t and an AR(1) for q_21,t; bivariate IBM-S&P example. Key comparative result: GARCH-with-time-varying-correlations gave smooth correlations (mean 0.55, sd 0.11) while SV gave jumpy month-to-month correlations (sd 0.17, occasionally negative) because q_21,t carries its own shock. The bivariate GARCH also produced a spurious 1993 S&P volatility peak induced by IBM dependence — model-induced artifacts are real.
- FFBS (forward filtering, backward sampling): transform to y_t = ln[(r_t − x'β)²/σ_0²] = z_t + ln ε_t²; approximate log χ²_1 by a 7-component normal mixture (Kim-Shephard-Chib); the model becomes a Gaussian state-space, the Kalman filter runs forward, and the whole z series is sampled backward in one pass using p(z_t|z_{t+1}, F_n) = p(z_t|z_{t+1}, F_t). Leverage (ρ between return and volatility innovations) handled by Artigas-Tsay time-varying Kalman linearization of the nonlinear transition z_{t+1} = αz_t + ρσ_η(r_t − x'β)e^{−z_t/2}/σ_0 + η*. S&P example: ρ̂ = −0.39, the classic negative leverage. Parameterization detail: η_t must shock z_{t+1} (not z_t) for the leverage correlation to be identified.
- Markov switching GARCH-M: state-dependent risk premia β_i√h_t and state-dependent GARCH dynamics, transition probabilities e_i with expected state duration 1/e_i; draws of the state vector element by element using forward likelihoods and Markov transition probabilities; e_i | S is Beta. GE monthly returns 1926-1999: risk premium difference between states significant (posterior of β_2−β_1 = 0.135 ± 0.063), state durations ≈ 11 and 9 months, state 1's persistence parameter hits 1.0 often — near-integrated volatility in the calm state.
- MCMC forecasting: simulate future paths from the model at each retained Gibbs iteration; the resulting predictive distribution includes parameter uncertainty. SV forecasts exceeded GARCH's, and the remark gives the reason: GARCH treats parameters as known and therefore systematically understates forecast variance — one reason GARCH vol forecasts sit below implied vols. The predictive distribution also yields VaR quantiles directly.

## Durable lessons

1. Test the squares before believing the means: serial correlation in returns is modest; in squared returns it is overwhelming. Any return model without a variance equation is misspecified for risk.
2. GARCH(1,1) is an ARMA on squared returns; persistence α+β governs the half-life of volatility shocks and IGARCH (α+β=1) never mean-reverts. RiskMetrics EWMA λ=0.94 is IGARCH(1,1) by another name.
3. Fat tails survive GARCH but are tamed by it: part of the leptokurtosis is variance-of-variance (Jensen), the rest needs Student-t/GED conditionals; skewness needs an asymmetric response (GJR/EGARCH) or a skewed law.
4. Range-based volatility (Parkinson, Garman-Klass, Yang-Zhang) extracts several times more information per observation than close-to-close; Yang-Zhang is the drift-robust choice.
5. Never regress one unit-root series on another in levels; test (ADF) first, difference or cointegrate. The spread of a cointegrated pair is the stationary object to trade.
6. Threshold cointegration formalizes the transaction-cost band: no adjustment inside the band, strong error-correction outside. Trade the spread only outside the band; inside it you pay costs for noise.
7. EVT for tails, GARCH for the body: POT/GPD above a high threshold (mean-excess plot picks it; ≈ 2.5-3%) with an inhomogeneous Poisson/GARCH-scaled threshold makes the tail estimate conditional. The α-root-of-time scaling rule is only valid under iid.
8. Multivariate volatility: BEKK guarantees positive definiteness but overflows with insignificant parameters; the Cholesky reparameterization has no constraints and a simple likelihood; DCC gives one dynamic for all pairs. Correlations rise when volatility rises.
9. The Kalman filter is the general answer to "the thing I want to measure is hidden behind noise": local-trend, time-varying beta, ARMA errors, structural components, and Hasbrouck's efficient price are all the same machine, estimated by prediction-error-decomposition MLE.
10. Bayesian machinery turns intractable likelihoods into conditional draws: augment with the hidden variables (H, states, missing points, outlier indicators), draw each block from its conjugate conditional, and the joint posterior emerges. Parameter uncertainty propagates into forecasts for free.
11. GARCH treats its parameters as truth; MCMC/SV predictive distributions do not. Parameter uncertainty is one concrete reason point volatility forecasts sit below implied volatilities.
12. Leverage effect is an identified model feature when the volatility shock is timed to z_{t+1}: ρ ≈ −0.4 on equity indices. Negative returns raise future volatility, and the data confirm it.
13. Outliers and missing values are parameters like any other: joint estimation of outliers with the model beats sequential detect-then-clean when multiple outliers interact.
14. Microstructure artifacts are data facts before they are theory: Roll bounce (ρ_1 = −0.5), nonsynchronous trading autocorrelation, and tick-level reversal probabilities (≈0.9) are all estimable and all fade at lower frequencies — but they contaminate any statistics computed on short windows.
15. Model choice leaves fingerprints: bivariate GARCH manufactured a spurious volatility peak in S&P vol via IBM dependence; smoothness of fitted correlations is itself a modeling assumption, not an empirical finding.

## Connections to prior books

- Levy (computational-finance-levy): Levy's Part III GARCH chapter is Ch 3 here in full: the same persistence condition, the same kurtosis-from-Jensen mechanism, the same GARCH-as-ARMA identification (Tsay derives it), EWMA λ, PCA-GARCH (Ch 9-10 formalize it), quasi-MC (Levy's Sobol vs Härdle's Halton; Tsay doesn't need MC pricing here). Tsay adds what Levy lacked: formal identification (EACF), exact vs conditional likelihood, forecasting recursions, and the EVT/ACD/state-space/MCMC layers.
- Härdle (applied-quantitative-finance-hardle): Ch 10 here is the textbook version of Härdle's BEKK chapter (same model, same PD argument, same over-parameterization lesson); Ch 11 Kalman/smoothing is the derivation behind Härdle's Ch 13 house-price state-space; Ch 2 ARFIMA is the formal long-memory model Härdle's Ch 14 measured with R/S (Tsay's fractional differencing d ≈ Härdle's H − 0.5); Tsay's Ch 7 EVT complements Härdle's Ch 1-3 VaR chapters (Tsay adds GPD/POT where Härdle stressed delta-gamma and copulas); Härdle's CUSUM regime monitoring (Ch 11 there) pairs with Tsay's Markov-switching Ch 12 and TAR Ch 4 as three formal regime frameworks.
- Javaheri (volatility-arbitrage-javaheri): Tsay's Ch 12 SV models with Gibbs/FFBS are the full-Bayesian counterparts of Javaheri's filtered MLE/EKF/UKF/particle estimation; Javaheri's consistency problem (options imply stronger skew/kurtosis than time series) meets its mechanism in Tsay's Ch 12 forecasting remark: GARCH underestimates forecast variance by ignoring parameter uncertainty, one reason implied vol exceeds GARCH vol. Tsay's leverage ρ ≈ −0.39 is the same quantity Javaheri estimated with Heston.
- Björk (arbitrage-theory-bjork): Tsay Ch 6 is the applied digest of Björk's continuous-time chapters: Wiener/Itô processes, Itô's lemma, GBM, Black-Scholes twice over, risk-neutrality; Tsay adds the discrete-data estimation question (quasi-ML, moments, MCMC) that Björk's theory brackets out.
- Hasbrouck (empirical-microstructure-harris-etc. stack): Ch 5 is the econometrics of Hasbrouck's subject matter: Roll's bounce, ordered probit, durations (ACD as ARCH-for-arrival-times), and the same efficient-price-plus-noise view whose optimal estimator (Ch 11 Kalman) Hasbrouck also uses. The nonsynchronous-trading and bid-ask artifacts here are the daily-frequency cousins of Biais/Stoll/Madhavan's intraday effects.
- Bouchaud (trades-quotes-prices-bouchaud): Tsay's leptokurtosis and EVT tails are the distributional consequences Bouchaud's impact and order-flow laws produce; Tsay Ch 7 makes Bouchaud's fat tails quantitatively usable for risk (GPD exceedances) without assuming normality anywhere.
- The Quants (the-quants-patterson): the 1987 lesson (fat tails, feedback loops) is operationalized as EVT/GPD and jump diffusion (Kou) here; Tsay's Ch 8 threshold cointegration is the statistical machinery under any statistical-arbitrage program of the kind the quant funds ran.
- Elliott Wave base (elliott-wave-balan): Tsay supplies the statistical license structure: TAR/Markov switching formalize "different rules in different regimes"; unit-root discipline prevents reading trends into noise; realized/range vol and GARCH persistence give the volatility-regime clock that wave counts implicitly depend on; long memory (ARFIMA/R/S) quantifies how much mean reversion a wave-terminal scenario can claim; threshold cointegration turns "overthrow then return" into an estimable band model.

## Strategy / research candidates (weekly NQ practice)

1. Volatility-regime clock, formal version: fit GARCH(1,1)-t (or EGARCH for leverage) to NQ daily returns; persistence α+β and the h-step forecast reversion rate give an objective "high-vol vs low-vol regime" measure to annotate weekly charts alongside wave counts. Compare against Härdle's PC1 level factor of the vol surface.
2. Yang-Zhang range-based weekly volatility: compute Parkinson/Garman-Klass/Yang-Zhang estimators from daily OHLC each week; compare their efficiency and their disagreement with close-to-close σ as a cheap realized-vol proxy for position sizing. Disagreement between range and close estimators is itself a microstructure signal (gap-heavy weeks).
3. Unit-root and long-memory discipline on NQ weekly data: ADF on log price (expect unit root, i.e. do not forecast levels); ARFIMA d and Härdle-style R/S on returns and on |returns| — measure whether weekly NQ volatility shows d ≈ 0.2-0.4 long memory, which would justify fractional position sizing rather than full mean reversion.
4. EVT/GPD tail sizing: fit POT-GPD to the negative tail of NQ weekly returns with a mean-excess-plot-chosen threshold (~3%); use the conditional tail quantile and expected shortfall for stop placement and worst-week budgeting, replacing Gaussian σ-multiples. Backtest hit frequencies with the Bernoulli independence check.
5. Threshold cointegration pairs practice (when a second instrument joins the research, e.g. NQ vs ES or NQ vs Nasdaq-100 cash): Johansen test on the pair, estimate the cointegrating vector, grid-search AIC for the no-trade band around the spread, and only signal outside the band; the futures-leads-cash result (Ch 8) says which leg to treat as the fast one.
6. Nonlinearity test battery before regime stories: run Q², RESET, Keenan, and Tsay's Ori-F/TAR-F on NQ weekly returns before asserting any regime or threshold model; let the tests justify whether a TAR/Markov-switching structure earns its complexity over linear + GARCH.
7. Kalman-filtered trend component: apply the local-trend structural model (or ARIMA(0,1,1) equivalent) to weekly NQ as a smooth trend line with honest confidence bands, replacing eyeballed trend channels; the filter's one-step prediction errors are a natural surprise measure for reversal timing studies.
8. Later-stage, with tooling: fit a Bayesian SV model with leverage (FFBS) to NQ monthly/weekly returns; the posterior of ρ and the predictive volatility distribution quantify leverage and parameter uncertainty directly, and the predictive quantiles give VaR without distributional assumptions.
