# Applied Quantitative Finance (Härdle, Kleinow & Stahl, eds.) — teaching notes

## What this book is

A contributed Springer volume (XploRe-based, 2002) where each chapter is a self-contained applied quant project with working code. Four blocks: Value-at-Risk (delta-gamma, copulas, spread-risk simulation), credit risk (rating migrations, copula portfolio dependence), implied volatility and state-price densities (PCA of the vol surface, implied binomial trees, nonparametric SPDs, and the implied-vs-historical density trading strategies), and financial econometrics (BEKK multivariate GARCH, SPC monitoring charts, empirical-likelihood goodness-of-fit, Kalman state-space house prices, long-memory R/S trading, locally time-homogeneous volatility, Halton quasi-Monte Carlo, nonparametric GARCH). The connective tissue: risk numbers are only as good as their distributional and dependence assumptions, and each assumption is testable against data.

## Part I — Value at Risk

### Ch 1: Delta-Gamma-Normal VaR (Jaschke/Jiang)

- The problem: portfolio value change is a nonlinear function V(r) of the risk factors r. Delta-normal linearizes (bad for options); full revaluation plus simulation is accurate but slow. Delta-gamma keeps the quadratic: ΔV ≈ θ^T r + ½ r^T Γ r, with the factor covariance from history.
- Tool kit: eigenvalue/spectral decomposition of the covariance matrix turns the quadratic form into a sum of independent squared terms; cumulants κ_r of the quadratic form can be computed in closed form (κ_1 = trace terms, etc.); the Cornish-Fisher expansion maps cumulants to a quantile of the non-normal ΔV distribution; Fourier inversion of the characteristic function recovers the exact density when CFs are known (the characteristic function of a quadratic form of Gaussians is analytic).
- Partial Monte Carlo VaR: simulate only the risk factors (not full revaluation), evaluate the quadratic payoff, take the empirical quantile. Variance reduction for the MC: antithetic variates, control variates (use the known delta-gamma quadratic as control), moment matching, stratified/Latin hypercube sampling, importance sampling (tilt the sampling density by exp(θ z) with θ from the cumulant-generating function; the likelihood-ratio correction is the θ(z) term; reported variance reductions up to 84.68%).
- Teaching point: the moment-generating/cumulant-generating function κ(ω) = log E e^{ωZ} is the engine of everything here; knowing κ means knowing all cumulants, and cumulants plus Cornish-Fisher approximate the VaR quantile without any simulation.

### Ch 2: Copulas for VaR (Rank/Siegl)

- Sklar's theorem: any joint distribution F(x,y) splits into its marginals and a copula C: F(x,y) = C(F_X(x), F_Y(y)). The copula is the pure dependence structure, scale-free, on unit-uniform margins.
- Examples: Gaussian copula (correlation ρ in the meta-distribution), Gumbel-Hougaard (upper tail dependence, C(u,v) = exp(-[(-ln u)^θ + (-ln v)^θ]^{1/θ})). Fréchet-Hoeffding bounds: max(u+v-1,0) ≤ C(u,v) ≤ min(u,v).
- Fitting: least squares on the empirical copula vs maximum likelihood on the pseudo-copula sample; ML generally better.
- Sampling a conditional-copula method: draw u1 uniform, invert the conditional distribution C_2^{-1}(u2 | u1) for the second margin. This is how dependent scenarios get simulated.
- Backtest on a real portfolio: copula-based VaR (three fitted families) beats variance-covariance (vcv) VaR in hit-rate tests; historical simulation beats both. The Gaussian copula itself is not enough when tail dependence exists.

### Ch 3: Spread risk of fixed-income portfolios (Frisch/Knöchlein)

- Risk decomposition of a bond portfolio: general market risk (parallel yield shifts captured by benchmark factors), residual risk (idiosyncratic spread changes), and event risk (rating jumps). The empirical target: daily spread changes are leptokurtic (kurtosis 6 to 200), so Gaussian VaR is indefensible here.
- Historical simulation variants built up carefully: V1 plain historical; V2 mean-adjusted; V3 volatility-updated (rescale past returns by an EWMA of recent volatility, γ = 0.94, RiskMetrics style); V4 combines mean adjustment, volatility updating, and full risk-factor sets (yield, benchmark, spread). Best performer: simultaneous simulation of all risk factors plus volatility updating. The conservative regulator-style approach overestimates risk massively (MSD ~14,000 in their metric).
- Diagnostics: P-P and Q-Q plots to check whether simulated loss distribution matches realized quantiles.

### Ch 4: Rating migrations (Höse/Huschens/Wania)

- Migration counts n_ij over a rating transition matrix; estimate rates p_ij with standard errors; the threshold normal model gives a latent-variable interpretation with an equicorrelation parameter ρ.
- The empirical question: are transition matrices stable over the business cycle? χ² homogeneity tests across cycle phases reject time-stability for the real data.
- Multi-period transitions: P^m by matrix power, or bootstrap the migration distribution to get confidence bands for multi-horizon default probabilities. Portfolio evolution: E[w(t+1)] = w(t) P. Durable point: rating-based risk numbers carry regime dependence; a long-sample average transition matrix understates stress-period migration.

### Ch 5: Credit portfolio sensitivity (Kiesel/Kleinow)

- Two ways to inject dependence into a defaultable portfolio: factor models (common macro factors drive defaults) vs copulas on default times.
- Striking empirical ratio: default correlation ρ^D is roughly asset correlation divided by 10 (asset correlations of tens of percent translate into default correlations of a few percent). Defaults are rare events; dependence lives in the extreme tail.
- Tail dependence: λ_L = lim_{u→0} C(u,u)/u. Gaussian copula: λ_L = 0 for any ρ < 1 (no tail dependence, tails are asymptotically independent). A t-copula with ν = 4 has λ_L > 0. Consequence measured: 99% VaR of an A-rated portfolio is roughly three times larger under a t_4 copula than under a Gaussian copula with matched correlation.
- Durable point: for tail risk, the copula family choice matters more than the precise correlation level; correlation (linear, average dependence) is the wrong summary statistic for joint crashes.

## Part III — Implied volatility and state-price densities

### Ch 6: Implied volatility surface and common PCA (Fengler/Härdle/Schmidt)

- Estimate the implied vol surface σ^(imp)(m, τ) from option quotes with a Nadaraya-Watson quartic kernel smoother over moneyness-maturity space; this gives a smooth, differentiable surface instead of a scatter of quotes (VDAX/DAX data).
- PCA of the surface time series: PC1 (~70% of variation) is a level shift (parallel up/down of the whole surface); PC2 (~13%) is curvature (smile steepness); PC3 a slope (term-structure twist). The surface effectively moves in a low-dimensional subspace — a fact that matters for hedging surface risk with few instruments.
- Flury's common principal components (CPC): test whether maturity groups share the same eigenvectors; a likelihood-ratio test (T = 31.8, p = 0.375) accepts the common structure, meaning shift/curvature/twist axes are stable across maturities. This licenses a compact surface dynamics model: level drives everything, with a curvature add-on.

### Ch 7: Implied binomial trees, Derman-Kani vs Barle-Cakici (Härdle/Zheng)

- Goal: extract the state-price density (SPD) / local volatility surface implied by today's option prices, consistent with discrete no-arbitrage.
- Derman-Kani (D&K) construction: known level i, node S_i; Arrow-Debreu prices λ obtained by forward induction; the upward transition probability p_i must keep the discrete tree consistent with the risk-neutral forward price and the known implied vol; the balancing inequality F_i < S_{i+1} < F_{i+1} must hold, else probabilities go negative (fixes: interpolation tricks, adjusted forwards, inflated dividends).
- Barle-Cakici (B&C) refinements: place strikes at the forward (not the spot), center the tree at the forward, use the condition S_i S_{i+1} = F_i²; this makes the construction far more stable at high interest rates.
- Local volatility from the lattice: σ_loc = √(p(1-p)) |log(s+/s-)| / √Δt; the SPD from Arrow-Debreu prices via λ e^{rτ}.
- Trading rules implied by tree mismatch (Table 7.1): if the fitted implied skew f is flatter than the market's g (skew(f) < skew(g)), the market prices downside states richer than the model, so sell OTM puts / buy OTM calls (and symmetric cases). A relative-value dictionary, not an absolute signal.

### Ch 8: SPD estimation via nonparametric regression (Huynh/Kervella/Zheng)

- Two nonparametric routes to the state-price density from today's option prices, both improving on the discrete implied binomial tree:
  1. Breeden-Litzenberger: ∂²C/∂K² = e^{-rτ} f*(K). The SPD is the second derivative of the call price in the strike; numerically brutal because it double-differentiates noisy quotes.
  2. Rookley's local polynomial regression: regress implied vol on moneyness with local cubic fits; the V' and V'' derivatives of the fitted surface convert back into the SPD's shape (implicit function differentiation). Smooth, and it supplies standard errors.
- Implied dividend estimation: back out the dividend adjustment from put-call-parity deviations plus a futures-based fix (their DAX example includes a tax-gap story).
- Wild bootstrap gives confidence bands for the estimated SPD.
- Cross-validation via butterfly spreads: a butterfly's price equals an integrated slice of the SPD, so butterfly prices test SPD estimates. Rookley beats the implied binomial tree; both underprice kurtosis relative to market butterflies (market prices fatter tails than either estimator captures).

### Ch 9: Trading implied vs historical density deviations (Blaskowitz/Schmidt)

- The core comparison: the implied SPD f* (from today's option prices via implied trees, Ch 7) versus the time-series SPD g* (from historical data via a nonparametric diffusion estimator à la Florens-Zmirou, with Milstein-simulated paths).
- Robust empirical finding (DAX, 30 rolling periods): the implied SPD is systematically more negatively skewed AND more leptokurtic than the time-series SPD, in 29 of 30 periods. The market persistently charges an insurance premium for crash states over and above what recent history says.
- Trade S1: since implied skew exceeds historical skew, sell OTM puts and buy OTM calls (sell the overpriced insurance). Net +9,855.50 EUR over the sample even with 5% transaction costs, but June/Sept 1998 brought large directional losses (the insurance you sold pays out exactly when the market crashes).
- Trade K1: the analogous kurtosis trade (sell the tails, both wings). +10,915.77 EUR, most of it earned at initiation (options sold rich); profits come from possibly mispriced options; substantial directional risk remains.
- The peso-problem caveat, stated honestly by the authors: a decade of profits from selling tail insurance can be one 1987 away from ruin; the sample may simply not contain the crash that justifies the premium. This is Javaheri's consistency problem turned into a live strategy, with its risk nakedly displayed.

## Part IV — Econometrics

### Ch 10: Multivariate volatility, BEKK (Fengler/Herwartz)

- The problem: portfolio VaR and hedging need the conditional covariance matrix, not just variances. Vec representation is infeasible (too many free parameters); diagonal and constant-correlation MGARCH are workable simplifications.
- BEKK(1,1): Σ_t = C'C + A' ε_{t-1} ε_{t-1}' A + G' Σ_{t-1} G, positive definite by construction (quadratic forms), at parameter cost.
- Estimation by QML with BHHH; the off-diagonal elements of A and G matter empirically (volatility spillovers between DEM/USD and GBP/USD, not just own-variance feedback).
- Forecast comparison: bivariate density forecasts beat univariate (aggregated) ones in 81.6% of evaluation windows; the success ratio tracks the covariance term. Dependence forecasting is a real, separate information channel.

### Ch 11: Statistical process control for financial time series (Knoth)

- Control charts imported from quality engineering to monitor financial ratios: Shewhart (level alarms), EWMA (smoothed level), CUSUM (cumulative sum of deviations, optimal for detecting a sustained shift to a known new level; k = (μ1+μ0)/2σ is the optimal CUSUM allowance).
- Performance measured by ARL (average run length to a false alarm / to detection) and the steady-state ARL (average delay); computed exactly via a Markov-chain approximation of the chart statistic: (I - Q) L = 1 where Q is the transition matrix restricted to the in-control region.
- Two-sided charts: L ≈ L_up L_low / (L_up + L_low).
- Application: monitoring CAPM beta via an EWMA on AR(1)-standardized residuals; fat tails raise the false-alarm rate of charts calibrated to normality, so calibrate to the actual return distribution.
- This chapter is a regime-change detector: a CUSUM on a rolling strategy statistic is exactly the tool that tells you when an estimated edge has died or a vol regime has flipped.

### Ch 12: Empirical-likelihood goodness-of-fit for diffusions (Chen/Härdle/Kleinow)

- Empirical likelihood (Owen): a nonparametric likelihood ratio R built from probability weights on the data; -2 log R → χ², giving confidence regions for parameters without specifying the innovation density.
- Application: goodness-of-fit of parametric diffusion models (CIR, etc.) against a kernel (Nadaraya-Watson) estimate of the drift and diffusion; smoothed parametric vs nonparametric comparison with automatic studentization; binning with k_n ≈ [1/(2h)] for the χ² test; wild bootstrap for the null distribution.
- S&P 500 result: a mean-reverting CIR-type drift a(1-x) is not rejected (the data tolerate linear mean reversion in the log-price state variable); test power decreases with bandwidth h. A disciplined way to let data speak against a parametric specification.

### Ch 13: State space house prices, Kalman filtering (Schulz/Werwatz)

- Hedonic regression (price on quality characteristics) plus an AR(2) common price component written in state space form: observation equation = hedonic noise, state equation = latent market price index.
- Kalman filter (forward, one-step) and Kalman smoother (backward, uses the whole sample) recursions; the prediction-error decomposition gives the exact log-likelihood for estimated parameters.
- Missing observations handled naturally by the filter (equivalence proof included: skipping the update step = treating the observation as missing).
- Empirics: φ1 + φ2 ≈ 1 (near-unit-root price level), and the smoothed common-component index is less volatile than the raw average-price index. Lesson: a latent-state approach extracts a cleaner signal of the common component from noisy heterogeneous observations.

### Ch 14: Long-memory effects and a trading strategy (Blaskowitz/Schmidt)

- Hurst's R/S analysis: for each window length n, rescaled range (R/S)_n; regress log(R/S)_n on log n; the slope Ĥ is the Hurst coefficient. H = 0.5 = random walk; H > 0.5 positive persistence (long memory); H < 0.5 anti-persistence (reverting).
- Formal long memory: Σρ(k) diverges (autocorrelations decay like c|k|^{-α}, α ∈ (0,1)); the sample mean's variance is no longer σ²/n (correlation-inflated, eq. 14.3); the spectral density has a pole at zero. Slowly decaying small correlations can hide inside the ±2/√n band, so insignificance by eyeball proves nothing.
- Self-similar processes: Y self-similar with parameter H if c^{-H} Y_{ct} ≡ Y_t in distribution; increments have ρ(k) = ½(|k+1|^{2H} − 2|k|^{2H} + |k−1|^{2H}). Fractional Brownian motion (FBM) with the covariance structure σ²/2(|t|^{2H} − |t−s|^{2H} + |s|^{2H}); its increments are fractional Gaussian noise (FGN). H = 0.5 recovers standard Brownian motion; H = 0.8 gives smooth trending paths, H = 0.2 jagged reverting ones. R/S recovers the simulation's H (simulated 0.8, estimated 0.83).
- The data experiment: four German companies with both voting and non-voting shares (WMF, Dyckerhoff, KSB, RWE), 7,290 days from 1973. Individual stocks: Ĥ ≈ 0.5. The log-difference X_t = log S^v − log S^nv: Ĥ = 0.33, 0.37, 0.33, 0.41. Negative persistence, significant against Brownian-motion bootstrap confidence intervals (99% band [0.491, 0.595] at N = 7289; asymptotic approximations are inaccurate for R/S, per Lo 1991, so the null distribution is simulated).
- The trading strategy: pairs-trade the spread. Estimate the average relationship by regression; when X_t is more negative than the minimum of the last M days (M = 50/100/150), go long voting, short non-voting (cash neutral); the M-day maximum triggers the reverse. Results: positive net cash flows for all pairs at 0%, 0.5%, 1% transaction costs (WMF 133.16 down to 58.48 as M and costs rise); performance ranks almost exactly with Ĥ: the most reverting pair (H = 0.33, WMF/KSB) earns the most, the least reverting (RWE, H = 0.41) the least, and dies at 1% costs with M = 150 (−3.01).
- Honest caveats: no risk adjustment, no interest on the short leg over 26 years. The strategy's own profitability is its second piece of evidence for reversion.
- Durable structure: measure the memory of the spread first (R/S with bootstrap bands), trade the reversion only if Ĥ is significantly below 0.5, size M to the number of reversals you can afford to trade after costs.

### Ch 15: Locally time-homogeneous modeling (Mercurio)

- The idea: instead of fitting a global parametric model (GARCH etc.) whose parameters are assumed constant for years, assume the parameters are piecewise constant with jumps, and estimate on-line the largest recent interval over which the model still holds (the interval of time homogeneity). Smaller intervals waste efficiency; larger intervals import bias. The data choose the interval.
- Method (Lepski/Spokoiny pointwise adaptive estimation): exponential probability bounds for OLS coefficient error, P(|θ̂_k − θ_k| > λσ√w_kk) ≤ 2e^{−λ²/2}, extended to stochastic regressors with a polynomial factor. Test each candidate interval I against its subintervals J: reject I if |θ̂_I − θ̂_J| > μσ√w_I + λσ√w_J for any component. Take the largest non-rejected interval. Tuning: grid step m0 = 10–50 (uncritical), sensitivity constants λ, μ (critical: too small = oversensitive, too large = blind to breaks) chosen by minimizing recent realized forecast error over a grid of (λ, μ) pairs.
- Simulation: three-parameter regression with jumps of decreasing size; the change point is caught within roughly the minimal delay m0 = 30 even for the smallest jump.
- Application 1: Thai Baht basket (pegged 1985 to July 2, 1997 to undisclosed weights of USD/DEM/JPY). Estimate the basket weights on-line with four estimators: recursive OLS (biased after breaks), rolling window (fixed k = 250), Kalman filter (random-walk coefficients with estimated Σ), adaptive. The interest differential made a mean-variance carry position profitable: borrow the basket, lend THB. Realized profits (normalized per 100 USD short): adaptive beats the others on realized profit and bias for both 1-month and 3-month horizons (e.g., 3-month: adaptive 1.182 vs recursive 1.166, window 1.141, KF 1.147). Getting the time-variation of parameters right is worth real money.
- Application 2: volatility. R_t = σ_t ε_t stylized facts (leptokurtosis, clustering, persistent ACF of squares). Instead of GARCH-as-global-model, treat σ_t as locally constant. Trick: the power transform |R_t|^γ = C_γ σ_t^γ + D_γ σ_t^γ ζ_t turns multiplicative noise into approximately additive, approximately Gaussian noise; γ = 0.5 (square-root of absolute returns) is nearly optimal for normality of the noise. Then average over the adaptively chosen interval: θ̂_I = |I|^{-1} Σ|R_t|^γ. Same interval-testing machinery, now with the exponential bound carrying a log B factor from bounded volatility.
- Interpretation: apparent long memory in volatility may partly be an artifact of time-varying parameters; a locally constant model with adaptive windowing competes with (G)ARCH/FIGARCH on forecasting without any global memory parameter. The estimated interval length m̂(t) is itself a diagnostic: it shrinks exactly when the market regime changes.

### Ch 16: Simulation-based option pricing, Monte Carlo and Quasi-Monte Carlo (Lüssem/Schumacher)

- MC pricing as integration: price = e^{r(T-t)} E[C_T(S_T)] = ∫ C_T(f(x))dx over [0,1)^d, with the inverse-CDF transform S_T = S_t exp((r − σ²/2)(T−t) + σ√(T−t) Φ^{-1}(x)). The estimator's error is O(1/√n) regardless of dimension (the dimension only enters through the payoff variance) — hence MC's advantage in high dimensions, versus finite differences' exponential cost growth.
- Confidence intervals from the CLT (Boyle: n > 1000 suffices for the normal approximation); k = 1, 2, 3 standard errors give p = 0.682, 0.955, 0.997. The empirical payoff standard deviation gives the error bar almost for free.
- Path-dependent options: simulate at the fixing times (Asian on geometric mean has a closed form; on arithmetic mean it does not — commodity markets use Asians because vanilla is too expensive under high vol). Multi-underlying baskets: Cholesky the log-return covariance; the chapter's example is a best-of-five basket with dividends.
- Quasi-MC: replace pseudo-random numbers with low-discrepancy sequences. Discrepancy D_n measures worst-case uniformity; star-discrepancy over corner boxes. Koksma-Hlawka: error ≤ V(f) D_n* (variation times discrepancy); low-discrepancy sequences achieve D_n* = O(n^{-1}(log n)^s), so QMC's deterministic error beats MC's probabilistic 1/√n for moderate dimensions and smooth integrands.
- The Halton sequence: radical-inverse in base p (dimension j uses the j-th prime); 1-D base 2: 1/2, 1/4, 3/4, 1/8, 5/8, ... Higher-dimension components (e.g., dimensions 49, 50 with large primes) fill space poorly for small n, but improve by n = 10,000.
- Randomized QMC (Tuffin): shift the sequence by a uniform ε (mod 1); repeated shifts give an empirical standard deviation, restoring an error estimate while keeping the low-discrepancy convergence (at proportionally reduced speed).
- Brownian bridge path construction: build the path by placing points of maximum time-distance first, concentrating the effective dimension into the leading QMC coordinates; keeps QMC converging fast for path-dependent options up to ~50 time points.
- Practical limits: rare-event payoffs (tiny probability of positive payoff) blow up the relative error — use importance sampling; generator cycles (~10^8 for Park-Miller) and inverse-normal approximation error (10^{-15}) are usually non-issues.

### Ch 17: Nonparametric GARCH estimation (Franke/Holzberger/Müller)

- Motivation: is the parametric GARCH(1,1) form σ_t² = ω + α ε²_{t-1} + β σ²_{t-1} (or TGARCH for leverage) actually the right shape? Let the data answer: estimate σ_t² = g(ε_{t-1}, σ²_{t-1}) with g a smooth unknown function.
- Obstacle: σ_t is unobservable, so you cannot kernel-smooth against it directly. Existing fix (Bühlmann-McNeil): iterate parametric fit, plug-in volatilities, re-smooth. Alternative (this chapter): deconvolution, which never needs proxy volatilities.
- Deconvolution kernels: if you observe X = ξ + η with known error density p_η, the target density is recovered by dividing the empirical characteristic function by the error's: p̂_h(x) = (1/2π) ∫ e^{-iωx} φ_K(ωh) φ̂_x(ω)/φ_η(ω) dω. Fan-Truong extend it to errors-in-variables regression. The price: bandwidth h = A/log N (logarithmic, brutally slow) and consistency only if the error density's CF decays at a known controlled exponential rate.
- The bridge from GARCH to ARMA: squaring a GARCH(1,1) gives ε²_t = ω + (α+β)ε²_{t-1} − βζ_{t-1} + ζ_t with ζ_t = σ²_t(Z²_t − 1) white noise — an ARMA(1,1) in the squares. And in logs: X_t = log ε²_t satisfies X_{t+1} = f(X_t, e_t) + e_{t+1} with e_t = log Z²_t, a nonlinear ARMA where f(x,v) = log g₁(x, x−v). So nonparametric ARMA estimation of f delivers nonparametric GARCH estimation of g.
- The ARMA estimator: combine ordinary kernel smoothing in x with deconvolution smoothing in the unobservable e-direction; match conditional distribution functions Q(v|x) (of the mean function) against P_e(v|x) (of the innovations) and invert: f̂ = Q̂^{-1}(P̂_e), monotonicity in the second argument doing the identification work. Bandwidths b = C/N^{1/5} and h = A/log N with rates from Holzberger's thesis.
- Demonstrations recover both a linear ARMA (0.3 + 0.6x + 1.6v) and a sigmoid nonlinear one; for GARCH: the true g(y,z) = 0.01 + 0.6y + 0.2z is qualitatively recovered, though the surface's tails stay imprecise at N = 1000. Asymmetric (leverage) generalization: estimate g⁺ and g⁻ separately on positive and negative return subsamples.
- Durable use: this is a specification test, not a competitor for production forecasting. If the nonparametric surface looks planar, parametric GARCH is vindicated; where it curves, TGARCH/EGARCH-type asymmetry terms or outright nonparametric dynamics are called for.

### Ch 18: Net-based spreadsheets (Aydınlı) — distilled lightly

- Client/server statistical computing: the XploRe Quantlet Server does the numerics; Excel with an add-in (MD*ReX) is the front end; the MD*CRYPT protocol links them. Same architecture lesson as Levy's Part I: separate the numerical engine from the interface, deliver state-of-the-art methods where the users actually are (spreadsheets; most time-series modeling in business happens in Excel). The chapter re-runs the Ch 2 copula VaR and Ch 6 implied vol surface analyses from inside Excel, including 3-D implied-vol surface visualization and the ambition of real-time tick-fed vol surfaces.

## Durable lessons

1. Dependence lives in the copula, not the correlation. Gaussian copulas have zero tail dependence (λ_L = 0); a t_4 copula with matched correlation triples the 99% VaR of a credit portfolio. For joint-crash risk, the family of the dependence structure dominates the level of the correlation.
2. The implied risk distribution is persistently fatter and more negatively skewed than the time-series distribution (29/30 periods, DAX). That gap is a premium, and harvesting it (selling OTM puts, buying OTM calls) is an insurance-underwriting business with peso risk: the profit stream contains a hidden short-crash position that the sample may never show.
3. The implied volatility surface moves in a 2–3 dimensional subspace: level (~70%), curvature (~13%), twist. Hedge surface risk as factor risk, and the shift/curvature axes are common across maturities (CPC accepted, p = 0.375).
4. Historical simulation with volatility updating beats both Gaussian VaR and plain historical VaR for fat-tailed spread risk; the conservative full-risk-factor approach grossly overprices risk (MSD ~14,000).
5. Rating transition matrices are not time-stable across the business cycle (χ² rejects); long-sample averages understate stress-period migration risk.
6. The delta-gamma-plus-cumulants route gives analytic or quasi-analytic VaR for option books: eigenvalue-decompose the quadratic form, use the cumulant-generating function, Cornish-Fisher or Fourier-invert for the quantile; importance sampling can cut MC variance by ~85%.
7. R/S analysis with bootstrap null bands is the honest test for memory: stock prices are near H = 0.5, but the voting/non-voting log-spread shows H ≈ 0.33–0.41, and the pairs strategy's profitability ranks monotonically with how far H sits below 0.5. Anti-persistence, measured properly, is a tradable mean-reversion license.
8. Apparent long memory in a series can be an artifact of time-varying parameters. Locally time-homogeneous (adaptive-interval) estimation competes with (G)ARCH/FIGARCH by letting the data pick the window: largest interval not rejected against its own subintervals. On the Thai Baht basket, the adaptive estimator produced the best realized trading profits of four coefficient-tracking methods.
9. Power-transforming returns (|R|^0.5) converts multiplicative volatility noise into nearly additive, nearly Gaussian noise — the trick that makes nonparametric volatility and interval-adaptive volatility estimation feasible.
10. QMC (Halton/Sobol) with the Koksma-Hlawka error bound beats MC's 1/√n for moderate dimensions; it dies in high-dimension coordinates with large prime bases unless the Brownian bridge concentrates effective dimension into the leading coordinates; randomized (shifted) QMC restores an error bar.
11. GARCH is an ARMA(1,1) in the squares, and in logs of squares a nonlinear ARMA — so deconvolution kernel methods can estimate the volatility response surface g without ever proxying σ_t. Use it as a specification test for whether parametric GARCH/TGARCH shapes are right.
12. Control charts (EWMA, CUSUM) with ARL calibrated to the actual fat-tailed return distribution give a principled alarm system for regime change in any monitored edge or ratio; (I−Q)L = 1 via Markov chains makes the false-alarm/detection tradeoff computable exactly.
13. Multivariate BEKK shows volatility spillovers between assets (off-diagonal A, G matter) and beats univariate density forecasts in ~82% of windows: forecast the covariance, not just the variances.
14. A Kalman state-space model with a smooth latent common component extracts a cleaner market index from noisy heterogeneous observations (house prices), handles missing data natively, and the prediction-error decomposition is the exact likelihood.

## Connections to prior books

- Levy, Computational Finance: the deepest overlap in the programme. Levy's PCA-GARCH is Ch 6's PCA logic applied to covariance dynamics; Levy's Sobol quasi-MC and Box-Muller machinery are Ch 16's Halton/QMC pricing toolkit (Levy rated Sobol/Niederreiter above Faure; this chapter's base-Halton construction degrades with dimension unless bridged); Levy's GARCH-MLE identification on the S&P 500 is the parametric counterpart of Ch 17's nonparametric specification test; Levy's EM/MREG missing-data imputation parallels Ch 13's Kalman treatment of missing observations. Levy's theta-weighted finite differences vs QMC pricing are the two poles of the pricing-numerics spectrum.
- Javaheri, Inside Volatility Arbitrage: Ch 9 is Javaheri's consistency problem (options imply more skew/kurtosis than the time series) executed as a live trading strategy with real P&L and the peso-risk caveat both books insist on; Javaheri's filtered MLE/particle filtering is the sophisticated cousin of Ch 13's Kalman filtering and Ch 15's adaptive windows; his Dupire/Derman-Kani local-vol instability discussion matches Ch 7's D&K negative-probability failures and B&C fixes.
- Björk, Arbitrage Theory in Continuous Time: Ch 7-9's state-price densities and Arrow-Debreu prices are the discrete shadow of Björk's martingale pricing and the financial market completeness theorem; the Breeden-Litzenberger second derivative is the finite-strike version of Björk's risk-neutral valuation; Ch 16's risk-neutral density g(S_T|...) is Björk's martingale measure in simulation clothing.
- Bouchaud et al., Trades, Quotes and Prices: their fat-tailed returns and square-root impact justify Ch 3's rejection of Gaussian spread VaR (kurtosis 6–200) and Ch 5's tail-dependence alarm; Bouchaud's long-memory order flow connects to Ch 14's R/S evidence that prices themselves sit near H = 0.5 while spreads revert.
- The Quants (Patterson): the 1987 portfolio-insurance feedback loop is the historical embodiment of Ch 9's S1 trade blowing up in June/Sept 1998; Mandelbrot's vindication is Ch 14's fractional Brownian motion going mainstream.
- Hasbrouck, Empirical Market Microstructure: his state-space efficient-price filtering (Roll descendant) is the direct ancestor of Ch 13's Kalman common-component extraction; his VAR/impulse-response machinery is the structural cousin of Ch 11's SPC monitoring; Ch 4's time-unstable transition matrices echo his regime-dependence findings.
- Bjais-Glosten-Spatt/Stoll/Madhavan surveys: Ch 5's asset-vs-default correlation ratio (≈/10) operationalizes the informed-tail-risk intuition those surveys develop verbally; Stoll's spread decomposition parallels Ch 3's risk decomposition into market/residual/event components.
- Harris, Trading and Exchanges: Ch 14's voting/non-voting spread is a textbook case of his two-instruments-one-fundamental principle; the strategy is a relative-value trade that microstructure theory says should be nearly information-free (same underlying cash flows), which is why its reversion is stable enough to trade.
- Elliott Wave base (Balan): Ch 6's level/curvature/twist factors give a quantitative vol-regime clock that can discipline wave-degree assumptions (a persistent PC1 rise with steepening PC2 is a fifth-wave-blowoff signature); Ch 14's H < 0.5 spreads license mean-reversion trading of wave-terminal overextensions, the statistical counterpart of wave-count exhaustion signals; Ch 11's CUSUM can act as the objective confirmation line that Elliott practitioners otherwise draw by eye.
- Williams, Master the Markets (VSA): Ch 9's finding that the market persistently overprices crash states relative to history is the quantified version of VSA's climactic-selling premium (effort vs results at the extremes); Ch 15's adaptive interval shrinking flags exactly the news/surprise bars VSA treats as regime markers.

## Strategy / research candidates (weekly NQ practice)

1. Implied-vs-historical skew/kurtosis monitor (Ch 9): with NDX/NQ options data, compute the implied SPD skew and kurtosis (via an implied tree or Rookley fit) and the time-series equivalents on a rolling window; track the ratio as a regime gauge. When the gap is historically wide, the market is paying a fat crash premium — an Elliott Wave practitioner can read that as crowd psychology (wave-5 euphoria with crash hedging), and it sizes contrarian wave-terminal positions. Do not trade the raw S1 tail-sale without explicit crash-hedge accounting.
2. Vol-surface factor clock (Ch 6): run PCA on a rolling implied-vol surface for NDX options; PC1 = vol level (regression proxy for realized-vol regime), PC2 = smile curvature (fear gauge). Combine with wave counts: rising PC1 with rising PC2 into a projected wave-5 = climax warning; falling PC2 with flat PC1 supports corrective-wave continuation trades.
3. Pairs-spread memory test before any mean-reversion trade (Ch 14): apply R/S with Brownian-motion bootstrap bands to any spread you contemplate (NQ vs ES, NQ vs relative sector baskets). Trade reversion only when Ĥ is significantly below 0.5, and set the M-day extremum trigger and holding logic exactly as in the chapter; expected performance ranks with (0.5 − Ĥ), and transaction costs decide viability.
4. CUSUM edge monitor (Ch 11): put a CUSUM/EWMA chart on any tracked statistic of your own practice (wave-count hit rate, rolling forecast error of a vol model, a strategy's rolling Sharpe); calibrate ARL to the fat-tailed return distribution so false alarms are priced correctly. An alarm = regime change = revisit wave degree and strategy assumptions before risking new positions.
5. Locally time-homogeneous volatility (Ch 15): estimate NQ vol by adaptive-interval averaging of |R|^0.5 rather than a global GARCH; the estimated interval length m̂(t) is itself a market-stability gauge (short windows = unstable regime = smaller position sizes, regardless of wave count).
6. BEKK spillover check (Ch 10): model NQ/ES (or NQ/bond futures) bivariate volatility; off-diagonal spillover terms measure cross-market fear transmission — useful for confirming or vetoing intermarket Elliott Wave scenarios.
7. Butterfly-surface cross-validation (Ch 8): when eventually working with real option quotes, validate any SPD or local-vol model against butterfly prices before trusting its implied signals; the market's butterflies price fatter tails than smooth models capture, so treat model-SPD kurtosis as a floor, not a truth.
8. Skew-flattening dictionary (Ch 7, Table 7.1): build the relative-value rule table for your own fitted surface — model skew flatter than market = sell OTM puts/buy OTM calls; steeper = reverse — as a paper-trading overlay that keeps implied-vol work honest between wave-count sessions.
