# Arbitrage Theory in Continuous Time (Björk) — study notes

This is the mathematical core of the quant stack: the machinery that makes "price = discounted expectation under a special probability measure" true, and shows exactly when a fair price is unique. Read as the formal backbone of everything The Quants' characters were trading on, and as the theory Hasbrouck's efficient-price martingales presuppose.

**How to read these notes:** Part I is self-contained foundations. Each chapter answers one question: Ch 2-3 *what does "fair price" mean* (in discrete time, where you can see every gear turn); Ch 4-5 *what calculus do random processes obey*; Ch 6 *what does a self-financing portfolio mean in continuous time*; Ch 7-8 *why is there a unique derivative price at all*; Ch 9 *how do traders actually manage derivative risk*.

---

## 1. The project of the book (Ch 1)

Two problems define derivative finance:

1. **Pricing**: what is a "fair" price today for a contract whose payoff depends on the future?
2. **Hedging**: how do you construct a portfolio in the underlying that replicates that payoff, so the price carries no risk?

The philosophy is **relative pricing**: we never price anything "absolutely". We take the underlying asset's price as given by the market (unexplained), and price everything derivable from it *consistently* with that price. The book's running example (a currency-risk contract between two companies) shows both naive candidate prices (expected value under real probabilities; the risk-free bank valuation) are wrong, which motivates the entire theory.

**Durable frame**: absolute pricing asks "what is this worth?" — relative pricing asks "given that the market has already priced S, what is anything defined on S forced to cost?" Arbitrage theory is the enforcement mechanism: any inconsistency with the underlying's price creates a money pump.

---

## 2. Discrete time: where every gear is visible (Ch 2-3)

### 2.1 One-period binomial model

One stock, two outcomes (u with real probability p, d with 1-p), a risk-free rate R over the period.

- **No-arbitrage condition**: `d ≤ 1+R ≤ u`. If the stock's worst case beats the bank, borrow and buy the stock (free money); if its best case loses to the bank, sell stock and lend. Arbitrage is impossible exactly when the risk-free return sits between the stock's two possible gross returns.
- **Martingale (risk-neutral) probabilities**: the unique q with `q_u·u + (1-q_u)·d = 1+R`, i.e.

  `q_u = ((1+R) − d) / (u − d)`.

  This is *not* the real probability p. It is the probability that makes the stock's expected future price equal its future bank-account value.
- **Pricing**: any payoff X is priced `Π(0) = (1+R)^(−1) · E^Q[X]` — discount the Q-expectation. The real probability p appears nowhere.
- **Replication**: with bond + stock you can build any payoff contingent on the two states: solve two linear equations in two portfolio weights. One bond + one stock spans two states, so *every* claim is replicable → the market is **complete**.
- **Key moral (Björk stresses it)**: objective probabilities only determine which events are *possible*; they are irrelevant to *prices*. Prices come from consistency (no arbitrage) plus replication, not from forecasts.

### 2.2 Multiperiod binomial

- A portfolio process is **self-financing** if each rebalancing is paid for entirely by the portfolio's own value: no money in, no money out.
- Replication proceeds by **backward induction**: at the final nodes you know the payoff; one step back, each two-node subtree is a one-period problem whose replicating cost is the node's price; recurse to the root. This is the binomial algorithm used everywhere in practice.
- Price: `Π(0) = (1+R)^(−T) · E^Q[X]`, Q now the martingale measure over the whole tree (unique again, by chaining the one-period condition).

### 2.3 General one-period model (N assets, M states) — the two Fundamental Theorems

Matrix D: rows = assets, columns = states (payoff in each state). The theorems, via **Farkas' lemma** (a linear system has a solution or a separating arbitrage exists):

- **First Fundamental Theorem**: the market is arbitrage-free **iff** there exists a martingale measure Q (state prices q_j > 0 summing to 1 that price every asset correctly).
- **Second Fundamental Theorem**: the market is **complete iff** the martingale measure is **unique**.
- Linear algebra test: complete ⟺ `rank(D) = M` (as many independent payoffs as states).
- **Stochastic discount factor view**: pricing can be written `Π(0) = E^P[ΛX]` where `Λ = L/(1+R)`, L the Radon-Nikodym derivative `dQ/dP` (statewise ratio of risk-neutral to real probabilities). Λ is the "price of probability itself": Arrow-Debreu state prices divided by the discount factor. Real-world and risk-neutral views are the same statement: multiply by Λ under P, or reweight by q under Q.

**Durable lesson**: "no arbitrage" = positive state prices exist; "completeness" = those prices are unique because every state can be synthesized with traded assets. Everything in Part III continuous time is these two facts with harder functional analysis.

---

## 3. The calculus of randomness (Ch 4-5)

### 3.1 Wiener process and the Itô integral

- **Wiener process W**: continuous paths, W(0)=0, independent Gaussian increments, variance = time elapsed. Continuous but nowhere differentiable — "velocity" does not exist, only differentials.
- **Filtration**: the information accumulated by time t. A process is **adapted** if its value at t depends only on information up to t (you cannot integrate what you cannot yet see).
- **Itô integral construction**: build `∫g dW` from simple (piecewise-constant, adapted) integrands, then take L² limits. Crucial detail: the integrand is evaluated at the *left* endpoint of each interval — you trade before you see the increment. Consequences:
  - `E[∫ g dW] = 0` — a fair game held to infinite horizon has zero expected gain;
  - **Itô isometry**: `E[(∫g dW)²] = E[∫g² dt]`;
  - the integral is a **martingale**: its conditional expected future value is its current value. Stochastic integrals are the mathematical embodiment of "no predictable drift from fair-game trading".
- A process is a martingale **iff** its differential has no dt (drift) term.

### 3.2 Quadratic variation and the multiplication table

The identity that changes all of calculus: over a small dt, the random term contributes

`(dW)² = dt` — with probability approaching 1, since Var[(ΔW)²] shrinks faster than its mean.

Formal multiplication table: `(dt)² = 0, dt·dW = 0, (dW)² = dt`. Second-order terms in W are *first-order* in t, so chain rules pick up extra terms.

### 3.3 The Itô formula

For `Z = f(t, X)` with `dX = μ dt + σ dW`:

`df = (∂f/∂t + μ ∂f/∂x + ½σ² ∂²f/∂x²) dt + σ ∂f/∂x dW`

The extra `½σ² ∂²f/∂x²` vs ordinary calculus is the direct algebraic consequence of (dW)² = dt. (Proof heuristic: Taylor-expand f, kill terms with (dt)² and dt·dW, keep (dW)².)

**Worked examples worth keeping** (they are the template for all later pricing computations):
- `E[W⁴(t)] = 3t²` (write Z=W⁴, take dZ via Itô, integrate, take expectations — the dW term dies);
- `E[e^{αW(t)}] = e^{α²t/2}` (exponential of Gaussian: the ODE trick — define m(t)=E[Z], get an ODE, solve);
- `∫₀ᵗ W dW = W²(t)/2 − t/2` — *not* the naive W²/2. Itô calculus ≠ ordinary calculus, and this −t/2 is the same correction that reappears inside GBM as the −σ²t/2 term.
- A standard computing technique (used all book long): to find E[Y], write Y = Z(t₀) for a process Z with an Itô differential, use the fact that the stochastic integral has zero mean, and convert to an ODE for the mean. Arbitrage pricing is mostly this trick plus a change of measure.
- Lemma: with deterministic integrand, `∫σ(s)dW(s)` is **normal** with variance `∫σ²(s)ds`.

### 3.4 Multidimensional and correlated

- Vector version: `dX = μ dt + σ dW` with n components driven by d independent Wiener processes; C = σσ* is the instantaneous covariance matrix; Itô formula gains `½ tr[σ*Hσ]` where H is the Hessian.
- **Correlated Wiener processes**: build W = δW̄ with unit-length rows; correlation matrix `ρ = δδ*`; then `dW_i · dW_j = ρ_ij dt` in the multiplication table. Any model written with independent drivers can be rewritten with correlated drivers, and vice versa (`ρ_ij = σ_iσ_j*/ (‖σ_i‖‖σ_j‖)`). Useful later for multi-asset and currency models.

### 3.5 Stochastic differential equations (Ch 5)

- **Existence/uniqueness** (Cauchy-Picard iteration) under Lipschitz conditions on μ and σ; the solution is adapted, continuous, Markov, and square-exponential bounded.
- **Geometric Brownian motion (GBM)**: `dX = αX dt + σX dW`. The building block of asset prices. Solve via Z = ln X:

  `X(t) = x₀ · exp{(α − σ²/2)t + σW(t)}`

  — lognormal, and note the **−σ²/2**: volatility drags the median below the mean. Expectation: `E[X(t)] = x₀ e^{αt}` (drift unaffected in the mean; the drag hits the median).
- **Linear SDE**: `dX = (AX + b)dt + σ dW` solves with matrix exponentials exactly like the ODE case (the Itô second-order correction does not interfere because the equation is linear in X).

### 3.6 Generator, Feynman-Kač, Kolmogorov

- **Infinitesimal generator** (Dynkin/Itô/Kolmogorov backward operator):

  `Ah = Σ μ_i ∂h/∂x_i + ½ Σ C_ij ∂²h/∂x_i∂x_j`

  — the drift + "diffusion-curvature" part of any Itô expansion. Itô's formula becomes `df = (∂f/∂t + Af)dt + (∇f)σ dW`.
- **Feynman-Kač (the bridge from PDE to expectation)**: if F solves `∂F/∂t + μ ∂F/∂x + ½σ² ∂²F/∂x² = 0` with terminal F(T,x)=Φ(x), then

  `F(t,x) = E_{t,x}[Φ(X_T)]`

  where X has the matching dynamics. With a `−rF` term in the PDE, the representation gains the discount factor: `F = e^{−r(T−t)} E[Φ(X_T)]`. **This is why every pricing problem can be attacked either as a PDE or as a discounted expected payoff — they are the same object.**
- `F(t,X_t)` is a martingale iff F solves `∂F/∂t + AF = 0`.
- **Kolmogorov backward equation**: transition probabilities/densities solve the PDE in the *backward* variables (s,y). **Kolmogorov forward (Fokker-Planck)**: the density in the *forward* variables evolves under the adjoint operator A*: `∂p/∂t = A*p`. Example: Wiener density spreads as the Gaussian heat kernel.

---

## 4. What "self-financing" means in continuous time (Ch 6)

Björk builds it discretely first, and the construction exposes a subtlety most books hide:

- Discrete budget equation: old portfolio sold at new prices = new portfolio + consumption. Naively letting Δt→0 gives `S dh = 0`... **which is wrong**, because the Itô integral was defined with *forward* increments while the budget equation has a *backward* h-difference. Fixing it (`S dh + dS dh + c dt = 0`) and inserting into dV = h dS + S dh + dS dh leaves:

  **Self-financing**: `dV(t) = h(t) dS(t) − c(t) dt`.

  The pleasing intuition ("wealth changes only because asset prices change") is correct, but only with forward-increment (Itô) bookkeeping — the cross term dS·dh is exactly the (dW)²-order correction.
- **Relative portfolio** `u_i = h_iS_i/V`: express holdings as fractions of wealth; self-financing becomes `dV = V Σ u_i dS_i/S_i − c dt`.
- **Dividends**: add cumulative dividend processes D; wealth gains `h dS + h dD`; equivalently price off the **gain process G = S + D**.

---

## 5. Arbitrage pricing: the Black-Scholes argument (Ch 7)

### 5.1 Setup

- Risk-free asset: `dB = rB dt` (a bank account; no dW term).
- Stock: `dS = αS dt + σS dW̄` under the objective measure P. With r, α, σ constants this is the **Black-Scholes model**.
- **Contingent claim**: any payoff X known by time T as a function of the price history; **simple claim** X = Φ(S(T)) with contract function Φ (European call: Φ(s)=max[s−K,0]).
- **Arbitrage**: self-financed, zero initial cost, non-negative always, positive with positive probability. Assumption: the market (stock, bond, derivative) admits none.

### 5.2 The equation

- Lemma: any self-financed portfolio whose value process has no dW term (locally riskless) must grow at exactly r — otherwise borrow-at-r / invest-at-k is a money pump. "Only one riskless rate can exist."
- Assume the derivative's price is Π(t) = F(t, S(t)) for smooth F (Markov argument). Itô gives F's dynamics in terms of F's partials. Form the stock+derivative relative portfolio choosing weights to **cancel the dW term** (both assets share one source of noise, so one combination is riskless). That riskless combination must earn r. After substitution and cleanup:

  **Black-Scholes PDE**: `F_t + r s F_s + ½ σ² s² F_ss − r F = 0`, with `F(T,s) = Φ(s)`.

Two observations Björk flags:
1. The derivation assumed the derivative *is traded* (a real market price exists) — shaky for OTC contracts; Chapter 8's replication argument repairs this.
2. **α (the expected stock return) does not appear.** The price of a derivative is the same whether the stock is expected to earn +10%, +50%, or −50%. Only σ survives. Counter-intuitive but structural: replication removes the risk, so nobody is compensated for bearing it, so nobody's forecast of drift matters.

### 5.3 Risk-neutral valuation and the formula

- The PDE is exactly of Feynman-Kač type, so with the dynamics `dX = rX dt + σX dW` (drift r, not α):

  **`Π(t) = F(t,S(t)), F(t,s) = e^{−r(T−t)} E^Q_{t,s}[Φ(S_T)]`**

- Reframing: define the **martingale measure Q** — the probability measure under which the stock's drift is r. Pricing = discounted expectation under Q. **Normalized prices are Q-martingales**: for any traded asset, Π(t)/B(t) has no drift under Q. This is the continuous-time echo of the discrete martingale measure of Ch 2-3, and the precise sense in which "efficient price" (Hasbrouck) means martingale.
- Interpretation: the formula is what *risk-neutral investors* would compute — but nobody is assumed risk-neutral; the formula is **preference-free** because replication makes preferences irrelevant.
- **Black-Scholes formula** (call): computing the expectation of max[s·e^Z − K, 0] with Z Gaussian gives

  `c = s N(d₁) − K e^{−r(T−t)} N(d₂)`, `d₁ = [ln(s/K) + (r + σ²/2)(T−t)] / (σ√(T−t))`, `d₂ = d₁ − σ√(T−t)`.

### 5.4 Forwards, futures, Black-76

- **Forward price**: the delivery price making the contract worth zero today. Risk-neutral valuation gives `f(t;T,X) = E^Q_t[X]`; for the stock itself, `f(t;T) = e^{r(T−t)} S_t` (cost-of-carry). Forward price ≠ value of the contract (the contract is worth 0 at inception).
- **Futures**: same delivery economics but gains/losses are settled continuously (daily marks), so with *deterministic* rates futures price = forward price. Options on futures are priced with **Black's (1976) formula**: `c = e^{−r(T−t)} [F N(d₁) − K N(d₂)]` with σ applied to the futures price — the formula the quants in Patterson's books would have been running on futures desks. Directly relevant machinery for anything NQ-related with options.

### 5.5 Volatility: the one unobservable input

- **Historic volatility**: sample log returns ξ_i = ln(S(t_i)/S(t_{i−1)); under GBM these are independent normals with variance σ²Δt; estimator σ* = s_ξ/√Δt, standard error ≈ σ*/√(2n). Standard practice: match the sampling window to the option's remaining life.
- **Implied volatility**: invert the BS formula using a traded benchmark option's price — the σ the market is implicitly using. Pricing your option off implied vol prices *consistently with* the market instead of from your own estimate.
- **The smile**: if BS-GBM were literally true, implied vol plotted against strike would be a flat line. Empirically it curves (and after 1987, skews): far-from-the-money options trade at higher implied vol. The smile is a direct, non-parametric falsification of the constant-volatility lognormal model — the market's own aggregate statement that real tails are fatter than Gaussian. This is the theoretical hinge of The Quants' Ch 4 (the post-1987 smile as fear made structural) and of Bouchaud's fat-tail program.
- **American options**: early exercise is an optimal-stopping problem (free boundary PDEs; no closed forms in general). One exception: **an American call on a non-dividend stock is never exercised early** (its live value always exceeds its exercise value s−K, because C ≥ c ≥ s − Ke^{−r(T−t)} > s−K), so it prices as European. Early exercise matters for puts and for dividend-paying stocks.

---

## 6. Why the price is unique: replication (Ch 8)

The deeper argument that repairs Ch 7's "assume the derivative is traded" gap:

- **Reachability/hedging**: a claim X is reachable if a self-financing portfolio h exists with V^h(T) = X surely. Holding the portfolio is then *financially identical* to holding the contract in every state of the world, so the only no-arbitrage price of the contract is the portfolio's value process. Any other price is arbitraged against the synthetic version. Two hedges of the same claim must have identical value processes.
- **The explicit hedge**: for a simple claim, if F solves the BS PDE, then the relative portfolio

  `u* = sF_s/F` in the stock, `u⁰ = 1 − u*` in the bond

  replicates X, with value process V = F(t,S(t)). In absolute terms: **hold F_s(t,S(t)) shares of stock** — the delta — and the rest in bonds. This is simultaneously the uniqueness proof and the actual hedge recipe.
- **Why the derivative is priced without knowing anyone's preferences**: in a complete market the derivative is *superfluous* — it can always be synthesized. Unique price = no degrees of freedom left.
- **Drift-independence explained**: replication works with P-probability 1, so it works under any *equivalent* measure (same null sets). Changing measure (Girsanov, previewed) changes drifts but never the diffusion coefficient — so nothing about α can matter to a hedge. Deep one-line summary: **diffusion, not drift, carries the pricing information**.
- **Path-dependent claims** (Asian: payoff on the time-average of S): augment the state with Z(t) = ∫g(S)du; F then solves a PDE in (t, s, z) and the same machinery works. Lookbacks are harder.
- **Meta-theorem (counting rule)**, one of the most practically useful heuristics in the book: with M traded risky assets and R independent random sources,
  - arbitrage-free **iff M ≤ R**
  - complete **iff M ≥ R**
  - complete and arbitrage-free **iff M = R**.

  Black-Scholes: 1 asset, 1 Wiener → both. Add a non-traded source of randomness (stochastic vol, jump risk) and markets become *incomplete*: prices are only bracketed, not pinned — the theoretical door through which the volatility risk premium enters.

---

## 7. Parity relations, the greeks, and delta hedging (Ch 9)

### 7.1 Linearity and static replication

- Pricing is linear in contract functions: Π(αΦ + βΨ) = αΠ(Φ) + βΠ(Ψ) (expectation is linear).
- **Put-call parity**: `Φ_{P,K} = KΦ_B + Φ_{C,K} − Φ_S`, so `p = Ke^{−r(T−t)} + c − s`. A put is a static portfolio: bond + call − stock (buy-and-hold, no rebalancing).
- **Static replication proposition**: *any* continuous compactly-supported payoff can be approximated arbitrarily well (sup-norm) by a fixed portfolio of bonds, stock, and calls. Piecewise-linear payoffs = call spreads; everything else is a limit of them. Bull spreads, straddles, binary-like payoffs are all exercises in this algebra.

### 7.2 The greeks

For portfolio pricing function P(t,s):

| Greek | Derivative | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| Delta | ∂P/∂s | exposure to the underlying's price |
| Gamma | ∂²P/∂s² | how fast delta itself moves (curvature) |
| Theta | ∂P/∂t | time decay |
| Rho | ∂P/∂r | rate sensitivity |
| Vega | ∂P/∂σ | volatility sensitivity (model misspecification gauge) |

For a European call: Δ = N(d₁); Γ = φ(d₁)/(sσ√(T−t)); Θ = −sφ(d₁)σ/(2√(T−t)) − rKe^{−r(T−t)}N(d₂); ρ = K(T−t)e^{−r(T−t)}N(d₂); V = sφ(d₁)√(T−t). Graphs: delta is a smoothed step (0→1), gamma/vega peak at-the-money, theta most negative near ATM.

### 7.3 Delta hedging and its limits

- Delta of the derivative = number of shares needed to hedge it (Example 9.6 inverted). Adding x units of a hedging instrument: `x = −Δ_P/Δ_F`.
- **Delta hedging is a tangent-line approximation** — exact only for infinitesimal moves. As s drifts, the old delta goes stale; hence **discrete rebalancing**, and the trade-off: rebalance often = good hedge but high transaction costs.
- **Gamma is the rebalancing frequency meter**: high gamma (ATM, near expiry) forces frequent rebalancing; low gamma lets you hold.
- **Gamma neutrality** cannot be achieved with the stock (Γ_S = 0), so you need one derivative to fix gamma and then the *stock* to re-fix delta (two-step triangular scheme; two derivatives also work but couples the equations). The greeks of a self-financing portfolio obey `Θ + rsΔ + ½σ²s²Γ = rP` — a delta-and-gamma-neutral portfolio is automatically riskless and earns exactly r.

---

## 8. The martingale approach in full generality (Ch 10)

Chapter 7-9's derivation leaned on the assumption that the derivative *is traded*. Chapter 10 rebuilds everything on the martingale machinery, and this is where the two Fundamental Theorems get their continuous-time, fully general form.

- **Admissibility (why "any self-financing portfolio" is too big a class)**: with unrestricted strategies and unlimited credit, a roulette-style **doubling strategy** (double the bet after each loss, stop at the first win, all financed by bank loans, played on a shrinking time grid) is a theoretical arbitrage. Real markets kill it with credit limits; the theory kills it by requiring the portfolio's value to be **bounded from below** (a credit constraint). Practical echo: any "can't-lose" martingale betting scheme (traders' averaging-down) is exactly this, and it dies on exactly the constraint Björk writes down — finite capital.
- **First Fundamental Theorem (precise form, Delbaen-Schachermayer)**: for locally bounded price processes, no arbitrage in the technically correct sense of **No Free Lunch with Vanishing Risk (NFLVR)** holds **iff** there exists an equivalent (local) martingale measure Q under which all *normalized* prices S_i/S_0 are (local) martingales. The proof idea: the set of reachable zero-cost claims and the nonnegative payoffs must be separable by a strictly positive random variable L, which then serves as the Radon-Nikodym derivative dQ/dP. The hard parts are functional-analytic (L¹ vs L^∞ duality, weak* topology, Kreps-Yan separation theorem, and NFLVR guaranteeing weak* closedness).
- **Numeraire invariance**: self-financing is independent of the choice of accounting asset (S-self-financing ⟺ Z-self-financing with Z = S/S₀), values just rescale. This "invariance lemma" is what lets all pricing be done in the normalized economy and is the seed of the change-of-numeraire technique used heavily in fixed income later.
- **Second Fundamental Theorem**: arbitrage-free market is complete **iff** the martingale measure is unique. The mechanism: hedging X ⟺ the Q-martingale M(t) = E^Q[X/S₀(T)|F_t] admits a representation as stochastic integrals against the normalized asset prices; uniqueness of Q pins the representation (via Jacod's theorem on extremal martingale measures).
- **The general pricing formula**: `Π(t;X) = S₀(t) E^Q[X/S₀(T) | F_t]`. With the money account as numeraire: the familiar `Π = E^Q[e^{-∫r ds} X | F_t]`. If X is attainable, every choice of Q gives the same price; if not, different Q's give different prices.
- **Stochastic discount factor formalism**: `Λ(t) = e^{-∫₀ᵗ r ds} L(t)` (discount factor × likelihood process). Prices can be written entirely under the objective measure as `Π(t;X) = E^P[Λ(T)/Λ(t) · X | F_t]`, and ΛS is a P-martingale for every asset price S. One-to-one correspondence martingale measure ↔ SDF. This is the bridge to the asset-pricing literature (Cochrane) and to the Hansen-Jagannathan bounds.
- **Who chooses the martingale measure? The market.** In a complete market Q is unique, so theory alone prices everything. In an incomplete market, no-arbitrage only constrains all derivatives to share one Q; *which* Q is a fact about aggregate risk aversion, supply and demand — an empirical, not theoretical, quantity. (Björk's emphatic summary in 10.7.)

## 9. The two workhorses (Ch 11)

- **Martingale Representation Theorem**: in a purely Wiener-driven world (filtration generated by W itself), *every* martingale is a stochastic integral against W: M(t) = M(0) + ∫h dW. Fails if there is any other randomness (e.g. an independent Poisson process: N(t) − λt is a martingale with jumps, and integrals against W are continuous). Abstract existence: h exists and is unique, but the theorem does not tell you what it looks like (the explicit Clark-Ocone formula needs Malliavin calculus).
- **Girsanov Theorem**: choose an adapted kernel φ; form the likelihood process L via dL = φL dW (the Doléans exponential, L(t) = exp{∫φ dW − ½∫‖φ‖²dt}); define dQ = L(T)dP. Then under Q, the old W acquires drift: `dW^P = φ dt + dW^Q`. **Change of measure changes drift, never diffusion.** The Novikov condition (E[exp(½∫‖φ‖²dt)] < ∞) guarantees L is a true martingale so Q is a genuine probability measure.
- **Converse Girsanov**: in a Wiener filtration, *every* absolutely continuous measure change is of Girsanov type. Combined: in a Wiener world we have complete control of the class of possible martingale measures — it is exactly the set of solutions of one linear equation.
- **Maximum likelihood via Girsanov**: estimating an unknown drift α from observed paths is a family of Girsanov transformations; the likelihood is exp{αX_t − ½α²t} and the ML estimator is X_t/t. Elegant, and a template for statistical inference on stochastic models.

## 10. Black-Scholes from the martingale viewpoint (Ch 12)

- The martingale measure equation α + σh = r has the unique solution **h = −λ** where **λ = (α − r)/σ, the market price of risk** ("risk premium per unit volatility"). So the Girsanov kernel that moves P to Q is minus the market price of risk. The market is arbitrage-free (Q exists) and complete (Q unique).
- Completeness proof via martingale representation: normalized price Z = S/B is a Q-martingale with dZ = ZσdW; any claim's Q-martingale can be written against W, hence against Z. The abstract hedge becomes concrete for simple claims: hold F_s shares (the delta), rest in bonds — recovering Chapter 8's result with far less machinery-specific hand-waving.

## 11. Multidimensional models (Ch 13-14)

- **Setup**: n stocks driven by n independent Wiener processes, **nonsingular volatility matrix σ** — the M = R rule enforced by linear algebra.
- **Market prices of risk vector**: no-arbitrage forces `α − r·1_n = σλ`, i.e. every stock's excess return is a linear combination of its factor loadings, with the *same* multiplier vector λ = σ⁻¹(α − r1) for all assets (CAPM-style). The pricing PDE follows; again α never enters — only through λ, which cancels.
- **State-space reduction (homogeneous claims)**: if the payoff is homogeneous of degree 1 in the assets (e.g. an **exchange option** max[S₁ − S₂, 0]), the n-dimensional PDE collapses to n−1 dimensions. The exchange option (Margrabe) prices as a Black-Scholes call with strike 1 on the ratio, with volatility √(σ₁² + σ₂²) (plus a correlation correction if the drivers are correlated). Big practical lesson: **relative-performance claims price off ratios and volatility spreads, not levels**.
- **Martingale version (Ch 14)**: with n assets and k Wiener processes:
  - the martingale-measure equation is `σφ = r − α`; **generic no-arbitrage ⟺ σ has rank n (surjective), so n ≤ k** — at least as many risk sources as assets;
  - **completeness ⟺ k = n and σ invertible** (in a Wiener filtration);
  - the hedging equation is the **adjoint** of the martingale-measure equation: absence of arbitrage and completeness are literally dual linear-algebra conditions (kernel vs image of σ*). This is the rigorous home of the M vs R meta-theorem.
- **The Hansen-Jagannathan bounds**: defining the minimal market price of risk λ̂ (minimum-norm solution), any asset's **Sharpe ratio** satisfies

  `|α_π − r| / ‖σ_π‖ ≤ ‖λ̂‖ ≤ ‖λ‖`

  Read left to right: an observed Sharpe ratio is a *lower bound* on the volatility of the stochastic discount factor. This is the formal backbone of the **equity premium puzzle**: historically observed equity Sharpe ratios are so high that any plausible risk-averse representative-agent SDF would need implausibly enormous volatility. λ also *is* the volatility vector of the SDF. A benchmark result for connecting market data to preferences without a model.

## 12. Incomplete markets (Ch 15)

The pivotal chapter conceptually: what happens when the underlying *cannot be traded* (Björk's running example: weather insurance on the temperature at Brighton's Palace Pier).

- With a nontraded underlying X and only a bank account, M = 0 traded risky assets vs R = 1 risk sources: incomplete. **No unique price exists.** Trying to "delta-hedge against X" is meaningless — you cannot put 15°C in a portfolio.
- What no-arbitrage *does* imply: **internal consistency across derivatives**. All derivatives written on X must share the same **market price of risk** λ = (α_F − r)/σ_F. So prices co-move in a rigid family even though no individual price is pinned.
- **Benchmark pricing**: take the market price of any one traded derivative as given; it identifies λ, and then *every* other derivative's price is determined relative to it. With n risk sources, n benchmark prices pin the whole surface. No-arbitrage prices derivatives *in terms of* benchmarks, never absolutely.
- Black-Scholes recovered as the special case where the underlying itself is traded: the trivial benchmark claim "one share at T" has known price S_t, which pins λ = (α − r)/σ, and everything reduces to Chapter 7.
- **Calibration**: since λ is chosen by the market, it must be backed out of observed prices (least squares over a parameterized family λ(t,x;β)) — "inverting the yield curve" in interest-rate theory, "implied volatility" in equity options. Same operation everywhere: price data → implied risk preferences.
- The chapter notes survey the canonical ways to pick a martingale measure in incomplete markets: quadratic hedging (Föllmer-Sondermann, Schweizer; minimal and variance-optimal measures), minimal entropy, Esscher, utility (Davis).
- **Deep summary (Result 15.6.1)**: in any arbitrage-free market, complete or not, every asset obeys `α_π − r = σ_π λ` with a *universal* λ. Complete market: λ pinned by the model. Incomplete: λ chosen by the market's aggregate risk aversion — an empirical object.

## 13. Dividends (Ch 16)

- **Discrete dividends**: at each dividend time the stock must drop by exactly the dividend (jump condition S_t = S_{t−} − δ[S_{t−}], else arbitrage across the ex-date). Pricing is piecewise Black-Scholes solved backwards with jump-glued boundary conditions. For proportional dividends δ[s] = δs: price with the ordinary no-dividend formula on the deflated initial price, `F_δ(t,s) = F₀(t, (1−δ)ⁿ s)`, n = number of remaining ex-dates.
- **Continuous dividend yield** dD = δS dt: the pricing PDE's drift becomes (r − δ)s, the Q-dynamics become `dS = (r − δ)S dt + σS dW`, and `F_δ(t,s) = F₀(t, se^{−δ(T−t)})`. Under Q it is the **normalized gain process** (price + reinvested dividends, all divided by B) that is a martingale, not the bare price. Cost-of-carry intuition: today's price = discounted expected dividends + discounted terminal price.
- General dividend structures (stochastic payout rate, needed for futures later) extend the same machinery with F depending on (t, S, D).

## 14. Currency derivatives (Ch 17, in progress)

- **A foreign currency is exactly a stock with a continuous dividend yield equal to the foreign interest rate.** Holding dollars, you do not stuff mattresses: they earn r_f in deposit. So FX options cannot be priced with naive Black-Scholes on the spot rate.
- The domestic-currency value of a foreign bank account is B_f·X; forcing its Q-drift to r_d gives the **Q-dynamics of the exchange rate: dX = X(r_d − r_f)dt + Xσ_X dW** — the *interest differential* is the risk-neutral drift (the Garman-Kohlhagen setup). Pricing shortcut: `F(t,x) = F₀(t, x·e^{−r_f(T−t)})`, i.e. the Garman-Kohlhagen formula = BS with the foreign rate as dividend yield. Forward FX: covered interest parity falls straight out (f = x·e^{(r_d−r_f)(T−t)}).
- Setting up the three-asset (FX, domestic equity, foreign equity) model with invertible 3×3 volatility matrix for quanto products.
- **Quanto adjustment (the chapter's payoff)**: a quanto product pays foreign equity converted at a *fixed* contractual exchange rate. Two pricing shortcuts fall out of the three-asset model: (a) a foreign equity struck in foreign currency prices as plain Black-Scholes with the foreign rate r_f, then converted at today's spot X; (b) a quanto (fixed-rate) equity option prices as Black-Scholes with the domestic rate r_d **minus a quanto correction**: the effective dividend yield becomes δ + σ_S·σ_X correlation term (the covariance between stock and FX vol). Concretely the quanto drift adjustment is the stock/FX covariance σ_S σ_X ρ, subtracted from the foreign equity's risk-neutral drift. The intuition: quanto holders are implicitly short the FX exposure of the equity, and the market prices that covariance.
- **Domestic vs foreign market prices of risk (Prop 17.10)**: the foreign market price of risk vector is the domestic one minus the FX volatility: `λ_f = λ_d − σ_X`. Changing the "currency of thought" changes the market price of risk by exactly the volatility of the exchange rate — market prices of risk are numeraire-dependent objects, not universal constants.
- **Why risk neutrality is numeraire-relative**: a neat Jensen-inequality argument shows that the expected value of a foreign-currency payoff under the domestic martingale measure differs from its value under the foreign measure; "expectation" itself depends on which asset you normalize by. This is the conceptual doorway to Chapter 24's change-of-numeraire technique.

## 15. Barrier options (Ch 18)

Barrier options (knock-out/knock-in, up/down) are priced by a beautiful reduction: **the contract can be chopped into standard pieces plus a reflection at the barrier**.

- Setup: geometric Brownian motion with r̃ = r − σ²/2 (the median drift). Key objects: hitting time of the barrier level L, the density of the process *absorbed* at L, and the distribution of the running maximum/minimum M_t / m_t (Prop 18.4). For GBM, running-max densities come from reflection arguments.
- **The master formula**: a down-and-out contract with payoff Φ at T equals

  `F_LO(t,s; Φ) = F(t,s; Φ_L) − (L/s)^{2r̃/σ²} · F(t, L²/s; Φ_L)`,

  where F is the standard Black-Scholes-type price and Φ_L is the payoff function modified (reflected) at the barrier. The first term is the unrestricted claim; the second subtracts the paths that hit the barrier, priced via the *reflected* process L²/s (the mirror image of the price through the barrier, on the log scale). The factor (L/s)^{2r̃/σ²} is the correction for the fact that reflection changes the drift.
- **In-out parity**: knock-in + knock-out = vanilla (`F = F_LO + F_LI`), because every path either hits the barrier or does not. So knock-ins price as vanilla minus knock-out.
- **Linearity + chopping**: Björk shows all barrier variants (in/out × up/down × calls/puts, ladders, and finally lookbacks) are combinations of four basic contracts: the stock (ST), the binary/no-touch (Heaviside H), the call (C), and the corresponding reflected versions. Ladder options (payoff on the running max) are portfolios of knock-outs.
- **Lookback put** (Prop 18.28): pays S_T − min S (buy at the low). Its closed form prices the running-minimum distribution; lookbacks are much more expensive than vanillas because they guarantee the perfect entry.
- **Trading relevance**: the whole chapter is the theory of **hitting probabilities** — the probability that GBM touches a level before a date, and the distribution of the running extreme. This is the exact probabilistic machinery behind stop-loss and trailing-stop placement: a stop at L is a bet on the hitting probability of L, and barrier pricing quantifies that bet under the risk-neutral measure.

## 16. Stochastic optimal control: HJB and Merton (Ch 19)

The general framework: control the drift and diffusion of a state process X through a feedback control law u(t,x), maximizing expected utility `E[∫F(t,X,u)dt + Φ(X_T)]` (with consumption running along the way). The state follows `dX = μ^u dt + σ^u dW`; admissible laws are adapted feedback functions.

- **Dynamic programming / the HJB equation**: embed the problem at every starting point (t,x); define the optimal value function V(t,x). Comparing "optimal throughout" with "arbitrary control for a small h then optimal" gives, as h→0:

  `∂V/∂t + sup_u {F(t,x,u) + A^u V(t,x)} = 0`, with `V(T,x) = Φ(x)`,

  where A^u is the controlled generator. A PDE in which the control enters through a pointwise optimization at each (t,x) — a *nonlinear* PDE because the optimizer depends on V's own derivatives.
- **Necessary vs sufficient**: the HJB equation is a necessary condition *if* a smooth optimum exists; the **verification theorem** is the easy-to-prove converse: if you can *guess* a smooth H (an ansatz) and a candidate control g attaining the supremum, then H = V and g is optimal. Practice: guess a solution form inherited from the payoff structure, solve for parameters, verify. Björk is honest that most solvable problems are "rigged" to be analytically tractable.
- **The linear quadratic regulator** (engineering classic): minimize E[∫(x'Qx + u'Ru)dt + x'Hx_T] for linear dynamics. The ansatz V = x'P(t)x + q(t) reduces the HJB to a matrix **Riccati ODE** for P; the optimal control is linear in the state: `û = −R⁻¹B'P(t)x`. Linear feedback of the state variable is the *derived*, not assumed, answer.
- **Merton's consumption-investment problem**: maximize `E[∫e^{−δt}c^γ dt]` (0<γ<1, CRRA utility) over consumption c and portfolio weight w, with wealth dynamics `dX = wx(α−r)dt + (rX − c)dt + wσX dW`, and the natural constraint that the game stops when wealth hits zero (domain D = positive wealth; the stopping time τ = first hit of zero). The degenerate unconstrained problem (infinite utility from consuming without repaying) is killed by this boundary.
  - Ansatz V = e^{−δt}h(t)x^γ collapses the HJB into a **Bernoulli ODE** for h.
  - Results: **the optimal portfolio weight is constant**: `ŵ = (α−r)/(σ²(1−γ))` — Merton's fraction, proportional to the excess return and *inversely* proportional to variance and risk aversion (1−γ). **The optimal consumption is linear in wealth**: ĉ = x·h(t)^{−1/(1−γ)}.
  - The myopic, time-separable structure (with constant investment opportunities) makes the portfolio rule independent of horizon; only the consumption rule depends on t.
- **Mutual fund theorems (Merton 1971)**: with n risky assets (no risk-free one), the optimal portfolio always lies on a *one-dimensional* line in portfolio space: `ŵ(t) = g + Y(t)h` — two fixed mutual funds span every investor's optimal portfolio, regardless of risk aversion; investors differ only in the *mix* of the two funds. With a risk-free asset: fund one is entirely the risk-free asset, fund two is the tangency portfolio `Σ⁻¹(α − re)` — every investor holds some combination of cash and the *same* risky fund. This is the theoretical genealogy of two-fund separation and of index investing.
- The chapter closes by connecting control to martingale theory (ex. 19.8): the total cost-to-go process of any control law is a submartingale, and a martingale exactly when the law is optimal — optimality = martingale property, a bridge to the dual/martingale approach of later literature (Cox-Huang, Karatzas et al., Schachermayer).

## 17. Bond markets: vocabulary and arbitrage-free spine (Ch 20)

- **Zero-coupon bonds** p(t,T) pay 1 at T; the whole market is a continuum of such assets (one per maturity) — an *infinite-dimensional* market. The term structure at t is the curve T ↦ p(t,T).
- **Rate definitions** (from bond prices, all consistent): simple (LIBOR) forward/spot rates from ratios p(t,S)/p(t,T); continuously compounded rates from logs; the **instantaneous forward rate** f(t,T) = −∂log p(t,T)/∂T; the **short rate** r(t) = f(t,t). Money account `B_t = exp{∫₀ᵗ r ds}`; Lemma 20.4: p(t,T) = exp{−∫ₜᵀ f(t,s)ds} — forward rates and bond prices are interchangeable encodings of the same curve.
- **Propagation relations** (Prop 20.5, measure-free bookkeeping): bond-price dynamics dp = p·m dt + p·v dW translate into forward-rate dynamics df = α dt + σ dW with α(t,T) = v_T·v − m_T, σ = −v_T; and forward dynamics force the short rate `dr = [f_T(t,t) + α(t,t)]dt + σ(t,t)dW`. Everything is related; specifying any one object implies constraints on the others.
- **The money account is a rolling-over strategy** in just-maturing bonds (folklore made precise only with measure-valued portfolios — Björk et al. 1997).
- **Coupon bonds** = portfolios of zeros (price by summation). **Floating-rate bond**: coupons reset to spot LIBOR; a replication argument (buy T_{i−1}-bond, roll into T_i-bond) shows its value at reset date is exactly par — the elegant telescoping `p(t) = p(t,T₀)`. **Swaps**: exchange fixed for floating; swap rate R set so the contract values zero at inception: `R = [p(0,T₀) − p(0,T_N)] / [δ Σ p(0,T_i)]`.
- **Yield and duration**: yield-to-maturity = the single flat rate repricing the bond; **Macaulay duration** D = discounted-coupon-weighted average maturity, and it is *literally the bond's delta with respect to yield*: dp/dy = −D·p. Convexity = the bond's gamma. Fixed income has its own greeks, and they are the standard risk vocabulary of the rates desk.

## 18. Short-rate models and the term structure equation (Ch 21)

The classical approach: specify `dr = μ(t,r)dt + σ(t,r)dW̄` under P, treat all bonds as derivatives of r. Two structural facts dominate everything:

- **Bond prices are NOT determined by the r-dynamics alone.** The exogenous market contains only the bank account (M = 0 traded risky assets vs R = 1 noise source): arbitrage-free but wildly incomplete. The short rate is *not* the price of a traded asset (Björk spends two pages on why "the price of money" is an informal misuse of "price": a price has currency units, r has units of 1/time) — so there is no underlying to hedge with, no unique price.
- What no-arbitrage *does* give: **internal consistency**. A two-bond riskless portfolio argument (identical to Black-Scholes') shows every bond, regardless of maturity, must share the same **market price of risk**: `λ(t) = (α_T − r)/σ_T` — the term structure equation's free parameter:

  **Term structure equation**: `F_t^T + (μ − λσ)F_r^T + ½σ²F_rr^T − rF^T = 0`, F^T(T,r) = 1.

  Like Black-Scholes but with the unknown λ appearing; λ is *not* determined within the model — it encodes the market's aggregate risk aversion, exactly the incomplete-market lesson of Ch 15 specialized to fixed income.
- **Risk-neutral valuation**: p(t,T) = E^Q[exp{−∫ₜᵀ r ds}] with the Q-dynamics `dr = (μ − λσ)dt + σdW` — a Feynman-Kač representation of the bond as discounted expected payout of 1.
- Once *one* benchmark bond's price is given, λ is implicitly fixed and all other bonds follow: arbitrage prices bonds **relative to** a benchmark, never absolutely.

## 19. Martingale models for the short rate (Ch 22)

- **Martingale modeling**: since only `μ − λσ` (the Q-drift) matters, specify r's dynamics *directly under Q* and skip λ altogether. The model list: **Vasiček** (dr = (b − ar)dt + σdW, mean-reverting Gaussian), **CIR** (dr = a(b−r)dt + σ√r dW, square-root), **Dothan/Black-Derman-Toy** (lognormal r), **Ho-Lee** (dr = Θ(t)dt + σdW), **Hull-White** (extended Vasiček with time-dependent Θ(t)).
- **Inverting the yield curve**: Q-parameters cannot be estimated from P-data (the drift is measure-specific; Girsanov changes only drifts — the diffusion may be estimated, the drift may not). Instead: pick the parameters so the model's *theoretical* term structure fits today's observed bond curve. "Who chooses the martingale measure? The market."
- **Affine term structures (ATS)**: if μ and σ² are affine in r, then bond prices are exponential-affine: `p(t,T) = exp{A(t,T) − B(t,T)r}`. Proof: substituting the ansatz into the term structure equation splits it into a Riccati ODE for B and a quadrature for A. Every model in the list except Dothan/BDT has an ATS — which is exactly why those models are the tractable ones: pricing reduces to solving two ODEs per maturity.
- **The Gaussian models' trade-off**: Vasiček/Ho-Lee/Hull-White give normally distributed rates → bond prices are expectations of lognormals → easy closed forms; *but* r can go negative with positive probability (economically absurd). CIR keeps r strictly positive (it is the square of a linear diffusion) at the cost of messier Riccati algebra. Dothan (lognormal r, always positive) is computationally nasty: integrals of lognormals have no usable distribution, and E[B(t)] = ∞ — an unusable money account. Model choice = a trade-off among tractability, positivity, and fit.
- **Perfect calibration**: a finite-parameter model cannot exactly fit a continuum of observed bond prices, and mispricing the underlying while pricing its options is unacceptable. Hull-White's fix: make Θ(t) an infinite-dimensional parameter and fit the initial curve exactly (numerically invertible; the price is possible parameter instability). The alternative route to a perfect fit is HJM, next chapter.
- **Vasiček/Ho-Lee/Hull-White/CIR term structures** all derived explicitly from the A/B ODEs; bond option formulas (Jamshidian-style): `c = p(t,S)N(d₁) − Kp(t,T)N(d₂)` with vol σ_p accumulating the model's rate vol over the relevant window — Black-Scholes in the bond-price numeraire.

## 20. Forward rate models: Heath-Jarrow-Morton (Ch 23)

Short-rate models govern a whole curve with one factor, which is economically implausible and makes realistic volatility structures hard. **HJM takes the entire forward rate curve as the state variable** — an infinite-dimensional model.

- Specify `df(t,T) = α(t,T)dt + σ(t,T)dW̄` under P, with the *observed* forward curve as the initial condition — automatic perfect fit to today's curve, no inversion needed.
- **The HJM drift condition** — the central result: no arbitrage forces the forward drift to be a functional of the volatilities alone:

  `α(t,T) = σ(t,T)∫ₜᵀ σ(t,s)'ds − σ(t,T)λ(t)`; under Q directly: `α(t,T) = σ(t,T)∫ₜᵀ σ(t,s)'ds`.

  The first term is a *variance-compensation* term (cumulative covariance of the forward curve); the second is the risk premium. **The moral**: in HJM you may freely choose the volatility structure; the (Q-)drift is then determined — arbitrage constrains drifts, never volatilities. This is the term-structure restatement of "diffusion, not drift, carries pricing information."
- Worked example: constant σ reproduces the Ho-Lee model fitted to the initial curve — short-rate models are special cases of HJM.
- **The Musiela parameterization** (time-to-maturity x = T − t): the forward curve becomes a random field r(t,x) obeying `dr = (∂r/∂x + D)dt + σ₀dW` — an infinite-dimensional SDE with a deterministic transport term ∂/∂x (the curve ages) plus the HJM drift. This is the working language of modern interest-rate theory.

## 21. Change of numeraire (Ch 24)

The computational master key of the book's final part: **a martingale measure exists only relative to a numeraire; choosing a smart numeraire collapses hard pricing problems into easy ones.**

- **The problem with the standard formula**: `Π = E^Q[e^{−∫r ds}X]` needs the *joint* law of the discount factor and the payoff — a double integral. With a T-bond as numeraire: `Π(t;X) = p(t,T)·E^T[X]` — one integral, and p(t,T) is *observed on the market*, not computed. (r and X are generically dependent under Q even when independent under P, so the naive split fails; the T-forward measure makes the split legitimate.)
- **The machinery**: numeraire changes are Girsanov transformations with Radon-Nikodym derivative `L₀¹(t) = (S₀(0)/S₁(0))·(S₁(t)/S₀(t))` — the likelihood process is the *relative price of the new numeraire*. The Girsanov kernel is the **volatility difference** σ₁ − σ₀. Numeraire must be a traded asset (nonfinancial indices, futures prices, or dividend-paying assets need separate care — remark 24.2.2).
- **Exchange option (Margrabe)**: with S₁ as numeraire, the option to swap S₂ for S₁ becomes a Black-Scholes call with zero rate, unit strike, on the ratio Z₂ = S₂/S₁ with volatility ‖σ₂ − σ₁‖ (or √(σ₁²+σ₂²−2ρσ₁σ₂) with correlated drivers) — with *no assumption whatever about interest rates*. Ratio-claims price off volatility differences: a durable relative-value principle.
- **Forward measures Q^T**: with the T-bond as numeraire, the likelihood is p(t,T)/(B(t)p(0,T)); Girsanov kernel = the T-bond's volatility. Q = Q^T iff rates are deterministic. Every forward rate f(t,T) is a Q^T-martingale, and f(t,T) = E^T[r(T)|F_t] — the **expectation hypothesis is true under Q^T, not Q**: forward rates are unbiased predictors of future short rates only under the maturity-matched forward measure, a different measure for each T. (The naive "forward rate = expected future rate under the risk-neutral measure" is false — a persistent piece of market folklore the theory corrects.)
- **Geman-El Karoui-Rochet option formula**: `Π(0) = S(0)Q^S(S(T)≥K) − Kp(0,T)Q^T(S(T)≥K)` — an option price is the stock times one probability minus the strike times another probability, each under its own numeraire measure. With deterministic vol of Z = S/p(t,T), the probabilities are Gaussian and the formula closes to Black-Scholes-type expressions in *any* model with deterministic volatilities — deriving the Hull-White, Ho-Lee and general Gaussian-HJM bond-option formulas as one-line corollaries.
- **Caps as put options on bonds**: a caplet (insurance against LIBOR above R) decomposes, via 1/p algebra, into R* put options on the T_i-bond exercised at T_{i−1}; whole caps = portfolios of bond puts. Every rates-market quote has a hidden decomposition into simpler claims.

## 22. LIBOR and swap market models (Ch 25)

The institutional climax: the market prices caps and swaptions with the logically shaky Black-76 formula (rates assumed deterministic in one step, stochastic in the next). The theorist's choice: beat the market or join it. **Market models join it** — they are arbitrage-free models *built* to reproduce Black-76 exactly.

- **The LIBOR market model**: model discrete, observable forward rates L_i(t) (between resettlement dates T_{i−1}, T_i), not instantaneous fictions. Key lemma: **each LIBOR rate is a martingale under its own forward measure Q^{T_i}** (because α_iL_i = p_{i−1}/p_i − 1 is a numeraire-relative price). Assume `dL_i = L_i σ_i dW^i` — lognormal *under its own measure* — and every caplet prices as Black-76 by construction.
- **Existence (the hard part)**: specifying all L_i simultaneously under one common measure (terminal Q^N) creates coupled drifts; the Q^N-drift of L_i is the cascade `μ_i = −Σ_{k>i} [α_k L_k/(1+α_k L_k)] σ_i σ_k*` — an inductively solvable system; Novikov holds because the kernels are bounded. The Girsanov chain between adjacent forward measures is what generates the drifts.
- **Calibration**: implied Black volatilities (flat vs spot/forward vol term structure — the same distinction as yields vs forward rates) pin the σ_i; structural parametrizations (piecewise-constant, β_iγ_j factor forms) break the underdetermination; exotics then price by Monte Carlo (log-Euler scheme on ln L_i preferred for stability). Typical cap-vol curve: hump at 2-3 years, downward-sloping long end.
- **Discrete savings account**: rolling over shortest bonds generates a predictable, locally risk-free B(T_n); the corresponding measure's Radon-Nikodym derivative is p(0,T_N)B(T_N) — the LIBOR world does contain its own money-market measure.
- **Swaps and swaptions**: the par swap rate `R_n^N = (p_n − p_N)/S_n^N` with S_n^N = Σα_i p_i the **accrual factor (PV of a basis point)** — itself the value of a traded portfolio, hence a *bona fide numeraire*. Under its own measure Q_n^N, the swap rate is a martingale; model it lognormal → swaptions price as Black-76 on R_n^N. The swap market model's drift conditions under the terminal measure solve a backward linear difference equation (recursion (25.81)).
- **The incompatibility (concluding comment)**: given a swap market model, the LIBOR rates are *not* lognormal, and vice versa — the two Black formulas (caplet and swaption) cannot both be exactly consistent in one model. The market prices *as if* both were true; the theory quantifies the exact price of this market practice.

## 23. Forwards and futures (Ch 26)

- **Forward price**: the delivery price making the OTC contract worth zero at inception: `f(t;T,Y) = Π(t;Y)/p(t,T) = E^T_t[Y]` — the forward price is the forward-measure expectation of the claim. (Forward price ≠ contract value; the contract is worth zero at t, the price f is paid at T.)
- **Futures**: exchange-traded, standardized; payment stream is **marking to market** — the holder continuously receives F(t) − F(s) over each interval, and F(T;T) = Y. Formal model: a zero-price asset whose *dividend* is the futures price increment. Result: the futures price is a **Q-martingale**: `F(t;T,Y) = E^Q_t[Y]`.
- **Forward ≠ futures in general**: forward prices take the T-forward-measure expectation, futures the risk-neutral expectation — the same number **iff rates are deterministic** (Q^T = Q). With stochastic rates the difference is a covariance between the claim and the discount factor — the theoretical content of the classical futures/forward basis (one reason NQ futures and their options behave slightly differently from OTC forwards).
- Cost of carry for dividend-paying assets: forward = (S_t − PV of dividends)/p(t,T) — the anchor of futures fair-value arithmetic.

## 24. Appendices A-C: the measure-theoretic foundations

Read for completeness; they are the machinery all of the above stands on, and worth a summary in Andrea's own library since Hasbrouck's measure-theoretic asides presuppose them.

- **A: Measure and integration**. Sigma-algebras (closed under complements and countable unions) as the domain of measurability; measures as sigma-additive mass distributions. The integral built in three steps (indicators → simple functions → monotone limits of nonnegatives); integrable functions (|f| ∈ L¹); the three convergence theorems — Fatou's lemma, monotone convergence, dominated convergence — which are why limits and integrals can be swapped. Partitions as the intuitive model of sigma-algebras (a function is σ(P)-measurable iff constant on partition cells). **Borel algebra** = the sigma-algebra generated by intervals; every practically constructible function is Borel. L^p spaces, Hölder/Minkowski inequalities, completeness (Riesz-Fischer); **Hilbert space L²** with orthogonal projection theorem and **Riesz representation** (every continuous linear functional is an inner product with a fixed vector). Product measures and **Fubini's theorem** (iterated integration). **Radon-Nikodym theorem** (proved via von Neumann's Hilbert-space argument using Riesz): if ν ≪ μ then dν = f dμ with f uniquely defined μ-a.e.; equivalence ν ~ μ when both absolutely continuous. The RN theorem is the mathematical engine of every measure change in the book — Girsanov, likelihood processes, martingale measures.
- **B: Probability theory**. Probability = measure of total mass 1; random variables = measurable maps; expectation = Lebesgue integral over Ω (with the change-of-variables bridge E[g(X)] = ∫g dμ_X). Partitions/sigma-algebras as *information*; a filtration as an increasing information flow; adaptedness = "does not look into the future" — the formal home of the trading-strategy admissibility requirement. Independence via sigma-algebras. **Conditional expectation** defined abstractly: E[X|G] is the a.s. unique G-measurable Z with ∫_G Z dP = ∫_G X dP for all G ∈ G — existence via Radon-Nikodym. Then the working rules: linearity, monotonicity, taking-out-what-is-known, independence, **iterated expectations (tower property)**, conditional Jensen. The L² interpretation: E[X|G] is the orthogonal projection of X onto the subspace of G-measurable variables — conditional expectation as *best mean-square predictor* (the exact bridge to Hasbrouck's VAR/gression machinery). Equivalent measures P ~ Q share null sets; likelihood ratio L = dQ/dP with E^P[L] = 1; the RN derivative restricted to a sub-sigma-algebra is the conditional expectation of the full one; the **Abstract Bayes formula** `E^Q[X|G] = E^P[LX|G]/E^P[L|G]` — the tool used everywhere for switching conditional expectations between measures.
- **C: Martingales and stopping times**. Martingale = adapted integrable process with X_s = E[X_t|F_s]; equivalent to zero-conditional-drift increments; discrete-time check on one step suffices. Convex functions of martingales are submartingales (Jensen); the harmonic-function analogy (martingale/sub/super ↔ harmonic/subharmonic/superharmonic). Square-integrable martingales converge a.s. and in L² to X_∞ with X_t = E[X_∞|F_t]. **Discrete stochastic integrals**: predictable integrands (known one step ahead) integrated against martingales stay martingales — the discrete seed of both the Itô integral and the admissibility requirement for trading strategies. **Likelihood processes** L_t = dQ/dP|F_t are P-martingales; M is a Q-martingale iff LM is a P-martingale. **Stopping times** ({T ≤ t} ∈ F_t: at t you know whether T has happened; hitting times are stopping times, last-exit times are not); the stopped martingale is a martingale; **optional sampling**: E[X_T|F_S] = X_S for bounded stopping times S ≤ T — the theorem behind American option analysis and every "you cannot beat a fair game by choosing when to quit" argument (with its celebrated exception: unbounded stopping times on non-uniformly-integrable martingales, the mathematical fine print beneath doubling strategies).

---

## Durable lessons

1. **Prices are relative.** A derivative's price is forced by consistency with the underlying, not by forecasts. The only inputs that survive into the price are r, σ, and the payoff shape — never the expected return α.

1. **Prices are relative.** A derivative's price is forced by consistency with the underlying, not by forecasts. The only inputs that survive into the price are r, σ, and the payoff shape — never the expected return α.
2. **Objective probabilities ≠ pricing probabilities.** The market prices assets as if everyone were risk-neutral (martingale measure Q); real-world probabilities enter only through the stochastic discount factor Λ. Reading market prices as if they were P-probabilities is the classic quantification error.
3. **(dW)² = dt is the whole trick.** Itô calculus, the −σ²/2 in GBM, the BS PDE's convexity term, gamma — all one algebraic fact.
4. **Replication, not expectation, is what pins a price.** Uniqueness comes from the ability to synthesize the claim. Where synthesis is impossible (incomplete markets), prices become ranges and risk premia appear.
5. **The smile is a model falsification printed in market data.** Implied vol that varies with strike/stress state is the market telling you the lognormal model's tails are too thin.
6. **Hedging is local.** Delta hedges decay like tangents; gamma tells you how fast. Risk management is the economics of rebalancing frequency.
7. **Change of measure changes drift, never diffusion.** Girsanov is why α never enters a price and why hedging works under any equivalent measure. All pricing-relevant information lives in the volatility structure.
8. **Arbitrage and completeness are adjoint.** The martingale-measure equation σφ = r − α and the hedging equation σ*D[Z]h* = g* are dual linear-algebra conditions on the same matrix — no-arbitrage needs σ surjective, completeness needs it injective, both need it square (M = R).
9. **The market chooses the martingale measure.** In incomplete markets, theory supplies only internal consistency (a universal market price of risk shared by all derivatives); the actual level is aggregate risk aversion made visible in prices, and it must be calibrated, not derived. Every "implied" parameter is an empirical fact about preferences.
10. **Sharpe ratios are bounded by the market price of risk.** The Hansen-Jagannathan bounds cap any achievable Sharpe ratio by ‖λ‖, and λ is the SDF's volatility. Historically extreme equity Sharpe ratios are the equity premium puzzle — markets either reward risk implausibly well, or the risk model is wrong. Both readings matter to a trader.
11. **Barriers price as reflections; stops are hitting-time bets.** Barrier option pricing is the risk-neutral theory of hitting probabilities and running extremes — the same math a trader invokes (usually without knowing it) when placing a stop or trailing stop.
12. **Optimal portfolios are simple because they must be.** Merton: constant risky-asset fraction (α−r)/(σ²(1−γ)), consumption linear in wealth; two-fund separation: everyone holds cash plus the *same* tangency fund, differing only in mix. Position sizing keyed to excess return over variance times risk tolerance is not a heuristic — it is the closed-form optimum under the classical assumptions.
13. **A model of the whole curve needs the whole curve.** One-factor short-rate models are tractable but economically thin; HJM makes the forward curve itself the state, and arbitrage then *determines the drift from the volatilities* (the HJM drift condition) — the strongest form of the book's recurring theorem that volatilities carry the pricing content and drifts are where risk preference hides.
14. **Numeraire choice is a computational weapon.** Any pricing problem simplifies under the numeraire in which its key variable is a martingale: T-bond numeraire for interest-rate claims (Π = p(t,T)E^T[X], one integral instead of two), S₁-numeraire for exchange options (Black-Scholes on the ratio with vol ‖σ₂−σ₁‖, no rate assumptions at all). The likelihood process between two numeraires is just their relative price.
15. **Market models are reverse-engineered conventions.** The LIBOR/swap market models exist to make the market's Black-76 habit *logically consistent* — join the market, don't fight it — and their incompatibility (lognormal LIBOR XOR lognormal swap rates, not both) measures exactly how much of everyday rates quoting rests on mutually inconsistent approximations. The expectation hypothesis, likewise, is true only under the maturity-matched forward measure — never under Q or P.

---

## Connections to prior books

- **Elliott Wave base (Balan)**: two deep bridges. (a) *Drift vs structure*: BS shows derivative value depends on structure (volatility, payoff geometry), not on drift predictions — an Elliott wave count is likewise a claim about *structure* (where the market is in its pattern) rather than a drift forecast, and its edge, if any, lives in the same place pricing edges live: second-order information (convexity, volatility regimes) that first-order drift-thinkers ignore. (b) *Humility*: wave theory itself concedes markets exceed prediction at turns; the vol smile is the options market's own testimony that the Gaussian clock breaks exactly at those turns.
- **Hasbrouck (Empirical Microstructure)**: Björk supplies the measure theory behind Hasbrouck's efficient-price martingales. "Normalized prices are Q-martingales" is the formal statement of "the efficient price is a martingale"; the Roll/Kyle decomposition of *observed* prices into martingale + microstructure noise is the empirical mirror of Q-dynamics + trading frictions.
- **The Quants (Patterson)**: Thorp's warrant pricing predated Black-Scholes with the same replication logic (Ch 3); the post-1987 volatility smile (Ch 4) is Björk §7.7's "falsification in market data" made historical; the August 2007 quake is what happens when everyone holds the *same* synthetic portfolio built with the *same* assumptions and must unwind simultaneously (BS hedging under crowd conditions).
- **Bouchaud et al.**: GBM's lognormal tails vs Bouchaud's power-law reality is the single most important quantitative disagreement in this stack; the smile is the market-priced version of Bouchaud's empirical claim. Anything sized on Gaussian assumptions (value-at-risk, BS delta) carries a fat-tail haircut obligation.
- **Foucault-Pagano-Röell**: FPR Ch 9's funding spirals and commonality in liquidity are the market-microstructure twin of Björk's meta-theorem world: when the number of *effective* tradable assets collapses in a deleveraging, M falls below R and markets gap through the "arbitrage-free" prices the models assumed.
- **Williams/VSA**: VSA's "effort vs result" reads the smart money's *hedging* activity (absorption, tests); delta hedging explains *why* large players leave footprint: a dealer running a book of options must trade mechanically against the delta, exactly the "effort" VSA watches for.
- **Stoll & Madhavan surveys**: option dealers' delta-rebalancing demand is one of the informed/uninformed order-flow categories the microstructure surveys treat at market level; gamma-implied rebalancing flows are a known accelerant at turns (1987 portfolio insurance being the canonical case).
- **Interest-rate chapters and the futures book**: the NQ contract Andrea charts is a *futures* price, and Ch 26's futures-vs-forward wedge (a covariance term whenever rates are stochastic) plus cost-of-carry arithmetic connect the practice's instrument to its theoretical valuation; caps/floors/swaptions vocabulary (Ch 20, 24-25) is the shared language of the rates desks that set the discount curves under every equity model.

---

## Strategy / research candidates (weekly NQ practice)

1. **Trade structure, not drift**: the BS lesson that α never enters a price formalizes a discipline for the NQ weekly practice: value the *shape* of the market situation (position in wave pattern, volatility regime, distance to invalidation) rather than arguing about direction. Position sizing should key off σ and invalidation distance, which the theory says carry the real information.
2. **Implied vol as a regime gauge**: track NQ/QQQ index implied volatility (VXN or NQ options) alongside weekly wave counts. Elevated implied vol + steep skew = the market pricing fat left tails, corroborating a wave-count that calls for a third-wave decline. A smile "regime dashboard" is a concrete, low-maintenance weekly indicator.
3. **Lognormal drag check (−σ²/2)**: when translating annualized vol into a range forecast for NQ over a horizon, use the median (−σ²/2 drag), not the mean — a small but systematic bias that makes downside scenarios more likely than symmetric Gaussian intuition suggests.
4. **Fat-tail haircut on any Gaussian sizing**: any stop distance or risk-of-ruin estimate computed from normal assumptions gets a haircut (Bouchaud/Mandelbrot evidence). Practically: weekly NQ stop placements should sit *outside* the lognormal 99% envelope, or position size reduced accordingly.
5. **Gamma-thinking for position management**: treat an open weekly position as a portfolio with implicit gamma: if the count is close to invalidation (high curvature zone, ATM-analogue), the required adjustment frequency and risk rise; treat "rebalancing cost" as the model's reminder that churn kills a low-hours-per-week practice. Weekly charts are structurally a low-gamma, low-rebalancing trading style, which fits the 10-20h/week cap.
6. **Research question (for the post-reading phase)**: does the August-2007 / 1987 pattern (crowded uniform models deleveraging together) leave weekly-chart signatures on NQ — e.g. clusterings of outsized weekly ranges when index implied skew is extreme? The stack now has the theoretical vocabulary (Björk's completeness/deleveraging logic + FPR Ch 9 + Bouchaud impact laws) to formalize this test.
7. **Sharpe-ratio realism check (new, from the HJ bounds)**: any claimed edge must clear the HJ frame — a strategy's Sharpe ratio is bounded by the market price of risk it is exposed to. A weekly NQ system that implicitly promises a Sharpe far above the market's λ for equity beta risk is either harvesting a different risk premium (vol, liquidity, funding) or is overfit. Useful discipline when the strategy-building phase starts: decompose any backtested edge into "risk premium harvested" vs "risk transferred", and treat the first component as the credible one.
8. **Martingale-betting warning (new, from Ch 10 admissibility)**: averaging down / doubling on losing positions is precisely the doubling strategy the theory excludes via credit bounds. It looks riskless until the capital constraint binds. Formal backing for the hard rule: position adds against a losing weekly count never increase total exposure beyond the pre-committed risk budget.
9. **Cross-market consistency reading (from Ch 15)**: because all derivatives on the same risk share one market price of risk, relative-value checks across instruments (NQ options at different strikes/expiries, VXN vs realized, calendar spreads) are tests of *internal consistency*, not direction. Deviations that persist are either model misspecification (fat tails) or stress signals — both tradeable information on a weekly horizon.
10. **Stop placement as hitting-probability calculus (from Ch 18)**: a stop at level L is a bet on the risk-neutral hitting probability of L within the holding window; the barrier formulas give the exact shape of that probability (and how it inflates with time and vol). Concretely: place invalidation stops *outside* the "one-sigma touching" zone the formulas imply, otherwise the position dies on noise; trailing stops are running-max bets and the running-max density says their probability of survival decays geometrically as the target approaches the current price.
11. **Merton sizing as the null model (from Ch 19)**: the fraction (α−r)/(σ²(1−γ)) is the benchmark against which any discretionary sizing rule must justify itself. A weekly NQ position larger than this, given realistic α uncertainty, is a leverage statement, not an edge statement. Two-fund separation also frames the practice: the wave count times position mix is the whole portfolio, so keep the sizing leg boring and mechanical.
12. **Forward-measure discipline for rate-dependent claims (from Ch 24, 26)**: whenever translating a forward/futures-implied level into an expected spot (or vice versa) — e.g. reading the NQ term structure or a rates-driven equity narrative — remember the expectation hypothesis holds only under the maturity-matched measure, and forward ≠ futures when rates are stochastic. The wedge is a covariance, i.e. a second-order term: exactly the kind of term the practice's "structure over drift" discipline says to check before acting on a basis trade.

---

*Book complete: all 26 chapters plus Appendices A-C read and distilled. The theory stack now runs from Balan's wave structure, through the microstructure canon (Harris, Hasbrouck, BGS, Stoll, Madhavan, FPR), the behavioral/history pair (The Quants, Dark Pools), VSA, and this mathematical backbone. Next in the queue: the remaining quant books — Computational Finance (14), Javaheri volatility arbitrage (15), Applied Quantitative Finance (12), Analysis of Financial Time Series (10), Mathematics of Financial Markets (16), then the algorithmic/HFT books (4, 5).*
