# Market Microstructure: A Survey of Microfoundations, Empirical Results, and Policy Implications (Biais, Glosten & Spatt, Journal of Financial Markets ~2005)

Study notes, Book 4 in the series. Saved November 2026. Written teaching-style so Andrea can follow the argument, not just the conclusions. This survey is the "grand unification" paper of the microstructure literature up to the mid-2000s: it builds one synthetic model and shows how each classic paper (Roll, Kyle, Glosten-Milgrom, Glosten 1994, Kyle 1989...) is a special case of it.

## Why this paper matters in our sequence

- Harris gave us the institutional taxonomy (who trades, why, and at what cost).
- Hasbrouck gave us the econometric tools to measure trade impact.
- Biais-Glosten-Spatt gives us the *unified theory* underneath both: one model where spreads, price impact, inventory, adverse selection, and market power all appear as different parameter settings. When we later build a strategy, this is the paper that tells us which "regime" the market is in and what that implies about costs and impact.

## Part 1: Competitive market makers and the cost of trades

### 1.1 The synthetic model (the heart of the paper)

Setup: N competing liquidity suppliers face one market order of size Q. Two pricing conventions:

- **Uniform pricing (call auction / Kyle world):** every share in a batch trades at the single clearing price. What actually happens in opening call auctions (Euronext, Xetra).
- **Discriminatory pricing (limit order book):** each limit order executes at its own posted price; a market order "walks up the book." Example: buying 2 shares fills 1 at $15 and 1 at $15.50. This is continuous trading everywhere today.

Key equilibrium objects (uniform-price case):

- Price: P = m + λQ, where m is the midquote and λ the price-impact slope (market depth is 1/λ).
- Midquote: m = π − (κ V(ε|θ)/(1−δ)) I. Inventory I tilts the midpoint away from the fundamental π: a loaded dealer quotes lower to attract buyers. κ is dealer risk aversion.
- λ = (c/N + κV(ε|θ)/N + γσ²δ)/(1−2δ), where c = order-handling cost, γ = *trader's* risk aversion, δ = adverse-selection severity (defined below).

### 1.1.3 Adverse selection: the δ parameter

The informed trader has signal s and hedging need L; her summary statistic is θ = s − γσ²L (signal minus inventory pressure). Then:

δ = V(s) / [V(s) + (γσ²)² V(L)]

- δ = 0: no private information → the model collapses to Roll/Ho-Stoll (pure inventory + order costs).
- κ = 0, c = 0: risk-neutral, costless dealers → collapses to Kyle (1985): prices are just updated expectations given order flow.
- General δ > 0: informational spread component adds on top of risk-premium and cost components. Bigger orders move prices more; the insider knows this and *limits her own size* to hide (the origin of "trade slowly to minimize footprint").

Two crucial comparative statics:

1. **Liquidity as an equilibrium object:** severe information asymmetry (high δ), weak dealer risk-bearing (high κ), or large handling costs (high c) all raise λ = illiquidity.
2. **Welfare:** with adverse selection, equilibrium trade responds to endowment shocks at rate |∂Q/∂L| = 1 − 2δ < 1 (first-best is 1). As δ → ½, trade → 0: **market breakdown**, a lemons problem (Akerlof) applied to the stock market. In batch models with exogenous noise traders the market never fully breaks down, because noise traders can always be taxed; in single-arrival models it can.

### 1.1.4 Orders that hit standing quotes: the upper-tail expectation

In the limit order book (discriminatory pricing), a quote at price p executes only when total trade size Q exceeds the cumulative depth up to p. So the competitive ask must equal the **upper-tail expectation** E[v | Q > q(p)] (Rock 1990, Glosten 1994).

Consequence: the **small-trade spread**. Even an infinitesimal buy impounds the possibility that Q is huge, so as ζ→0 the half-spread → ½[E(v|Q=2) − E(v)], bounded away from zero. In a uniform-price auction, by contrast, price impact is proportional to size and small trades face ~no spread. This single result explains why limit order books have a discrete spread even for tiny trades.

### 1.2 The empirical counterpart (this is Hasbrouck's system, derived from theory)

- Price equation: P_t = m_t + λ_t Q_t (effective half-spread at trade time).
- Midquote: m_t = π_t − b I_t.
- Inventory dynamics: I_{t+1} = a I_t − Q_t + u_{t+1}, with a < 1 for mean reversion (dealers exit, hedge elsewhere, or attract offsetting flow).
- Trade equation: Q_t = A_t + b d I_t + η_t: loaded dealers sell, so the flow buying from them is predictable.
- Permanent value update: π_{t+1} = π_t + z₀ η₀_t + z₁ η_t + z₂ η_t² + v_{t+1}, where η₀_t is the trade *direction* indicator (+1 buy, −1 sell). Jones-Kaul-Lipson: the *occurrence* of a trade, not its size, carries much of the information, hence the direction term (Glosten-Harris 1988).

Mapping the classics into the system (memorize this table):

| Model | Parameter restrictions | Spread source |
|---|---|---|
| Roll (1984) | constant λ, b = 0, η = ±1 i.i.d., z's = 0 | order-handling cost c |
| Ho-Stoll / inventory | z's = 0 | inventory risk premium |
| Glosten-Harris (1988) | b = d = A = 0, z's free | adverse selection |
| Hasbrouck (1991) VAR | the whole system with a < 1 | joint inventory + information |

Empirical verdicts:

- **Inventory control is real but slow:** specialist inventories mean-revert over a day to a month (Hasbavan-Sofianos 1993, Madhavan-Smidt 1993). CME floor traders sell from long positions (Manaster-Mann 1996). But the *price* effect of inventory is ambiguous: NYSE specialists adjust quotes with inventory (Madhavan-Smidt 1993), yet CME makers sell at high prices from long positions, opposite the pure theory. So pricing reflects more than inventory control.
- **Adverse selection is real and permanent:** trades have a *permanent* price impact (Hasbrouck 1991); block trades predict subsequent earnings innovations (Seppi 1992); spreads widen and depth falls before earnings announcements (Lee-Mucklow-Ready 1993); specialists lose money on positioning, recouping it on the spread (Sofianos 1995).
- The permanent/transient distinction is the identification key: inventory effects are transient, information effects permanent. (Exactly the decomposition Hasbrouck's notes built with the VAR.)
- Caveat from Neal-Wheatley (1998): closed-end funds, where asymmetric information is minimal, still show large "adverse-selection" estimates → the empirical models may be misspecified, or adverse selection operates on something other than liquidation value.
- Macro link: signed order flow explains exchange-rate movements that macro variables cannot (Evans-Lyons 2002); order imbalance moves stock returns (Chordia-Roll-Subrahmanyam 2002).
- Time between trades is autocorrelated and shortens after large trades/large spreads (Biais-Hillion-Spatt 1995); Engle-Russell built the ACD model for exactly this; volatile times bring more frequent trades AND larger impact (Dufour-Engle 2000).

## Part 2: Strategic liquidity suppliers (second-generation models)

Drop perfect competition. With N finite, liquidity suppliers play an auction game: inventory models parallel *private-value* auctions; adverse-selection models parallel *common-value* auctions.

### 2.1 Without adverse selection: market power

- Uniform-price mechanisms (call auctions): Klemperer-Meyer supply-function equilibria, multiple equilibria, rents possible even with risk-neutral dealers → Cournot-like behavior.
- Discriminatory-price mechanisms (limit order books): rents only if dealers are risk averse, because that is what makes marginal cost of supplying shares increasing (π − κσ²I + κσ²q/N) → Bertrand-like competition.
- **Empirical bombshell:** Christie-Schultz (1994) documented NASDAQ dealers avoiding odd eighths to sustain wide spreads (implicit collusion). After the scandal and the 1997 reform letting the public place limit orders, spreads dropped sharply (Barclay et al. 1999); same in London (Naik-Yadav 1999). Anonymity in ECNs makes collusion harder (Sims/Simaan-Weaver-Whitcomb 2003).
- Entry doesn't fix everything: entering market makers rarely capture share; one dealer dominates each stock (Ellis-Michaely-O'Hara 2002).

### Dynamic order placement (directly actionable material)

- **Foucault (1999):** choose limit order when the spread is wide, market order when it's tight. Confirmed empirically: on a ¼ spread, limit orders inside the quotes outperform market orders (Harris-Hasbrouck 1996); Paris Bourse order flow shows exactly this pattern (BHS 1995). Consequence: **mean reversion in the spread** and negative autocorrelation of quote changes.
- **Parlour (1998):** trade-off between price improvement and time priority (queue position); investors step inside the quotes more when depth at the quotes is large.
- **Order flow is persistent:** a limit order tends to follow a limit order (BHS 1995, Griffiths et al. 2000). Goettler-Parlour-Rajan (2003) show this arises from persistence in the *state of the book*.

### 2.2 With adverse selection: screening instead of signalling

Flip the Kyle sequence: dealers post schedules *first*, informed trader hits them after. This is a **screening game**:

- Monopolist dealer (Glosten 1989): A(q) = E(v|Q>q) + m₁(q), a classic monopoly markup on top of adverse-selection cost.
- Oligopoly (Bernhardt-Hughson 1997, Biais-Martimort-Rochet 2000): A(q) = E(v|Q>q) + mᴺ(q), markup shrinks in N and vanishes as N→∞, converging to Glosten's (1994) competitive book. With finite N there is *no* zero-profit equilibrium (unlike Kyle's signalling game). Increasing marginal cost comes from adverse selection itself in the common-value case.
- **Empirics:** Sandas (2001) structurally tests Glosten (1994): rejected for about half the stocks because the real book is *steeper* than theory predicts, i.e., possible market power. Biais-Bisière-Spatt (2002): Island limit order traders earned oligopolistic rents by undercutting the NASDAQ spread by one tick, pre-decimalization; decimalization annihilated those rents. Tick size interacts with competition *across* markets.

### Informed liquidity suppliers

- Kyle (1989): uniform-price competition among strategic informed quoters; Cournot flavor; informed quoters' profits do not necessarily vanish as N grows.
- Calcagno-Lovo (1998): an *informed market maker* randomizes his quotes to avoid full revelation, preserving his edge until the last round.
- Empirical echo: CME market makers systematically sell high/buy low (Manaster-Mann 1996), consistent either with private information from watching the floor or with pure market power.
- Vayanos (1999): agents with private endowments trade slowly to hide their price impact, which reduces risk sharing; a *call auction helps because it forces commitment to a single trade*. Trades move prices even when the dividend news is public, because trades reveal endowments, and endowments affect the price.

## Part 3: Market design

### 3.1 Call vs continuous

- Reasons call auctions help: (1) coordinate liquidity (multiple equilibria problem solved by mandate), (2) restore commitment against order splitting (Vayanos), (3) level the timing asymmetry behind adverse selection (Copeland-Galai: quoters commit before information arrives; in a call, quotes arrive *just before* the call, minimizing staleness). Best for thinly traded stocks where information arrival is fast relative to order arrival.
- Trading halts are an institutional cousin: freeze the market so quote refreshers can catch up (Corwin-Lipson 2000: cancellations and new orders flood in during halts).
- NYSE opening prices are noisier than closes (Amihud-Mendelson 1987), but that is the *overnight uncertainty*, not the call mechanism (Amihud-Mendelson 1991, Tokyo midday call shows no such noise).
- Uniform vs discriminatory pricing costs: small-trade spread exists only in the discriminatory book; large trades are *cheaper* in the discriminatory book. Empirically confirmed on Frankfurt: small trades cheaper in the call, large trades cheaper in continuous (Kehr-Krahnen-Theissen 2002).
- Rustichini-Satterthwaite-Williams (1994)/Satterthwaite-Williams (2002): the double-auction call market converges to efficiency at rate O(1/N²) and *no other mechanism converges faster*. The call auction is in that sense the optimal market structure.

### 3.2 Who should supply liquidity? (the specialist's adverse selection)

- Rock (1990): a specialist who can see the book and stop/undercut incoming orders creates adverse selection *for the limit orders*; in the risk-neutral limit, the book dries up entirely. Ready (1999): stopped orders were indeed more profitable for the specialist than book-executed ones.
- At the opening the specialist sees everyone's orders first (Stoll-Whaley 1990: open-to-open volatility > close-to-close, overnight return partially reverses during the day). Madhavan-Panchapagesan (2000): the specialist's opening trade moves price toward fundamental value; the authors read this as price discovery, BGS read it as opportunistic buying of underpriced books.
- Counterpoint (Benveniste-Marcus-Wilhelm 1992): repeated non-anonymous interaction with brokers creates reputational discipline that mitigates adverse selection; consistent with NYSE being more liquid than Paris Bourse for comparable stocks (Venkataram 2001).
- Policy upshot now implemented everywhere: level playing field, all investors can place limit orders.

### 3.3 Transparency

- Ex-ante (quote/book visibility): reduces adverse selection, narrows spreads (Pagano-Roëll 1996; experimental evidence Flood et al. 1999; NYSE OpenBook rollout attracted more displayed liquidity and cut specialist participation, Boehmer-Saar-Yu 2005). But Toronto's increased transparency raised spreads (Madhavan-Porter-Weaver 2000): not unanimous.
- Ex-post (trade disclosure): helps the block dealer unwind without extra impact because the information is already impounded (Naik-Neuberger-Viswanathan 1999); London evidence shows no liquidity gain from *delaying* disclosure (Gemmill 1996).
- Dynamic twist (Bloomfield-O'Hara 1999/2000): in *opaque* markets, spreads start tight (dealers compete to attract flow and learn from it) then stay wide (double winner's curse: vs the informed trader AND the informed dealer); in transparent markets spreads start wide then shrink. Testable via intraday spread U-shapes across markets.

### 3.4 Tick size

- Coarse grids mechanically force wide spreads (Harris 1994). Decimalization cut spreads without hurting execution quality (Bessembinder 2003, Bacidore et al. 2001).
- But finer ticks: (1) make undercutting cheap, worsening Rock's adverse selection (Seppi 1997), (2) devalue time priority, discouraging limit orders, (3) reduce displayed depth (Goldstein-Kavajecz 2000, Jones-Lipson 2001), (4) increase hidden orders (Harris 1998).
- With coarse ticks, dealers race to quote the one-tick spread and enjoy time priority (Cordella-Foucault 1999). Tick size is a magnifying glass on every other imperfection.

### 3.5 Fragmentation and intermarket competition

- Liquidity externalities pull everything to one venue (Pagano 1989, Chowdhry-Nanda 1991), yet strategic suppliers fragment: **quote matching** (promise execution at the primary market's quote without honoring cross-market time priority) skims uninformed small flow, worsening adverse selection in the primary market, widening spreads there; profitable at any tick size.
- But when the primary market has market power, a competitor *disciplines* it: NYSE spreads fell when regional exchanges and Madoff's order-payment flow diverted business (Battalio 1997, Battalio-Greene-Jennings 1997).
- Glosten (1998): two books with cross-market order routing can have *more aggregate depth* than one, because competition forces quoters to compete on the average share, not the marginal share.
- The problems disappear if (i) price-time priority is enforced across markets and (ii) nobody has privileged status, i.e., competition between open electronic limit order books is benign.

## Part 4: Conclusion

- Microstructure matters: trades move prices; efficiency is not automatic.
- Design principles that follow from the whole survey: free entry into liquidity supply, not-too-coarse price grids, transparency, level information and priority across liquidity suppliers.
- Glosten's "inevitable electronic open limit order book" is the predicted endpoint; several books coexisting, competing, is fine and even desirable.
- Next challenge (their words): translate the analysis into *applicable methods for order placement* and market design tools. This is precisely the strategy-building phase Andrea and I have scheduled after the reading program.

## Connections to earlier books

- **To Balan (Elliott Wave base):** the survey formalizes the friction layer underneath wave counting. Three specific bridges: (1) δ, the adverse-selection severity, is a natural candidate for what makes Wave 3 (the slope phase, everyone informed, trend obvious) dynamically different from Wave 1 (recognition phase, disagreement) and Wave B (trap phase, false information). (2) The equilibrium-multiplicity/endogenous-liquidity result (Dow 2005: high-liquidity and low-liquidity equilibria can both persist) rhymes with Balan's alternation and the observation that the "same" pattern resolves differently depending on crowd positioning. (3) Market breakdown as δ → ½ is the microstructure picture of a Wave 4 or B correction: participation collapses because the marginal trader fears counterparties with better information.
- **To Harris:** this survey is the theory of Harris's taxonomy. Harris's five pillars map onto the cost components of λ: order-handling (c), inventory/risk (κσ² terms), adverse selection (δ terms), plus the market-power rents Harris only described institutionally (Part 2 formalizes them). Harris's "winner's curse" chapter is Rock (1990)/Bloomfield-O'Hara here with the full math.
- **To Hasbrouck:** equations (13)-(18) of this survey ARE Hasbrouck's VAR system written down as structural equations. Hasbrouck's notes treated them as measurement tools; this paper shows they are equilibrium objects whose coefficients are pinned by c, κ, γ, δ, N. The Roll estimator's positive-autocovariance failure and Hasbrouck's Gibbs-sampler fix appear here (p. 13) as the same story. Hasbrouck's PIN, information shares, and limit order book models (Sandas, Foucault, Parlour, Seppi) all reappear with their theoretical grounding.

## Strategy and research candidates (compounding toward the final build)

1. **Spread-regime order placement rule (Foucault/Harris-Hasbrouck):** if we ever route orders, the base rule is limit orders when the spread is wide relative to its recent distribution, market orders when tight; expected spread mean reversion gives the limit order extra edge. For weekly-chart trading this mostly matters for execution quality, not signal.
2. **λ (Kyle slope) as a regime filter:** estimate price impact (even crudely, via Amihud-style |return|/volume on daily data) as a proxy for δ and liquidity conditions. Hypothesis for the strategy build: Elliott wave phases should line up with shifts in estimated λ; Wave 3 should show high but *stable* impact, Wave 1/B erratic impact. Testable on NQ daily/weekly data.
3. **Permanent vs transient impact decomposition** (Hasbrouck VAR) as a confirmation filter: only trust a breakout on the weekly chart if the *permanent* component of the preceding order flow points the same way. This is the empirical, objective version of "volume confirms the wave."
4. **Clustering/timing effects:** U-shaped intraday volume and the order-flow persistence results suggest avoiding the open when executing (noisy prices, overnight uncertainty reversal per Stoll-Whaley) and give micro-timing rules for entries (e.g., working orders mid-session when spreads have reverted to normal).
5. **Transparency and news:** the earnings-announcement result (spreads widen, depth collapses ahead of announcements, Lee-Mucklow-Ready) argues for a standing rule: never initiate new weekly positions in the immediate run-up to scheduled macro events (FOMC, CPI, NFP); the adverse-selection cost of being wrong is asymmetric.

## Reading-completeness note

Read in full, pages 1-45 (all four sections plus the three summary tables), plus the reference list (pages 46-59) scanned for source mapping. No skipped sections.

# CMT Level III: The Integration of Technical Analysis (CMT Association / Wiley, 2020)

Source identity: "CMT LEVEL III — The Integration of Technical Analysis", CMT Association, 2nd edition, Wiley 2020. 942 pages, 33 chapters, 6 sections. Not an original monograph: every chapter is an excerpt from a published Wiley title (Kirkpatrick & Dahlquist; Kaufman, Trading Systems and Methods 5th ed.; Aronson, Evidence-Based Technical Analysis 2006, intro + ch 4-5; Burton & Shah; Asness, Frazzini, Israel & Moskowitz, "Fact, Fiction, and Momentum Investing", JPM Fall 2014; Montier; de Kempenaer & Verdouw RRG; Lim; Katsanos; Pompian; Ciana; Rhoads; Nison; Bulkowski; Bauer & Dahlquist, Technical Market Indicators 1999 ch 10; Weigand). Upload: pdfcoffee.com_cmt-level-3-pdf-free.pdf, OCR clean (two defects flagged below).

DISPOSITION HONESTY: FULLY READ = ch 6 (Statistical Analysis, pp 250-294), ch 7 (Hypothesis Tests and Confidence Intervals, pp 295-326), ch 16 (Fact, Fiction, and Momentum Investing, pp 471-495), ch 33 (Conclusions, pp 917-923), plus the opening of ch 3 (Kaufman system evaluation, pp 65-67). STRUCTURED SKIM = all remaining 28 chapters via TOC, chapter learning objectives and full section-header maps. This is a curriculum compilation, not a research monograph; the excerpt structure means the two chapters that matter to this programme (Aronson's statistics pair) were given the full read and the rest honestly skimmed. Deeper chapter-level study can be queued if a specific chapter ever becomes relevant; no claim below rests on skimmed material without saying so.

OCR DEFECTS: (a) PDF page 328 is corrupted garbage (repeated "m = 3/11" fragments; a throwaway transition page, no content lost). (b) Table 16.4's OCR body wrongly duplicates Table 16.2's percentages; the true long/short decomposition values come from the surrounding text (short 5.1%, long 5.5%, combined 10.6%).

## Section I: Risk management and system testing (ch 1-7)

Ch 1-2 (Kirkpatrick & Dahlquist): system design and money management, position sizing. Skim: standard curriculum treatment, no new claims for this programme.

Ch 3-5 (Kaufman, TSM 5th ed. ch 21-23): system evaluation and testing. Opening fully read: "testing should be the process of validating your ideas, not discovery"; expectations must be defined before seeing results, otherwise no validation is possible and everything is overfitting; optimization ("torture the data until it confesses") described as the industry's core self-deception; the best backtested parameters "never live up to expectations and are often a financial tragedy when traded"; computers compute, they do not think. Rest of the chapters skimmed via headers: in-sample vs out-of-sample, two-parameter surface visualization, step-forward testing, robustness, chi-square significance of trade counts, Sharpe/information ratio, stops, sizing. Connection: this is the practitioner-language restatement of what the programme's kill lines and pre-registration already enforce, but with none of the multiple-testing machinery (Kaufman's answer is visual parameter continuity; the programme's answer is White RC/SPA plus the frozen kill-line design, which is stricter).

Ch 6 (Aronson EBTA ch 4) FULLY READ. Keyword bank:

    statistical analysis: null hypothesis default; sampling distribution; population vs sample;
    LLN; CLT; standard error; data mining bias; best-of-many-rules significance inflation;
    nonstationarity; serial correlation breaking sqrt(n); "immediate practical future" as population;
    TA profits possibly = risk premia (liquidity provision, price discovery, risk transfer), not alpha

Core content: statistics is the science of drawing valid inferences from limited data; the immediate practical future of the markets is the population a rule is tested against; a rule's backtested performance is a sample statistic, not a truth. Data mining raises the significance bar mechanically: the best of 1000 tested rules carries a much fatter right tail under the null, so the same t-stat means less. Footnote 33: serial correlation slows the sqrt(n) error shrinkage (the heteroskedasticity/autocorrelation problem; matches the programme's HAC/newey-west handling). Footnote 35: nonstationarity means larger samples are not automatically better; old regimes can dilute rather than inform. Footnote 13: even genuine TA profits may be compensation for providing liquidity and transferring risk, i.e. risk premia rather than market-beating information, which is the same distinction the Asness chapter hammers for factor investing. Connection: footnote 13 is the trading-system version of the factor risk-premium argument; footnote 33 matches the weekly-n~300 autocorrelation discipline already in the programme.

Ch 7 (Aronson EBTA ch 5) FULLY READ. Keyword bank:

    hypothesis testing vs estimation; confirming-the-consequent fallacy; H0/Ha exhaustive+exclusive;
    why the null gets the benefit (skepticism, parsimony, single claim); p-value definition;
    type I error worse than type II (lost capital vs lost opportunity); bootstrap (Efron 1979;
    zero-centering; resample N with replacement); White's Reality Check (White 2000, best-of-many-rules
    null, Quantmetrics patent); Monte Carlo permutation (Masters; scramble detrended returns,
    pair with rule +/-1 outputs without replacement); CI vs test conflict under skewness

Core content: the confirming-the-consequent fallacy is the practitioner version of the programme's "a good backtest never proves the mechanism" rule. Type I worse than type II is the explicit philosophical endorsement of the kill-line design: false positives cost capital, false negatives cost only opportunity. The bootstrap section is the direct lineage of the programme's validation spine: zero-centering the rule's own returns is the detrended-data practice; White's Reality Check (2000) is literally the same White 2000 RC the programme already uses for best-of-many-rules correction, here encountered first as a commercial product; Masters' Monte Carlo permutation method is a cousin of the programme's circular-shift/persistence-preserving surrogate family, with one design difference recorded: MCP pairs the rule's +/-1 signal with scrambled detrended returns without replacement, the surrogate family instead scrambles the data and keeps the signal fixed; both destroy signal-return alignment while preserving marginals, so they are alternative implementations of the same null. Worked example (rule TT-4-91, 91-day channel breakout on DJ Transports, traded S&P Nov 1980-Jun 2005): +4.84% annualized on detrended data, bootstrap p = 0.0692 (Aronson's verdict: "possibly significant", honest refusal to round it to a discovery), MCP nearly identical; 80% CI +0.62% to +9.06%; the CI contains economically trivial values, so test-significance and economic-significance can disagree, especially under skew. Connection: this chapter is the CMT curriculum itself teaching the programme's entire validation philosophy; the closest single crossover of any book in the collection.

## Section II: Intermarket analysis (ch 8-15)

Ch 8-14 (Katsanos): intermarket relationships via regression with tolerance, market surveys (S&P vs European indices, gold, dollar index, VIX, XAU, CRB), intermarket divergence indicators, Bollinger band divergence, congestion index. Skim: a practitioner's toolkit for pair/cross-market studies, pre-registered indicators with explicit tolerance windows; useful as a template for cross-market descriptive features (relative strength, divergence counts). Connection: the cross-market SMH Amendment 9 (Gold/Nikkei/DAX) is the programme's own intermarket exercise; Katsanos's tolerance-window regression is a candidate descriptive layer for the cross-market booking, nothing more (any use needs a new amendment).

Ch 15 (de Kempenaer & Verdouw): Relative Rotation Graphs, JdK RS-Ratio, RS-Momentum, heading/velocity/angles. Skim: a visualization of relative-strength ranking and its second derivative. Connection: conceptually a cross-sectional momentum display (ranks relative to peers), linking to the Asness distinction below and to the programme's momentum12 (which is absolute/time-series, not cross-sectional).

## Section III: Portfolio management (ch 16-18)

Ch 16 (Asness, Frazzini, Israel & Moskowitz, JPM Fall 2014) FULLY READ. Keyword bank:

    cross-sectional momentum UMD 1927-2013: 8.3% ann, Sharpe 0.50 (vs HML 4.7%/0.39, SMB 2.9%/0.26);
    long side = short side (5.5% vs 5.1% contributions); large-cap momentum robust (UMD Big 6.8%);
    value nearly dead in large caps (HML Big 1991-2013 0.7%, Sharpe 0.06); FIM study: >$1T live AQR
    trades 1998-2013, 19 markets, real costs ~10x lower than naive daily-data estimates; tax myth
    (momentum ~5-6x value turnover but similar tax burden: short-term losses harvested, long-term
    gains deferred; value's dividend income tax-inefficient); value-momentum correlation -0.4
    (French HML; -0.7 vs "pure" AF value); 60/40 HML/UMD Sharpe 0.80; momentum crashes
    (Daniel-Moskowitz: long bear + abrupt upswing, conditional beta, crashes short-side driven,
    1932/2009, hedgeable); worst drawdowns: value -43%, momentum -77%, 60/40 -30%; standard
    definition = 12-month return skipping most recent month (microstructure bias); Novy-Marx 7-12m
    variant fails replication in 35/36 countries (Goyal-Wahal); behavioral vs risk-based theories;
    212 years of evidence (Geczy-Samonov 1801-2012); cross-sectional momentum != time-series trend

Core content: the single best-evidenced chapter in the book. Momentum (rank stocks/bonds/indices against their peers over the past 12 months, skip the last month, buy winners, sell losers) earned 8.3% annualized 1927-2013 with Sharpe 0.50, better documented than value or size. The long and short sides contribute roughly equally, contradicting the common claim that momentum is a short-side artifact. It survives real trading costs (measured on a trillion dollars of live AQR trades, not daily-data fiction) and the tax argument against it is weaker than folklore claims. It crashes hard and fast in rebounding bear markets, driven by the short side, and those crashes are partially hedgeable. Behavioral explanations (underreaction, delayed overreaction, disposition effect) and risk-based explanations both predict persistence. Key definitional distinction for this programme: cross-sectional momentum ranks each asset against peers; time-series momentum/trend following is the absolute version. The programme's momentum12 is a time-series trend measure, so the Asness evidence is adjacent, supporting the general phenomenon, but does not directly certify the programme's implementation; a cross-sectional (vs peers, e.g. NQ vs its own cross-sectional rank or Gold vs Nikkei vs DAX ranks) version is a different hypothesis that would need its own amendment. Connection: also the standing rebuttal to "momentum is dead" folklore, and the risk-premium framing (momentum may be a premium, not free alpha) matches Aronson's footnote 13.

Ch 17-18 (Weigand): macro indicators, performance attribution, beta. Skim: standard macro-factor attribution treatment.

## Section IV: Behavioral finance (ch 19-24)

Ch 19-21 (Pompian; Lim; Montier): behavioral bias taxonomy, investor psychology, two-heads model, bubble anatomy, de-bubbing alpha. Ch 22-24 (Kaufman): behavioral techniques, event studies, news trading (Raschke). Skim level. Connection: bubble anatomy and climax/de-bubbling chapters link to the Wyckoff BC/UTAD and VSA climax layers already studied; mechanism stories for why climaxes exist, no statistics, no standalone claims adopted.

## Section V: Volatility (ch 25-26)

Ch 25-26 (Rhoads): VIX as an indicator, VIX derivatives for hedging. Skim. Connection: volatility context only; the programme's sigma_w already handles volatility as data, not signal.

## Section VI: Classical methods and candlesticks (ch 27-33)

Ch 27 (Kaufman "Advanced Techniques"): neural networks and genetic algorithms for trading (training, mutation, propagation, input selection, simulated vs real performance, fractal efficiency). Skim. Connection: reinforces the programme's standing skepticism; Kaufman's own text warns simulated NN performance diverges from live performance; nothing here changes the no-ML-without-amendment stance.

Ch 28-29 (Kaufman): classical chart patterns updated (intraday time patterns 2000-2011, Tubbs/Merrill patterns, gap trading, DeMark projected ranges, Elder triple-screen, Pring KST). Skim: pattern restatement, no new evidence layer.

Ch 30-32 (Lim, Nison): candlestick patterns. Skim: descriptive candle anatomy, no statistics. Filler relative to this programme's needs.

Ch 33 (Bauer & Dahlquist, TMI 1999 ch 10, "Conclusions") FULLY READ. Twelve conclusions from testing 60+ indicators on a broad stock sample:

    1. strictly following indicator signals underperforms buy-and-hold on average;
    2. indicators are conservative: they signal cash a large fraction of the time;
    3. indicators beat buy-and-hold on DECLINING stocks, lose on RISING stocks;
    4. BUT indicator signals DO on average contain information: 50 of 60 indicators' long-signal
       days beat the 0.055%/day buy-and-hold average; 12 beat 0.070%;
    5. short signals weaker: average short-signal day return is still positive, just below average;
       short signals are yellow flags, not red;
    6. portfolio management (signal frequency/distribution across names) matters as much as signals;
    7. results vary widely stock to stock;
    8. results vary period to period for the same stock;
    9. costs/slippage ignored = best case; signals come in streaks so costs are not the worst case;
    10. combining indicators improves reliability at the price of frequency (more cash drag);
    11. a consistently wrong indicator is valuable reversed; interpretation of signals is a variable;
    12. leverage (margin/options) may be needed to exploit infrequent good signals; Russian-roulette caveat

This is the honest empirical layer of the whole CMT edifice, from the CMT's own curriculum: raw indicator-following loses to buy-and-hold, the information content is real but modest and mostly on the long side, and everything decays into costs and cash drag. Conclusions 1-3 and 9 match the programme's default stance (no edge until proven); conclusion 4 is the honest counterweight (signal information content is measurable and was measurable in 1999). Connection: conclusion 3 (indicators help in declines, hurt in rises) is a conditional-performance statement of the same shape as the programme's conditional claims (primed states, compression-cross); conclusion 11 (reverse a consistently wrong signal) is a pre-registration trap: deciding to reverse after seeing the results is data mining, only a pre-registered reversal rule is legitimate.

## Connections to the programme (summary)

1. Aronson ch 6-7 are the CMT curriculum's own endorsement of the programme's validation spine: null-default, type-I-worse-than-type-II, White's Reality Check, detrended/zero-centered resampling, data-mining-adjusted significance, economic vs statistical significance. Nothing new to adopt; confirmation that the approach is the professional standard, from the source one would least expect to hold that line.
2. Asness ch 16 supplies the strongest real-evidence numbers in the book (momentum 8.3%/0.50 over 86 years, cost-survival on live trades, crash anatomy) and the cross-sectional vs time-series distinction that separates it from the programme's momentum12; a cross-sectional variant is a distinct future hypothesis, amendment required, nothing registered here.
3. Bauer-Dahlquist ch 33 supplies the honest negative-and-positive empirical baseline for indicator trading as a whole; its conditional structure (works in declines) rhymes with the programme's conditional claim design.
4. Katsanos ch 8-14 is a candidate descriptive-feature template for cross-market work (Amendment 9 context), bookkeeping only.
5. Behavioral and VIX sections are mechanism-story and context layers only; candlestick/classical chapters are filler for this programme's purposes.

Nothing in this book changes any standing verdict, kill line, or amendment. Nothing was tested against market data. The programme's standards are at or above the book's own recommended standards throughout; the two places the book is weaker than the programme (Kaufman's visual-robustness answer to data mining; the behavioral chapters' evidence-free mechanism stories) are recorded above.

## PER-CHAPTER KEYWORD BANKS (all 33 chapters, per Andrea's directive)

Read-depth flags, honestly labeled: FULL READ = complete text read this programme (ch 1, 2, 8 now; ch 6, 7, 16, 33 in the first pass). DENSE READ = opening + all section headers + the chapter's core sections read in full (ch 3, 4, 5, 9, 12, 13, 14, 15, 25, 27, 30). HEADERS+OPENING = complete section map + learning objectives + opening text; no claim below goes beyond that (ch 10, 11, 17-24, 26, 28, 29, 31, 32).

CH 1. System Design and Testing (Kirkpatrick/Dahlquist, FULL READ)
    system defined (rules+variables+parameters); discretionary vs nondiscretionary vs partial;
    buy-and-hold = survivorship bias of US/UK only (Burnham); rules vs variables vs parameters;
    Turtle decision list (markets, size, entries, stops, exits, tactics); mind-set requirements;
    four system types: trend-following, pattern recognition, range trading, exogenous signals;
    moving average crossovers (Donchian 1940s; academics unprofitable but no risk control used);
    breakout systems (Donchian channel, Bollinger volatility breakout); whipsaws <50% wins;
    countertrend systems to damp trend-system losing streaks; Futures Truth: best systems have
    4 or fewer variables, breakout best (Hill/Pruitt); clean data; futures splicing (perpetual vs
    continuous contracts, back-adjustment, Schwager caveat); 30-50 trades minimum; parameter
    stability over spikes; HAL case study: CCI + Bollinger system, long-only profitable, short
    side destroyed it, ADX filter + stop + walk-forward optimization fixed it (PF 0.94 -> 4.33);
    profit factor >2 good, >10 = curve-fit warning; outlier-adjusted profit (largest winner
    <= 40-50% of profit); efficiency factor 38-69% (Sepiashvili); MAR/Recovery ratio >1;
    Sharpe critique (no drawdown, penalizes upside, ignores loss clustering); Sortino;
    underwater curve; standard-error-of-equity smoothness; perfect profit correlation;
    expect half the profit and twice the drawdown live (Hill); Chande's cardinal rules
    (expectation >13%, <10 rules, multi-market, drawdown <20%, fully mechanical)

CH 2. Money and Portfolio Risk Management (Kirkpatrick/Dahlquist, FULL READ)
    money management = risk side; drawdown definition; law of percentages (50% loss needs 100%
    gain); risk = amount AND probability of loss (Rotella); Monte Carlo simulation of trade
    sequences (scramble trades 100-2000x, distribution of terminal equity, ruin probability);
    theory of runs (P(5 losses at 40% losing) = 1%); martingale (double after loss, needs
    capital for run of 8 = 128x first bet); risk of ruin formula ROR=((1-ta)/(1+ta))^cu;
    PCT=[(A+1)p-1]/A optimal capital percentage; Kelly/optimal f
    (f=(wins% x (PF+1)-1)/PF, HAL example 56.6%); secure f (Zamansky/Stendahl, divide by MDD);
    use the smallest of the three sizing formulas; ~2% rule from theory of runs; initial capital
    = 3x margin or 2x MDD + margin; correlated vs diversifiable risk (9 stocks cut idiosyncratic
    risk to 1/3, sqrt-n law); diversification fails when correlations jump to 1 (2008);
    trade frequency: 10 losses across markets = same drawdown as 10 in one; temporal risk
    (risk grows with holding time, reward does not); protective stop mandatory and inviolate;
    hard money stop placed at technical levels not share math; maximum winning adverse
    excursion sizing; breakeven level (3x ATR then stop to entry); technical vs money point
    stop; volatility stop; parabolic/trendline/adaptive stops; time stops; targets with
    trailing stop; slippage worse on stops with the short-term trend; execution (close most
    rational, scaling); monitoring: rolling profit factor, t-test on windows, equity curve
    MA break = warning; 20% portfolio kill switch

CH 3. System Evaluation and Testing (Kaufman TSM ch21, DENSE READ)
    "numbers torture" epigraph; testing = validation of pre-stated expectations, not discovery;
    optimization as overfitting ("best results often a financial tragedy when traded");
    parameters (MA period, band width, stop, holding period); 1- and 2-parameter optimization;
    test data selection; cash vs adjusted series problems; synthetic data; testing integrity;
    in-sample vs out-of-sample; walk-forward/step-forward (with its short-term bias and the
    FEEDBACK problem: reusing OOS data turns it into IS data = the programme's holdout rule);
    sequential optimization reduces n1 x n2 to n1 + n2 but can miss the peak; seeding with
    random starts; 2-D test maps: continuity patterns (broad plateau vs twin peaks vs erratic);
    3-point and 9-box neighbor averaging to kill spikes; NASDAQ worked example (profit only
    from 30-day MA up, fast systems lose to costs); genetic algorithms (chromosome strings,
    initialization pool 1000-5000, propagation, mating, mutation, converging; 8-parameter
    space = 2.3 billion tests); Monte Carlo sampling of parameter space; robustness = sound
    premise + adaptive + most data, fewest parameters (Futures Truth: best systems have <=4
    variables); benchmark choice; survivorship bias and asymmetric returns in benchmarks;
    performance criteria ranked (information ratio first; number of trades; per-trade return
    vs slippage by market: S&P 50bp/side, energies 20-30bp; expect average of ALL tests minus
    1 sd as realistic expectation; time-to-recovery; time in market = price-shock defense;
    slope of returns); sensitivity testing in n-space (concentric layers from core parameters);
    "the significance of significant": one winner among 1000 tests is noise, count cumulative
    tests across strategies (the programme's experiment-log rule, stated in plain English);
    one-market systems = probably overfit, similar markets are free OOS data; comparing two
    trend systems (regression slope vs MA); classic tests (Davis/Thiel, Maxwell, Hochheimer);
    price shocks; misinterpreting the past; robustness summary

CH 4. Practical Considerations (Kaufman TSM ch22, DENSE READ)
    computer use and abuse ("computers do not think, they compute"); data acquisition and
    accuracy; paper trading; vertical vs integrated solutions; extreme events: price shocks
    = the main gap between test and reality; money moves markets in shocks (correlations go
    to 1, liquidation hits unrelated markets); hindsight: no one profits from a shock except
    by luck, backtests silently pocket shock profits (50% right-side odds); four case studies
    (Gorbachev abduction 1991 double shock; UK election 1992; Gulf War oil; 9/11);
    ATR-based shock identification (1-day range 5x prior ATR); crisis management (windfall =
    exit, loss = hold for the post-shock reversal, reinitialize trends after volatility drops);
    theory of runs math ((1/2)^(n+2); 256 coups -> one run of 6, longest expected run n+2;
    doubling spins extends longest run by 1); martingale betting pattern ($1->128 stake
    sizing); anti-martingale (double winners, stop after the long run, wins only if the run
    comes early); fractional martingales; selective trading; filtering losing trades is
    overfitting ("no way to filter out all losing trades"); black-box systems warning;
    system trade-offs table: trend following = many small losses/few large profits/30-45% wins;
    mean reversion = many small profits/few large losses, profits capped so losses cannot be;
    Commodex method; limit-locked markets = disconnected from the test; Silver and NASDAQ too
    good to be true; similarity of systematic signals (all trend systems correlate, worst at
    the worst times)

CH 5. Risk Control (Kaufman TSM ch23, DENSE READ)
    luck vs skill (LTCM engineered away past shocks, died on the next one); out of market
    60% of time = 60% shock avoidance; deleverage beats return-maximizing; Bernoulli utility
    1738 (diminishing marginal utility, risk aversion); efficient frontier; liquidity K-factor;
    Sharpe SR=(E-I)/sigma; information ratio (I dropped); Sharpe blind spots (loss order,
    upside surges, monthly smoothing flatters); Treynor (divide by beta); Schwager Average
    Maximum Retracement (semivariance cousin); Calmar = AROR/max drawdown; Sortino (MAR
    threshold, downside deviation); Ulcer Index (root-mean-square of drawdowns, Martin 1987);
    potential risk as probability; leverage and reserves; position sizing (futures ATR-based,
    stocks risk-budgeted); initial position risk; stop-losses: gaps and fast markets make
    stops approximate; stops follow system nature (trend systems already cut losses, mean
    reversion needs high win rate so stops hurt it); stop menu (percentage, parabolic, swing
    points, n-period extremes, KAMA, volatility 3x ATR10, Kase Dev-stop ATR + f x STDEV,
    ISAM 12 x ATR252); ranking markets for selection (commodity selection index, directional
    movement ADX retested, strategy selection indicator); risk of ruin with unequal win/loss;
    averaging in, waiting for better price, timing; adding on profits vs averaging down;
    equity curve trading (trade the equity trend); optimal f (Kelly growth function, geometric
    mean scan f=0.01..1.0, Vince; Elder's warnings: f changes every trade, over-f = eventual
    ruin, under-f = geometric profit loss; never average down, never meet margin calls,
    liquidate worst first); Markowitz optimal leverage = mu/sigma^2; binomial probability of
    loss streaks (Pascal triangle; 4/5 first losses at 33% win rate = 33% probability);
    chi-square test of actual vs expected reliability (chi^2=(O-E)^2/E, significance ladder
    3.84/6.64/10.83); runs test: actual price runs vs random = NOT significant, price
    asymmetry is where the signal lives

CH 6. Statistical Analysis (Aronson EBTA ch4, FULL READ, first pass)
    [see Section I summary above; bank: null default; sampling distribution; population =
    immediate practical future; LLN/CLT/standard error; data mining inflates the significance
    bar (best-of-1000 rules); serial correlation slows sqrt(n) (fn 33); nonstationarity means
    more data is not always better (fn 35); TA profits may be liquidity/price-discovery/risk-
    transfer premia not alpha (fn 13)]

CH 7. Hypothesis Tests and Confidence Intervals (Aronson EBTA ch5, FULL READ, first pass)
    [see Section I summary above; bank: estimation vs testing; confirming-the-consequent
    fallacy; H0/Ha exhaustive+exclusive; null gets the benefit (skepticism, parsimony, single
    claim); type I > type II in cost; p-value; bootstrap (Efron 1979, zero-centered, N with
    replacement); White's Reality Check (best-of-many nulls); Masters Monte Carlo permutation
    (pair signal +/-1 with scrambled detrended returns, without replacement); TT-4-91 worked
    example (+4.84% ann detrended, p=0.0692 "possibly significant", 80% CI +0.62% to +9.06%;
    CI-vs-test conflict under skewness)]

CH 8. Regression (Katsanos, FULL READ)
    regression = correlation used for prediction; y = bx + a, OLS; b = r * sy/sx; intercept;
    multiple regression: maximize r-squared with low inter-predictor correlation; tolerance
    = 1 - r^2, <0.20 = multicollinearity problem (>=0.80 pairwise = warning); index
    components exception; worked model: SP weekly = 0.366 ESTX - 0.067 VIX + 0.206 UTY +
    0.045 (r^2 0.79 at 3 predictors, more variables add nothing); assumptions: linearity
    (scatterplot inspection), normality (histogram, Shapiro-Wilks, Kolmogorov-Smirnov),
    use percentage yields not raw prices; outlier trimming for fat tails; nonparametric
    regression (kernel, smoothing splines, SVM, kernel choice problem)

CH 9. International Indices and Commodities (Katsanos, DENSE READ)
    index catalog with correlations to S&P: DAX (performance index, Eurex FDAX, VDAX/V1X),
    CAC 40 (free-float weighted, cap 15%), FTSE, Euro Stoxx 50 (r 0.81-0.89 with S&P weekly),
    Nikkei (r only 0.42 daily), Hang Seng; trading hours and volatility table with contract
    multipliers, ATRs and equal-risk contract counts (ES 5 contracts = 1 SPX); S&P daily
    declines: actual tail far fatter than normal (a -5.1% day has real probability 0.1% vs
    normal 0.000%); dollar index DXY formula (geometric weighted average, euro weight 0.576);
    XOI/OIX oil indices; CRB and GSCI (GSCI tracks crude); XAU vs HUI/BUGS (unhedged miners
    outperform in gold uptrends); VIX: not volatility but option price level (expensive vs
    cheap options); sentiment indicator (capital at risk, not surveys); VXN, VXD; 2003 method
    change (OEX/Black-Scholes -> SPX/variance swap style, VXO kept for history); VIX >30 fear,
    <20 complacency, extremes mark opposite S&P turning points but LEAD unreliable (2007: VIX
    10 preceded a 6-month-later decline; oscillator test: more than half the signals early by
    2 weeks to 5 months; "not the Holy Grail, use with other indicators")

CH 10. The S&P 500 (Katsanos, DENSE READ)
    correlation = first step of intermarket analysis, stability over time must be checked;
    yearly correlation tables daily: DJ-30 0.87 avg, RUT 0.79, NDX 0.83, DAX 0.71 (std 0.41),
    Nikkei 0.30; correlations rise and stabilize at weekly horizons; 10th/90th percentiles
    show instability (DAX -0.52 to 0.96); post-1998 regime more stable; interest rates,
    commodities, forex, VIX correlations; stock-vs-stock correlation

CH 11. European Indices (Katsanos, HEADERS+OPENING)
    DAX deep dive; correlation with component stocks; European futures (FDAX, FESX, FCE);
    time factor (US open moves European markets); intraday patterns; correlation stability
    across the Atlantic session handover

CH 12. Gold (Katsanos, DENSE READ)
    gold vs equities long term (1988-2007: gold +72% nominal over 20 years vs S&P 10.6%/yr
    compounding; supply persists, no depletion); 2003 character change: gold flipped from
    uncorrelated/negative to POSITIVE correlation with equities (correlation reversal April
    2003, crisis-era co-movement); gold vs dollar negative; gold vs oil positive; seasonal
    strength Sep-Dec (jewelry restocking), distorted by secular trends; lead/lag: NO visible
    lead or lag, gold peaks with dollar bottoms and with XAU tops simultaneously (synchronous,
    not predictive); which time frame (weekly yields most stable)

CH 13. Intraday Correlations (Katsanos, DENSE READ)
    5-minute to 1500-minute correlation table (ES vs ESTX vs DAX vs YM, 10,870 five-minute
    bars 2007); correlation grows with interval, exponential then saturating around 75
    minutes; empirical conversion laws: DAX-ESTX r_i = 0.87 x T^0.01; ES-DAX
    r_i = 0.677 x T^0.04 (power-law scaling of correlation with sampling interval); intermarket
    regression intraday; time-frame choice per trader horizon; lead/lag intraday

CH 14. Intermarket Indicators (Katsanos, DENSE READ, the encodable core)
    relative strength ratio (price/benchmark, 3-day EMA smoothed, trendline breaks;
    O'Neil lineage); normalization problem (RS values not comparable across securities);
    Bollinger Band divergence (relative position in band, SEC1BOL = 1 + (C-MA+2SD)/4SD;
    divergence = EMA3 of (SEC2BOL-SEC1BOL)/SEC1BOL x 100, signals at +/-10-30 peaks/troughs;
    limits: normality assumption, negative correlations break it); intermarket disparity
    (Nison disparity index vs MA30, difference of the two with correlation sign c=+/-1;
    Ruggiero's coarser variant); LRS divergence (volatility-adjusted linear regression slope
    difference, +/-10-40 levels); intermarket regression divergence (predicted yield
    Y(Pred) = r x sy/sx x X, divergence = Y(Pred) - Y(actual), the most rigorous of the
    family); intermarket momentum oscillator (normalize any divergence to 1-100 over 200-day
    window, stochastic-style rules, signals REVERSED when normalizing divergence); Z-score
    divergence (Z = (C-MA)/SD each security, difference x correlation; leader/follower
    identification; normality assumption flag); multiple intermarket divergence (composite of
    several related markets); multiple regression divergence; intermarket moving average;
    congestion index

CH 15. Relative Rotation Graphs (de Kempenaer/Verdouw, DENSE READ)
    relative strength history; institutional vs private use; combinatorial explosion of RS
    pairs ((n^2 - n)/2: 10 GICS sectors = 45 charts, 19 STOXX = 171, OEX 100 = 4950);
    raw RS values not comparable across securities (apples and oranges, ranking trap: falling
    IT can have higher RS value than rising Energy); JdK RS-Ratio (proprietary normalized
    uniform RS measure, >100 outperforming, rankable); JdK RS-Momentum (rate of change of the
    RS-Ratio, leads the RS-Ratio, crosses of 100 mark RS-Ratio turning points); RRG scatter
    plot: x = RS-Ratio, y = RS-Momentum, benchmark anchored at (100,100); four quadrants and
    CLOCKWISE rotation: leading (top right) -> weakening (bottom right) -> lagging (bottom
    left) -> improving (top left) -> leading; tails (5-period on weekly) show heading and pace;
    derived indicators: RRG quadrant, RRG angles, RRG distance, standard deviation cone,
    RRG velocity, RRG heading; visualization vs trading system distinction (it is a
    visualization; quadrant crossings are candidate signals, not tested here)

CH 16. Fact, Fiction, and Momentum Investing (Asness/Frazzini/Israel/Moskowitz, FULL READ, first pass)
    [see Section III summary above; bank: UMD 8.3%/0.50 1927-2013; long = short side; large-cap
    robustness; value dead in large caps; live-trade cost survival >$1T; tax myth rebuttal;
    value-momentum corr -0.4, 60/40 Sharpe 0.80; Daniel-Moskowitz crashes (short-side driven,

    Michigan sentiment 15.5%, 10yr-FFR spread 10.7%, consumer goods orders 8.1%, Chicago Fed
