# Empirical Market Microstructure notes — Joel Hasbrouck (NYU Stern teaching notes, Draft 1.1, 2004)

Source: uploaded "7. Hasbrouck's book.pdf" (identical copy also uploaded as "2. Hasbrouck's book.pdf"). Studied November 2026, read cover to cover. These notes are written in teaching style so they explain the ideas, not just list them. This is the empirical/statistical layer of the knowledge base: it shows how to measure everything Harris described conceptually.

## The central idea of the whole book

Prices you observe are a noisy version of a hidden "efficient" price. The job of empirical microstructure is to decompose what we see (transaction prices) into (a) a permanent, information-driven component and (b) a temporary, trading-mechanism-driven component. Formally:

- Efficient price: m_t = m_(t-1) + w_t (a random walk; w_t is new information)
- Observed price: p_t = m_t + (pricing error s_t, e.g., bid-ask bounce)

If you learn one equation from this book, it is the random-walk decomposition: p_t = random walk + stationary noise. Everything else is machinery for identifying and estimating those two parts.

## Part I: The economics and the univariate machinery

### Why prices should be martingales (Ch. 2)

- From a consumption/investment first-order condition: p_t = E_t[m_(t+1) p_(t+1)] where m is the stochastic discount factor. Under risk neutrality (implicitly assumed in most empirical microstructure), p_t = E_t p_(t+1): a martingale. Price changes are then unforecastable.
- Practical points about microstructure data: (1) "event time" (one observation = one trade) is often better than clock time; (2) moments may not exist: extreme-value evidence suggests finite return moments only up to order ~3 and volume moments only up to ~1.5, so variances of volume are literally undefined; (3) estimate the mean return as zero: the honest estimate of the daily expected return has a standard error ~2.5x the true mean, so bias-for-variance tradeoff favors zero.
- With a bid-ask spread, the martingale property of observed prices breaks (the FOC becomes an inequality p^b <= E[m x] <= p^a). The martingale survives only as the conditional expectation of terminal value given expanding information sets. This conditional expectation is what we call the "efficient price."

### The Roll model (Ch. 3) — "the little black dress of microstructure models"

Setup: efficient price m_t = m_(t-1) + u_t; competitive dealers with per-trade cost c quote bid b_t = m_t − c, ask a_t = m_t + c. Trades occur at bid or ask: p_t = m_t + c q_t, where q_t = +1 (customer buys at ask) or −1 (customer sells at bid). q_t are i.i.d. coin flips, independent of u_t.

Key derivation (worth internalizing): price changes are
Δp_t = c q_t − c q_(t-1) + u_t.
The bid-ask bounce creates negative autocorrelation: Cov(Δp_t, Δp_(t-1)) = γ1 = −c², and Var(Δp_t) = γ0 = 2c² + σ_u². So from just the first two autocovariances of price changes you can estimate:
- Half-spread: c = sqrt(−γ1)
- Efficient-price innovation variance: σ_u² = γ0 + 2γ1.
This is the whole trick of empirical microstructure in miniature: spread and information volatility are hiding in the autocorrelation of price changes. Empirically, the Roll estimate for CBL on a high-volume day was ~0.01 vs a quoted average spread of 0.022: the Roll model tends to understate spreads when the assumptions (constant spread, no adverse selection) fail.

### Time series representations (Ch. 4, 9, 11)

- Covariance stationarity: mean and autocovariances don't depend on t. Price LEVELS are never stationary (variance grows with t); price CHANGES can be. Ergodicity: the process eventually "forgets" initial conditions; price levels aren't ergodic, changes are. Asymmetric information models with a one-time value draw are NOT ergodic or stationary, so we treat each trading day as a separate sample path (an "ensemble").
- Wold theorem: any covariance-stationary series can be written as a moving average of white noise. This licenses starting from MA representations without knowing the structural model.
- AR ↔ MA conversion via lag polynomials; forecasting by recursive substitution; impulse response = impact multipliers = MA coefficients.
- Unit roots: if the AR lag polynomial has a root of exactly 1, the series has a random-walk (nonstationary) component. "Take first differences" is shorthand that only works for I(1) processes.
- Invertibility: among the two MA parameter solutions, only the one with |θ| < 1 lets you recover ε_t from current and past Δp_t. The two solutions are reciprocals: θ_inv = 1/θ_noninv.
- Estimation: MLE, direct moment (autocovariance) estimates, or AR-based; distributions of derived (structural) quantities via the delta method (Jacobian of the mapping) or subsampling (estimate per day, then mean across days: the Fama-MacBeth approach). Practical rule: drop the overnight return; treat each day as a fresh sample; microstructure models fit within the day.
- Case study workflow: WRDS → NYSE TAQ trades and quotes → SAS; compute Roll spread, σ_w and pricing-error std dev from MA(1) and MA(3) fits.

### Sequential trade models: Glosten-Milgrom (Ch. 5)

Setup: security worth V̄ (prob 1−δ) or V_ (prob δ). Informed fraction μ of traders know the value and always trade the right direction; uninformed trade randomly (buy/sell equally). Quotes are set to the dealer's zero-profit conditional expectations:
- Ask A = E[V | next trade is a buy], Bid B = E[V | next trade is a sell].
The spread A − B is purely adverse-selection driven. Each trade updates δ via Bayes rule. Dynamic features: trade prices form a martingale; order flow is serially correlated (informed always trade same direction); each trade has permanent price impact; the spread narrows over time as uncertainty resolves.

Extensions: (1) add transaction costs c: quotes ± c; ask and bid sequences stay martingales but trade prices don't. (2) Price-sensitive liquidity traders: wider spreads drive them out, and if too few remain, no quotes are possible: market failure, repaired by halts or forced dealer presence; this is the economic-efficiency argument for insider trading bans. (3) Event uncertainty (Easley-O'Hara 1992): whether an information event even happened is unknown; a no-trade outcome is informative (suggests no event). (4) Order sizes: informed trade large; pooling equilibria require large orders to sometimes come from uninformed traders. (5) Order types: informed never use stop orders; stop orders reduce informational efficiency and raise the probability of large moves (election cascades).

### Strategic trade models: Kyle (Ch. 6)

One risk-neutral informed trader who chooses size strategically vs. a market maker who sets price as a linear function of total order flow: p = μ + λ(y), y = u + x (noise u plus informed x). Results:
- Informed demand x = (v − μ)/(2λ): the deeper the market (small λ), the more aggressively the informed trades. λ = sqrt(Σ_0)/(2 sqrt(σ_u²)) is "Kyle's lambda", the inverse of market depth: price impact per unit of order flow.
- Informed profit E π = (sqrt(σ_u²) (v − p_0)²)/(2 sqrt(Σ_0)): more noise trading = more camouflage = more profit. Noise traders are the informed trader's cover.
- Exactly half of the insider's information is impounded in the single auction price (conditional variance halves), regardless of noise intensity.
- Multiperiod version: the informed trader spreads trades over time (Δx_n = Δt β_n (v − p_(n−1))); depth λ_n and informativeness evolve deterministically; in the continuous limit, information diffuses gradually into price. This formalizes the most common real-world strategy: distribute orders over time to minimize impact.

### The generalized Roll model and filtering (Ch. 7)

Add adverse selection to Roll: efficient price innovation w_t = λ q_t + u_t. Now the trade direction moves the efficient price permanently (λ = adverse-selection price impact) while c is still the transitory bounce. Price changes: Δp_t = −c q_(t−1) + (c + λ) q_t + u_t; autocovariances γ0 = c² + (c+λ)² + σ_u², γ1 = −c(c+λ).

Big insight: the best forecast of the future price f_t = p_t + θε_t (from the MA representation) is NOT the efficient price m_t, but it IS E[m_t | current and past prices]: a filtered estimate. With more data (smoothing) you can do better. The variance of the pricing error s_t = p_t − m_t has a data-identified LOWER bound θ²σ_ε², attained when information is exclusively private (u_t = 0); there is no statistical upper bound, only economic arguments about how stale prices can be.

### Univariate random-walk decompositions (Ch. 8)

Start from any MA representation of Δp_t (Wold guarantees it exists) without any structural model. You can always identify: σ_w² (random-walk variance per period = Var of the permanent component), the projection of m_t on past price changes, and a lower bound on the pricing-error variance σ_s². The autocovariance generating function machinery: σ_w² = [sum of all MA coefficients on the permanent shock]² × variance. This is how you measure "how much of price movement is information" with almost no assumptions.

## Part II: Trades, inventory, and multivariate models

### Inventory models (Ch. 10)

- Garman (1976): buys and sells arrive as independent Poisson processes with intensities λ_B(p), λ_S(p) that depend on price; the dealer exists because order flow doesn't self-synchronize; dealer inventory is a random walk and will eventually hit ruin if passively quoted at one price. Dealer's problem = choosing bid/ask to balance revenue per "turn" (pair of trades) against arrival rates.
- Amihud-Mendelson (1980): dealer moves quotes monotonically down with inventory to elicit offsetting order flow; quotes fluctuate around a preferred position; inventory-driven price changes are TRANSIENT (they revert) unlike information-driven changes. Inventory effect and adverse-selection effect are the two competing explanations for spread and dynamics.
- Reality check: real dealer inventories are mean-reverting and stable, but the visible quote is NOT the control variable; inventory control happens via non-public quotes, interdealer channels, selective participation. Quote-based inventory control is empirically refuted for dealers but survives as the backbone of order-strategy models (limit order traders DO do this).
- Trade direction q_t is strongly positively autocorrelated in practice (buys follow buys), unlike the i.i.d. Roll assumption; this autocorrelation is the statistical signature of order splitting and persistence.

### VAR machinery (Ch. 11-12)

- Vector AR/MA representations: estimate a VAR in (Δp_t, q_t), invert to VMA by forecasting after unit shocks; impulse response functions trace out how a trade shock moves prices over time. The cumulative IRF of Δp after a trade = the price impact function; its permanent level is λ (adverse selection).
- Cholesky factorization = imposing a recursive causal ordering when innovations are contemporaneously correlated. Ordering matters: put the "cause" first. In microstructure, the natural ordering is trade → price (trades cause quote revisions), which is why the bivariate VAR is estimated with q_t affecting Δp_t contemporaneously.
- Forecast variance decomposition: attribute forecast error variance to each innovation (trade vs public information). With correlated innovations, compute bounds over orderings, not point estimates.

### Statistical trade/price models (Ch. 13)

Model 1 (generalized Roll with q observed): estimate by OLS of Δp_t on q_t and q_(t−1); coefficients give c and λ directly. Random-walk variance decomposes into trade-related (private information, λ²σ_q²) and non-trade (public information, σ_u²) parts. The relative measure λ²σ_q²/σ_w² = R² of trades in explaining efficient-price innovations.
Model 2 (autocorrelated trades): only the INNOVATION in q carries information; using raw q overstates private information. Informed-order persistence means innovations are smaller than raw trade flips.
Model 3 (endogenous trades): price changes feed back into trades (momentum traders, hedgers, inventory-induced order flow elicitation). Now you need a genuine joint system (VAR) and cannot interpret single equations causally.
Model 4 (contemporaneous public information): public news and trades both move prices in the same period; identified via recursive structure.
Summary measures: λ (trade impact coefficient, the per-trade adverse-selection cost) and variance decompositions (share of efficient-price variance attributable to trades).

### Structural models (Ch. 14)

- Glosten-Harris (1988): decomposes impact into transitory and permanent components of signed trade size.
- Madhavan-Richardson-Roomans (1997): full structural model fitted to NYSE data; spread dominated by adverse-selection component at the start of trading.
- Huang-Stoll (1997): unified spread decomposition separating order-processing, inventory (dealer risk aversion), and adverse-selection components by exploiting differences across stocks and dealer inventory positions.
- General lesson: structural models give economic content but are fragile; VARs are robust but need causal interpretation imposed.

### PIN (Ch. 15) — probability of informed trading

Easley-Kiefer-O'Hara framework: each day, an information event occurs with prob α; if it did, informed traders arrive at Poisson rate μ, uninformed buyers/sellers at rates ε_B/ε_S. Daily counts of buys (B) and sells (S) are a mixture of Poissons. The summary statistic:

PIN = αμ / (αμ + ε_B + ε_S)

= unconditional probability that the counterparty to your trade is informed. Estimation from just daily buy/sell counts via maximum likelihood.
- Identification insight (the mixture analysis): α and μ separately are poorly identified (many mixtures look alike), but their PRODUCT is well identified: a few very informed days vs many mildly informed days look the same, and PIN captures exactly that product. This is why PIN is estimable with useful precision.
- High PIN ≡ positive intraday autocorrelation in trade directions (informed runs). Kyle-type models instead imply serially UNCORRELATED net order flow: autocorrelation evidence distinguishes the paradigms.
- Information asymmetry measures have spread beyond equities (corporate finance, accounting), but caveats: closed-end fund spreads too large to be adverse selection; index-revision spreads move without cash-flow news; FX and bond price impacts may reflect transient, not fundamental, information (Lyons: private info about TRANSIENT price components is also profitable).

### Limit order book models (Ch. 18)

- Sandas / Södås model: build the equilibrium book from break-even conditions of risk-neutral suppliers facing an exponential distribution of incoming order sizes; book depth at each price = expectation of profit zero over the arriving order distribution. Implication: books should be much steeper than observed price impact if suppliers could condition on order size; empirically the book IS steeper than the dynamic price impact function, and the assumed exponential order size distribution has too few midsize and too many small/large orders. "Backfilling" after executions is a conjectured non-feature of the pure model, plausibly present with event uncertainty.
- Seppi (1997) dealers/specialists in a limit order market: dealers can condition on incoming order size and must yield to better-priced customer orders; they profit by selling into the book's shadow ("quantity improvement" = price improvement for the customer, but it cannibalizes limit-order profits, so liquidity suppliers post less). This is the economic critique of internalization.

### Bidding/offering with execution uncertainty (Ch. 19-21)

- Stoll (1978): a dealer at her portfolio optimum needs compensation for being moved off it; with CARA utility the bid is set below the benchmark price by a term increasing in risk aversion × variance: quotes encode the dealer's risk aversion, which is why observed spreads exceed pure order-processing costs.
- Foucault: limit order choice under pick-off risk; higher fundamental volatility σ widens spreads (fading limit orders) and shifts the mix toward limit orders that execute less often; the winner's curse of being executed right before adverse moves.
- Parlour (1998): queuing model of the book; crowding-out: heavy same-side depth pushes arrivals to market orders; heavy opposite-side depth attracts limit orders. The book's shape feeds back into order choice. Liquidity begets liquidity on the opposite side.

### Microstructure and asset pricing (Ch. 22)

- Amihud-Mendelson (1986): clients sort by spread: long-horizon investors hold high-spread assets ("spread amortization"); equilibrium gross return is a CONCAVE, increasing function of the relative spread. Empirically confirmed: sorting CRSP stocks by spread produced monotone excess returns (0.35%→1.02% monthly across 7 spread groups).
- Constantinides (1986): with fixed proportional costs, investors adapt by trading less (no-trade region widens with cost k); liquidity premia are second-order small (δ(k)/k ≈ 12-17%), BUT the model then predicts turnover an order of magnitude below observed ~100%. Same volume puzzle in FX.
- Heaton-Lucas (1996): labor income + incomplete markets + asymmetric quadratic costs; transaction costs alone can't explain the equity premium unless implausibly large.
- Takeaway for a trader: spreads matter for expected returns cross-sectionally, but trading volume in the real world is far beyond what any frictional asset-pricing model justifies: most volume is not rebalancing, it is trading in Harris's zero-sum sense.

### Cointegration and price discovery (Ch. 17)

- Two prices for the same instrument (two markets) share one efficient price: p_i,t = m_t + c_i q_i,t. Their difference is stationary (cointegrated with vector (1, −1)). The VMA of the price-change vector gives information shares: the contribution of each market's innovations to the common efficient-price variance. The market whose innovations account for more of the common random walk "does more price discovery."
- Microstructure applications assume integration and known cointegrating vectors (arbitrage logic) rather than testing them: high-frequency data has no power over macro questions.
- Directly relevant to NQ futures vs the cash index: the information-share framework is how you'd measure whether the futures market leads the cash market (Harris's "tail wags the dog").

### US market institutions (Appendix)

Open outcry (price priority, time priority, parity, size precedence; trade-throughs), dealer markets, electronic limit order books (price-time priority, anonymous, customers supply liquidity to customers). NYSE hybrid: floor + monopolist specialist (one per stock, ~15% participation rate) + book agent + automatic executions (Direct+); affirmative/negative obligations; price improvement by the floor's last-mover advantage; opening = single-price call auction. Nasdaq: dealer market transformed by the Manning rules (no trading ahead/through customer limit orders), the 1994 Christie-Schultz collusion scandal (quotes on 1/4 grid to protect spreads; spreads collapsed days after the scandal broke; ~$1B civil settlement), and the SEC Order Handling Rules (limit order display rule, 30 seconds; quote display). ECNs/ATSs, SIPs (CTA), intermarket access links. Average NYSE trade size peaked 1988 (~2,300 shares) and fell back to ~600 by 2002 as automation took over.

## Connections to the earlier layers

- Harris's transitory volatility = Hasbrouck's stationary pricing error s_t; Harris's fundamental volatility = the random walk m_t. Harris's adverse-selection spread component = λ; Harris's inventory component = Stoll's CARA quote-shading (Ch. 19).
- Balan's corrections/impulses now have a statistical identity: corrections are (partly) transitory components that a random-walk decomposition would classify as noise; wave-3 thrusts are permanent (information) moves. A quantifiable research idea: fit the generalized Roll/VAR machinery on intraday data around suspected wave turns; waves should show up as changes in λ and in the trade-related share of σ_w².
- Kyle's gradual information diffusion in multiperiod auctions = an alternative microfoundation for 3rd-wave extension dynamics (news entering price in stages).
- PIN and wave analysis: a wave count that implies "informed accumulation" (e.g., during a suspected Wave 1) predicts high |B−S| imbalances; PIN estimated around Elliott turn dates is a testable hypothesis.
- The q_t autocorrelation result (buys follow buys) matches Balan's 3rd-wave persistence and the front-running/order-splitting stories in Harris.

## Strategy and research candidates from this layer

1. Estimate λ (trade impact) and the private-information share of σ_w² on index futures around weekly-chart wave positions: is the "impulse vs correction" call supported by the information decomposition?
2. Random-walk decomposition on intraday data at wave targets: if the permanent component stalls while transitory pushes continue, the market is "fighting" the level (order flow without information: exhaustion).
3. Avoid being the noise: Hasbrouck's mean-return estimation lesson (use zero) and moment-existence warnings argue for robust statistics (medians, trimmed estimators) in any strategy research.
4. Execution: for entries at Fibonacci targets, prefer marketable limit orders in narrow-spread conditions; the Foucault pick-off risk says don't leave resting orders near fast markets (Balan's diagonal-triangle thrust zones).
5. Measure everything per-day and average across days (Fama-MacBeth style): the single most practical inference lesson of the book for a solo researcher.

# Finite Volume Methods notes — Eymard, Gallouët & Herbin, "Finite Volume Methods" (Handbook of Numerical Analysis, 2019 update of the 1997 LATP preprint)

Source: uploaded book "21. Five.pdf", 254 pages. Structured study November 2026: principles chapters and the mathematical treatment of elliptic and hyperbolic problems read closely; the Navier-Stokes and porous-media application sections skimmed for structure. Companion to the Ferziger-Perić-Street notes; this is the rigorous-mathematics version of the same method.

## What this text adds over the CFD book

Ferziger et al. teach finite volume as an engineering tool with error-estimation practice. This text is the proof layer: it constructs the schemes and proves stability, convergence and uniqueness for the main PDE classes. What it demonstrates, rather than merely asserts:

- Local conservativity as the defining property: on each cell the discrete flux leaving K equals the flux entering the neighbour across the shared edge. The scheme is built by writing an integral balance per cell and only then choosing a flux function in terms of discrete unknowns.
- The upwind choice as the stability mechanism: for the transport equation, taking the upstream cell value at each face is what makes the scheme L∞-stable (a discrete maximum principle: solution stays within the initial data bounds) and is "crucial in the mathematical analysis". Central/averaged fluxes lose this.
- Finite volume consistency is not finite difference consistency: the cell-centered FV scheme can fail the FD consistency test at node locations yet be consistent and convergent when the unknown is interpreted at the right location. Consistency statements must specify where the discrete unknown is supposed to live.
- CFL condition as a convex-combination argument: under the step restriction, the explicit update is a convex combination of neighbouring values; that is literally the proof of the maximum principle. One-line takeaway: stability proofs are often bookkeeping of signs and convex weights.
- Monotone/flux schemes for nonlinear hyperbolic conservation laws: L∞ stability, weak BV estimates, entropy conditions and entropy-process solutions; convergence to the entropy weak solution (not merely any weak solution). For nonlinear conservation laws, extra admissibility criteria (entropy inequalities) are required to select the physical solution; a scheme that converges to a non-entropic weak solution is wrong even if stable.
- Nonlinear elliptic problems: existence via fixed point arguments (Schauder, Brouwer) plus discrete energy estimates; convergence via compactness lemmas. Discontinuous diffusion coefficients are handled by harmonic-mean-type flux formulas that distribute interface contributions correctly.
- Singular source terms: conservation of the flux across an interface holding only up to the source, with the source split across the interface by coefficients.
- Dual meshes and vertex-centered unknowns: FE methods reinterpreted as FV methods, showing the families are not as separate as textbooks imply.

## Durable methodological content (the reason this is on the shelf)

- Schemes should inherit the invariants of the underlying quantity. If the continuous law conserves something, the discrete version should conserve it exactly, not approximately.
- Stability often reduces to: can the update be written with convex weights and correct signs? This is a design principle for any discrete accumulator (including rolling flow/delta features): if the update rule is a convex combination of bounded inputs, bounds propagate; if not, one oscillation-generating term suffices.
- Consistency must be stated relative to an interpretation (which point the unknown represents). Mismatched interpretation makes a good scheme look inconsistent and vice versa.
- Nonlinear selection problems (which weak solution the limit converges to) require explicit admissibility criteria. The analogy in research work: many limit objects satisfy the weak constraints; you need extra criteria to select the meaningful one.
- CFL intuition: explicit propagation of information cannot move faster than one cell per step. Any forward-coupled discrete process has an analogous information-propagation constraint.

## Honest assessment of NQ relevance

Same verdict as the CFD book: no direct trading content, no strategy implications. Its value is the rigorous version of the conservation and stability discipline that cumulative-flow accounting should obey, plus the habit of asking "what does this discretization preserve exactly, and what does it only approximate". Recorded alongside Ferziger-Perić-Street as the theory counterpart. No changes to the strategy programme follow.

# Fractal Forecasting notes — Ian Copsey, "Fractal Forecasting: The New Eliott [Elliott] Wave Theories Applied" (2015, ISBN 978-1-326-42131-1)

Source: uploaded book "22. F-forecasting...pdf", 213 pages, 218 figures. Elliott Wave extension layer. Studied November 2026, full text. This book supersedes and formalizes Copsey's earlier "Harmonic Elliott Wave" (2010); the upload of that earlier book (23) failed OCR and is unreadable, but this book restates the full method, so the HEW content is covered.

## The core claim: Harmonic Elliott Wave (HEW)

- Every impulsive wave (i), (iii), (v) develops internally in THREE waves (a)(b)(c), not five. Only Wave A and Wave C of corrections develop in five waves. The entire market is a nested 3-wave fractal: 3 waves make a wave, which combines with two others to make a higher-degree wave.
- This eliminates, by construction, most of traditional Elliott's escape hatches: extended waves, failed fifths, leading and ending diagonals, and most running-triangle/expanded-flat justifications. Where classic Elliott (Balan base) explains an overlap or anomaly with an extension or diagonal, HEW instead relabels the structure as an internal (b) wave of a 3-wave impulse leg.
- Practical consequence: an impulse leg (i) = (a) [5 waves] + (b) [any corrective shape] + (c) [5 waves]. Wave (b) is the "wild card / cushion" that absorbs market uncertainty: it can retrace 5.6% to 100% of (a). This is Copsey's replacement for the extended-wave concept.
- Copsey's own positioning: HEW is a price indicator and target-forecasting tool, NOT a signal system. He states markets cannot be expected to adhere to a limited set of ratios everywhere; the (b) wave is the catch-all.

## Ratio ladders (the durable, testable content)

- Retracement ladder: 9.02%, 14.6%, 23.6%, 33.3%, 38.2%, 41.4%, 50%, 58.6%, 61.8%, 66.7%, 76.4%, 85.4%, 90% (100% allowed for (ii)/(b)).
- Projection ladder (wave as multiple of prior wave): 105.6%, 109.2%, 114.6%, 123.6%, 138.2%, 161.8%, 166.7%, 176.4%, 185.4%, 190.2%, 195.7%, 198.7%, 223.6%, 238.2%, 261.8%, 276.4%, 285.4%, 290%, 294.4%, 298.4%, 323.6%, 338.2%, 361.8%, 398.4%, 461.8%, 594.4%.
- Key discovered ratio: 223.6% between 3-wave moves (i.e., (c) projected at 223.6% of (a), and wave iii of an internal 5-wave leg at 223.6% of i). The 123.6% and 114.6% values appear for truncated/weak v waves. Inverted ratios (85.4%, 58.6%, 41.4%, 14.6%, 9.02%) are the same ladder read downward: 100 minus 14.6 = 85.4 etc.
- Wave (v) of an impulse averages around 50% projection (of the wave from origin through (iii)); can be as weak as 23.6% (barely exceeds (iii), then reverses). In a Wave (A) position the (v) range is 33.3%-85.4%, too wide to trade without lower-degree confluence.
- Wave (ii) range: 14.6% to 100%; Wave (ii) NEVER develops as a triangle. Wave (iv) cannot breach, and normally does not even reach, the (b) of Wave (iii).
- Alternation, quantified: (ii) + (iv) retracement SUM in the 80%-120% band is a guideline, not a rule. Copsey's own estimates: holds 90-95% of the time (no statistics collected anywhere in the book). By market: forex strong impulses 80-100%, corrections >100%; US equities 100-110%. Documented failures: 68%, 76% sums. Compensation channels when alternation falls short: a deep Wave (b) of (iii) or of (v) (e.g., NASDAQ: deep (b) of (v)); deep (ii) >90% risks a deeper (iv) up to 38.2%-50%.
- Flat corrections: EFB (wave b) expansion 14.6%-38.2% of EFA (wave a), up to 50% in equities and less liquid markets. Wave (x) minimum 5.6%, rarely below 14.6%, normally stalls beyond the prior (b).

## Natural target rules (the most operational content)

After a completed impulse leg or reversal, price tends to stall at:
1. The span of the prior Wave (b) of Wave (v) (first target after a completed 5-wave move).
2. The prior Wave (iv) extreme (prominent swing high/low).
3. Prior support/resistance or a consolidation area.
4. A triangle or significant range it came from.
- Other guidelines: after a 3-wave move ends, price has a strong chance of reverting to the span of the prior Wave (b). Following Wave (a) of a reversal, expect retest of the prior Wave (b) span. First or second reversal after a completed (v) moves to the prior swing high/low, quite often the Wave (iv).
- Wave iii specifically is LESS likely to stall at natural S/R; weight its own projection ratios and lower-degree development instead.
- After a Wave (b) in any corrective ABC, price will retest the prior Wave (b) [guideline].

## Method and workflow

- Spreadsheet convergence: grids of ratio projections stacked across 2-4 wave degrees; a valid target is where lower-degree and higher-degree projections coincide (the "sudoku" consistency requirement across degrees). Target ranges narrow fractally: monthly (v) projections span thousands of points; hourly (v) spans 0.7 (75.19-75.92 in the USDJPY example).
- Workflow is top-down: establish higher-degree structure first, derive what wave is "due" (5-wave or 3-wave), then confirm lower degrees. Lower-degree fractals validate the count: every (a) and (c) must itself be 5 waves with valid ratios and internal alternation. A count that looks right at one degree but fails internal validation is invalid ("always check the internal count").
- Direct, low-noise moves are more likely 3-wave (two 5-wave legs); a 5-wave assumption on a sharp move without internal confirmation is "a total lottery and in the end retrospective".
- Break levels: the analysis must specify in advance where the count breaks down; knowing break areas is described as vital (this is the closest Copsey comes to falsifiable trading rules).
- Sharp moves that hide Wave i/ii: use tick-bar charts (fixed tick counts per bar), 1-minute, or wait for a correction large enough to satisfy alternation, then work backwards from it.
- Count revision is openly documented as routine: Fig 5-16/5-18 shows a "perfect" count revised after a Wave (iv) failure; the DOW final high at 18,351.36 (May 2015) was initially misjudged because Wave v of -iii- made 172.5% instead of the expected 176.4% and the final -v- was only 12%. Revisions are triggered by: alternation failure, divergences that persist, (iv) retracements that fall short, and conflicting cross-index counts.

## Indicators and cycles layer (chapters 6-7)

- Momentum indicators (raw momentum, RSI, MACD, Stochastics, CCI) all measure the same one-dimensional information: direction and speed. They cannot say WHERE price will stall, nor distinguish correction from reversal. Copsey's standard: use an array of indicators that each measure different things, not several momentum oscillators.
- Divergences require confirmation: break of the most recent swing low/high or a completed reversal pattern; blind divergence-following loses money. His "double divergence" (momentum peak, minor higher peak, lower peak into the price high) claims higher reliability. Divergences that persist and fail are a signal the COUNT is wrong, not that the market is wrong.
- Trend lines: mostly abused. A trend line requires an actual trend (sequence of higher highs and higher lows, direct, not part of a larger swing); price deviating too far from a line invalidates it. Copsey rarely draws them and never relies on them; pattern lines (wedges) are acceptable when they coincide with a wave expectation.
- Bollinger Bands: useful as consolidation S/R in multi-timeframe use (lower TF band penetration, higher TF band as the stall). Moving averages: dismissed entirely (a guru's 20/60 MA cross rule failed every call in his early career).
- Price Equilibrium Clouds (his proprietary Ichimoku-inspired tool): trend-hugging support/resistance bands; flattening before penetration signals reversal conditions; multi-timeframe (60m/240m/daily) use where corrections stall at the next-higher-TF cloud. Proprietary, formula not disclosed in the book; not independently testable.
- HEW RSI (proprietary modified RSI): claims more frequent OB/OS touches and cleaner divergences. Formula not disclosed; not independently testable.
- Fixed time cycles: restricted to a limited set of FX pairs (Dollar Index, USDJPY, GBPUSD, AUDUSD). EURUSD invalid (history too short; direct-to-indirect quote inversion flips the chart). Cyclic principles: summation, harmonicity (neighbouring cycles related by 2), synchronicity (troughs together), proportionality (amplitude related by 2). Specific cycles cited: USDJPY 33-year (and 24/48-week; combined 82-week and 164-week cycle lows at the 2011 75.57 low and a 2008-11 example); GBPUSD second-largest cycle 196 months with a 2017 confluence low; AUDUSD ~15-year cycle; Dewey/Ehrlich/Bressert background (11-year sunspots, 60-year depression, etc.). Gold and USDCHF reportedly split cycles into THREE rather than two. Right translation (peaks displaced toward cycle end) read as bullish. Cross-market cyclic outlooks combine directly- and indirectly-quoted pairs to bracket Dollar highs/lows.

## Honest assessment (as with every book, required)

- Zero statistical evidence anywhere. The author admits this. All ratio claims, the 90-95% alternation estimate, cycle claims, and forecast accuracy are in-sample anecdotes on selected examples. No hit rates, no significance tests, no out-of-sample discipline, no multiple-testing awareness.
- The method has heavily discretionary degrees of freedom: which pivot ends a wave, which ratio in a 20+ value ladder "counts", how much shortfall is tolerated, when to invoke the (b) wild card, when to revise the count. The documented revisions (Fig 5-16/5-18, the DOW top) are honest but demonstrate label-revision risk: the same chart admits multiple valid-looking counts, and the "correct" one is often known only after.
- The ratio ladders are dense enough that SOME ratio will always be near any reversal. Without a pre-registered tolerance band and a null comparison, apparent precision is partly mechanical. Note: ratio histograms computed from ZigZag-type pivots are smooth by construction (a known artifact), so clustering claims at 0.382/0.618/223.6 need a matched random-walk/GARCH null, exactly as the ORDERFLOW platform spec's Elliott null tests N1-N4 prescribe.
- The fixed-cycle claims (82/164-week, 196-month, 33-year) are low-n observations (a few cycle lows each) on four hand-picked pairs; synchronicity/harmonicity are assumptions, not findings. NQ/equity-index cycles are explicitly NOT covered (Copsey avoided equities for most of his career; the DOW forecast chapter is price-count-based, not cycle-based).
- What survives scrutiny as CANDIDATE structure: (1) alternation as a diagnostic (shallow (ii) implies deep (iv) and vice versa, with a quantified 80-120% sum band and named failure modes); (2) the natural-target list (retest of prior (b) span, prior (iv) extreme, consolidation zones) as reference levels; (3) the pre-committed break level concept; (4) multi-degree ratio convergence as a target-narrowing device; (5) 3-wave internal structure of impulse legs as an alternative labeling hypothesis to classic EW extensions.

## Connections

- To the Balan base: HEW is a direct contradiction of classic impulse structure. Balan's three unbreakable rules survive unchanged (they concern the 5-wave sequence in A/C of corrections and the no-overlap rule maps to HEW's (iv) cannot breach (b) of (iii)). Balan's extensions, diagonals, and failed fifths are eliminated by Copsey's (b)-wave reinterpretation. Where both authors quantify: Balan's C = 1.618xA vs Copsey's (c) ladder centered near 100-123.6% with 223.6% for the strongest legs. Alternation in Balan is qualitative (complex vs simple); Copsey quantifies it as a retracement-sum band. Both agree triangles never appear in wave 2/(ii) positions and both flag subjective counting as the method's main risk.
- To VSA/Williams: the effort-vs-result divergence concept appears here as momentum divergence needing price confirmation; Copsey's requirement that a divergence be confirmed by a swing break parallels VSA's requirement that a test be confirmed by subsequent bars.
- To the AVFI programme: the natural-target rules and alternation band are directly usable as objective feature definitions for any future wave-annotation track (H3): e.g., retest-of-prior-(b)-span as a reference-level feature; alternation-sum as a state variable; break-level pre-commitment as the falsification arm. Any such use must be pre-registered with tolerance bands and matched nulls; nothing in this book licenses a live rule.
- To the ORDERFLOW platform spec (Addendum A3): this book is exactly the kind of source that spec's kb_additions contemplates: extract each rule as a falsifiable claim, log the discretionary escape hatches as NOT directly testable until parameterized. The spec's mandatory null tests (N1 random-walk pattern frequency, N2 Fibonacci-ratio clustering vs simulated paths, N3 predictive test beyond momentum controls, N4 incremental value over Wyckoff/order flow) are the correct machinery for HEW's claims, and the label-revision behavior documented in Fig 5-16/5-18 is the revision-rate metric A3 requires reporting.
- Forward to strategy candidates: none directly. Weekly NQ work stays with the AVFI/H8-H11 pipeline. HEW is parked with H3 until an amendment exists that parameterizes one rule (most plausible first candidate: alternation-sum band or retest-of-prior-(b)-span) with a matched null. The cycles chapter is out of scope for NQ (no cycles claimed for equity indices by the author himself).

## Status

COMPLETE: full text studied (all 8 chapters + appendix, 213 pages). Fills most of the gap left by the failed OCR of upload 23 (Harmonic Elliott Wave, 2011), which restates the same method at book length; re-upload of 23 only if the earlier formulation itself matters.

# Geometry, Topology and Physics (Nakahara) — Distilled Notes

## What this book is

The physics-side companion to Reid and Szendrői. Reid and Szendrői build the mathematical objects (invariants, groups, quotients, winding numbers) for their own sake; Nakahara shows what they are for: path integrals, gauge fields, monopoles, instantons, defects in condensed matter, general relativity, Berry's phase, strings, anomalies. Written for a first-year physics postgraduate: proofs only when instructive, otherwise examples that make the theorem plausible (his own preface, p.14 of the upload).

The architecture is a single ladder: classify spaces topologically (homology, homotopy: chapters 3-4), make them differentiable (manifolds: 5), measure them (de Rham cohomology, Riemannian geometry: 6-7), complexify them (8), bundle them (9), connect them (connections: 10), and then the payoffs that only exist at the top of the ladder: characteristic classes (11), index theorems (12), anomalies (13) and string amplitudes (14). Each rung exists because the physics at the top demands it.

## Chapters 3-4: homology and homotopy — counting holes and winding loops

Homology: build a space from simplexes (points, edges, triangles, tetrahedra); chains are formal sums, cycles are chains with zero boundary, boundaries are boundaries; H_r = Z_r / B_r. Verified in the text (p.137): the structure theorem H_r = Z^f + torsion, where the torsion subgroup detects the twisting (real projective plane H_1 = Z_2; Klein bottle H_1 = Z + Z_2), and the reason integer coefficients are preferred: Z_2 and R coefficients erase the torsion (R/mR = 0). Betti numbers b_r = dim H_r(K; R); the Euler-Poincare theorem chi = sum (-1)^r I_r = sum (-1)^r b_r makes the vertex-minus-edge-plus-face count an invariant (torus (1,2,1), sphere (1,0,1): verified p.137).

Homotopy: loops up to continuous deformation; pi_1 classifies them; pi_n abelian for n >= 2; universal covers compute the higher groups. The physics payoff is in 4.8-4.9, and it is the most programmatically interesting part of the book: ORDER PARAMETERS AND DEFECTS. Below the critical temperature the free energy F_0 = alpha|Psi|^2/2! + beta|Psi|^4/4! (with alpha ~ alpha_0(T - T_c) flipping sign) forces the order parameter into a nonzero value, and the gradient energy K|grad Psi|^2 makes spatial change costly (p.174-175, verified: superfluid 4He and the BCS spin-singlet pairing Psi = Delta_0 e^{i phi} i sigma_2). The order-parameter manifold M is then a space whose homotopy groups CLASSIFY THE DEFECTS the medium can carry: line defects (vortices) by pi_1(M), point defects by the higher pi_n; a defect's winding number is a topological charge, conserved because un-winding it would require the order parameter to pass through |Psi| = 0, which the free energy forbids. This is the book's cleanest example of stability that is not dynamical but structural: the defect cannot be smoothed away no matter how patient you are.

## Chapters 5-7: manifolds, de Rham cohomology, Riemannian geometry

Manifolds: charts and atlases, smooth structure, tangent spaces as derivations. De Rham cohomology: closed modulo exact r-forms, H^r_dR(M); the Poincare lemma (closed = exact on R^n) fails globally, and the failure is the cohomology. The de Rham theorem identifies the analytic object (form cohomology) with the combinatorial one (singular homology): two languages, one invariant. H^1_dR(S^1) = R is the prototype: the form d(theta) is closed but not exact, and its integral over the circle is 2 pi regardless of representative — the invariant IS the integral.

Riemannian layer: metric tensors, the Levi-Civita connection as the symmetric compatible choice, Killing vector fields (the flows that preserve the metric generate conserved quantities: the bridge to Noether already noted in Reid and Szendrői), non-coordinate bases and Cartan's structure equations, the Hodge star and the Laplacian, harmonic forms, Hodge decomposition and its link to de Rham cohomology: every cohomology class contains exactly one harmonic representative per metric. Physics: Einstein-Hilbert action, spinors in curved space, the Polyakov string action.

## Chapter 8: complex manifolds

Almost complex structure J (J^2 = -1), holomorphic maps, Dolbeault operators partial and partial-bar, Hermitian metrics, the Kaehler form, holonomy groups, Hodge numbers h^{p,q}; orbifolds as the singular limit relevant to string theory. The trading relevance here is effectively nil; the durable concept is that a complex structure is extra rigidity that splits forms into types and forces strong theorems (the h^{p,q} decomposition) that plain smoothness cannot.

## Chapters 9-11: fibre bundles, connections, characteristic classes — the gauge chapter, the heart of the book

Fibre bundles: locally trivial products glued by transition functions; tangent/cotangent bundles; principal G-bundles; associated bundles; pullbacks; triviality (verified example p.239: M = R^3, G = SO(3), point p = (0,0,1), isotropy group = rotations about the z-axis ~ SO(2) — the coset space R^3 = SO(3)/SO(2)). A gauge field IS a local connection form on a principal bundle; the global questions (can it be globally gauged away?) become topological questions about the bundle.

Connections: the connection one-form omega on P decomposes into local gauge potentials A; horizontal lift and parallel transport; CURVATURE F = dA + A^2 as the obstruction to path-independence, with the Ambrose-Singer theorem (holonomy algebra generated by curvature) and the Bianchi identity dF + A x F = 0 (the homogeneous Maxwell equations as a geometric identity). The physics examples are the payoff: the Dirac monopole as a nontrivial U(1) bundle over S^2 (charge quantization forced by topology: e g = n/2), the Aharonov-Bohm effect as a flat connection with nontrivial holonomy (verified p.423: the phase IS the holonomy of the connection; local field strength zero everywhere on the electron's path, global phase shift nonetheless), Yang-Mills theory, instantons as (anti-)self-dual curvatures minimizing the action, and Berry's phase: the adiabatic quantum phase is literally a connection on a bundle over parameter space, with observable curvature.

Characteristic classes (ch 11): invariant polynomials and the Chern-Weil homomorphism; Chern classes c_i and the Chern character ch = sum exp(i F/2pi); Chern-Simons forms (boundary terms whose descent matters in anomalies); Stiefel-Whitney classes via Cech cohomology (obstructions to spin structure). The unifying theorem shape:GLOBAL TOPOLOGICAL OBSTRUCTIONS ARE MEASURED BY LOCAL CURVATURE INTEGRALS. You learn the space by integrating its field strength.

## Chapter 12: index theorems

Elliptic complexes, Fredholm operators, the Atiyah-Singer index theorem: index(D) = dim ker D - dim coker D is a topological invariant computable as an integral of characteristic classes. Instances: de Rham (Euler characteristic), Dolbeault (Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch), signature, spin (Dirac) complexes. The heat-kernel route (spectral zeta-functions) and the Atiyah-Patodi-Singer extension (eta-invariant, spectral flow) for manifolds with boundary; the supersymmetric quantum mechanics proof: the index is Witten index of a SUSY system, the difference of bosonic and fermionic zero modes, invariant under continuous deformation of the superpotential. The theorem shape matters more than the proof: a quantity defined analytically (dimension of a kernel) is pinned by a quantity defined topologically (an integral), and neither can drift without the other moving. It is the archetype of a rigidity statement.

## Chapters 13-14: anomalies and bosonic strings

Anomalies: a classical symmetry broken by quantization. Fujikawa's method (verified in the text ch 13.2): the path-integral MEASURE fails to be invariant under the classical symmetry, and the Jacobian of the transformation is the anomaly. Non-Abelian anomalies, the Wess-Zumino consistency conditions, the BRS operator and Faddeev-Popov ghosts, the descent equations (an anomaly in m dimensions descends from an invariant polynomial in m+2 dimensions), the parity anomaly in odd dimensions and the dimensional ladder 4-3-2. The lesson general: quantization is itself an operation that can break symmetries the action respected; the anomaly is computable, topological, and not "fixed" but classified.

Bosonic strings (ch 14): differential geometry on Riemann surfaces, Polyakov action, moduli spaces, ghost determinants, one-loop amplitudes; the conformal anomaly as the string-theoretic instance of chapter 13 (critical dimension 26 from canceling the anomaly). For this programme this chapter is reference only.

## Durable lessons for the research programme

1. Topological charges are the model of stability. A winding number cannot change continuously; it changes by creation/annihilation events. The regime machinery already in the record (Hamilton, Maheu-McCurdy) has the same shape at the level of vocabulary — states persist until transition events — but I am honest: weekly market marks are NOT topological. There is no quantization, no conserved integer, no obstruction theorem in price data. What transfers is the discipline of asking "what would have to happen for this state to end," which decompression already measures (the dwell) rather than assumes.

2. The free-energy structure of regimes. Nakahara's F_0 + gradient energy is the cleanest formal picture of why regimes persist: the order parameter sits in a well, and changing it costs K|grad|^2; defects are exactly the places where the cost was paid. This is vocabulary for the measured persistence of regime states (Diebold-Inoue's rare-break switching), not new evidence, and no strategy follows.

3. Holonomy: the accumulated result depends on the path, not the endpoints. The Aharonov-Bohm phase (flat connection, nontrivial holonomy) is the cleanest statement of path dependence: locally nothing is detectable; the loop integral still is. Loose kinship, honestly labeled: price impact is path-dependent (the record's square-root and transient/permanent decomposition), and the record's own refusal of within-sample reshuffling on near-integrated series (circular-shift nulls preserving the path) is the same instinct — the path is information the summary destroys.

4. Local integrals measuring global invariants (Euler-Poincare, de Rham, Chern-Weil, index theorems). The archetype of bookkeeping that compiles to a countable object: every design statistic is a local computation, and the forward record is the invariant it must answer to; the programme's 57-of-104 statement is a count of exactly this kind, and the kill lines are its Euler characteristic done honestly (events counted, cells all reported, none selected). This is a structural analogy, not a theorem about markets.

5. Anomalies: measure breaking symmetry. A rule that looks right in the classical design can be broken by the estimation/booking process itself (multiplicity is the programme's Fujikawa Jacobian: the many-tests operation on data that looks innocent at the single-test level). The response is the same as the physics: compute the anomaly's form, pre-declare its descent (the validation spine), refuse to "fix" post hoc.

6. NOT transferable, stated plainly: nothing in this book is a market claim. There is no hidden scientific support here for wave counts, "phase transitions" in price charts, or any resonance metaphor. The programme's curated uses are the five points above, all at the level of discipline and vocabulary. The honest verdict matches the CFD book's: low and indirect, methodological only. No strategy changes follow.

## Cross-links

Reid and Szendrői (the direct predecessor: their Erlangen program, winding number, pi_1, quaternions/spin here reappear as tools WITH physics jobs: spin structures, Dirac operator, gauge groups); Hamilton and Diebold-Inoue (regimes as the measured stand-in for order parameters, rare breaks vs long memory the same phenomenon); Maheu-McCurdy (duration-dependent hazards; the dwell of a compressed market as a measured quasi-defect lifetime); Bouchaud (locally flat impact laws with global path-dependent consequences); Cont/Degond/Xuan (the simulator's stochastic flow on a state space, where matching axioms play the role Nakahara's bundles play for fields); White/Hansen/Bailey (the anomaly discipline: the measurement process itself breaking the nominal symmetry); the SMH record (design bookkeeping compiles to counts no more than Euler-Poincare tolerates bookkeeping errors).
