# Geometry and Topology (Reid & Szendrői) — Distilled Notes

## What this book is

A pure mathematics book, not a trading book. Its role in the quant stack is mathematical maturity: coordinates and change of coordinates, invariants, transformation groups, quotients, and the habit of asking "what is preserved when everything else changes". I keep the trading commentary in a separate section at the end, and I am honest there: the direct applications are limited; the value is in how this book trains structural thinking that the microstructure and stochastic-control books assume silently.

The organising philosophy: geometry is the study of properties invariant under a transformation group (Klein's Erlangen program). Every chapter picks a group, and the "geometry" is whatever survives that group's action. Topology is the limiting case: the group is all continuous deformations, and only the most robust properties survive.

## Chapter 1: Euclidean geometry

The metric on R^n is defined first; everything else is derived from distance. The triangle inequality is proved by a sum-of-squares identity (sum of (u_i v_j − u_j v_i)^2 ≥ 0). Lines and collinearity are determined by the metric alone. E^n is a metric space isometric to R^n but with no fixed origin: there is no "Greenwich Observatory without Divine Grace", meaning no point is distinguished until you choose a frame.

Motions are distance-preserving bijections. Key structural theorem: every motion is affine T(x) = Ax + b with A orthogonal. The normal form theorem for orthogonal matrices: block diagonal with ±1 blocks and 2×2 rotation blocks; complex eigenvalues come in conjugate pairs e^{±iθ} with isotropic eigenvectors (z·z = 0). Euclidean frames correspond one-to-one with motions, which is the bridge between geometry and group theory used everywhere later.

Classification of E² motions: translation, rotation, reflection, glide (the composite of a translation and a reflection in a parallel line). Classification of E³ motions: six types (translation, rotation, twist, reflection, glide, rotary reflection). Sample theorems done in coordinate-free style: pons asinorum, angle sum = π (equivalent to the parallel postulate), similar triangles, the four centres of a triangle (centroid, circumcentre, orthocentre with OH = 3OG, incentre), Feuerbach 9-point circle.

## Chapter 2: Composing maps

Composition is the basic operation on transformations. The affine composition rule: (A₂,b₂)∘(A₁,b₁) = (A₂A₁, b₂+A₂b₁). Two reflections compose to a rotation (through twice the angle) or a translation (twice the vector). Associativity of composition is a tautology: composition of maps is always associative, so transformation groups get associativity for free. Every motion of E^n decomposes into at most n+1 reflections (induction on the dimension of the fixed set). The alternative proof that three reflections in the plane compose to a glide previews transformation groups.

## Chapter 3: Spherical and hyperbolic geometry

S²: lines are great circles, spherical distance d(P,Q) = arccos(p·q). Main formula of spherical trigonometry: cos α = cos β cos γ + sin β sin γ cos a. Motions are O(3), the orthogonal matrices. Properties unlike E²: no parallel lines, bounded distances, intrinsic curvature, and angle sum = π + area, with an elegant Venn-diagram-style proof.

Hyperbolic plane H²: the upper sheet of the hyperboloid q_L = −t²+x²+y² = −1 in Lorentz space. Hyperbolic distance d = arccosh(−v·_L w). Main formula: cosh α = cosh β cosh γ − sinh β sinh γ cos a. Motions are the Lorentz group O⁺(1,2). Incidence of two hyperbolic lines comes in three cases (space-like: divergent with common perpendicular; time-like: intersecting; light-like: asymptotic/ultraparallel), versus the two Euclidean cases. The parallel postulate fails: through a point off a line L there is a continuum of non-intersecting lines, with a limiting angle θ = arccot y₀.

Angular defect: a+b+c = π − area, proved twice (explicit integral with area element r dr dθ/√(1+r²); and subdivision into ideal triangles, which are all congruent with area ≤ π, using additivity and monotonicity). The Poincaré upper half-plane model in exercises: H-lines are vertical half-lines plus semicircles orthogonal to the real axis; d_H = arccosh(1 + |z₁−z₂|²/(2 Im z₁ Im z₂)); motions are PSL(2,R), the Möbius fractional linear transformations.

The cos/cosh parallel is the durable structural lesson: the two trigonometries are the same subject with cos, sin swapped for cosh, sinh, and the sign difference in the main formula is exactly the sign of the curvature. Curvature is detectable locally by angle sums (excess = positive curvature, zero, defect = negative), proportional to area.

## Chapter 4: Affine geometry

A^n is a vector space with the origin forgotten: an affine subspace is E = P + U, a point plus a direction subspace. The dimension-of-intersection formula dim(E∩F) = dim E + dim F − dim⟨E,F⟩ holds with no exceptions (empty intersection ⇔ parallel hyperplanes). Affine transformations are x ↦ Ax + b; affine frames correspond to Aff(n). The centroid of points is affine-invariant but not Euclidean: ratios along a line and collinearity survive, but distances and angles do not. Affine geometry is what remains of Euclid when the metric is dropped but parallelism is kept.

## Chapter 5: Projective geometry

P^n is the set of lines through 0 in R^{n+1}; homogeneous coordinates write points as (x₀:…:x_n). Projective subspaces P(U) have dim P(U) = dim U − 1. The dimension-of-intersection formula now holds with literally no exceptions (dim ∅ = −1): two projective lines in a plane always meet. The group is PGL(n+1), acting 3-transitively on P¹. A projective frame needs n+2 points.

The cross-ratio {P,Q;R,S} = λ/μ is the single invariant of four collinear points: PGL(2) is exactly 3-transitive, so any four points can be sent to any four points with matching cross-ratio. Under the 24 permutations of four points the cross-ratio takes at most 6 values, with the Klein four-group as stabiliser. This is the prototype of "invariant" as a measurable quantity that captures exactly what the group cannot touch.

Perspectivities, then the two big incidence theorems: Desargues (two triangles in perspective from a point are in perspective from a line; proved by lifting the planar picture to P³ where the statement becomes trivial) and Pappus (proved by coordinates). The principle of duality: in P², points and lines are interchangeable, and the dual of Desargues is its converse. Axiomatic projective planes (4 self-dual axioms). Hilbert's construction is the algebra payoff: a projective plane satisfies Desargues iff its coordinate ring is associative, and Pappus iff it is commutative. Geometry determines algebra: incidence axioms reconstruct the number system.

## Chapter 6: Geometry and group theory

Formal setup: Trans X is the group of all transformations of a set; a transformation group is a subgroup. The Erlangen program: a geometry is the study of properties invariant under a transformation group G. The chain Eucl(n) ⊂ Aff(n) ⊂ PGL(n+1): as the group grows, fewer properties are invariant (distance, then parallelism, then only incidence and cross-ratio). Each geometry is a coarsening.

Conjugacy gTg⁻¹ is "T viewed from a different angle": Principle 6.4 says a conjugate has the same properties as the original with g applied to all the data. Normal forms (Jordan form, cycle decomposition of permutations, the reflection decompositions of Chapter 2) are exactly representatives of conjugacy classes: a normal form theorem is a classification-up-to-change-of-basis.

Generators: a group can be specified by generators and relations, and understanding a group often means understanding the graph of its generators (the 15-puzzle as a walk on A₁₅). Structure of Aff(n) as semidirect product GL(n) ⋉ R^n: translations form a normal subgroup, the linear part does not; the group law is (A₂,b₂)(A₁,b₁) = (A₂A₁, b₂+A₂b₁). Discrete reflection groups: the kaleidoscope (dihedral), the barber's shop (two reflections generating an infinite dihedral D₂∞), the Musée Grévin honeycomb.

## Chapter 7: Topology

Topology is the Erlangen program taken to the extreme: the group is Homeo(X), all continuous deformations, and the surviving invariants are connectedness, compactness, separation, and eventually the winding number. Topology axioms motivated by metric spaces; continuous maps characterised purely by open sets (the epsilon-delta of analysis becomes f⁻¹(open) is open). The homeomorphism problem is the classification task: when are two spaces the same up to continuous deformation with continuous inverse?

Core invariants: connected and path connected; compact (every open cover has a finite subcover; Heine-Borel: compact in R^n ⇔ closed and bounded; the continuous image of compact is compact). Subspace and quotient topologies, both defined by universal mapping properties: the quotient is the target of gluing. Glueing constructions: S¹, cylinder, Möbius strip, torus, surface with g handles. The Möbius strip is not the cylinder: boundary connectedness distinguishes them; and an interior point differs from a boundary point because the punctured disc D* is not homeomorphic to D, which is the content of the winding number. Topology of P^n = S^n/±: P² is a Möbius strip and a disc glued along their boundaries. Non-metric quotients exist: the "mousetrap topology", and the quadratic-form strata topology (six normal forms of bilinear forms with open sets reflecting degeneracy).

Basis for a topology: it suffices to specify sufficiently small neighbourhoods of each point; finite intersections of balls generate the metric topology; the profinite topology on a group has cosets of finite-index normal subgroups as a basis. Product topology: boxes U×V generate, and on finite products it agrees with any product metric (max, sum, Euclidean). Hausdorff property (T₂): points are separable by disjoint opens; metric spaces are automatically Hausdorff; X is Hausdorff iff the diagonal is closed in X×X. Compact versus closed: closed in compact is compact; compact in Hausdorff is closed; so in a compact Hausdorff space the two notions coincide. Closed maps: the projection [a,b]×R → R is closed, but R² → R is not (the hyperbola's image is R minus 0); compact-to-Hausdorff continuous maps are automatically closed.

Homeomorphism criterion: a continuous bijection from compact to Hausdorff is a homeomorphism. This is the workhorse used in Chapter 8, and it is the formal license for the following pattern: to show two spaces are the same, exhibit a continuous bijection and check compactness and Hausdorffness, with no need to prove the inverse is continuous by hand.

Loops and the winding number: the punctured disc D* is not homeomorphic to D because D is simply connected (every loop contracts) while a loop in D* has an integer winding number ν(φ) around the puncture, and it is contractible iff ν = 0. The winding number is defined by lifting the loop to a continuous argument function Θ(t) with Θ(1) = 2πn, constructed by covering the circle with overlapping sectors and using compactness of [0,1] to choose branches inductively. Theorem: the winding number is constant in a continuous family of loops (each level set is open; the interval is connected; done). Corollaries: D* ≄ D (finishing the boundary-point argument), and the fundamental theorem of algebra (if a complex polynomial never vanished, the loops t ↦ f(K e^{2πit}) would form a continuous family from a constant loop to a loop of winding number n; contradiction). First glimpse of the fundamental group: π₁(D*) = Z.

## Chapter 8: Quaternions, rotations, and the geometry of transformation groups

Topological groups: a group with a topology in which multiplication and inversion are continuous. All the geometric groups qualify: GL(n,R), O(n), Eucl(n), PGL, the Lorentz group O⁺(1,2).

Dimension counting: dim Eucl(n) = C(n+1,2), dim O(n) = C(n,2), dim GL(n) = n², dim PGL(n+1) = n(n+2). Counted by building frames: a Euclidean frame is a point (n parameters), then a point on a unit sphere (n−1), and so on down; the general lesson is that dimension of a group = number of continuous parameters, and frames-to-elements dictionaries turn geometry into parameter counting.

Compactness: O(n) is compact (closed and bounded inside R^{n²}, since orthogonal matrices have columns of norm 1, so the whole matrix lies in a ball of radius √n). Noncompact groups decompose: R* = R₊ × {±1}; C* = S¹ × R₊ (polar coordinates, the same decomposition that sits under every complex-number trick in analysis); and the substantial one: GL(n,R) = O(n) × T₊(n) as a space, via Gram-Schmidt. Every invertible matrix factors uniquely as orthogonal times upper-triangular-with-positive-diagonal, continuously. Many questions about GL(n) reduce to O(n) plus a flat factor. The dimension check: C(n,2) + C(n+1,2) = n².

Components: O(n) has two components distinguished by det = ±1, each path connected (rotate the blocks of the normal form continuously to the identity). SO(3) is the rotation group of E³ around a fixed point.

Quaternions: H = {a+bi+cj+dk} with i² = j² = k² = −1, ij = k and anticyclic variants. Associative but noncommutative division algebra (a "skew field"); conjugation q* is an antiinvolution ((pq)* = q*p*); |q|² = qq* is positive definite; every nonzero q has inverse q*/|q|². Each non-real quaternion generates a copy of C inside H. Two imaginary quaternions anticommute exactly when they are orthogonal as vectors in R³, which is the mechanism connecting quaternions to rotations.

Unit quaternions form S³. The map r_q: x ↦ qxq* fixes the real axis and rotates the imaginary subspace: r_q = Rot(I, 2θ) when q = cos θ + I sin θ. The homomorphism ψ: S³ → SO(3) is surjective with kernel ±1, so SO(3) = S³/±: the rotation group of three-space is a 3-sphere with antipodes glued. Similarly SO(4) = (S³ × S³)/± via x ↦ pxq*. This is the spinor cover: Spin(3) = S³, Spin(4) = S³ × S³. SU(2) = unit quaternions in matrix form: SU(2) = {[[a,b],[−b̄,ā]] : |a|²+|b|² = 1}, and the two-to-one map SU(2) → SO(3) is invisible from the matrices and obvious from the quaternions.

Electron spin (after Feynman): the Stern-Gerlach device measures spin along a direction; a spin state is a vector ψ in a 2-dimensional complex Hermitian space; measurement probabilities are |α|², |β|² in the eigenbasis of the device's Hermitian operator. Rotating the device corresponds to an element of SO(3), but the change of basis on spin states must be a representation, and there is no nontrivial homomorphism SO(3) → GL(2,C). The resolution is that the representation lives on SU(2), the double cover: rotation by 2π takes the wave function to its negative, and only a 4π rotation returns it to itself. Topologically, this is the statement that π₁(SO(3)) = Z/2: a loop in SO(3) (a full turn of the device) lifts to a path in S³ that ends at −1, and only traversing the loop twice closes it. The topology of the rotation group is physically observable.

Lie group preview: dimension (constant across the group), components (all homeomorphic; the identity component is a normal subgroup), maximal compact subgroup (a connected Lie group is compact-group × R^N with contractible loops), universal cover (the simply connected cover; S³ → SO(3) is the example), complexification and real forms (GL(n,C) complexifies GL(n,R); both O(n) and the Lorentz group are real forms of the same complex group, because over C the forms Σx_i² and −t²+Σx² are equivalent), and linear representations (homomorphisms to matrix groups; the eigenbasis-change matrices of spin are a representation of SU(2)).

## Chapter 9: Concluding remarks

History: Greek geometry's real contribution was rigour, argument from the ground up rather than bags of computational tricks. The parallel postulate stood out as unobvious ("to meet or not to meet" cannot be verified in principle because it speaks about the arbitrarily distant). Between 1810 and 1830, Bolyai, Gauss, Lobachevsky and Schweikart independently discovered that it is not a consequence of the other axioms: non-Euclidean geometry exists, as revolutionary as Copernicus or Darwin. Spherical geometry had been excluded because great circles fail the order/betweenness condition (and antipodal points), which Euclid never stated: his rigour was not watertight by modern standards. Coordinates (Descartes) and axioms interact in both directions: coordinate geometry gives models of axioms, and axiomatic geometry reconstructs number systems.

Group theory: historically groups were always transformation groups (Galois' permutations, polyhedral symmetries, crystallography, Lie groups); the abstract group concept (Cayley 1854) came later. A G-set is a group fulfilling its destiny as transformations. Homogeneous spaces: G acts transitively; Σ ≅ G/Stab(x). A torsor is a homogeneous space with trivial stabiliser: the set of frames is a torsor; affine space is a torsor under its translation group, which formalises "a vector space with no chosen origin". Eucl(n) = O(n) ⋉ R^n as a semidirect product, and the whole Erlangen program can be restated: the geometry is the space of conjugates of the stabiliser subgroup.

Geometry in physics: Newtonian spacetime E³×R has the 10-parameter Galilean group (rotations, boosts, translations, time shifts). Noether's principle: one conserved quantity per group parameter; momentum, angular momentum, centre of mass, energy are exactly the conserved quantities of the Galilean group's symmetries. Special relativity replaces the group with the Poincaré group preserving the Lorentz pseudometric ds² = −c²dt² + dx²; it is a semidirect product as before, and hyperbolic geometry sits inside relativity as the space-like hypersurface q_L = −1, which is where the time-like/space-like distinction of Chapter 3 comes from. Wigner's classification: an elementary particle is an irreducible unitary representation of the universal cover of the Poincaré group; the classification by rest mass m and spin s (half-integers allowed precisely because of the double cover) is the group-theoretic origin of the particle zoo; the Standard Model's internal symmetry U(1)×SU(2)×SU(3) extends the same logic.

The curvature trichotomy: spherical/Euclidean/hyperbolic = positive/zero/negative curvature, detectable locally (circle perimeter 2π sin R, 2πR, 2π sinh R; angle sum >, =, < π with excess/defect proportional to area) and globally (lines always meet, one parallel, many ultraparallel). For compact oriented surfaces the trichotomy is genus g = 0, 1, ≥ 2, or Euler number χ = 2, 0, < 0; all surfaces of genus ≥ 2 carry hyperbolic geometry, so the hyperbolic plane is "much bigger" than its competitors: it tiles infinitely many distinct compact surfaces. Shape and fate of the universe: whether spacetime is Euclidean, spherical or hyperbolic in the large, and whether it ends in a Big Crunch, unbounded expansion, or the exact boundary between them, are two trichotomies, possibly independent.

## Appendices

A (Metrics): the metric axioms; balls; isometries; examples of real-world non-metrics (air fares are asymmetric, city driving is one-way); a metric space can be isometric to a proper subset of itself (Hilbert's hotel); the four-point space with one distance 2 and the rest 1 embeds in a sphere but not in any Euclidean space (the bear-riddle).

B (Linear algebra): symmetric bilinear forms and quadratic forms determine each other (φ(x,y) = ½(q(x+y) − q(x) − q(y))); Euclidean form (matrix I) and Lorentz form (matrix J = diag(−1,1,…,1)); Gram-Schmidt builds orthonormal bases and splits R^n = W ⊕ W⊥; Lorentz analogues: a time-like vector has a space-like complement, and every vector with q_L = −1 extends to a Lorentz basis; a linear map preserves the quadratic form iff it preserves the bilinear form iff its matrix satisfies ᵀAKA = K, with det A = ±1 forced when the form is nondegenerate; orthogonal matrices = orthonormal columns; Lorentz normal form has hyperbolic rotation blocks (cosh, sinh) where the Euclidean form has circular blocks (cos, sin); Hermitian forms over C (conjugate-linear in the first argument), unitary matrices (ᵃAA = I), and the fact that unitary transformations are the building blocks of quantum mechanics.

## Durable lessons

1. A geometry is defined by its transformation group; the geometry's content is the list of invariants under that group. Enlarging the group (Euclidean → affine → projective → topological) coarsens the geometry: fewer quantities survive, but more spaces become equivalent.
2. Frames and group elements are interchangeable: Euclidean frames ↔ Eucl(n), projective frames ↔ PGL. This converts geometric statements into parameter counts: dim Eucl(n) = C(n+1,2), dim O(n) = C(n,2), dim PGL(n+1) = n(n+2).
3. Normal forms are conjugacy classes. A "classification up to change of basis" is exactly a set of representatives for conjugacy; Principle 6.4 (conjugate = same properties, data relabelled) is the license for every normal-form argument.
4. The cos/cosh parallel: spherical and hyperbolic trigonometry are one subject with a sign change, and the sign is the curvature. Curvature is locally measurable (angle sum vs area; circle perimeter), which is the model for "measurable curvature signatures" in any geometry-like problem.
5. The parallel postulate is not derivable from the others, and its failure has degrees: one parallel in Euclid, none on the sphere, a continuum with a limiting angle in H². Incidence of lines is a global curvature detector.
6. Projective geometry has no exceptions: every two lines meet; dimension-of-intersection needs no side cases; the cost is that distance and angle die, leaving the cross-ratio as the unique invariant of four collinear points.
7. Geometry reconstructs algebra: Desargues ⇔ associativity, Pappus ⇔ commutativity of the coordinate ring. Incidence axioms determine the number system you are allowed to use.
8. The homeomorphism criterion (continuous bijection, compact domain, Hausdorff codomain) is the standard tool for proving two quotient constructions give the same space. It is how SO(3) = S³/± is proved.
9. The winding number is an integer-valued, homotopy-invariant quantity: constant in any continuous family because its level sets are open and the parameter interval is connected. This discrete-value-from-continuous-setup pattern is the prototype of the fundamental group and of every topological invariant thereafter.
10. Compactness is the workhorse finiteness principle: finite subcovers (Heine-Borel, the lift construction for Θ), finite covers in the path-family proof, closed maps out of compact spaces, and uniform results (a continuous function on a compact space is bounded and attains its bounds).
11. Gram-Schmidt gives the polar-type decomposition GL(n) = O(n) × T₊(n): every noncompact group in this book splits as compact group times a flat contractible piece (C* = S¹ × R₊ is the n = 1 case). Reduction of questions to the compact factor is a standard simplification.
12. Quaternions: a noncommutative division algebra exists (dim 4), unit quaternions are S³, and SO(3) = S³/±. The 2π/4π behaviour of electron spin is the physical detection of the double cover: π₁(SO(3)) = Z/2 is an observable fact of nature.
13. Symmetry ⇔ conservation (Noether): each parameter of the spacetime symmetry group buys one conserved quantity; the particle classification (mass, spin) is the representation theory of the (cover of the) symmetry group. Group theory is not bookkeeping around physics; it is the physics.
14. The torsor concept formalises "space without chosen origin": affine space is a torsor under translations; frames form a torsor under the group. Choosing an origin/frame is extra structure, not given data, and results that do not depend on the choice are the well-defined ones.

## Connections to prior books

- Björk (Arbitrage Theory) and Elliott-Kopp (Mathematics of Financial Markets): both live in R^n state space and lean on linear algebra (bases, quadratic forms, orthogannormality) that this book axiomatises in Appendix B. The normal-form theorem for orthogonal and Lorentz matrices is the same change-of-basis machinery as diagonalising a covariance matrix or the Cholesky-type decompositions used in filtering. The Gram-Schmidt decomposition GL = O × T₊ is the matrix factorisation underlying least-squares and Kalman-filter derivations.
- Bartholomew-Biggs (Nonlinear Optimization): the mean-variance objective is a quadratic form, and Sylvester-type thinking (signature of a form, definite vs indefinite) is exactly Appendix B's Euclid-vs-Lorentz dichotomy. The optimisation book's trust-region and quasi-Newton subproblems are geometry of level surfaces; this book supplies the coordinate-free view of what is invariant under changes of basis. Normal forms as conjugacy classes is the same lesson as the optimiser's canonical form of a matrix (eigenvalue decomposition): classify up to admissible relabelling.
- Balan (Elliott Wave) and the wave framework: the geometry of channels, trendlines and ratios is affine/projective in spirit. Two claims from this book sharpen wave practice: (a) ratios along a line are affine invariants but not Euclidean ones, so Fibonacci-style retracement ratios are invariant under scaling of the chart, which is the mathematical justification for using ratios at all; (b) the cross-ratio is the genuinely projective invariant of four collinear points, strictly more robust than simple ratios under changes of viewpoint; a four-anchor swing measure built on cross-ratios is worth testing against naive retracement ratios. The failure of the parallel postulate is a regime metaphor: in a curved (trending/mean-reverting) market, "parallel channel lines" is a Euclidean assumption that the price process does not have to obey.
- Tsay (Analysis of Financial Time Series) and Levy (Computational Finance): every multivariate model there is a metric-space model; distance-based clustering, PCA (orthogonal projections, Gram-Schmidt again), and cointegration's common-trend decompositions are all computations inside the structures this book defines. Tsay's VAR/cointegration work in Cartea's pairs-trading chapters is linear algebra on quadratic forms; Appendix B is the clean statement of what those estimators assume about the form.
- Cartea-Jaimungal-Penalva (Algorithmic and HFT) and Johnson (Algorithmic Trading/DMA): their state spaces (inventory, prices, order flow) are affine spaces, and their control laws are expressed via dynamics that are invariant under changes of numeraire and scale in several places (log-price vs price, midprice vs microprice). The torsor lesson applies: results that depend on a chosen origin (a reference price, an arrival price) are conventions, while results invariant under the shift (implementation shortfall vs arrival price is defined relative to a choice) should be stated in invariant form.
- Harris, Stoll, Madhavan, Foucault, Hasbrouck, Biais-Glosten-Spatt: the microstructure books repeatedly use symmetry arguments informally (invariance of the limit order book under relabelling of traders, anonymity, the irrelevance of identity in the queue). This book is the formal background for that habit: invariance under a group is the precise meaning of "structure that survives relabelling".
- Bouchaud and the empirical books: the Hurst exponent, power-law tails, and scaling behaviour are statements about what is invariant under rescaling of time and size; the Erlangen habit (ask what the transformation group is, then list the invariants) is the correct frame for scaling analysis of returns.
- The Quants and Dark Pools (Patterson): the geometry of state spaces sits underneath every stochastic model the quants built; this book contributes the vocabulary for the symmetries that models silently assume.

## Strategy / research candidates (weekly NQ practice)

Honest framing first: this is a pure mathematics book. It contributes no market data, no empirical regularities, and no tradable anomalies. Its contribution is methodological discipline for the research stack, and a small number of concrete geometric ideas worth testing. Candidates, ordered by plausibility:

1. Invariance audit of every signal (cheap, do immediately). Before trusting any chart-derived measure, ask the Erlangen question: under what group is this quantity invariant? NQ log-returns are invariant under price-level shifts; ratios of swing sizes are invariant under uniform rescaling (affine along the price axis); neither is invariant under arbitrary monotone distortion (the group log-price charts implicitly quotient by). Any NQ signal that changes value when the chart is redrawn with different axis scaling is measuring the chart, not the market. This is a filter for indicator choice, not a signal itself.
2. Cross-ratio of four swing anchors (testable, low priority). For four collinear-in-time anchors on the weekly chart (e.g. two swing lows, two swing highs projected onto a trendline), the cross-ratio {P,Q;R,S} is invariant under projective transformations, strictly stronger invariance than simple ratios. Elliott Wave retracement analysis uses one-dimensional ratios (0.618, 0.5, 0.382); a cross-ratio-based measure of four-anchor configurations would be invariant under any rescaling of both axes, which simple retracement ratios are not (they are only affine-invariant along one axis). Test: compute cross-ratios of anchor quadruples at historical wave-completion points vs random quadruples, and check whether completions cluster at privileged cross-ratio values. Expectation: weak or null; the value is in running one well-designed test of a geometric hypothesis rather than in expecting success.
3. Curvature trichotomy as regime vocabulary (conceptual). Spherical (angle excess, lines converge) maps to strongly mean-reverting, range-bound regimes; Euclidean (flat, random-walk) maps to efficient driftless markets; hyperbolic (angle defect, exponential divergence of "parallel" channels, area growing exponentially) maps to strongly trending regimes with volatility expansion. The NQ weekly practice can borrow the book's local-detection principle: measure curvature signatures locally (deviation-from-flatness of a price channel, how quickly projected "parallel" lines diverge from price) rather than assuming a global geometry. This is a metaphor-level classification, useful for naming regimes and choosing between mean-reversion and trend-following logic, not a quantified signal yet.
4. Torsor discipline on reference prices (execution hygiene). An arrival price, a midprice, a microprice are all choices of origin; the book's torsor lesson says consequences of the choice are conventions, and only origin-invariant statements are well-defined. For the eventual paper-trading programme: define execution quality metrics in invariant form (relative to the realised path, not to an arbitrary reference), otherwise the evaluation is partly an artefact of the reference choice. This connects directly to Cartea's implementation-shortfall discussion.
5. Compactness/finiteness as a modelling habit (methodological). The book's repeated pattern (finite subcover → finite construction → integer invariant) is the template for any discretisation of the NQ research programme: a continuous space of strategies must be reduced to a finite, compact candidate set before statistical claims mean anything; out-of-sample validation on a non-compact hypothesis class is the overfitting failure mode. This is the deepest practical takeaway: constraint of the search space is not a limitation, it is what makes conclusions possible.

Net assessment: close the book with the reading queue complete. This file's role is the mathematical foundation layer: when the strategy-building phase starts, the Erlangen question (what group, what invariants) and the torsor question (what is convention vs structure) should be part of the checklist for every candidate signal, alongside the empirical and control-theoretic machinery from the market-studies books.

END OF SECTION 0018

---
book: "Market Liquidity: Theory, Evidence, and Policy"
author: "Thierry Foucault, Marco Pagano & Ailsa Röell (Oxford University Press, 2013)"
status: "COMPLETE — full text studied (OCR pages 1-359, print pp.xx-388; references from p.389 skipped)"

# Market Liquidity (Foucault, Pagano & Röell) — study notes

The theory-and-policy counterpart to Harris (practitioner) and Bouchaud (econophysics): a coherent model-based account of what liquidity is, how to measure it, what spreads and depth are made of, how market design (tick sizes, transparency, fragmentation, regulation) shapes it, how it is priced into asset returns, and how it feeds back into corporate finance. Central tensions repeated throughout: **price discovery vs liquidity** (informed trading makes prices informative but markets illiquid), **competition vs risk-sharing** in dealer supply, and **transparency vs opacity** (both have bright sides).

## The four dimensions of liquidity (framing)

Tightness (spread), depth (volume absorbable without price change), resilience (speed of recovery), and the implicit cost of immediacy. Liquidity is produced by intermediaries facing three costs: order-processing, inventory-carrying (risk), and adverse selection. Every spread model in the book is one of these three channels, alone or combined.

## Ch.1-2: Trading mechanics and measurement (durable toolkit)

- Market types: limit order book (discriminatory auction) vs dealer/OTC vs periodic call auction; hybrid markets; algorithmic trading and its effects.
- **Case boxes**: Kerviel/SocGen Jan 2008 — covert unwinding of €40-50bn (~10% of daily volume) moved markets as a fire sale; Flash Crash May 6 2010 — a 75k E-mini sell order executed via a constant-participation algo (price-insensitive) drained book depth to ~20%, Accenture printed at $0.01, and the crash propagated cross-market because AT reacts to noisy price signals across related assets. Lesson: liquidity evaporation, not information, is what turns a large order into a crash.
- **Spread measures**: quoted, effective (vs execution price vs mid at decision time), realized (future mid − current price). Effective ≤ quoted in normal conditions; a spread narrower than half the effective spread signals trades inside quotes (internalization).
- **Lee-Ready rule**: classify trades as buy/sell by comparing price to the prevailing quote with the *earlier* quote (or 5-second lag) to avoid endogeneity; tick test as fallback.
- **Price-based liquidity proxies** (no quote data needed): Amihud ratio |return|/$ volume (avg 0.041% for small caps, ~0.003% large caps, NYSE 1993-2006); Roll's measure implied spread = 2√(−cov(Δp)) from bid-ask bounce; bias corrections: with adverse selection, cov = −γ(λ+γ) so the Roll spread 2√(γ(λ+γ)) UNDERSTATES the true spread 2(λ+γ) (informed trading attenuates the bounce, same direction); order-processing fraction of the spread ≈ (S_Roll/S)². Amihud is the workhorse for Andrea's weekly NQ work (no LOB data needed).
- **VWAP** and **implementation shortfall** (Perold): the true cost of trading = benchmark price at decision minus final execution price, including delay, spread, impact, fees. Kenny the institution box: real-world all-in costs are several multiples of the visible spread.

## Ch.3: Order flow and price dynamics (adverse selection core)

- EMH as martingale: E[p_{t+1}|p_t] = p_t; market efficiency vs liquidity are distinct: a price can be a martingale while trading is costly.
- **Glosten-Milgrom**: with informed fraction π and value gap (v^H − v^L), the spread S = π(v^H − v^L); quotes are Bayesian posterior means. Higher informed share π: prices more informative (faster discovery) but wider spread. **The tradeoff: liquidity vs efficiency**.
- Price discovery speed is proportional to the fraction of informed traders; the marginal price impact of a trade measures its information content.
- **Inventory channel (Ho-Thomas-Stoll lineage)**: risk-averse dealers widen the spread by a risk premium ≈ 2ρσ_ε (per-unit risk aversion × innovation risk); midprice tracks m_t = μ_t − ρσ_ε z_t so that price pressure from a large trade decays back (transitory component); expected inventory-holding cost = ρσ_ε². Small-cap permanent impact ~1.01 bps per $1k vs 0.02 bps large caps (Hendershott-Menkveld).
- **Full picture decomposition**: price change = permanent information shift + transitory bid-ask bounce + inventory pressure decay. This trichotomy (information, processing, inventory) is the book's analytical spine and maps onto Huang-Stoll below.

## Ch.4: Trade size and depth (Kyle)

- **Kyle 1985 equilibrium**: p = μ + λq, with λ = σ_v/(2σ_u) in the Gaussian benchmark (σ_v = value uncertainty, σ_u = noise-trade volume scale); informed aggressiveness β = σ_u/σ_v; informed expected profit = ½σ_uσ_v (he earns the same regardless of σ_u: more noise trading both hides him and deepens the book). Half of the prior value uncertainty σ_v is resolved by informed trading in one auction.
- λ (Kyle's lambda) = the market's depth in price units: the *only* summary of liquidity that matters to a strategic trader. Everything that reduces informed profit (competition, fragmentation of informed orders) lowers λ... but beware chapter 7's fragmentation reversal.
- **Imperfect dealer competition**: with K dealers, λ = α(K−1)/(K−2), need K ≥ 3: strategic dealers keep spreads above competitive even with entry. With inventory risk and a call market, λ = ρσ_v² (competitive) or (K−1)/(K−2)·ρσ_v² (strategic): more dealers deepen the market through *both* risk-sharing and competition, two separate channels.

## Ch.5: Econometrics of trading costs

- **Price impact regression**: Δp_t = λd_t + γΔd_t + ε_t; λ captures adverse selection (permanent), γ order-processing (transitory); Roll covariance cov = −γ(λ+γ).
- **Huang-Stoll three-way decomposition**: after correcting for the probability of informed trade φ, order-processing dominates (~62%), inventory ~28.65%, adverse selection ~9.59% for the typical stock; φ estimated negative in naive specifications (correction matters). *Do not use the uncorrected decomposition on data where order types are unknown.*
- Intraday spread U-shape: adverse-selection component high at the open (overnight information), processing component high at the close (crowding at the close, MOC imbalances).
- **Hasbrouck 1991 VAR**: impulse response of prices to order flow; permanent component = information content of the trade α_T(q). Trade-by-trade informativeness measurable with a simple vector autoregression.
- **PIN** = αε_i/(ε_b + ε_s + αε_i): probability a trade comes from an informed trader. Median PIN ~19% (Easley-Hvidkjaer-O'Hara); higher in small, anonymous, low-analyst-coverage stocks. PIN is the standard control for adverse selection in event studies; Duarte-Young show its liquidity-priced component vs information component behave differently.
- **Low-frequency proxy toolkit** (no quote data needed): zero-return days (Lesmond-Ogden-Trzcinka LOT), effective tick from price clustering (Holden), and Hasbrouck's 2002 Gibbs sampler (Bayesian Roll with latent bid/ask/direction); all validated against TAQ effective spreads (Goyenko-Holden-Trzcinka), so daily OHLCV data suffices for credible illiquidity estimates — directly usable for weekly NQ research.

## Ch.6: Limit order book markets

- Discriminatory vs uniform auctions; in an LOB each limit order is executed at its own price (discriminatory), the opposite of the call auction.
- **Limit order economics (zero-profit condition)**: P(Y_k)(A_k − μ) = C. A limit order is a free option (Copeland-Galai): it earns the spread if executed, is picked off if the price moves through it. Execution probability P(Y_k) = 1 − F(Y_k) falls with adverse selection λ and with the monitoring cost C.
- **Glosten 1994**: even with a zero tick and competitive liquidity supply, a nonzero small-order spread must survive (the "market touch" = 2λ/θ): market orders cannot be free because they carry information. Book depth shrinks with informativeness λ; the best quotes are placed by the most optimistic quotesetters (winner's curse).
- **Tick size**: a rent to inframarginal queue positions ∝ Δ; tick reductions narrow spreads for retail-size orders while cumulative depth at old round prices can stay the same (US decimalization experience). Pro-rata allocation generates more depth than pure time priority at the same tick.
- **Make-or-take (maker-taker) pricing**: maker rebates vs taker fees; spread decreasing in execution probability τ of makers; volatility effect is non-monotone: spread U-shaped in σ with threshold σ̂ = 4L/(4+τ) (L = expected loss from being picked off).
- **Order aggressiveness and book state (Biais-Hillion-Spatt, Ranaldo)**: execution probability decreases with own-side depth, increases with opposite-side depth; trades are positively serially correlated endogenously (queue dynamics), no exogenous herding needed.
- Hybrid markets: specialist's last-mover advantage (flash orders, SOES bandits) widens spreads; algorithmic trading and monitoring lowered spreads in the 2000s (Hendershott-Jones-Menkveld: algo use improves liquidity most where discretion was previously exploited).

## Ch.7: Fragmentation

- US 2009 landscape: NYSE 14.7%, Nasdaq 19.4%, internalizers 17.5%, dark pools 7.9%: more than half of volume off the primary exchanges.
- **Informed trading across markets**: fragmented λ_j = σ_v/2σ_j exceeds consolidated λ; informed traders gain, liquidity traders lose from fragmentation of informed flow, but with two markets price discovery is *better*: var(v|p_A,p_B) = σ_v²/3 < σ_v²/2 (two partially revealing prices beat one). Fragmentation hurts liquidity but can help efficiency.
- **Risk-sharing**: consolidation merges risk-bearing capacity: impact falls from ρ̄σ_v²/K_A to ρ̄σ_v²/(K_A+K_B) even with the same number of dealers per market.
- **Tipping and liquidity externalities (network effects)**: critical mass γ^c = 4C/(Δ+2C); once one venue loses critical mass trading tips away entirely (Matif→Eurex). Low-liquidity traps are an entry barrier; chicken-and-egg problems for new venues (Tradepoint).
- **Internalization/cream-skimming**: retail-flow internalizers trade uninformed flow away from the lit market, raising the informed proportion remaining there; order flow is a resource worth paying for (payment for order flow, agency problems, price manipulation).
- **Benefits of fragmentation**: competition in fees (Euronext fee war), venue proliferation, and queue-jumping across markets: with two LOBs the *consolidated* depth can exceed one venue's (Foucault-Menkveld EuroSETS evidence). The fragmented-order externality can outweigh the fragmentation-of-informed-flow externality.
- **Regulation**: RegNMS (order protection = no trade-through of automated quotes, access fee cap, sub-penny rule, market data rules; protected quotations only automated ones) vs MiFID (no concentration rule, RMs/MTFs/SIs, multi-dimensional best execution, no trade-through ban, fragmented clearing). Design lesson: trade-through rules protect the lit consolidated tape but entrench incumbent prices; pre-trade transparency mandates can tip liquidity.

## Ch.8: Transparency

- Pre-trade opacity as Diamond monopoly: opaque quotes allow spread 2τ (full monopoly spread) vs competitive zero; muni-bond retail evidence: identical bonds traded at widely dispersed prices when dealers quote opaquely.
- Post-trade transparency: opaque dealer markets double uninformed trading costs (TC^O ≈ 2×TC^T): with transparent prints the first dealer's informational rents vanish; opaque markets allow dealers to condition on their own past trades. First-trade information can even produce negative first-period spreads (crossed quotes as an investment in information).
- **Opacity has a bright side**: hidden/iceberg orders carry >45% of CAC40 depth; anonymity of limit order placers narrowed Euronext spreads (2001); pre-trade anonymity protects liquidity providers from sharks. Sunshine trading: pre-announced uninformed flow trades at (near) zero spread; revealing motives lets recognized uninformed traders escape the pooled spread (κ_U raises the pooled spread slightly but lowers the average cost of uninformed trading).
- **Opacity survives competition**: TRACE post-trade reporting halved muni transaction costs despite dealers lobbying against it; two-tier markets (opaque wholesale, transparent retail) persist because opacity is a rent, not a technical necessity. Late reporting windows are used to evade transparency.
- **Market transparency (this chapter) vs fundamental transparency (Ch.10)**: two distinct objects, both reduce adverse selection, and are substitutes: an issuer can compensate for opaque trading by more corporate disclosure (or vice versa).

## Ch.9: Liquidity and asset prices (pricing layer)

- **Amihud-Mendelson clientele model**: R ≈ r + s/h (spread s amortized over holding period h); longer-horizon investors hold high-spread assets: a *concave* return-spread relation. Evidence: T-notes vs bills ~43 bps yield premium for illiquid issues; AM 1986: 1pp spread → 0.211%/month extra return, implied holding period ~5 months; on/off-the-run Treasuries premium; small-firm effect partly a liquidity premium.
- **Asymmetric information premium**: R ≈ r + 2πσ (Garleanu-Pedersen style): informed share π priced as a level; PIN priced in returns (Easley-Hvidkjaer-O'Hara), though Duarte-Young show PIN's significance weakens with an Amihud control: information vs illiquidity components price differently.
- **OTC search frictions (Duffie-Gârleanu-Pedersen)**: ask = 1/r minus an illiquidity discount ∝ dealers' bargaining power z and search cost c/φ: explains the persistent wide OTC bid-ask structures even without asymmetric information. Bid-ask spreads can be huge without any informed trading (bond markets).
- **Liquidity-adjusted CAPM (Acharya-Pedersen)**: four betas; beyond β1 (market), β2 (return covariance with *market illiquidity*, + priced), β3 (return covariance with market illiquidity *changes*... sign conventions: β3 negative premium = covariance of stock illiquidity with market returns), β4 (covariance of stock illiquidity with market return). Liquidity risk ~1.1%/yr between liquid and illiquid deciles; β4 (flight-to-liquidity covariances) dominates. **Liquidity commonality is priced: a stock that becomes illiquid exactly when the market falls must pay a higher return.**
- **Limits to arbitrage**: performance-based arbitrage (Shleifer-Vishny: arbitrageurs' capital is scarcest when mispricing is widest → do nothing is often optimal), margin requirements (150% of short proceeds as collateral), LTCM box. Intervene-now vs wait threshold φ̂ = (M0 − κM1)/κM1.
- **Limited speculative capital and crises**: fire sales, self-reinforcing mispricing equilibrium; crisis-state mispricing M1^crisis = (½φ̂*² + 1)/δ, small δ (investor discount) → crash. **Funding liquidity and market liquidity reinforce each other** (Brunnermeier-Pedersen): losses → tighter funding → less market liquidity → more losses.
- **Noise trader risk and crashes**: correlated positive-feedback trading (De Long et al.), Gennotte-Leland: dynamic hedgers create a reverse-S (downward-sloping in stress) demand curve → price gaps, crashes without news. Correlated order flow, not information, drives most crashes.

## Ch.10: Liquidity, price discovery, and corporate policies

- **Liquidity → growth**: Levine-Zervos turnover/growth, market liberalizations raise growth ~1%/yr (Bekaert-Harvey-Lundblad), Fang-Noe-Tice tick-size shocks raise firm value; Ellul-Pagano: pre-IPO liquidity (spreads 4.5%) predicts aftermarket spreads (2%): IPO underpricing ~47.7% partly compensation for expected aftermarket illiquidity.
- **Governance: exit vs voice**: liquid markets enable exit (Hirschman), but exit is a public good: Grossman-Hart free-rider problem (nobody monitors if gains accrue to all free-riding holders); lock-in effect: illiquidity locks a blockholder in and can raise intervention probability π = min{1 − (1/G)(C/φ − S1), 1}; activism certain if S1 ≥ C/φ. Liquidity is double-edged for governance: easy exit discourages voice, but Edmans-Fang-Zur: blockholders in liquid stocks file 13G (passive) vs 13D (active) depending on their expected trading path; hedge fund activism ~7% abnormal return (Brav et al.). Coffee/Bhide: liquidity vs control tradeoff. Block accumulation is the mirror image: illiquidity at date 0 discourages block formation (double-edged sword), and an anticipated blockholder loses −φS0 − min(C, φS1) to free-riding (Grossman-Hart) unless he can build a toehold camouflaged behind noise trading (Kyle-Vila) — hence the US rule design: opacity below the 5% disclosure threshold, full transparency above it.
- **Prices guiding investment**: Chen-Goldstein-Jiang: investment responds to price more where PIN is higher; the book's model: manager invests on a stock market buy signal only if π ≥ (I−G)/(I+G) (informativeness threshold); the stock market adds V_public − V_private = informational gain − loss from noise; higher informed share π raises firm value *and* the spread S = πG − (1−π)(1−γ)(I−G)/2: **the price-discovery-vs-liquidity tradeoff shows up inside the firm's own investment decision**. Reversed case (G > I): the market acts as a brake on bad investment. Luo 2005: merger cancellations follow negative stock reactions; HP's double U-turn (Box 10.1).
- **Executive compensation (Holmstrom-Tirole)**: stock-based pay beats earnings-based pay when the interim stock price is a more precise effort signal: earnings-based w^H = c/Δθ leaves the manager a rent (limited liability); stock-based w̄ = c elicits effort with no rent; value gain = cθ_/Δθ. But informativeness requires informed trading → illiquidity discount (Ch.9): firms trade off managerial discipline against liquidity premium; stock/option pay correlated with accounting fraud proxies (Burns-Kedia, Peng-Röell) and blamed for bank risk-taking in 2007-09.
- **Firms managing their own liquidity**: (i) DMMs: only relatively illiquid firms hire them; DMM announcements earn abnormal returns 1% (Oslo) to 3.5% (Amsterdam), 5% (Paris), 7% (Stockholm); DMM hires cut liquidity betas (Menkveld-Wang) and predict future equity issues (Skjeltorp-Ødegaard: high Tobin's Q firms hire DMMs, then issue shares). (ii) Disclosure: transparency → narrower spreads, lower cost of capital (Lang-Lins-Maffett); costs: dissemination costs, information leaks to competitors (Yosha: confidential R&D firms stay private), tax transparency, and information-processing asymmetry: coarse disclosure can be optimal for complex securities (Pagano-Volpin ABS: opacity at issuance can hide adverse selection until it resurfaces in secondary trading); blind auctions cut trading costs 48% (Kavajecz-Keim); agency MBS TBA market: extremely opaque, extremely liquid. (iii) Capital structure: leverage makes equity information-sensitive: r_E = ((E+D)/E)r − (D/E)r_D; safe debt is information-insensitive and liquid (Gorton-Pennacchi); high leverage concentrates informed trading on equity, raising price discovery and the illiquidity premium; firms balance the two (Chang-Yu: low value of market information → low debt, a candidate solution to the low-leverage puzzle).

## Durable rules & ratios (quick reference)

- Spread components: S = order-processing + inventory (≈2ρσ_ε or ρσ_ε² per trade) + adverse selection (π(v^H−v^L) in GM; λq in Kyle).
- Kyle: p = μ + λq; λ = σ_v/2σ_u (competitive); informed profit = ½σ_uσ_v; half of uncertainty resolved per informed auction round.
- Strategic dealers: λ = α(K−1)/(K−2), K ≥ 3; call-market inventory: ρσ_v² competitive, (K−1)/(K−2)·ρσ_v² strategic.
- Roll implied spread = 2√(−cov(Δp)); bias: cov = −γ(λ+γ); processing share ≈ (S_Roll/S)². Amihud: |r|/$vol.
- Three-way decomposition (Huang-Stoll, corrected): ~62% processing / ~29% inventory / ~10% adverse selection for typical stocks; φ must be corrected or goes negative.
- PIN = αε_i/(ε_b+ε_s+αε_i); median ~19%, higher in small/anonymous/no-coverage stocks.
- Glosten 1994 market touch: small-order spread = 2λ/θ > 0 even at zero tick; make-or-take volatility threshold σ̂ = 4L/(4+τ).
- Fragmentation: two markets → var(v|p_A,p_B) = σ_v²/3 vs σ_v²/2; tipping threshold γ^c = 4C/(Δ+2C).
- Opaque dealer market ≈ 2× transparent uninformed cost; muni TRACE halved costs; hidden orders >45% of CAC40 depth.
- Amihud-Mendelson: R ≈ r + s/h, concave in s; 1pp spread ≈ 0.211%/month; T-note vs bill premium ≈ 43 bps.
- Liquidity CAPM: β2 > 0, β3 < 0, β4 < 0 premia; liquidity risk ≈ 1.1%/yr liquid vs illiquid deciles; β4 dominates.
- Crisis mispricing: M1^crisis = (½φ̂*²+1)/δ, small δ → crash; funding liquidity ⇄ market liquidity spirals (Brunnermeier-Pedersen).
- Investment-guidance threshold: π ≥ (I−G)/(I+G); governance intervention: π = min{1 − (1/G)(C/φ − S1), 1}, certain if S1 ≥ C/φ.
- DMM announcement abnormal returns: 1% (Oslo), 3.5% (Amsterdam), 5% (Paris), 7% (Stockholm); hired by illiquid, high-Q, soon-to-issue firms.
- Leverage: r_E = ((E+D)/E)r − (D/E)r_D; debt = information-insensitive = liquid (Gorton-Pennacchi).

## Connections to the Elliott Wave base

- **Informed trading and wave-degree transitions**: Glosten-Milgrom/Kyle predict that precisely when new fundamental information arrives (news shocks, regime changes), spreads widen and price impact λ jumps. In Elliott terms, wave terminations and degree changes are the highest-uncertainty, highest-π moments: expect the widest spreads and the most violent price impact exactly where a wave count is about to change. Wide-spread, high-impact, high-volume weeks in the NQ are structural signals, not noise.
- **Noise traders and crashes (Ch.9) give the microfoundation for Elliott's "herd" psychology**: Gennotte-Leland reverse-S demand and De Long positive-feedback traders explain how wave-C capitulations and fifth-wave extensions overshoot: correlated order flow, not new information, generates the largest moves. A selling climax (Williams) is the practitioner's name for a correlated-noise-flow liquidation meeting an inventory-risk-premium demand curve.
- **Liquidity spirals as the crash mechanism**: funding-liquidity/market-liquidity loops and the crisis-state mispricing formula M1^crisis = (½φ̂*²+1)/δ formalize why wave-3-of-3-style accelerations occur: losses tighten speculator capital exactly when margins are needed, so mispricing feeds itself. This is the structural reason bear-market waves are steeper (Williams' "bear markets fall faster") and why third waves are the most profitable and most dangerous.
- **Acharya-Pedersen betas as a regime filter**: β4 (a stock's illiquidity when the market falls) is the dominant priced liquidity risk. For position sizing: reduce exposure to instruments whose liquidity evaporates in down markets (small caps, wide-spread futures rolls) when the weekly wave count is in a corrective or terminating phase; favor them in confirmed impulsive phases.
- **Roll/Amihud measurements for Andrea's NQ weekly research**: both are computable from price/volume series alone; a rising Amihud ratio on weekly data flags drying liquidity, which historically precedes trend fragility; the Roll bounce component separates microstructure noise from the wave count's permanent path (Bouchaud link).
- **Pin-of-the-market (Ch.10) and wave-count feedback**: managers learn from prices, prices guide investment, liquidity begets growth: at macro degree, Elliott wave degrees in indices interact with capital-formation cycles; the Chen-Goldstein-Jiang evidence (investment follows prices where PIN is high) is a mechanism by which crowd psychology at large degree feeds back into fundamentals, Elliott's own claim that wave patterns reflect real economic rhythm.
- **Limits to arbitrage justify why mispricings persist between wave counts**: performance-based arbitrage (do-nothing threshold φ̂ = (M0−κM1)/κM1) means a trader waiting for a wave-5 termination should not expect instant correction: arbitrage capital is scarcest at the extremes, exactly when the count says the turn is near. Patience is structurally rational.
- **Transparency's bright side and the "hidden hand" of Williams**: Williams' professionals operating on hidden information (up-thrusts, traps) map to the book's cream-skimming and sunlit vs opaque tiers; anonymity narrows spreads, opacity preserves rents: the practitioner's "they hide volume, not price" is exactly the post-trade opacity mechanism.

## Forward links for the strategy end-goal

- Strategy filter candidate 1: weekly Amihud ratio and Roll spread as liquidity regime indicators layered on the Elliott count; cut size when liquidity deteriorates into a potential wave termination.
- Strategy filter candidate 2: Acharya-Pedersen-style liquidity-beta screen for instrument choice (β4 dominance) once the count signals corrective phases.
- Crash insurance rule: recognize liquidity-spiral signatures (simultaneous funding stress, margin rises, flight to on-the-run Treasuries) as the mechanism by which wave-C/third-wave accelerations occur; these periods argue for flat positions rather than counter-trend heroics.
- Liquidity provision premium candidate: contrarians act as low-frequency market makers and are compensated when everyone else demands immediacy (Khandani-Lo contrarian profits); a mechanical weekly rule that adds a measured increment of exposure after crash bars exhibiting fast transitory-impact signatures (spike + immediate partial retrace, VSA-style "stopping volume") is a candidate harvest of this premium on index futures.
- Announcement-window discipline: spreads widen and informed flow concentrates before announcements (Ch.3/Ch.5 intraday PIN patterns), so event bars (FOMC, CPI, NFP weeks on the NQ weekly chart) carry transitory stop-run extremes; prefer entry/exit confirmation on the bar(s) following the event bar rather than trading its extremes.
- Execution rules from Ch.5-6: benchmark fills against decision-time mid (implementation shortfall), expect effective < quoted in liquid names, monitor own-side LOB depth for entry aggressiveness (Biais-Hillion-Spatt: fill probability rises with opposite-side depth).
- Cross-check with Williams (VSA): VSA's "effort vs results" maps to the permanent/transitory impact decomposition; absorption volume at a wave-4 low = inventory-risk premium demand absorbing a noise-flow liquidation, consistent with Kyle's λ dropping once informed flow dries up.
- Cross-check with Bouchaud: Kyle's λ is the linear ancestor of the square-root law; Foucault's fragmentation math (two venues beat one for discovery) parallels Bouchaud's latent-liquidity argument that impact reflects the liquidity really available, not the visible book.

# Trading and Exchanges notes — Larry Harris, "Trading and Exchanges: Market Microstructure for Practitioners" (2002 draft)

Source: uploaded draft copy ("1. Trading-Exchanges-Market-Microstructure-Practitioners Draft Copy.pdf", March 2002 draft, OUP). Studied November 2026. This is the microstructure layer of the knowledge base, built on the Elliott Wave foundation.

## The five market-quality characteristics (the book's framing)

1. **Liquidity** — ability to trade large size quickly at low cost when you want to trade.
2. **Transaction costs** — market frictions; high costs slow or stop trading activity.
3. **Informative prices** — prices near fundamental values; essential for resource allocation.
4. **Volatility** — fundamental vs. transitory components (below).
5. **Trading profits** — trading is a zero-sum game relative to the market average; informed and clever traders win from poorly informed and foolish traders (investors, borrowers, hedgers, asset exchangers, gamblers supply the losses).

Key recurrent themes: information asymmetries; trading options (limit orders are free options to the market); the order flow externality (offers of liquidity give uncompensated trading options, attracting and binding traders to markets); market structure as determinant of power between informed/uninformed, public/professional, large/small; free entry and exit eroding excess profits of any strategy; communications/computing technology changing how people trade; price correlations and competition among markets; principal-agent problems (brokers); trustworthiness.

## Trading as a bilateral search

- Buyers search for sellers and vice versa; liquidity is the object of that search. Search economics: continue searching while expected benefit of another inquiry > its cost; impatient traders pay for immediacy, patient traders get better prices.
- Exchanges are search engines: they collect and organize information about who wants to trade, lowering search costs; network externality attracts traders to where other traders already are.
- Trading is a search problem at every level: even block trading is matching buyers to sellers (a "concentration" game where brokers' productivity grows superlinearly with client count).

## Orders (Ch. 4)

- Orders are trade instructions (instrument, size, side, conditions). Order submission strategy is the most important determinant of trading success.
- Vocabulary: bids/offers, BBO/NBBO, bid/ask spread ("inside spread", "the touch", vigorish in betting), firm vs. soft prices.
- Limit orders and standing quotes **offer (supply) liquidity**; market orders **take (demand) liquidity**. Immediacy is a service being bought and sold.
- Order properties matter: validity window, all-or-nothing, display, exposure, who you allow as counterparty. Traders who can react fastest to changing conditions have the advantage; carefully written contingencies substitute for constant monitoring.

## Market structures (Ch. 5-6, 23-27)

- Market structure = rules + information systems; it determines what traders can do and know, hence strategies and profits.
- Continuous vs. call markets; order-driven (auction, price priority, time precedence, minimum increment) vs. quote-driven (dealer) markets; floor (open outcry) vs. screen-based systems.
- **Minimum price increment (tick)** is a critical determinant of market quality: it sets the cost of gaining precedence, drives the profitability of order anticipators and quote matchers, and balances the supply of limit orders against free-option exposure.
- Fungible instruments concentrate trading; one market among closely related markets tends to dominate via the order flow externality; arbitrage, preferencing and internalization connect/fragment markets.
- Index markets: price-weighted vs. value-weighted index math (divisors, splits, additions/deletions); index funds replicate by owning proportional shares; index products are now more liquid than underlying cash markets, and index price changes lead cash markets ("tail that wags the dog").
- Specialist systems (NYSE): designated primary market makers with affirmative obligations (meaningful two-sided quotes, traders of last resort) and negative obligations, compensated by unique privileges (order book visibility, dealer/broker/official triple role); concentration among a few specialist firms. Designated multiple market maker systems (e.g., CBOE index options) assign liquidity obligations in rotation.
- Internalization (dealers fill client orders), preferencing (brokers route marketable orders for payment for order flow), and internal crossing: benefit small market-order traders, harm limit-order traders, decrease transparency; best execution (NBBO guarantees) is the standard defense against the conflicts.

## Speculators (Ch. 10-12)

- **Informed traders**: estimate fundamental values (expected PV of all future benefits/costs) and trade on mispricing; include value traders, news traders, information-oriented technical traders, arbitrageurs. Their trading pushes price toward fundamental value; markets aggregate their diverse estimates into prices more informative than any single estimate. Fundamental-value changes are unpredictable (random walk); only unexpected events move informative prices. Fischer Black: prices are "informative" anywhere between half and twice fundamental value. Informed traders can still lose short-term when price moves away from value, but profit long-run if estimates are right.
- **Order anticipators** (parasitic traders): front runners (trade ahead of orders they've learned of, legally or illegally; steal the price discrimination benefits of large traders who split orders), sentiment-oriented technical traders (predict uninformed traders' future actions), squeezers (corner traders who must trade). They make prices less informative and more volatile, and add no liquidity.
- **Bluffers/manipulators**: rumormongers and price manipulators (painting the tape, wash trades) fool uninformed traders into thinking they are well-informed. Profitable when buying and selling price impacts are not symmetric.

## Liquidity suppliers (Ch. 13-18)

- **Dealers** buy low/sell high, selling immediacy. Realized spreads are usually smaller than quoted spreads. Core problems: setting quotes, attracting order flow, inventory management, avoiding better-informed traders. Dealers are passive traders who must be disciplined; in competitive markets they must speculate (position trading) since pure spread trading no longer pays.
- **Bid/ask spread components** (Ch. 14): order processing costs, inventory costs (carrying/risk of unbalanced positions), and the **adverse selection component** — the part that compensates dealers for losses to informed traders, earned back from uninformed traders. The accounting and information perspectives give the same size.
- **Block traders** (Ch. 15): find latent liquidity for large trades, play "concentration" matching buyers to sellers, manage the order exposure problem (front running accelerates price impact), move prices to discover latent demands, give price concessions.
- **Value traders** (Ch. 16): the ultimate suppliers of liquidity. They trade when price deviates from fundamental value, which happens when uninformed liquidity demanders push prices away and when dealers misread order flow (or demand inventory-risk concessions). They supply immediacy (direct) and depth (indirect, absorbing dealer inventory at discounted prices), making markets **resilient**. Their prices define the **outside spread**; they face adverse selection and the **winner's curse** (winning an auction and regretting it).
- **Arbitrageurs** (Ch. 17): trade on relative values, enforce the law of one price, connect fragmented markets, move liquidity between markets, and create financial products (spreads, strips); arbitrage opportunities arise from quantity (liquidity) mismatches, not just price discrepancies.
- **Buy side traders** (Ch. 18): the market vs. limit order decision depends on patience, spread width, and order exposure risk. Impatient -> market orders; patient -> limit orders. Wide spread -> offer liquidity; narrow spread -> take it. But the rule flips when you know value (an aggressively priced limit order creates a misleading "narrow spread"). Execution probability of limit orders depends on queue size ahead, volatility, and interest; econometric optimizers and experienced market-not-held brokers both solve this.

## Liquidity and volatility (Ch. 19-20)

- **Liquidity is multidimensional**: the outcome of a bilateral search; traders rarely distinguish width (spread), depth (size at the quotes), immediacy (speed), and resiliency (tendency of prices to return). Impatient traders take liquidity; dealers, limit order traders, value traders offer it; brokers and exchanges organize it.
- **Volatility splits into**: fundamental volatility (unexpected changes in values, random-walk price changes, driven by news and adverse-selection repricing) and **transitory volatility** (uninformed traders' demands pushing price away from value, reversed when value traders step in). Transitory volatility is what transaction costs create for uninformed traders, and what regulators can actually affect. Storage costs, inelastic demand and long production pipelines make commodity prices volatile; options values depend on volatility, so volatility prediction is a core options-trading skill.

## Measurement and performance (Ch. 21-22)

- You cannot measure what you cannot define: liquidity measurement requires the width/depth/immediacy/resiliency framework. Implementation (execution) performance is measurable and often matters more than selection performance, because most traders cannot consistently beat the market. Beware performance evaluation traps: past performance does not predict future results when skill is absent; survivorship and luck dominate short-horizon track records.

## Market quality, regulation and extreme events (Ch. 9, 28, 29)

- Welfare economics framework: positive analysis (predictions) vs. normative analysis (values); a proper normative argument names alternatives, criteria (social welfare function), positive evaluation, and a choice.
- Well-functioning markets produce informative prices and liquidity, which is why free-market economies are wealthy.
- Bubbles, crashes, circuit breakers: market structure contributes to extreme volatility; trading halts can help or harm depending on whether they allow value traders time to act.
- Insider trading: the deepest issues are about incentives to produce information (labor economics), not just fairness.

## Connections to the Elliott Wave base (Balan)

- Harris's zero-sum game framing matches Balan's "cardinal sins" money management: your edge must come from someone else's need (impatience, misinformation, forced trading). Elliott Wave identifies where uninformed crowd action is likely to culminate (3rd waves, B-wave extremes, multipliers); microstructure explains who is on the other side and why prices overshoot/undershoot there.
- Transitory volatility and wave corrections are two lenses on the same phenomenon: uninformed liquidity demand pushing price away from value, then mean reversion (value traders = the corrective force; corrections consume ~70% of time, matching Harris's point that most volume is uninformed liquidity trading, not information trading).
- Balan's 3rd-wave thrusts with record volume correspond to Harris's informed-trading price impact on high volume; 5th-wave blow-offs on light volume are sentiment-driven (uninformed) moves, prone to transitory volatility and reversal.
- The "free trading option" concept warns limit-order Elliott traders: placing resting orders at Fibonacci targets gives options to order anticipators; consider exposure.
- Index-market leadership over cash markets supports trading index futures (NQ) rather than cash baskets for wave analysis: the futures price leads the underlying index.

## Notes for the eventual strategy build

- Strategy candidates from this layer: (1) liquidity provision at identified wave extremes (value-trader style outside spread), (2) taking liquidity only when spread is narrow and wave position is confirmed, (3) treating transitory-volatility overshoots of Fibonacci targets as entries, (4) measuring execution quality (spread, depth, resilience) around wave turning points.
- Key risks to encode: adverse selection at turning points (news breaks the count), winner's curse on limit orders at "value" levels, order-anticipator extraction from exposed resting orders.
