# Market Microstructure: A Survey (Madhavan) — study notes

Madhavan's 2000 survey is the third leg of the survey trilogy (with Stoll 2002 and Biais-Glosten-Spatt). Its distinct angle: everything is organized around **information economics** — "looking inside the black box by which latent demands are translated into realized prices and volumes." Its four pillars: (1) price formation, (2) market structure and design, (3) transparency, (4) interfaces with asset pricing, corporate finance, and international finance. It adds two things Stoll doesn't emphasize: the **experimental** literature, and the **transparency** literature in depth (Madhavan's own specialty).

## 2. The canonical model (the survey's unifying notation)

Start from efficient markets: p_t = μ_t (price = public-information expectation), so returns r_t = ε_t are serially uncorrelated. Microstructure relaxes this in steps:

1. **Trading frictions**: p_t = μ_t + s_t where s_t = s·x_t (half-spread times signed trade indicator). Then r_t = ε_t + s(x_t - x_{t-1}) and Cov(r_t, r_{t-1}) = -s², which inverts into **Roll's estimator ŝ = 2√(-Cov)**. The full round-trip spread can be estimated from transaction prices alone.
2. **Private information**: ε_t = λx_t + u_t. Price impact of a unit purchase = s + λ. Key practical implication: the quoted spread is good only for stated depth; **for large orders the true cost is s + λ, which can dwarf the quoted spread**. Corollary (Subrahmanyam 1991): asymmetric information is a *single-stock* problem — nobody has private information about the whole market — which is the economic rationale for **stock index futures**: trading the basket strips out the adverse-selection component.
3. **Structure**: a call auction with multilateral trading has no spread at all (E[|s_t|] = 0) — the spread is not a law of nature, it is a property of *continuous, bilateral* trading protocols.

## 3. Price formation and the role of information

- **Cross-section of spreads** (Demsetz tradition): volume (negative), risk (positive), price, and size explain most of the spread; **no economies of scale in market making**.
- **Smidt (1971)**: market makers are not passive; they actively manage inventory. **Garman (1976)**: with finite dealer capital K and order flow a random walk, inventory follows a random walk and **market failure is certain** (gambler's ruin) unless dealers actively move prices. This is why the dealer's *capital* matters — a lesson replayed in every funding crisis (August 2007, 2008).
- **Inventory models** in canonical form: p_t = μ_t - φ(I_t - I*) + s·x_t. The market maker brings buyers and sellers together **in time** (via inventory) as physical marketplaces do in space — a stabilizing institution an automated auction lacks.
- **Asymmetric information**: Bagehot's informed/uninformed split; **Glosten-Milgrom**: quotes are conditional expectations given trade direction ("ex post rationality"), spread = **ωσ** where ω = fraction informed and σ = value uncertainty range — the spread exists even with zero costs and perfect competition. **Kyle (1985)**: p_t = μ_{t-1} + λq_t, informed trading camouflaged by noise; prices eventually impound everything. **Holden-Subrahmanyam (1992)**: multiple competing insiders reveal information fast, so competition among informed traders makes markets *more* efficient and insider profits much lower. **Admati-Pfleiderer (1988)**: discretionary liquidity traders concentrate in time (open/close) — explaining intraday volume/volatility patterns. **Barclay-Warner (1993)**: informed traders hide in *medium-sized* trades (**stealth trading**).
- **Are market makers informed?** No: inventory changes correlate negatively with subsequent prices, and dealers earn *less* than the quoted spread per round trip — consistent with them being losers to informed flow, not front-runners.
- **Easley-O'Hara (1987)**: price adjustment to information is *dynamic*, history-dependent, and need not be immediate; efficiency is a convergence speed, not a static property.
- **French-Roll (1986) quantified**: open-to-close variance ~5x close-to-open; hourly trading variance ~20x non-trading. Only **at most 12%** of daily variance comes from the trading process itself (mispricing); holiday evidence points to **private information impounded while markets are open** as the dominant volatility source.
- **Block decomposition**: price impact = permanent component π (information) + temporary component τ (transitory accommodation). Loeb (1983): large-block impact can exceed **15%** in small caps vs ~1% in large caps. Keim-Madhavan (1996): impacts are **concave in order size**, decreasing in market cap; and the **pre-trade benchmark matters enormously** (sell impact -4.3% measured from prior close vs -10.2% from three weeks prior) — evidence of **information leakage** as blocks are "shopped" upstairs, implying earlier block-impact studies were downward biased.
- **The combined estimation model**: Δp_t = (ωσ/2 + s)Δx_t - φΔI_t. In a pure dealer market ΔI_t = -x_{t-1} so trades and quotes suffice; in hybrid markets you need real inventory data, or a reduced form (Hasbrouck's VAR) using the fact that **information effects are permanent, inventory effects transitory**.
- **What the empirical horse race found**: information effects dominate intraday price dynamics; inventory effects are real but slow. Madhavan-Smidt (1993): specialist inventories mean-revert with a **49-day half-life**, falling to **7.3 days** once shifts in *target* inventory are controlled — the dealer is also an active investor. Madhavan-Sofianos (1997) and Lyons (1995): dealers control inventory largely by **selectively participating** in trades rather than moving quotes — which is why inventory shows up weakly in prices.

## 4. Market structure and design

- **Architecture taxonomy**: continuity (periodic vs continuous), dealer reliance (order-driven vs quote-driven), automation, independent price discovery, order forms, protocols (ticks, halts, circuit breakers, opens), transparency. Fig. 1 arrays real systems: Nasdaq (continuous, dealer, quotes displayed), NYSE open (batch auction, dealer presence), Paris Bourse (continuous, no dealers, full book display), POSIT (crossing, no price discovery), CBOT (floor, free prices), FX (dealer, opaque).
- **The network externality puzzle**: theory says trading should consolidate into one market (volume → lower costs → more volume; informed traders split orders, so pooling lowers the informed fraction; pooled orders give more efficient prices; Hasbouverck 1995: the primary market does virtually all price discovery, satellites just match). Yet fragmentation persists. Related sub-puzzle: call auctions are the theoretically efficient aggregator (used exactly when uncertainty is largest — opens, closes, restarts after halts), yet investors demand continuous trading "for reasons not well understood."
- **Off-exchange/upstairs trading** as rational fragmentation: large traders fear being front-run and strategy-leaked in a transparent downstairs market (Madhavan 1995); upstairs markets aggregate latent demands (Grossman), enable risk-sharing (Keim-Madhavan), and let those who credibly signal they are *not* informed trade cheaply (Seppi). Cream-skimming: competing venues attract the uninformed flow and pass the informed to the primary market (Easley et al.).
- **The dealer puzzle**: why do market makers persist in the most active markets (FX!) where pure limit order books are feasible? Dealer functions: price discovery, liquidity/continuity, stabilization. Competition among dealers (Ho-Stoll 1983): inter-dealer trading reduces spreads; a dealer is competitive only on one side of the market (the side that reduces his inventory); **actual spreads are much narrower than quoted spreads** (beware quoted-spread empirics).
- **Limit order economics**: every order is a limit order (market order = limit at the ask). A limit order's expected profit is a weighted average of a gain against the uninformed and a loss to the informed (the two equations); equilibrium fills the book (Foucault 1999). The limit order supplies a **free option** and must be monitored, and monitoring is costly — "it is perhaps for this reason that dealers of some form or the other arise so often in auction markets."
- **Decimalization/tick size**: reducing δ lowers liquidity-supply profits and raises losses to informed flow → thinner depth away from the inside even as the quoted spread narrows; **a smaller tick can reduce overall market liquidity** (Harris). Discreteness can also *facilitate tacit collusion* (Kandel-Marx, Dutta-Madhavan) — the grid gives colluders focal prices.
- **Empirics**: batch-auction opens vs continuous closes produce measurably different variances (Amihud-Mendelson 1987); moving stocks to continuous trading on the Tel Aviv exchange produced **large increases in asset values** — structure affects value, not just costs.
- **Upstairs empirics**: Madhavan-Cheng (1997): for the average block, upstairs benefits are small; the upstairs market's real beneficiaries may be the *counterparties* — institutions reluctant to post large limit orders (free options) get broker-screened, adverse-selection-filtered participation. The upstairs market enables trades that would not occur downstairs at all.
- **Experimental markets**: bubbles are easy to generate even with known value bounds; prices in auctions **need not converge to full-information values** — agents can learn incorrectly and settle at the "wrong" price; results are sensitive to instructions (Schnitzlein, Bloomfield, Bloomfield-O'Hara).

## 5. Transparency (the survey's deepest section)

- **Definition**: ability to observe the trading process; pre-trade (quotes, depth, the book) vs post-trade (time, price, volume, identity); dimensions of extent, speed, and anonymity.
- **Theory, result by result**:
  - Forster-George (1992): disclosing the *direction* of liquidity flow lowers its trading costs (less adverse-selection premium); disclosing *magnitude* is ambiguous.
  - Benveniste et al. (1992): floor brokers' knowledge of trader identity and reputation-building can lower spreads below an anonymous market for everyone.
  - **Sunshine trading** (Admati-Pfleiderer 1991): traders who preannounce identify themselves as informationless and trade cheaper — but those who *cannot* preannounce pay more. Transparency redistributes; it does not uniformly bless.
  - Madhavan (1996): disclosing noise-trading information can *increase* volatility — **"noise is necessary for markets to operate, and disclosure robs the market of this lubrication."** Adverse effects greatest in **thin markets**; floors may beat anonymous automation for active issues, the reverse for inactive ones.
  - Chowdhry-Nanda (1991): dealers may *voluntarily* disclose to deter insiders, building a reputation for a "clean" market — a race to the top.
  - Madhavan (1995): large institutional traders prefer **opaque** venues, where they can break up orders without being front-run; too much primary-market transparency pushes flow off-exchange.
- **Empirics**: Toronto 1990 book disclosure → **spreads widened** (Porter-Weaver; the free-option problem in action). London delayed-reporting changes → little measurable effect (Gemmill). Nasdaq's late reporting of large blocks at away prices → systematic, dealer-beneficial (Porter-Weaver).
- **Experiments** (Bloomfield-O'Hara): non-disclosing dealers profit more and capture more flow; when dealers can choose, most choose not to disclose, but there are advantages to being the *sole* discloser — no simple race to top or bottom.
- **Madhavan's four-part verdict**: (1) transparency matters — more of it, more informative prices; (2) complete transparency can *reduce* liquidity; (3) some disclosure beats none; (4) every transparency change redistributes welfare between informed (want anonymity) and uninformed (want disclosure). **No single structure is best for all parties.**

## 6. Interface with other areas of finance

- **Liquidity and asset pricing**: Amihud-Mendelson (1986) formalized: with round-trip costs (λ+s), equilibrium value m = m* - (1+(1+r_f)^{-T})(λ+s) — **trading costs reduce the value of the asset and raise its expected return**. Measured with midquote prices (ignoring λ), you *see* a return premium that is really compensation for illiquidity — partly explaining the size effect. Brennan-Subrahmanyam: λ (price impact) is itself priced.
- **Commonality**: Hasbrouck-Seppi (1999), principal components: common factors in order flows account for ~50% of the commonality in returns (the statistical seed of the commonality-in-liquidity strand that FPR Ch.9 and The Quants' August 2007 dramatize).
- **Behavioral bridge**: overconfident informed traders (Daniel et al.); Black's (1986) "Noise" — without noise traders, every trade is information and dealers widen spreads to the point of no trade; DeLong et al. (1991): noise traders *survive*, move prices collectively, and create a risk others must be paid to bear. Barber-Odean: retail trading is hazardous to your wealth.
- **Corporate finance**: the underwriter as IPO-aftermarket market maker (Ellis et al.: the lead underwriter is almost always the primary market maker). Madhavan's model: the underwriter optimally balances IPO commission revenue (higher price → lower spread) against aftermarket market-making profit, resolving the puzzle of why small firms list on Nasdaq despite higher measured costs — the *relationship market* gives them cheaper capital. Stock splits as tick-size management (Angel): firms split to keep price in an optimal band relative to the tick.
- **International finance**: B-share premia/discounts across foreign-restricted share classes are liquidity phenomena (relative s+λ); DR arbitrage bounds set by cross-market trading costs.
- **FX microstructure**: **Lyons' hot-potato model (1997)** — a customer block lands on a dealer, who passes the inventory to other dealers who pass it on; inter-dealer churn multiplies volume far beyond the original trade, and depends on exactly two things: the dealer structure of FX and the *opacity* of inter-dealer trading. **Evans-Lyons (1999)**: adding signed order flow to macro interest-differential models lifts R² from under 0.10 to over 50% (DM/$): **order flow, not macro fundamentals, is the proximate driver of exchange rates at daily horizons.**

## 7. Madhavan's own conclusions

Markets and protocols are more complex than believed; frictions matter, up to and including **market failure under bad protocols**; beware one-size-fits-all policy (more transparency ≠ more liquidity, always); open puzzles: why continuous beats periodic in demand, why network externalities don't unify markets, how much of expected returns is liquidity, and why trading mechanisms differ so widely across assets.

## Durable lessons (teaching distillation)

1. **The spread is a protocol artifact, not a law**: a batch auction has no spread; a continuous market needs someone to supply immediacy, and that supply is priced. Choose your venue metrics accordingly.
2. **s + λ is the real cost**: quoted spreads are the small visible part; impact λ is the big invisible part, concave in size, larger in small caps — and large enough to be *priced into expected returns*.
3. **The basket strips adverse selection** (Subrahmanyam): index futures exist because trading the market has no single-stock informed-trader problem. For an index-futures trader this is foundational: your instrument is structurally the low-adverse-selection one.
4. **Dealer capital is a market-stability variable** (Garman's gambler's ruin): when dealer balance sheets shrink, failure is a matter of time, not chance.
5. **Information effects are permanent, inventory effects transitory** — the single identification principle behind virtually all empirical microstructure.
6. **Transparency is redistribution**: informed want anonymity, uninformed want sunshine; thin markets suffer most from too much disclosure ("noise is the lubricant"); structure changes move flow between venues rather than making everyone better off.
7. **Order flow explains prices where fundamentals fail** (Evans-Lyons): at short horizons, *who is buying and selling* beats *why*.

## Connections to earlier books

- **To the Elliott Wave base (Balan)**: Madhavan's canonical model gives the cleanest formal statement of what a wave analyst implicitly assumes: that prices are expectation-plus-noise plus slow information flow. Easley-O'Hara's dynamic efficiency (prices converge to value at a *speed* determined by volume, depth, variance) is the quantitative version of Elliott's insistence that crowd psychology unfolds in *time*; the finding that auction prices can settle at the "wrong" value for long stretches (experimental section) is the microstructure sanction for the wave practitioner's core claim that market prices deviate from value persistently and predictably.
- **To Harris**: the architecture taxonomy (continuity, dealer reliance, automation, order forms, protocols) is Harris's market-taxonomy chapter compressed into one design space, with the order-flow economics filled in.
- **To Hasbrouck**: Section 3.3 is a map of Hasbrouck's own program — the VAR reduced form, the permanent/transitory identification, the trade-signing (tick test, Lee-Ready), the discreteness models (Hasbrouck 1999), and Hasbrouck 1995's multi-market information share. These notes' companion to the Empirical Microstructure book.
- **To Biais-Glosten-Spatt**: the same synthetic models (Glosten-Milgrom, Kyle, inventory control) in Madhavan's notation; BGS's market-design and transparency chapters are the formal descendants of Sections 4-5 here.
- **To Stoll**: sibling survey with a different center of gravity — Stoll prices the spread's *components*, Madhavan prices *information and disclosure*. Stoll's "fragmentation is just another word for competition" is Madhavan's network-externality puzzle; both conclude no single structure is best. The Nasdaq scandal appears in both (Madhavan adds Kandel-Marx's SOES-bandit defense and Chen-Ritter's 7% underwriting cartel).
- **To Foucault-Pagano-Röell**: FPR's transparency chapter (Ch. 8) is the book-length development of Madhavan's Section 5, including the same Toronto/London evidence and the same redistribution verdict; FPR's fragmentation chapter extends Section 4's externality puzzle; Hasbrouck-Seppi's commonality work here is the seed of FPR Ch.9's commonality evidence.
- **To Bouchaud**: Keim-Madhavan's concave block-impact and benchmark-sensitivity findings are early evidence for the square-root impact law; stealth trading (Barclay-Warner) matters for anyone inferring information from volume.
- **To Master the Markets (Williams/VSA)**: VSA reads volume as professional intent; Madhavan supplies the caveats: informed traders hide in *medium* trades (stealth trading), upstairs leakage shows in price *before* the block prints, and the open concentrates activity partly for attention (Admati-Pfleiderer) — volume at the open is the least informative per unit. Practical refinement for AVFI-style indicators.
- **To Dark Pools and The Quants**: Madhavan 2000 is the theory pre-history of both. The upstairs market's screening function is the dark pool's economic ancestor; cream-skimming is the payment-for-order-flow business model; Madhavan's "opaque venues attract large flow" predicts the dark-pool migration Dark Pools documents. Garman's gambler's ruin (dealer capital) and the liquidity-commonality statistics are the mechanism behind The Quants' August 2007.

## Strategy / research candidates (for Andrea's weekly NQ Elliott Wave practice)

1. **Index futures as the low-friction instrument, formally**: Subrahmanyam's basket logic (adverse selection lives in single stocks) plus Evans-Lyons (order flow drives short-horizon prices) justify NQ as the primary instrument: its information is flow-driven, not stock-specific. Corollary for the regime notebook: watch for weeks when NQ moves *decouple* from the sum of stock-level moves — that is liquidity provision/demand in the futures market itself, not information.
2. **Stealth-trading correction for volume signals**: Barclay-Warner's result warns that the informative volume is medium-sized, and that climactic prints may be the *least* informed. For weekly NQ work, weight effort-vs-result reads (VSA) by *where* volume concentrates relative to normal distribution, and discount blow-off weeks at opens/holidays (Madhavan's thin-noise windows).
3. **Thin-market transparency rule**: Madhavan's sharpest policy result — disclosure hurts most where noise is scarce. The analogue: signals that rely on "everyone can see it" (obvious trendline breaks, widely broadcast levels) have their edge taxed in exactly the thin, low-participation weeks (holiday periods, late August, late December). Downgrade the confidence of pattern completions that occur in those windows on the weekly chart.
4. **Dealer-capital regime flag**: Garman's ruin logic says liquidity suppliers fail when their capital drains; modern proxies (prime-broker stress, VIX, credit spreads) mark that state. When the flag is up, treat third-of-a-third legs and C-wave extensions as the mechanical cascade the quant literature describes, and expect overshoot beyond "fair value" — the same regime notebook item proposed from The Quants, now with its mechanism.
5. **Permanent-vs-transitory decomposition as a wave-end validator**: the π/τ block decomposition (permanent information vs temporary accommodation) suggests a concrete research project: estimate on weekly NQ data whether high-volume reversal weeks are followed by retracement of more than half (temporary, liquidity-driven, VSA climax) or by continuation (permanent, informational). That split would give a statistical footing to the difference between shake-outs and genuine trend changes in the wave count.

# Market Microstructure (Stoll) — study notes

Stoll's handbook survey is the field's own retrospective map, written by one of its founders (inventor of the inventory-based spread model). It organizes everything around one measurable object — the bid-ask spread — and one question: what determines it, and what does it do to short-run prices? Where Harris gives the practitioner's taxonomy and Biais-Glosten-Spatt the unified theory, Stoll gives the *economics*: the five sources of the spread, the algebra linking each source to a distinct time-series signature, the empirical magnitudes, and the market-design lessons drawn from the Nasdaq controversy. It is also a primary source: several models (Stoll 1978, Huang-Stoll 1996) are presented by their author.

## I. The frame: three markets, one service

- **Demsetz (1968)**: what a market sells is **immediacy**. Demanders (market orders) pay the spread; suppliers (dealers, limit-order placers) earn it. Stoll's frame: an investor participates in three markets at once — the market for **information**, for **securities**, and for **transaction services**; microstructure is the study of the third, but it is inseparable from the first because "the difficulty and cost of a trade depends on the information possessed by the participants."
- Market types (most real markets are mixtures): **call auction** (orders batched, single clearing price, e.g. NYSE open) vs **continuous**; **auction** (investors trade against resting orders / the crowd) vs **dealer** (public must trade at dealer quotes). NYSE = continuous auction with a dealer (specialist) inside; Nasdaq = dealer market with displayed limit orders; bond and FX markets = pure dealer markets; the "purest" auction market is an electronic LOB (Island, Paris Bourse).
- Traders: **active vs passive** (active demand immediacy and move prices; passive supply it and stabilize; "passive traders tend to earn profits from active traders" — the core slogan of market making); **liquidity vs informed** (liquidity traders trade portfolios for cash/risk reasons and try to identify counterparties; informed traders trade the specific asset and try to hide); **individual vs institutional** (institutions dominate volume and need block-sized solutions); **public vs professional** (professionals carry affirmative obligations — firm quotes, fair and orderly markets — that public traders do not).
- **Rules of precedence**: price priority, then time priority. Trade-throughs are banned within a market but not across markets. **Harris (1991)'s tick-size insight**: time priority is meaningless if the tick is tiny — with a $0.01 tick anyone can step in front of your 1,000-share bid for $10 total, so small ticks cut the incentive to post limit orders and thin displayed liquidity. **Price matching and payment for order flow (1-2 cents/share) violate time priority**: the market that first posted the best price is stranded, so who will bother posting best prices? This is the earliest clear statement of the perverse incentive that Dark Pools later dramatizes.
- The trading process: **information** (real-time CTS/CQS dissemination; tape revenue), **routing** (DOT to the specialist; SelectNet; SOES for auto-execution), **execution** (dealers resist instant auto-execution for fear of being picked off by the speedy — they want even 15 seconds of delay), **clearing and settlement** (t+3 book entry).

## II. The five sources of the bid-ask spread

Stoll's taxonomy — every spread is some mixture of:

1. **Order handling costs** — real resources (labor, capital, systems) to quote, route, execute, clear. In a pure limit-order market these are small; where dealers earn a living, they are large.
2. **Non-competitive pricing** — explicit collusion or rules (e.g. a fat minimum tick) that raise spreads above cost. (The Nasdaq even-eighths scandal, Section VI, is the worked example.)
3. **Inventory risk** — the supplier of immediacy who buys at the bid bears the risk that price falls before the position is unloaded.
4. **The free trading option** (Copeland-Galai 1983) — a resting bid is a **free put** written to the market; a resting ask is a **free call**; a dealer quoting both writes a **strangle**. Quotes that are monitored and adjusted fast have short maturity (cheap); unattended limit orders have long maturity (expensive). Black-Scholes example: a one-hour unreviewed bid $0.25 below a $40.25 stock with 200% annualized vol has an option value of $0.23 — roughly the whole discount. The option premium is paid by the uninformed who sell at the bid absent news.
5. **Asymmetric information** — Bagehot (1971): quotes lose to the informed; the losses must be recouped from the uninformed or the market fails.

**The inventory model (Stoll 1978a), in full**: a dealer solves a portfolio problem — what discount $C$ on a $Q$-dollar purchase makes him indifferent between his optimal portfolio and the optimal portfolio plus unwanted inventory? Taylor-expanding expected utility gives the proportional discount:

  c = C/Q = ½ · (z/W₀) · σ² · Q

where z is relative risk aversion, W₀ dealer wealth, σ² the stock's return variance (variance, not beta — the position is undiversified). With initial inventory I, the discount becomes c = (z/W₀)σ_IQ·I + ½(z/W₀)σ²Q: initial inventory shifts the *level* of both quotes (a long dealer bids less and asks less) but **drops out of the spread**: S/P = 2c = (z/W₀)σ²Q. Quote dynamics prediction: after a sale at the bid, *both* bid and ask fall (discourage more selling, encourage buying); after a buy at the ask, both rise. Inventory control moves quotes; it does not widen them.

**The Glosten-Milgrom (1985) adverse-selection model, in full**: asset worth v^H or v^L with equal probability; an informed trader is present with probability π. The ask must be the expected value conditional on trading at the ask: A = v^H·π + v̄·(1-π), and similarly B = v^L·π + v̄·(1-π), so **A - B = π(v^H - v^L)**: the spread scales with the probability of facing informed flow and the value uncertainty. Prices follow a **martingale**: each trade's direction is impounded into quotes permanently.

## III. The diagnostic logic: each spread source leaves a different time-series fingerprint

This is the survey's most practically valuable section. Write the midpoint change as ΔM_t = λ(S/2)Q_{t-1} + ε_t and the trade price as P_t = M_t + (S/2)Q_t + η_t. Then:

| Pure source | Quote response λ | Trade reversal π | Realized half-spread | Price-change serial covariance |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Order processing only | 0 | 0.5 | S/2 | **-S²/4** (Roll 1984) |
| Adverse selection only | 1 | 0.5 | 0 | 0 (martingale) |
| Inventory only | 1 | >0.5 | (2π-1)·S/2 | (S²/4)(1-2π) <0 but attenuated |

- **Roll (1984)**: if only order processing matters, prices just bounce between bid and ask, giving cov(ΔP_t, ΔP_{t-1}) = -S²/4, invertible to the famous **spread estimator S = 2√(-cov)**. Teaching point: the Roll spread measures only the *cost* component; it is blind to the adverse-selection component precisely because adverse selection leaves no bounce.
- Why the empirical Roll spread is smaller than the quoted spread: (a) quote adjustment (λ>0) attenuates the bounce; (b) **demand-side momentum is positively autocorrelated** and offsets supply-side mean reversion; (c) order splitting creates runs of same-sign trades; (d) discreteness hides small moves.
- The realized spread decomposition: quoted/effective spread = total execution cost; realized/traded spread = what liquidity suppliers actually earn; **effective minus realized = losses to the informed**. This one subtraction is the standard adverse-selection estimator used everywhere in the empirical literature.

## IV. The empirical magnitudes (Table 1, 1706 NYSE stocks, Dec 1997-Feb 1998)

- Quoted half-spread averages **7.87 cents** (8.28¢ in small/low-priced, 6.49¢ in large/high-priced); effective half-spread **5.58¢** (price improvement exists); traded (realized) half-spread **3.74¢** → **losses to informed traders ≈ 1.84 cents/share**, smaller per share in large stocks. Roll spread smaller still. Conclusion: a significant part of the spread is real cost, another part is insurance against informed flow.
- Cross-section (Table 2, R² = 79% — "one of the strongest and most robust relations in finance"): proportional spreads **fall** with volume, fall with price, fall with size, rise with return variance, rise with order imbalance. Surprising sign: rises with number of trades (proxy for adverse selection intensity). This regression is the ancestor of Amihud-type illiquidity measures.

## V. Price effects of trading

- **Blocks**: temporary price impact only **0.70% for block sales, ~0% for block purchases** (Kraus-Stoll 1972) — the famous asymmetry: block buys are partly informative (price stays up), block sales are mostly liquidity (price bounces). Institutional data (Chan-Lakonishok): buy programs +0.34%, sell programs only -0.04%. Sales are cheaper to execute than buys: the world suspects bad news less when you sell than you fear when you buy.
- **Herding**: Kraus-Stoll (1972a) found institutional parallel trading occurs no more often than chance; Wermers (1999): mutual-fund buying follows positive past returns, moves price contemporaneously, and precedes positive future returns (feedback, not coordination).
- Order-imbalance regressions (ΔP_t on I_t = (B_t-S_t)/(B_t+S_t)) show significant positive λ, "in the spirit of Kyle (1985)": pressure moves prices, at least in the quote midpoint.
- **French-Roll (1986)**: overnight return variance is **one fifth** of daytime variance — some of the difference is news timing, but part is that **trading itself generates volatility**. Trading is not a neutral window onto information; it is a volatility source.

## VI. Market design

- **Call auctions** batch liquidity, eliminate the free-option problem for aggressive limit orders, and reduce adverse selection (large visible orders let others adjust). Used for opens and restarts after halts. Design issues: transparency of the book, cancellability (cancel fees to deter manipulation), dealer participation. **Kyle (1985) presented as a call auction**: one informed trader, noise flow u ~ N(0,σu²); clearing price p = p₀ + λ(x+u) with **λ = 2(σp²/σu²)^½** — the price impact is larger when noise trading is scarcer (nowhere to hide).
- **Opening evidence**: Amihud-Mendelson trick — open-to-open and close-to-close returns both span 24h, so public information is held constant. Variance ratio σ²(open)/σ²(close) = 1.13 → **opening prices overshoot and reverse**; Roll-implied spread at the open is **36 cents on a $40 stock vs 3.9 cents close-to-close**: the open is an expensive place to trade. Overshooting is price discovery under incomplete information (stocks open before their relatives), not news. Specialist intervention helps (Madhavan-Panchapagesan 2000).
- **Circuit breakers** post-1987: 10% Dow drop halts 1 hour, 20% two hours, 30% the day.
- **The Nasdaq controversy** — the field's flagship scandal: Christie-Schultz (1994) found Nasdaq market makers almost never quoted odd eighths, flooring effective spreads at 25¢, implying tacit collusion. Huang-Stoll (1996): Nasdaq execution costs **about twice** NYSE for matched stocks (quoted half-spread 24.6¢ vs 12.9¢; effective 18.7¢ vs 7.9¢; Roll 18.3¢ vs 3.4¢) — and *not* because of adverse selection (identical in both markets), but because limit orders weren't displayed and because **internalization and preferencing** (order flow pre-committed to affiliated/chosen dealers who promise to match the best quote) destroy the incentive to compete on price: narrowing a spread when your rivals match it just cuts everyone's revenue. SEC 1997 **Order Handling Rules** (display limit orders; honor ECN quotes) cut quoted spreads ~30%. Structural lessons: dealers left alone quote wide; a pure limit-order book cannot always carry liquidity alone (specialist participated in 27.5% of NYSE volume in 2000); **the mixed dealer/auction market is optimal** — matching Fischer Black's (1971) prediction that automation shrinks but does not eliminate dealers.
- **Transparency**: benefits price discovery, broker monitoring, and competition; costs are incentive effects — displayed limit orders can be picked off (free option) and front-run by information, so display can *reduce* liquidity supply. **Anonymity**: informed want it; dealers want reputation; Admati-Pfleiderer's **sunshine trading** — an uninformed trader credibly announcing himself to avoid adverse price impact. **Automation** reallocates the free option: auto-execution gives it to the fast customer (SOES bandits); dealer-acceptance routing gives it back to the dealer. **Tick size** (Harris): penny ticks make queue-jumping cheap and depth thin; fat ticks guarantee wide spreads for small orders. **Order types**: markets increasingly ban plain market orders (use marketable limits) to protect against thin books; contingent orders could shrink the free option.

## VII. The market for markets: centralization vs fragmentation

- 1970 NYSE was a textbook **cartel**: 1,366 seats (limiting entry), fixed commissions, Rule 394 banning off-exchange trading. May Day 1975 abolished fixed commissions (volume exploded, industry thrived); Rule 394 → 390 → abolished 2001, midwifing the **third market** (Madoff & Co. matching NYSE prices and paying brokers for retail order flow — the origin of payment-for-order-flow) and **soft dollars** (commission overpayment recycled as research).
- The **National Market System** mandated by Congress (1975): transparency (CTS/CQS) and links (ITS) exist; the single consolidated limit order book (CLOB) never happened — too much integration, too little competition.
- Centralizing forces: **economies of scale** in processing and **network externalities** (you trade where others trade) — both give first-mover advantage. Fragmenting forces: transparency makes price-matching by satellites credible; payment for order flow; technology lowering switching costs; regulation forcing links. Stoll's verdict is the section's headline: **"fragmentation is just another word for competition"** — its main cost is that cross-market time priority cannot be enforced.
- Exchanges demutualize into for-profit businesses; ATS/ECNs regulated as broker-dealers; several ECNs apply to become exchanges to sell quote data.

## VIII. Other markets (brief)

- **Bonds**: dealer market, indicative quotes, anonymous **inter-dealer brokers** (the inter-dealer exclusion was later banned on Nasdaq precisely because it raised public spreads); institutional clientele; trading costs *lower* for larger trades (size is a sign of sophistication, not urgency); Treasury auctions price the on-the-run issue at a premium.
- **FX**: dealer market; Lyons (1995): dealers manage inventories, want flat by day-end, and watch other dealers' inventories for information.
- **Futures**: open-outcry pits, hundreds of traders, not every quote/trade hits the tape; liquidity from **scalpers**; Manaster-Mann: floor-trader inventories mean-revert but without paying price concessions.
- **Options**: 400+ strikes per stock; CBOE (1973) competing market makers; spreads proportionally large; options-lead-stock evidence mixed, so informed flow is not reliably detected in the leveraged market.

## IX. Microstructure and asset pricing

Real friction (cost of transacting) unambiguously **lowers asset values**: a share that costs 20% to exit is worth less today than one costing 2%. Informational friction's effect is distributional. Evidence: Stoll-Whaley (1983) — part of the small-firm effect is its higher transaction costs; Amihud-Mendelson (1986) — expected returns compensate for spreads; Brennan-Subrahmanyam (1996) — required returns relate to the **Kyle λ** (price impact priced as a risk). Liquidity is not just a trading cost; it is priced into the level of prices.

## Durable lessons (teaching distillation)

1. The spread is a **bundle of five prices** (handling cost, rents, inventory insurance, option premium, adverse-selection insurance). Any spread number you see — even your own broker's — is this mixture; the mix, not the total, tells you what the market is afraid of.
2. **Fingerprints, not opinions**: order processing → price bounce (Roll); adverse selection → martingale prices, zero realized spread; inventory → quote mean reversion with reversal-prone flow. You can diagnose a market's spread composition from time series alone.
3. **Effective minus realized = informed-flow tax.** The single most-used empirical decomposition in the field.
4. **Quoted depth, effective spread, and impact respond differently to design changes** (tick size, transparency, internalization) — which is why every market-design "fix" has a shadow cost (penny spreads but thin books; transparency but picked-off limit orders).
5. **Preferencing/internalization is a competition killer**: guaranteed order flow removes the incentive to improve prices; matching-the-best-quote while pocketing rebates is not competition.
6. **Trading is a volatility source** (French-Roll), and **the open is the most expensive hour** (overshoot and reversal; 9x the implied spread of close-to-close trading).
7. **Liquidity is priced into asset values** (small-firm premium partly costs; Amihud-Mendelson; Kyle-λ priced): microstructure is not a detail under asset pricing, it is one of its inputs.

## Connections to earlier books

- **To the Elliott Wave base (Balan)**: Stoll gives the mechanics beneath several Elliott/VSA-style observations. The Roll bounce and inventory mean reversion are the *quantitative* form of the idea that short-term swings contain a mechanical, non-informational component — a floor for the "noise" inside which wave counts live. The French-Roll finding (trading itself makes volatility) and the opening-overshoot result support treating climactic/panic wave endings (third waves, C-waves) as partly mechanical overshoot that mean-reverts, exactly the premise behind VSA climax-and-reversal reads. The Amihud-Mendelson/Brennan-Subrahmanyam result — illiquidity is priced — connects to the risk-premium intuition behind why extended, thin-liquidity fifth waves terminate.
- **To Harris**: this is Harris's taxonomy with the economics filled in — Harris's order types, precedence rules, tick size (Harris 1991 is cited as the origin), and floor intermediaries all reappear with the models that price them. Stoll's Section I is nearly a compressed Trading and Exchanges Part 1.
- **To Hasbrouck**: the model in Section III (trade-indicator regression, λ, quote response) is the simplified ancestor of Hasbrouck's VAR/Gibbs-sampler machinery; the Roll estimator's bias discussion (attenuation by λ>0, order-splitting runs, discreteness) is the exact diagnosis Hasbrouck's methods were built to fix. The Gibbs sampler's low-frequency toolkit in the Foucault notes builds on the same realized-vs-effective decomposition (Section IV here).
- **To Biais-Glosten-Spatt**: same genealogy from a different angle. Glosten-Milgrom and Kyle appear here in their minimal closed forms (A-B = π(v^H - v^L); λ = 2(σp²/σu²)^½) — BGS's synthetic model, in symbols. Glosten (1994)'s open LOB dominance result is cited; BGS's stability discussion connects to Stoll's circuit breakers and halts.
- **To Foucault-Pagano-Röell**: FPR's fragmentation/transparency chapters (Ch. 7-8) are the formal theory of Stoll's Section VII: network externalities vs competition, and the transparency trade-off (price discovery vs picked-off limit orders) map one-to-one. The Nasdaq order-handling episode is FPR's worked example in narrative form. Stoll-Whaley/Amihud-Mendelson anticipate FPR Ch. 9's liquidity-in-asset-prices channel.
- **To Bouchaud**: the block-trade asymmetry (sale impact temporary, buy impact permanent) and the imbalance regressions (Section V) are early evidence for the impact laws Bouchaud's group formalized; Stoll's "order splitting induces runs" is the mechanism behind the square-root law's execution context.
- **To Master the Markets (Williams/VSA)**: VSA's "effort vs result" gets a quantitative anchor: temporary impact ≈ 0.70% for block sales (Kraus-Stoll) is *measured* absorption; French-Roll's trading-induced volatility and the intraday U-shape (widest spreads at the open) tell you when volume-based reads carry the most noise (the open) and the least (mid-session).
- **To Dark Pools and The Quants**: Stoll 2001 is the pre-history those books dramatize. The Nasdaq collusion scandal, SOES bandits vs dealer reaction time, payment for order flow (Madoff's third market), price matching, and ATS/ECN rise are all here as dry economics — Dark Pools turns each into a story. The Quants' August 2007 cascade is the negative of Stoll's Section V: what happens when his mild, mean-reverting price impacts meet leverage and become feedback loops.

## Strategy / research candidates (for Andrea's weekly NQ Elliott Wave practice)

1. **A mechanical-noise budget for wave counts**: use Roll-style logic to estimate how much of a short-term swing is bid-ask bounce / inventory overshoot rather than information, and treat sub-noise moves as uncountable. On a weekly chart this mostly validates the discipline of ignoring intraweek wicks when they fail to exceed the noise budget — a quantified version of Balan's rule that only valid, completed counts matter.
2. **Open-gap overshoot as an entry filter**: Stoll-Whaley shows open-to-open returns overshoot and reverse (36¢ vs 3.9¢ implied spread) while close-to-close returns are cleanest. Research rule for NQ: for wave-4 or pullback entries, weight **closing prices over extremes**; treat Monday opens and post-holiday opens as the most overshoot-prone (the futures analogue of the expensive opening auction), and prefer signals taken off weekly closes.
3. **Asymmetric impact awareness in trade direction**: block-sale impact is temporary (liquidity), block-buy impact permanent (information) — the futures equivalent: down-moves on volume in a bull count are more likely mechanical liquidation (VSA "selling climax" logic) while equivalent up-moves carry more information. Test on NQ weekly data whether retrace behavior after high-volume down weeks vs up weeks is systematically different, matching the Kraus-Stoll asymmetry.
4. **Liquidity-as-risk flag**: since illiquidity is priced (Amihud-Mendelson; Brennan-Subrahmanyam), build the Amihud ratio (|return|/dollar volume) into the NQ weekly regime notebook: rising illiquidity alongside a mature wave count raises the odds of the bearish alternative, because the next liquidity event will be priced into a thinner market.
5. **Regime-fragmentation awareness**: Stoll's centralization/fragmentation economics predicts where liquidity migrates when stress hits (toward the primary venue, away from satellites — and, in modern NQ terms, between futures and ETF venues). For a futures trader the actionable version: in high-stress weeks, watch for the "market of markets" effect where ETF-related flows (satellite) and futures flows decouple, briefly corrupting index-based signals like the AVFI indicator.

# Market Microstructure Theory notes — Maureen O'Hara, "Market Microstructure Theory" (1995, Blackwell)

Source: uploaded book "31. market-microstructure-theory-maureen-ohara-pdf-free-2.pdf", 293 pages. Full text studied November 2026. This is the authoritative theoretical foundation text of the stack: the models the empirical and survey books (Harris, Hasbrouck, Madhavan, Biais-Glosten-Spatt, Stoll) reference and the source of the mechanisms behind every order-flow feature used in the strategy programme.

## Why this book matters for the stack

It is the derivation-level source. Harris covers institutions and taxonomy; Hasbrouck covers the econometrics; this book covers why the spread exists, why volume moves prices, why time between trades carries information, and when markets collapse. Every claim in the AVFI/order-flow layer ultimately traces to one of the models here.

## Chapter 2: Inventory models (the spread without asymmetric information)

- Demsetz (1968): immediacy is a service with a price; the spread is the cost of synchronizing traders with different time preferences. Demand/supply for immediacy sets the spread.
- Garman (1976): dealer as gambler against the market. Order arrival is a stochastic process; without a spread the dealer's inventory follows a gambler's ruin and hits a absorbing boundary (bankruptcy or inventory limit) with probability 1. The spread is necessary for dealer survival. First explicit stochastic-process model of the dealer problem.
- Amihud-Mendelson (1980): monotone spread schedule in inventory. Dealer's optimization over a semi-Markov inventory process gives the spread as monotonically increasing in inventory distance from the preferred level; spreads adjust around a preferred position.
- Stoll (1978): supply of dealer services. Cost function for a trade with a dealer whose certainty equivalent requires compensation for inventory risk: c_i = (z/W)·σ²_ip·Q_p + (1/2)·(z/W)·σ²_ip·Q²_p style terms. The transitory price component compensates inventory risk; implied spread depends on trade size and dealer risk aversion. Half-spread = compensation for holding undesirable inventory until it can be unwound at the true price.
- Ho-Stoll (1981): intertemporal inventory optimization with stochastic order flow. Two conclusions: (1) spread decomposes into an adverse-selection-free risk premium plus a markup term; (2) a fixed spread can prevail with prices moving around a true value, the earliest formal version of "spread fixed, mid drifts."
- Copeland-Galai (CMSW): limit-order supply as free options. Quote exposure = writing free options to informed traders; the "hole in the book" is the gravitational attractor for information-motivated flow. Wider spreads are the fee for option writing.
- Ho-Stoll (1983): competitive multi-dealer equilibrium. Spread s = σ²_R·Q with risk tolerance weighting; competition narrows spreads to zero expected profit but only second-best pricing is achieved, not first-best.

Durable for NQ work: inventory effects are real but transitory; in a competitive electronic market the persistent spread component must come from adverse selection, not inventory risk. This is the mechanism justification for treating spread state as an information proxy rather than a dealer-risk proxy.

## Chapter 3: Sequential trade models (information models)

- Bagehot (1971, pseud. Jack Treynor): split the spread into a component for trades with informed (losing) and liquidity (winning) counterparties. The spread is an insurance premium against adverse selection, not a reward for risk bearing.
- Glosten-Milgrom (1985): formal sequential-auction version. Prices are martingales conditional on the sequence of trades; the ask always exceeds the conditional expected value because a buy is informative. Key results:
  - No-trade theorem: absence of trade is itself informative; the bid/ask collapse toward (but never reach) the full-information price.
  - Market collapse: if adverse selection is severe relative to gains from trade, no spread supports a viable market and trading breaks down. The lemons problem (Akerlof) applied to securities.
  - Spread exists even with risk-neutral, competitive, zero-cost market makers. Adverse selection alone suffices.
- Easley-O'Hara (1987): trade size matters. Informed prefer larger trades; market responds by making prices a function of size. Separating equilibria (price fully reveals type) vs pooling equilibria (all sizes pay an adverse-selection premium); pooling is more robust, which is why large trades move prices on average.
- Appendix: Bayesian learning mechanics. Beliefs converge to the full-information value at a geometric rate; entropy measure of the informativeness of trade sequences, I_q(p), and the convergence rate -I_q(p). Speed of adjustment is determined by the information content of the trade flow. This is the formal backbone for the "time is information" results in chapter 6.

## Chapter 4: Strategic models (the informed trader optimizes)

- Kyle (1985): single informed trader chooses quantity knowing his trades move the price. Equilibrium: order flow x = β(v - p0), pricing rule p = p0 + λ·(net order flow). λ (Kyle's lambda, illiquidity) is the price impact per unit of noise-trader standard deviation structure: λ = sqrt of the signal-to-noise ratio in the flow. Half the informed trader's information is impounded in one round of trading. Multi-period versions: informed trades smoothly, prices converge to full information by the final round.
- Back (1992): continuous-time version; the informed trader's optimal strategy requires Brownian order flow and Gaussian value, which is why the equilibrium is fragile to real-world distributions (fat tails, discrete ticks).
- Kyle (1984): informed entry is endogenous; costly information acquisition means prices can be less than fully efficient. Beyond the model: the number of informed traders, not just their behavior, is an equilibrium variable.
- Sequential-auction paradox: strategic behavior is not possible in a truly continuous market (informed demand would be infinitely elastic); discreteness of trading is what makes market power possible.

## Chapter 5: Strategic models with multiple informed traders

- Holden-Subrahmanyam: competition among informed traders makes them trade faster and reveal more; with many informed, prices approach full information almost immediately.
- Admati-Pfleiderer (1988): liquidity traders clumping. Discretionary uninformed traders concentrate their trading when liquidity is highest (open/close in their calibration); informed trading follows the liquidity, so volume and volatility are U-shaped and λ is decreasing in the number of informed n. Predicted pattern: volume/volatility concentrated where noise flow concentrates. This is the theoretical basis for intraday volume profiles (the auction-market layer).
- Spiegel-Subrahmanyam (1992): hedging-based liquidity demand, not noise. Risk-averse hedgers' demands are sensitive to the price; linear Kyle equilibria break; spreads can exceed the usual levels because market makers exploit the price-inelastic hedging demand.
- Foster-Viswanathan: long-lived vs short-lived information; with long-lived information informed traders trade less aggressively early, and returns display serial dependence.

## Chapter 6: Information, time, and volume (most operationally relevant chapter)

- Brown-Jennings (1989), Grundy-McNichols (1989): sequences of prices jointly reveal information that no single price does. The formal rationale for technical analysis: past prices are informative because they aggregate noisy signals. This is the strongest theoretical justification in the book for using price history as an input.
- Blume-Easley-O'Hara (1994): volume as signal. Volume is correlated with signal precision disagreement; the V-shaped relation between volume and price change. Traders who condition on volume and price outperform traders who use price alone. Volume is a statistic of the market, not just a nuisance. This is the single most important theoretical result for the programme's volume/delta features.
- Diamond-Verrecchia (1987): short-sale constraints change the no-trade result; absence of trade then signals possible bad news, so the flow is asymmetric.
- Easley-O'Hara (1992): time between trades is informative. Trade times follow a Poisson process whose rate depends on the state; no-trade intervals lower the spread toward the unconditional value V* and slow price adjustment; volume accelerates it. "Unexpected volume moves prices" because it shifts beliefs about the rate parameter. Durable rule: pace (events per unit time), not just signed size, is an information carrier. Directly supports the volume-clock reasoning in the ORDERFLOW platform spec and the VPT/pace features in the AVFI layer.
- Forster-George, transparency of trade origin: knowledge of who is trading changes prices even without new public information.

## Chapter 7: Market viability and the price process

- Market viability conditions: a trading mechanism is viable only if it recovers the costs of intermediation (order handling, inventory, adverse selection). Monopoly vs competitive provision; Glosten (1989): a monopolistic specialist can set a spread wide enough to guarantee viability, which competitive dealers cannot; monopolist spreads can therefore Pareto-dominate competitive ones when adverse selection is severe.
- Bernhardt-Doyle, price stability: prices can be manipulated by inventory concerns; Slezak-type results on the value of private information in repeated trade.
- Market breakdowns (Bhattacharya-Spiegel): markets can break down not only from adverse selection but from the inability of the mechanism to reveal demand; general-equilibrium existence of trade fails for open-ended parameter regions.

## Chapter 8: Liquidity, block trades, multiple markets

- Liquidity definitions: tightness (spread), depth (size at the quotes), resilience (speed of return to equilibrium). Liquidity is a property of the mechanism, not just the asset.
- Grossman-Miller (1988): liquidity as the cost of immediacy across time; liquidity provision is a production activity; volume arises from traders' desires not to synchronize.
- Pagano (1989): endogenous market thickness. Two-market equilibrium with identical markets is a knife-edge result; scale economies in liquidity give a winner-take-most structure; multiple markets can exist only if transaction costs offset variance differences. With strategic informed traders (Kyle reasoning), persistent cross-market liquidity differences cannot survive.
- Chowdhry-Nanda (1991): multi-market Kyle. Large discretionary traders split orders across markets inversely to price impact; informed trades are perfectly correlated across markets; informativeness Ψ = N / ((N+1) + (σ²_d/σ²)(N-1)) can exceed the Kyle value of 1/2 with multiple venues. Fragmentation can make prices more informative.
- Burdett-O'Hara (1987): block syndication as a search problem with endogenous search costs: every phone call risks moving the price against the trade. Optimal stopping rules: stopping prices rise as syndication succeeds; the trader always keeps some of the block. Better-informed block traders dominate, so block trading consolidates and carries monopoly rents.
- Grossman (1990): upstairs vs downstairs markets. Upstairs market maker knows unexpressed demand; expressed order flow alone may be uninformative enough that the upstairs market dominates and the downstairs market disappears, a suboptimal equilibrium. Explains why futures markets prohibit prearranged trades.
- Subrahmanyam (1991): trading the basket vs the constituents. Diversification of security-specific information makes baskets (index futures) less subject to adverse selection; discretionary flow migrates to the basket, reducing liquidity of the single names. First microstructure treatment of systematic vs idiosyncratic information. Directly relevant to NQ (a basket): index futures spreads should be more inventory-driven and less adverse-selection-driven than single stocks. NQ's deep liquidity and narrow spreads are consistent with this model.
- Kumar-Seppi (1990): futures vs underlying index price gaps; the gap is normally distributed, converges to zero non-monotonically, and mean-reverts; arbitrage with speed advantage widens spreads in both venues.

## Chapter 9: Transparency, anonymity, and market design

- Madhavan (1992): batch (call) markets vs continuous markets. With information asymmetry severe, continuous markets can be non-viable while batch markets are viable: batching nets out noise and reduces adverse-selection exposure per trade. Thin call markets can worsen price formation.
- Pagano-Roell (1993): transparency and the distribution of gains. In transparent markets uninformed traders do weakly better because informed traders cannot hide; but under normality (Kyle assumptions) transparency is irrelevant, and with heterogeneous trade sizes separating equilibria can make small trades cheaper in opaque dealer markets. Post-trade delays (London's 5-day block reporting) create market-maker information monopolies and stale prices, eroding price discovery. Durable rule: transparency helps the uninformed on average, but the effect is distribution-dependent, not a monotone law.
- Dual trading: Roell (1990): dual trading makes identified uninformed traders better off and unidentified ones worse off, but on average reduces asymmetric-information costs; Fishman-Longstaff (1992): if brokers mimic informed customers, banning dual trading improves prices; Pagano-Roell (1992): front running hurts noise traders but, if known, not necessarily the broker's own customers. The common thread: who observes the flow determines who profits.
- Market design: Domowitz's public-interest criteria (reliable price discovery, broad dissemination, effective hedging). O'Hara's closing tension: faster information incorporation requires more informed trading, whose profits are the uninformed's losses; minimizing uninformed costs and maximizing price efficiency are conflicting objectives. "Is a market better if prices reach full-information faster?" is not resolved; markets that adjust rapidly exhibit large volatility. Optimal market design remains open in the literature.

## Distinctive content vs the rest of the stack

- Harris: taxonomy and institutions. O'Hara: the models behind them (gambler's ruin, free options, martingale spreads).
- Hasbrouck: VAR/RF econometrics of the same phenomena. O'Hara: the equilibrium theory that motivates his identifying assumptions.
- Madhavan (2000), Biais-Glosten-Spatt (2005): surveys that post-date and summarize this book; no new model layer beyond it.
- Stoll (1978), Foucault: inventory and dynamic limit-order versions of chapters 2 and 7.
- Trades, Quotes and Prices (Bouchaud): the empirical/statistical-physics layer; complements O'Hara's equilibrium derivations. Read O'Hara as the mechanism prior, Bouchaud as the measured stylized facts.

## Honest assessment

- The book is 1995 pre-electronification: specialists, floor trading, upstairs markets. The mechanisms are venue-independent, but calibration details (batch markets, block syndication) map poorly onto CME order-book-only markets.
- Equilibrium fragility is O'Hara's own repeated theme: results flip with distributional assumptions (normal vs non-normal flow), with who observes what (transparency), and with entry conditions. Any strategy assumption like "order flow is informative" must state which model class it invokes and its failure modes.
- Nothing here is a statistical edge. It is mechanism priors: falsifiable starting points for why a feature could carry information, and for why it might not (pooling vs separating, transparency irrelevance under normality, knife-edge liquidity equilibria).

## Strategy-programme connections

- VPVI / volume-features legitimacy: Blume-Easley-O'Hara 1994 (volume carries signal-precision information) is the theoretical license for volume-based features; Easley-O'Hara 1992 licenses pace/time features. Both also define the null cases where the features carry nothing (pooling equilibria, normal flow).
- Spread-state interpretation: competitive electronic markets push inventory spreads toward zero, so observed persistent spread state is an adverse-selection proxy (Glosten-Milgrom), consistent with VSA/effort-vs-result framing in the Wyckoff addendum of the ORDERFLOW spec.
- NQ as basket (Subrahmanyam): expect lower adverse-selection spreads, higher noise-flow share, and stronger liquidity-clumping (Admati-Pfleiderer) than single names; session U-shapes follow.
- ORDERFLOW platform mapping: Phase 3 feature families (OFI, sweeps, pace) map directly onto Kyle λ, Glosten-Milgrom spread dynamics, and Easley-O'Hara time-between-trades; the platform's null tests are the empirical version of this book's equilibrium-fragility warnings.
- AVFI weekly-bar programme: no direct strategy change; the book confirms the standing discipline that volume and pace features must be justified feature-by-feature against pooled nulls, not assumed informative.
