# James F. Dalton, Robert B. Dalton & Eric T. Jones — Markets in Profile: Profiting from the Auction Process (Wiley Trading, 2007)

Source: full book, 225 PDF pages (193 numbered + appendix/index), full text studied 18 Nov 2026 from the uploaded OCR-clean PDF. The Market Profile layer of the collection: Steidlmayer's CBOT graphic (sponsored by Dalton as Markets and Market Logic, 1986) operationalized by the same authors in Mind Over Markets (1990); this book is the 2007 restatement aimed at absolute-return portfolio management. Charts from CQG and WINdoTRADER. ISBN 978-0-470-03909-0.

Honesty flags, stated up front: ZERO statistics anywhere in the book. No counts, no nulls, no probabilities beyond author's verbal "odds" ("odds based upon logic, not... rules of mathematics"). Every worked example is from a single period (Nov 2005-Oct 2006: S&P 500, T-bonds, gold, IBM, MER, MRK). One genuinely real-time element: the May 2006 S&P top call is documented as made on May 5 (long OTM puts because "risk from the long side was very high"), and Appendix A (written Oct 23, 2006) reports the outcome (8%+ break, then 100% retracement of the break by Oct 18). That is a documented forward call, not a backtest, but n=1 and self-selected for publication. Treat the whole book as a hypothesis generator with encodable definitions, not as evidence.

## The framework in one paragraph

All markets are continuous two-way auctions. Price is the advertisement of opportunity (fastest-moving variable); time regulates each opportunity (the constant, horizontal axis); volume measures each auction's success or failure (gradual variable). Markets alternate between balance (brackets: overlapping value areas, price contained, symmetric risk) and imbalance (trends: value migration, asymmetric opportunity). Market Profile (TPO histogram: 30-minute letters against price) makes the price/value/structure relationship visible. The trader's job is not forecasting but assessing the risk of auction continuation, stagnation, or reversal, and locating trades so that reward exceeds risk (asymmetric opportunities, "the theme of successful investing"). Markets are "not efficient, but effective" (Steidlmayer): price must auction too high before the market discovers it is too high; that overshoot is where asymmetric risk lives.

## Chapter-by-chapter content

Ch 1 (The Only Constant): industry-history frame. ERISA (1974) → consultant/style-box era → relative-return management codified through MPT during the 1982-2000 bull; the fall of the great bull ($31T→$18T global equity, Ineichen/UBS estimate) exposed relative return as a fee machine that "masked poor absolute performance." Absolute return (measure vs risk-free rate, both sides, shorting) is the new requirement. Bracketing markets dominate: Mauldin cited — shortest bear on record eight years, average 16 (a claim to verify, not adopt). Kuhn's paradigm shifts and Gladwell's diffusion spectrum (innovators → laggards) are the book's recurring change model; "once the majority recognizes change, all asymmetric opportunity is lost."

Ch 2 (Information): fundamental information must be viewed in context to have any relevance (5% yield read differently from 7% vs from 3.5%; the Greenspan conundrum). Kahneman: people overweight self-collected information. Market-generated information = the composite of all influences, observable via actual order flow. Fair value = the price levels attracting the greatest volume; markets are only "fair" in the day timeframe. Market Profile construction: TPOs, bell curve, value area = 70% of TPOs (~one standard deviation) computed by the two-price-above/two-below expansion from the POC (full worked example, Figure 2.3). Price within value = fair; outside = opportunity; acceptance (TPO accumulation) vs rejection determines whether value migrates or price reverts. The Dec 2005 S&P bracket example: POC drifting lower while pundits demanded a Santa Claus rally — "volume was indicating a consensus that said something altogether different."

Ch 3 (Timeframes): the five timeframes (scalper, day, short-term 3-5 days, intermediate "swing," long-term) defined by behavior, not clock time (intermediate = distance traveled, not duration). Core mechanism, stated twice through GM/housing analogies: "a buyer for every seller" is trivially true and analytically empty — what matters is WHICH TIMEFRAME holds the inventory/volume. Inventory held by short timeframes forces price lower; long money is "sticky." Long-term investors break new ground and start trends precisely because they place large one-directional orders while shorter timeframes merely facilitate. Timeframe mix per information type (scalpers order flow; day traders market-generated only; long-term fundamental-first). Bracket/trend is timeframe-relative: a trend to a scalper is noise to the long-term investor. "Your timeframe is your strategy cornerstone."

Ch 4 (Auctions and Indicators): the art-auction parable establishes the day mechanics: opening too high shuts off activity, auction lowers until first response, then value builds where bidders engage, and the final lone bid = excess. Key indicators with encodable definitions: (1) Value-area comparison across days (higher/lower/overlapping/inside/outside — six relationships); (2) Initial balance (first two periods' range); (3) Range extension (auction beyond IB; direction + whether new volume follows gauges buyer/seller strength); (4) Attempted direction (which way the market tried to auction; if undeterminable, volume analysis is void and the day was balanced); (5) Volume transparency (price×time proxy pre-electronic; real volume now): rising price + rising volume = value rising; rising price + falling volume = auction losing momentum; declining price + rising volume = bearish; declining price + falling volume = neutral/bullish; (6) Symmetry: symmetric profile = balance, symmetric risk (the closest markets get to EMH); elongated = trend (multiple timeframes in concert); upper-tail-heavy asymmetry = short covering by limited timeframes; lower-heavy = long liquidation. Attempted breakouts on decreasing volume revert to the mean; on increasing volume, new aggressive participants and new value areas. A "correction" need not be a price decline — new long-term buyers can absorb short-term sellers at flat-to-higher prices (old longs replaced by new longs).

Ch 5 (Long-Term Auctions): the compound-auction process: trends do not reverse directly into opposite trends; they transition through balancing ranges (S&P Oct 1998-May 2006 worked example: Mar 2000 top → 7-month balance → Oct 2000 decline → Oct 2002 low → balance → May 2003 breakout → uptrend). Bracket = market "fair" again to both long-term sides; responsive selling at the upper extreme and responsive buying at the lower extreme contain it. Bracket-breakout odds: heavy volume at an extreme → containment likely to fail; low volume → containment affirmed. Volume as force/gravity: escaping a developed distribution with a well-defined mean requires excess volume-force, else price is pulled back. UBS worked example: three-year $35-59 bracket, four-month narrow balance, breakout to $122. Long-term strategy: run the same profile analysis on market/sector/single-name/ETF; the greatest risk is confirmation bias ("cherry-picking positive information"). Nonlinearity warning: once a market leaves balance, price moves fast, liquidity shrinks, opportunity is lost; the balancing process takes months, which is the window for repositioning large portfolios.

Ch 6 (Intermediate-Term Auctions): the trend-aging anatomy, all encodable: (1) trend = staircase of successively higher balance areas; trend aging = shrinking distance between successive balance areas, late-stage balance areas overlapping the prior ones; (2) trend ends two ways — volume drying up in the trend direction (buying exhaustion: four highs within one point before the S&P's 3.62% slide) or excess: a dramatic extreme on LOW volume, quickly and aggressively rejected; often accompanied by a gap (reorganization of beliefs: "the laggards are finally all in"); (3) the capitulation myth, stated explicitly and contrarian: the excess spike itself is made on light volume; the heavy volume people misremember as capitulation belongs to the counter-auction; (4) countertrend auctions as strong as or stronger than with-trend auctions = aging signal; (5) late-trend volatility rises without directional progress. The May 2006 real-time sequence: new life-of-contract highs May 5 on 1.6B shares (bottom of the 1.6-2B normal range) — "the low volume that accompanied the upside breakout was the bell"; countertrend strength, poor volume distribution, overlapping balances since December; May 11 nonlinear break; -8.18% S&P futures by June 13 while experts forecast 12,000 Dow. Trend channels ~5% width, 8-10% when volatility expands. Bracketed markets absorb news (big announcements barely dent trends but jolt brackets; news often merely the final trigger of a bracket→trend transition). Estimated: markets bracket "in excess of 75 percent of the time" — an eyeball estimate, flag as unverified. "Long-only, fully invested" is trend following whether or not it is sold as investing.

Ch 7 (Short-Term Trading): trade location as risk control: buy at/below value, sell above value; probability ≠ risk/reward (a 75%-odds continuation can still be a short if remaining upside is 3% against 20% downside — auction-stage logic). Mind traps: repetition ≠ prediction; averages of profile patterns are the freezer/oven fallacy; "everything is always different." Technical indicators used only as reaction-reference points (200-day MA: volume increasing into it → it will be taken out; decreasing → it holds; May-June 2006 example worked). Trades catalogued: breakouts from balance (especially after an inside day; go with it, monitor continuation), fading balance-area extremes on decreasing volume (the ad at that price level unsupported), bracket breakouts (multiple timeframes converge; volatility rises). "I will fade a balance area extreme if volume doesn't support the auction's advertisement at that price level." Long day-by-day forensic walkthrough of the two countertrend auctions after the May 2006 break, establishing: b shape = long liquidation with patient longer-timeframe buyers accumulating (sell conviction absent; short should be exited even in profit); p shape = short covering met by longer-timeframe sellers; late spikes after b/p = auction completion; spike resolution rule — low-volume spike → price returns to value; heavy-volume spike → value migrates to price; inside/balancing (symmetric) day → trade with the first directional auction away from it; trend day → never fade, ideally join; one auction against the longer-term trend is less significant than the same auction with it; "clean break" vs unclean (break of a reference that doesn't immediately continue = early warning of auction exhaustion); unfilled gaps and prior balance-area edges as reference points; laggard short (long) = weak money, last in first out; the reversal-read: nonelongated, fat profiles while price still grinds lower = market struggling = patient accumulation underneath. Gold 2006: speculative trend without balancing staircases = no structural support on reversal; "elevator stops" analogy for balance areas as memory; advice: in markets with no structural support, use options not outrights. Steenbarger: "Where the novice thinks concretely, the expert reasons contextually."

Ch 8 (Day Trading Is for Everyone): "everyone is a day trader on the day they enter or exit a trade"; even institutional fill-vs-work decisions are day-trading problems. Noise debate: Steidlmayer/Dalton — "every trade occurs to satisfy some condition of the market," no such thing as noise (vs Fischer Black's noise-trading quote, which Dalton prints against himself). Homework protocol (trending market): previous day's volume, past excesses/gaps, past balance ranges from shortest to longest, prominent POCs (unusually wide lines = gravitational centers likely revisited). Nontrending/volatile markets: add daily/weekly highs-lows, trend lines, correlated markets; peripheral influences matter more without conviction. Never trade in front of economic releases (five listed failure modes of anticipation). Crystallized vs fluid intelligence (Cohen); information overload degrades fluid intelligence; no mechanical system that worked would be purchasable. The July 5-6 2006 T-bond case: ADP 386k forecast vs 175k consensus; gap lower, b formation (long liquidation), close 7 tics off a 3-month bracket low, 68% of volume in the lower range → 68% of shorts under water → next-day squeeze; also the scoreboard: all 56 economists missed inflation; ADP missed jobs by ~300k. Top-down checklist: drivers (fundamental/economic/stock-specific), conviction (yes/no), flight to safety (during which "price and volume simply don't matter" until unwinding), market condition (trend age via balance-area spacing; inventory too long/too short; bracket position by quadrants), inventory imbalances (multiple failed lows without excess = too short to go lower; "market needs to rally before it can break"; shorts are potential buying power; the mirror: too long to go higher), old vs new business (short covering and long liquidation are old business: no net new positioning; "long-only, fully invested" = trend-following). IBM bracket example: stop runs just beyond balance-area extremes — push beyond the extreme that instantly dries up = day-trader stop raid, fade it back into the bracket; acceptance beyond = real breakout, go with it; intra-bracket trends run extreme to extreme, don't fade them. Yesterday's trade: four elements (attempted direction, value-area placement, shape, volume) + three worked examples (balancing day failing both directions; b-then-p with stop-run below multi-day low reversed by a single-print buying tail; gap + elongation + 1.8B volume + close near low → continuation). Openings taxonomy (Steidlmayer): Open-Drive (highest confidence; if price returns to the open, something changed — lowest odds of revisiting opening prices; context modulation: with-trend-and-out-of-balance Open-Drive = large move, against-trend-within-balance = moderate), Open-Test-Drive (test beyond a reference fails, reversal through the opening; failed test often secures the day's extreme; second most reliable), Open-Rejection-Reverse (opposite response strong enough to drive back through the opening range; check tail speed: one period = confident, two = not), Open-Auction (no conviction; inside previous range/value → normal-range day; outside → out of balance, dramatic move either way; no reason to trade the first five periods). One-timeframing defined (no subsequent period takes out the prior period's low in an up-auction): a costly error is fading one-timeframing. Exit philosophy: exit on deteriorating structure, not stops — "when a stop is triggered, the market is responsible; when you exit on structure, you are in charge." Day trader's checklist reproduced in full (yesterday's clues, overnight, expected open vs prior range/value, three reference points each side, opening type, attempted direction + volume support, in/out of balance, projected shape, competitors' inventory, flat before major numbers).

Ch 9 + Appendix A: timeframe diversification = long/short portfolio (the State Street 70/30 long-short mandate, rebalance at 25/35% shorts; +5.44% vs benchmark over the May break, ~$0.5B book). Behavioral information exists alongside fundamental information: behavioral forces (herd, momentum, forced flows) drive price away from fundamental value, and the fundamentalists' own reaction to mispricing is the mean-reversion mechanism. Siegel's "new paradigm" quote (prices subject to temporary noise shocks obscuring true value) adopted as the book's thesis statement. All decisions under uncertainty; the well-positioned minority profits from the majority's actions (Neill). Appendix: the May 2006 break fully retraced by Oct 18 2006; the market was bracketing, not bear — consistent with the book's own transition model.

## Encodable rule set (for any future platform work; nothing here is adopted without a dated signed amendment)

- EXCESS: extreme + volume in the low tail of its N-day distribution + fast rejection in the following period(s). The book's flagship reversal signal. Contradicts popular climax-on-heavy-volume doctrine; encodes cleanly on daily OHLCV+volume.
- VOLUME-AT-REFERENCE: volume conditioned on distance from value (or from a balance-area edge) predicts continuation vs reversion: increasing volume into/beyond a reference → violation likely; decreasing → containment/reversion. This is VSA's effort-vs-result and the 200-MA rule in one general law.
- VALUE-MIGRATION: rolling value area (needs intraday or daily approximations from OHLCV); overlapping/higher/lower/inside/outside as a categorical state series; trends = persistent migration, brackets = reversion to a stable area.
- BALANCE-BREAKOUT: inside/balancing day → next directional auction is with-trade; breakout on volume ≈ or > recent range → continuation; breakout on low volume → fade candidate.
- ONE-TIMEFRAMING: period-over-period higher lows (or lower highs) sequence; do not fade while active.
- P/B SHAPES: p = short covering (sell into it late), b = long liquidation (buy into it late); late-day spikes after p/b = completion.
- STOP-RUN FADE: break of a balance extreme that dries up within k periods and re-enters the bracket = raid; acceptance beyond = breakout.
- OPEN-TYPE CLASSIFIER: four-way open taxonomy + location relative to prior value (in/out of balance) as first-period state variable.
- INVENTORY-STATE: too long/too short as an inference from failed auctions and repeated tests without excess ("rally before it can break").
- Prominent-POC magnetism: unusually wide TPO lines are revisited with above-average odds (testable on any TPO-reconstructable series).

## Assessment and contradictions

- vs Black/noise: Dalton prints Black's noise-trading passage and rejects the concept (Steidlmayer's "every trade satisfies a condition"). The microstructure canon (O'Hara's uninformed/liquidity traders, Glosten-Milgrom noise traders) sides with Black: some flow has no information content. Dalton's position is best read as "even liquidity trades satisfy an inventory condition," which the canonical models agree with — the disagreement is about information, not motivation. Not a real contradiction once translated; flag for the contradictions log.
- vs VSA/Wyckoff on climax volume: Williams's selling climax and Forte's SC are high-volume absorption events; Dalton's excess is a LOW-volume terminal spike with the heavy volume arriving in the counter-auction. These are different moments of the same sequence (absorption phase vs terminal overshoot), and both are encodable; the distinction belongs in any Wyckoff/VSA feature overlap test. Also Dalton's b-shape long-liquidation read vs VSA's "no demand": related but different constructs (inventory vs effort).
- The 75% bracketing and Mauldin's 8/16-year bear claims: unsourced estimates, flag as unverified.
- The book's entire empirical base is one year of one equity market plus three spot examples; every "odds" statement is author intuition from ~40 years of screen time. The framework's components are individually testable (list above), and the value-area/time-at-price construct is mathematically clean (a TPO profile is a histogram of time spent at price; the value area is a ~1-SD region of that distribution).
- Consistency with the collection: the bracket/trend alternation is Diebold-Inoue-compatible (long durations in each state mimicking persistence), and the balance→breakout→staircase anatomy is the same structure as the Elliott base's correction/impulse alternation and Wyckoff's campaign phases. The "which timeframe holds the inventory" question is an informal multi-horizon version of Harris's timeframe inventory logic and Stoll's dealer inventory cycle, moved from the dealer to the whole market. The value area is a time-clock: first cousin of the ELO volume clock (sampling by activity rather than calendar), and the acceptance/rejection rule is the OHLCV-observable shadow of CKS's OFI-impact relation.

Links: [Balan Elliott base](elliott-wave-balan.md) (brackets as corrections, trends as impulses, breakout = impulse initiation; Dalton's aging rules as objective wave-exhaustion proxies), [Williams VSA](master-the-markets-williams.md) (effort vs result instrumented; the volume-at-reference law is shared; the climax-volume distinction recorded above), [Forte Wyckoff](wyckoff-anatomy-trading-range-forte.md) and [Pruden](wyckoff-nine-tests-pruden.md) (accumulation/distribution campaigns vs auction ends; stop-run fade = spring/UTAD mechanics; the 3:1 gates match Dalton's asymmetry objective), [O'Hara](market-microstructure-theory-ohara.md) and [Harris](market-microstructure-harris.md) (the formal inventory/information models underneath the timeframe taxonomy; noise-trader debate), [Stoll](market-microstructure-stoll.md) (inventory transitory vs information permanent ≈ b/p old-business vs new-business), [CKS OFI](orderflow-cont-kukanov-stoikov-2014-ofi.md) (acceptance = flow response at price), [ELO volume clock](orderflow-easley-lopezdeprado-ohara-2012-vpin-volume-clock.md) (value area as activity-clock sampling; TPO time-at-price as the poor man's volume), [Hamilton](regime-hamilton-1989-markov-switching.md)/[Diebold-Inoue](regime-diebold-inoue-2001-long-memory.md)/[Maheu-McCurdy](regime-maheu-mccurdy-2000-bull-bear.md) (bracket/trend as duration-dependent regime states; Dalton's transition = their switching), [Bai-Perron](regime-bai-perron-structural-breaks.md) (bracket→trend breaks as structural change; WATCH's buffer detector as the streaming analog), [CMT/Aronson](cmt-level3-curriculum.md) (Dalton's explicit rejection of mechanical rules is the mirror image of Aronson's null-default requirement: every Dalton "odds" statement is an untested family member until scored), [The Quants](the-quants-patterson.md) (Dalton's Thorp anecdote; model-risk contrast), [Cartea/Jaimungal/Penalva](algorithmic-high-frequency-trading-cartea-jaimungal-penalva.md) and [Johnson](algorithmic-trading-dma-johnson.md) (inventory imbalance = the order-execution side of the same condition), and the AVFI programme (value-migration as concept vs AVFI's measured job: the AVFI verdicts stand untouched; any Dalton-derived feature is a new pre-registration, not a reopening).

# Master the Markets (Tom Williams, VSA) — study notes

Foundation course in **Volume Spread Analysis (VSA)**: reading the joint behaviour of price, spread (bar range) and volume to infer the activity of professional operators ("smart money": market-makers, specialists, syndicate traders). Cause of price moves = supply/demand imbalance created by professionals; VSA seeks to read the cause from the bar chart. Volume shows *activity*; the spread shows the *result* (where professionals let the price close). Volume must always be read relatively (vs the previous ~30 bars), never in isolation. Core inversion: **true strength appears on down-bars, true weakness appears on up-bars**.

## Market worldview
- Markets appear chaotic, not random; they move on supply/demand, but not the naive way: a market rises not because buying exceeds selling, but because there is *no substantial selling to stop the up-move* (buying already happened at lower levels during accumulation). A bear market falls because there is *insufficient professional buying/support*, not because selling exceeds buying. This explains why **bear markets fall faster than bull markets rise**.
- **Perceived value vs intrinsic value**: trade on perceived value to professionals, not fundamental value. Indices rise long-term partly because creators weed out weak constituents.
- Professionals see both sides of the book (orders, stops, large blocks). Retail traders do not. Professionals exploit the herd's two fears: fear of losses and fear of missing out. No smoke-filled-room cartel: no single player controls a liquid market for long; professionals simply share aims and act on the same windows of opportunity (bad news, thin trading, late day, pre-holiday).
- Markets can be temporarily marked up/down (gaps, up-thrusts, shake-outs, tests) to trap traders, catch stops, and improve professional entry prices. Low volume marks a non-genuine move; the path of least resistance is the opposite direction.
- Cash index sets limits for futures (arbitrage channel), but futures often move first because professionals hedge/position there first. High option/futures volume = professional activity; never ignore abnormal volume anywhere in related markets.
- London rule (historical): market-makers could withhold large-trade info for 90 minutes — they hide volume, not price, because "the price gives you fear or hope, the volume gives you the facts".

## Core concepts and signals (durable reference)

### Accumulation & distribution
- **Accumulation**: buying as much floating supply as possible without putting the price up; after a down-move. Classic signs: stock reluctant to fall when the Index falls; low volume on rallies; sudden increased-volume selling hits every small rally (more bought than sold); ends with a test or shake-out, then a bull move. "Battery" analogy: accumulation stores energy for the move; capacity measurable as a point-and-figure count for a price objective.
- **Distribution**: selling large holdings at specified price ranges without putting the price down; market-makers support price, sell on each wave up. Early distribution = high volume on up-moves (supply swamping demand); late distribution = low-volume up-moves (no demand). After distribution completes, a bear market is "guaranteed" (no support).
- **Strong vs weak holders**: bull market = substantial transfer of stock from weak holders to strong holders (at a loss to the weak); bear market = transfer from strong to weak (at a profit to the strong). Weak holders are undercapitalised, locked-in, hope-driven, shaken out on bad news; strong holders cut losses quickly, treat losses as business expense, may have more losing trades than winners but winners dominate P&L.
- **Buying climax**: bull → bear imbalance. Ultra-high volume, wide (or narrow) spread up-bars into new high ground with price closing well off the highs; after a substantial bull market. Public/funds buying, professionals selling. Rare, hard to see because news is euphoric.
- **Selling climax**: bear → bull. Ultra-wide spread down, exceptionally high volume, usually closing on/near the highs, on bad news. Public panic selling absorbed by professionals. Add bullishness if news is bad.
- **Resistance & floating supply**: old trading ranges hold locked-in traders who want to sell at breakeven = latent supply = resistance. Supply must be removed (accumulated) before a rally can succeed. Herd instinct makes crowds predictable; professionals use it.

### Effort vs results
- Effort = wide spread + increased volume; result = follow-through in the next bar(s).
- High volume up-bar with *no result* (next bar level/down) = the high volume contained more selling than buying = weakness that persists ("markets must rest after high volume up-days"). Effort with a positive result confirms professionals are not selling.
- Accelerator analogy: price moving up on low volume is a car going uphill with no throttle — momentum only, not genuine.

### Key named signals
- **No demand**: low volume, narrow spread up-bar; especially after weakness in background. Rally cannot be genuine; short/exit signal.
- **No selling pressure / no supply**: down-bars on volume lower than the previous two bars, narrow spread, closing middle/high = no professional selling = strength.
- **Test** (the most important low-volume buy signal): mark-down during the bar, dipping into a previous high-volume area, recovering to close on/near the high, on **low** volume = supply has been removed = expect higher prices immediately. High-volume test = failed = supply still present, expect retest ("W"/double bottom). A test that does not respond within a day or so (in a bear market) is a bearish indication. Failed tests are one of the five supply signs.
- **Up-thrust**: wide spread up during the bar, close on/near the LOW, after a rise, with weakness in background. Volume low (= no demand, trap) or high (= supply overcoming demand). Rarely seen in strong markets; classic top / short-the-turn signal. Designed to catch short stops above and entice breakout buyers.
- **Absorption volume**: very high volume down-bar closing on the HIGH (or next bar up) = professionals absorbing the selling = strength; typically ends a down-trend; needs a following accumulation phase before a real rally.
- **Stopping volume**: high volume down-day closing on the highs (or next day up) = big buy orders stopping the move; subsequent low-volume test = buy signal.
- **Shake-out**: sudden wide spread down, usually on bad news, engineered to panic weak holders into selling stock back to professionals; seen after a bull move has been running (vs selling climax: behind a shake-out there is no bear market, behind a selling climax there is). Gap down then gap up next day is the telltale; expect higher prices.
- **Falling pressure**: wide spread down on low volume closing on the low = few sellers left; not a buy alone, but market unlikely to decline much further; buy signal if closing on the high into old support. Caution: low-volume down-days also occur *early* in bear markets — check background.
- **Professional support (reverse up-thrust)**: mini selling climax; wide spread down into low ground closing on/near the highs on high volume; more reliable if gapped down then next day gapped up; low-volume down-day after = strength.
- **The five supply signs that stop a rally** (essential list): 1) buying climax; 2) failed test (not on low volume); 3) narrow spread + high volume on an up-day into new high ground; 4) up-thrust; 5) sudden high volume up-bar with next bar down, wide spread, closing below the prior bar's low.
- **Signs of strength** (bottoms): successful test (low volume, close on highs); reaction into a previous high-volume area now on low volume; stopping volume; shake-out. Strong background = no distribution behind (no up-thrusts, no high-volume up-days without progress, no mushroom rounding over).
- **End-of-rally/market-top signal**: up-day + high volume + narrow spread + new high ground; each element essential. Narrow spread = market-makers capping the top while selling into demand.
- Gapping up through resistance on wide spreads with rising volume = strength (designed to lock you OUT, not in); weak gap-ups (low volume, early-day, into new highs on good news) = trap to suck you IN.

### Trends & VSA
- Markets trend only ~30% of the time. Trend lines: uptrend = two lows + intervening high; downtrend = two highs + intervening low. Use as gauges, not mechanical rules.
- **Higher bottoms** = medium-term strength; **higher lows** (each bar) = short-term strength (professionals supporting); lower tops/lower highs symmetric (weakness). First lower top in a bull move / first higher bottom in a bear move may be the first trend-change clue.
- Trend properties are **fractal/scale-free** (coastline analogy): a short-term trend on a weekly chart is an intermediate trend on a daily chart. The intermediate trend is most useful with VSA.
- Overbought/oversold defined by trend channel: above the supply line = overbought, below the support line = oversold; more reliable than traditional oscillators. Mid-range = no vulnerability, anything can happen.
- Broken trend line flips: resistance becomes support (and vice versa); the longer prices stay beyond the line, the stronger the new resistance. Volume near the line decides: approaching a line on low volume = unlikely to break; wide spread + high volume approaching = likely to break; high-volume effort at the line with no result = line holds.
- **Trend clusters**: old trend lines converging at the live edge mark real resistance (hedgerow, not wall; gaps between clusters are windows of opportunity). Closing prices tend to avoid clusters.
- Perceived-value model: trend lines approximate the average pain/gain (breakeven, stop, panic points) of the thousands of locked-in, profitable and watching traders.

### Trader discipline (Section 4)
- No magic systems exist; "secret systems" work because of the trader's skill, not the formula. News is mostly hype, often manufactured or timed (late Friday/holiday mark-ups force weekend narratives); never let news override volume facts; the market is never trend-changed by news — good news clusters at tops (helps distribution), bad news at bottoms (helps accumulation/shake-outs).
- System requirements: get out fast when wrong; risk management = loss management; money management = profit management; cut losses, let profits run. Expect losses; ~90% of traders lose.
- Always plan, write the plan down, and have plan B ready before entry. Don't fix future price targets in your mind (they blind you to contrary signals).
- Timing is the key skill: short only on an up-day after background weakness; buy on reactions on low volume in strong markets; bull markets run longer than you think; bear markets fall until the shake-out.
- Stops: place under the last reaction low (a resistance area professionals must spend effort to break); avoid even numbers (pros gun for them); thousands of stops sit just above/below active markets.
- Stock selection: only index constituents (guaranteed professional interest); fundamentals are already priced; benchmark against the Parent Index — buy stocks **acting stronger than the Index** (reluctant to fall on index reactions, reverse first off lows), short those acting weaker (reluctant to rally). Stocks rotate on lows; the index churns. Weekly charts make individual stocks clearer; use daily for entry timing.
- Psychology: you are the cat with nine lives; the objective is minimum losses and fast exits, not maximum wins; after losses, stop trading and study the market away from fear.

## Connections to the Elliott Wave base

- **VSA supplies the micro-mechanism behind wave development**: Elliott's corrective waves and "structures" map naturally onto Williams' accumulation/distribution ranges (wave-4 style re-accumulation, bases, mushroom tops), while VSA's named signals (tests, up-thrusts, shake-outs, climaxes) describe how wave terminations are engineered and confirmed bar-by-bar. A selling climax maps well onto the end of a wave-C/ending-diagonal-type capitulation; a buying climax onto a fifth-wave blow-off; up-thrusts onto B-wave traps.
- **Fifth-wave failure verification**: the book's top signals (no demand into new high ground, up-thrust, high volume up-bar with no follow-through) are exactly the volume signatures to expect at a truncated fifth wave or a wave-5/ending-diagonal termination. For Andrea's NQ weekly work: a weekly bar that makes a new high on narrow spread + ultra-high volume, or an up-thrust week closing near the low, is direct evidence against extending a fifth-wave count.
- **Fractal trends = Elliott's nested degrees**: the book's explicit scale-free trend property (short-term on weekly = intermediate on daily) is the same nesting principle as Elliott's degrees; both frameworks warn against mixing timeframes inconsistently. Use the weekly chart for the wave count, daily/intraday VSA signals for entry timing (the book's own multi-timeframe advice).
- **Background matters = wave context matters**: both methods forbid reading single bars/waves in isolation; a successful test after a selling climax is a buy, the same test after distribution means little — just as a zigzag in wave-4 position differs from the same pattern in a B-wave position.
- **Herd psychology and the two fears** give the behavioural engine for crowd Elliott patterns: truncations and overshoots happen where stops cluster; expect mark-ups/downs around obvious wave-channel boundaries and even-number levels.
- **Validation humility**: Williams' "90% lose", "no magic systems", and years-long learning curve agree with Bouchaud's 9-year statistical validation rule — position sizing and loss control dominate signal quality for a weekly-chart trader with limited hours (Andrea's 10-20h/week cap).
- **AVFI indicator link (trading-research)**: Andrea's Pine "AVFI v3 vs v2" indicator is precisely a relative-volume tool in the VSA spirit; VSA's signal set (no demand, tests, up-thrusts, absorption volume) is a candidate ruleset to layer on top of the AVFI spread axis for signal classification.

## Forward links for the strategy end-goal
- Candidate strategy building block: weekly Elliott Wave count for direction, VSA signals on daily bars for entries/exits (test after accumulation at wave-4 lows; no demand/up-thrust at fifth-wave highs), stops under last reaction low, odd-number levels.
- Cross-check with Bouchaud notes: VSA "effort vs results" is a practitioner version of price impact decay; "high volume with no result" ≈ impact absorbed by opposing latent liquidity; "markets fall faster than they rise" is a stylised fact consistent with skewness of returns.

## Durable rules & ratios (quick reference)
- Markets trend ~30% of the time. ~90% of traders lose.
- Volume read relatively vs ~30 previous bars; judge close position within the bar (high = strength context, low = weakness on up-bars).
- Strength on down-bars, weakness on up-bars.
- Five supply signs (listed above); four strength signs at bottoms (test, low-volume retest of high-volume area, stopping volume, shake-out).
- Bull moves slower than bear moves; tops rotate slowly, bottoms reverse in clusters (selling climax/shake-out exceptions).
- Stops: under last reaction low, odd numbers; short on up-days, buy on down-days/reactions with low volume.
- Compare stock to Parent Index; strength = reluctance to fall, weakness = reluctance to rally.

# Mathematics of Financial Markets (Elliott & Kopp, 2nd ed.) — study notes

This book is the rigorous-mathematics counterpart to Björk: it builds discrete-time arbitrage theory from first principles (with full proofs), then develops continuous-time theory, and closes with risk measures and shortfall-risk hedging. What it adds that Björk does not: complete proofs of both Fundamental Theorems in finite discrete time (separating hyperplanes, Kreps-Yan), the (d+1)^T bound showing completeness is mathematically exceptional, Snell-envelope optimal stopping in full generality, the American put free-boundary treatment, term-structure models through Markov-chain HMM calibration, and the Artzner-Delbaen-Eber-Heath coherent-risk-measure theory with Föllmer-Leukert quantile/efficient hedging.

## Chapter 1: Pricing by Arbitrage (single period)

The single-period model with one riskless asset (return r) and one risky asset. A trading strategy (θ^0, θ^1) costs θ^0 + θ·S_0 now and pays θ^0(1+r) + θ·S_1 later.

Arbitrage: a strategy with V_0 ≤ 0, V_1 > 0 somewhere, and V_1 ≥ 0 always. The Law of One Price: if two portfolios have identical payoffs in every state, they must have the same price now.

Forward pricing: F(t,T) = S_t e^{r(T-t)} follows by a cash-and-carry argument with zero initial cost. This is the model for index futures: the fair futures value is spot grown at the carry (r minus dividend yield q: F = S e^{(r−q)(T−t)}).

The minimal-variance hedge of a payoff H using the risky asset holds θ = cov(H,S_1)/var(S_1) and leaves residual risk var(H)(1−ρ²). The market is complete exactly when |ρ| = 1: only then can every payoff be replicated.

Binomial (two-state) risk-neutral probability: q = ((1+r)S_0 − S_a)/(S_b − S_a), the unique probability making the discounted stock a martingale. Option price = discounted expectation under q, never under the real-world probability.

Elasticity of an option H: E_H = (S_0/H_0)θ, so σ_H = E_H σ_S and μ_H − r = E_H(μ_S − r). A call on a non-dividend stock has E_H ≥ 1: options are leveraged stock, more volatile than the underlying, and their expected excess return scales by the same elasticity. This discrete relation has an exact continuous analogue in Chapter 7 (equation 7.72) and is the cleanest sizing discipline for leveraged instruments.

The Capinski counterexample: with a risk-averse pricing rule the option price need not be monotone in volatility. "More volatility, more option value" is a theorem only under linear (risk-neutral) pricing.

Incomplete single-period market (three states, two assets): an interval of equivalent martingale measures (EMMs) exists, and the option's admissible prices form an interval, here (1, 1.6). Arbitrage pins prices into bands, not to points; anything strictly inside the band admits no arbitrage either way. This is the honest mathematical statement of why real markets can disagree about value without anyone being wrong.

## Chapter 2: Martingale Measures (multi-period discrete)

The securities market model: finite time set, finite state space, information filtration (F_t), price processes, and trading strategies. Self-financing means wealth changes only through trading gains: V_t = V_0 + G_t where G_t is the gains (martingale transform) process. Admissible strategies keep V_t ≥ 0.

Key theorem: a strategy is self-financing if and only if the discounted wealth process satisfies the same identity; and the discounted gains process is a martingale transform of the discounted price process under any EMM. Martingale transforms are martingales (the discrete analogue of "the stochastic integral of a predictable process against a martingale is a martingale"), which is why discounted gains have zero expectation under any EMM: no betting system beats a fair game.

Pricing: π(H) = E_Q(β_T H) for an attainable claim H, independent of the replicating strategy (Law of One Price at the level of strategies). Super-replication bounds: π_s(H) ≥ E_Q(β_T H) ≥ π_b(H); the seller's price dominates every EMM expectation, which dominates the buyer's price. In an incomplete market, any price in (π_b, π_s) is arbitrage-free.

Modigliani-Miller: equity is a call option on the firm's assets with strike the debt face value; debt holders hold the assets minus that call. Capital-structure irrelevance is arbitrage logic, not economics.

CRR convergence to Black-Scholes: u_N = (1 + rT/N)e^{σ√(T/N)}, d_N its reciprocal; the binomial price converges by the triangular-array central limit theorem, and the limits of the binomial probabilities are the Φ(d_±) terms. The Black-Scholes replicating strategy in the limit: θ^0 = −K e^{−rT} Φ(d_−) in bonds and θ^1 = Φ(d_+) in stock: at inception, hold Φ(d_+) shares financed partly by borrowing the discounted strike weighted by exercise probability. The option's price does not depend on the stock's drift μ: only volatility and the riskless rate enter. (Economically, drift is replaced by r under the martingale measure; the hedge removes directional risk.)

## Chapter 3: The First Fundamental Theorem

Finite market viable (no arbitrage) iff an EMM exists (Theorem 3.2.2, proved in full). The engine is the separating hyperplane theorem: the cone of gains from self-financing strategies with zero initial cost either intersects the positive orthant (arbitrage) or can be separated from it by a positive linear functional; that functional normalized is the EMM density.

Local (one-step) versus global arbitrage: no global arbitrage iff no single-step arbitrage. Geometric form: no arbitrage at step t iff today's discounted price S_{t-1} lies strictly inside the convex hull of the possible next-period prices. The EMM can be built step by step, conditional probability by conditional probability, which is why binomial trees work: at each node the risk-neutral probability is the normalized barycentric coordinate of today's price in the convex hull of child prices.

Example: with r ≥ 1/8 in a particular two-step market, viability breaks (the stock price escapes the convex hull), so interest rates themselves constrain no-arbitrage. The Dalang-Morton-Willinger theorem extends everything to arbitrary finite markets; the Kreps-Yan theorem extends to infinite time via the separating functional on closed cones.

Change of numeraire: dQ̃/dQ = S̄_t^1/S̄_0^1; using a traded asset as unit of account gives an equivalent measure under which the chosen asset is the natural martingale benchmark. This small lemma grows into the forward-measure machinery of Chapter 9.

## Chapter 4: Complete Markets

A market is complete iff every martingale under Q is a martingale transform of the discounted price (martingale representation). Second Fundamental Theorem: a finite viable market is complete iff the EMM is unique. Proof sketch: uniqueness forces every claim's Q-expectation to be a martingale, hence representable; conversely two EMMs would give two prices for some claim.

Harrison-Kreps splitting index K(t,A) counts how many distinct successor nodes are needed to support a conditional price; completeness requires enough distinct successor prices at every node. The d-dimensional independent random walk model is incomplete: it has many EMMs.

In incomplete markets, the arbitrage-free prices of a non-attainable claim form an open interval Π(H) = (π_−, π_+), where the endpoints are sup/inf of EMM expectations.

Theorem 4.6.1 is the punchline: a complete viable finite market over T periods with d risky assets has at most (d+1)^T states. Completeness is mathematically exceptional: real markets (continuum of states) are incomplete, and option pricing there is always a matter of criteria beyond arbitrage. This is the formal license for the entire risk-measure/efficient-hedging programme of Chapter 11, and the reason Javaheri's stochastic-volatility world needs a pricing criterion, not just a measure change.

## Chapter 5: Discrete-time American Options and Optimal Stopping

The tools: stopping times, optional sampling for bounded stopping times and for uniformly integrable martingales, the Doob decomposition (submartingale = martingale + increasing predictable), and quadratic variation of martingales.

The Snell envelope: for an adapted reward process X_t, define Z_t = esssup E(X_τ | F_t) over stopping times τ ≥ t. Z is the smallest supermartingale dominating X. Optimal stopping theory:

- τ* = min{t : Z_t = X_t} is the smallest optimal stopping time: stop the first time the continuation value equals the reward.
- ν = the largest optimal time: the first time the Doob increasing process A leaves zero (A is the "cost of waiting"; after A moves, stopping is no longer free).
- A stopping time σ is optimal iff Z_σ = X_σ and the stopped process Z^σ is a martingale: you stop when indifferent, and only then does the value process have no further downward drift.

American option pricing recursion: V_{t-1} = max{f_{t-1}, (1+r)^{-1} E_Q(V_t | F_{t-1})}: value is the maximum of immediate exercise and discounted expected continuation, and the rational price is the supremum over stopping times of discounted expected payoff, attained at τ*.

Writer's arbitrage: if the buyer exercises at a non-optimal time, the writer profits; hence the unique no-arbitrage price is the optimal-stopping value. American call on a non-dividend stock equals the European call (Theorem 5.5.1): the smallest supermartingale dominating the payoff never dips to the payoff early, because the discounted strike's purchasing power declines. With dividends, early exercise can become optimal.

Consumption-investment: a plan finances consumption iff E_Q(discounted total consumption) ≤ x: the budget constraint is just "expected discounted spending under the martingale measure cannot exceed initial wealth," and discounted wealth is a Q-supermartingale under admissible plans (it can be consumed away, never created).

## Chapter 6: Continuous-Time Stochastic Calculus

The usual conditions on filtrations; stopping times ({τ ≤ t} ∈ F_t); the σ-field F_τ of events determined before τ. Process equivalence: same finite-dimensional distributions (Kolmogorov extension), then modification, then indistinguishability; progressive measurability.

Brownian motion and its martingales: B_t, B_t² − t, and exp(σB_t − σ²t/2) are martingales; the Lévy characterization (Theorem 6.4.16) closes the circle: a continuous local martingale starting at 0 whose square minus t is a local martingale is a Brownian motion. Doob's maximal inequality: E(sup_{s≤T}|M_s|²) ≤ 4E|M_T|²: control of the running maximum comes free from terminal control.

Construction of the stochastic integral: simple (piecewise-constant predictable) processes first, with the isometry E(∫H dW)² = E∫H² ds, then extension by localization to general predictable integrands and to integrators that are local martingales, with ∫H dM defined so that the isometry survives via quadratic variation. Itô processes, uniqueness of the decomposition (Lemma 6.4.2), ⟨W⟩ = t, informally (dW)² ≈ dt and (dW)(dt) ≈ 0.

Itô's formula: f(X_t) = f(X_0) + ∫f'(X)dX + ½∫f''(X)d⟨X⟩. The workhorse result: for GBM, dS = μS dt + σS dW gives log S_t = log S_0 + (μ − σ²/2)t + σW_t: the −σ²/2 correction is where all the Itô content lives.

Multidimensional Itô with covariation; Gronwall's lemma; existence and uniqueness of SDE solutions under Lipschitz + linear growth via Picard iteration; the Markov property of SDE solutions via the flow property.

## Chapter 7: Continuous-Time European Options

Girsanov theorem: with the exponential density process Λ_t = exp{∫θ dW − ½∫θ² ds} (Novikov condition E exp(½∫θ²) < ∞ suffices), the process W_t = B_t + ∫θ ds is Brownian under Q_θ. Changing measure adds drift to a Brownian motion, and drift is all it can change: volatilities are measure-invariant, which is why the risk-neutral measure is usable at all.

Hitting times: E(e^{−λT_a}) = e^{−√(2λ)|a|}; for Brownian with drift, P(T_a < ∞) = e^{μa − |μa|}: with μ ≤ 0, the probability that level a > 0 is ever hit decays exponentially in a. This single result prices barrier options and sizes the risk of stop-losses: a stop at distance d from price in volatility units is a bet on a hitting-time tail.

Martingale representation: every martingale of a Brownian filtration is a stochastic integral (Theorem 7.3.9, via stable subspaces and orthogonality), extended to SDE filtrations, with an explicit integrand in the Markov case through the Jacobian D_{s,t} (Theorem 7.3.13). Representation + Girsanov = the continuous-time completeness machinery.

Self-financing in continuous time: discounted wealth satisfies dV̄ = H^1 dS̄; admissible wealth is a Q-supermartingale (Lemma 7.5.3), giving no arbitrage. The rational price of a claim: C(T, f_T) = E(e^{−rT} f_T(S^1(r))), computed under the stock's risk-neutral dynamics, again independent of the true drift μ. The minimal hedge comes from the martingale representation of the discounted claim; for the lognormal call, H^1_t = Φ(d_+), H^0_t = −K e^{−rT} Φ(d_−).

Multifactor: with m stocks driven by n Brownian motions, the market price of risk θ solves μ_i − r = Σ_j σ_ij θ_j. No-arbitrage iff the system is solvable; complete iff the solution is unique (σ nonsingular, m = n). The same linear algebra governs both theorems.

Black-Scholes formula and the Greeks: Δ_C = Φ(d_+), Δ_P = Δ_C − 1, Γ = φ(d_+)/(Sσ√(T−t)), Θ_P = Θ_C + rK e^{−r(T−t)}, ρ_C > 0, ρ_P < 0, and vega is the same for puts and calls: vega = S√(T−t)φ(d_+). Option elasticity: σ_C = (S/V)Δ·σ_S with E_C ≥ 1 and μ_C − r = E_C(μ_S − r): the exact continuous analogue of the Chapter 1 binomial elasticity, and the formal statement that options are leveraged stock.

Barrier options via the reflection principle: P(B_T < b, max B > c) = P(B_T > 2c − b); the joint density of (B_T, running max) comes via Girsanov; each knock-in/knock-out call is a combination of vanilla terms with the replacement S → H²/S and the factor (H/S)^{2r/σ²−1} (Lemmas 7.8.2-7.8.7). In + out = vanilla. Lookback calls and puts priced the same way. The down-and-out call equals a vanilla call minus the reflected vanilla call struck at H²/S: a barrier option is a difference of two European claims, which is why its Greeks can flip sign near the barrier.

The BS PDE two ways: from the martingale argument (discounted option value must be a Q-martingale, forcing the drift terms to cancel) and from the original delta-hedging derivation; reduction to the heat equation; variational and finite-difference treatment follow in Chapter 8's numerics.

## Chapter 8: The American Put

The deep problem of the subject. Extended trading allows buy-and-hold option positions; arbitrage is defined for both buyer and seller. The value process: X_t = esssup over stopping times τ ≥ t of Ẽ(e^{−r(τ−t)}(K − S_τ)^+ | F_t).

Structure results: the Snell envelope J = M − A (Doob-Meyer); the hedging strategy (8.10); X_0 is the unique no-arbitrage price (Theorem 8.2.5). The value function P(x,t) is convex in x, decreasing in t, continuous; P(x,t) > 0 always (an American put never trades at intrinsic-only value before the end, since waiting retains option value); ∂P/∂x ≥ −1 (value falls at most dollar-for-dollar with the stock: the hedge ratio is capped at a full short).

Continuation region C and stopping region S: the exercise boundary S_t* is increasing in t and stays below K. Exercise is optimal only deep in the money, more so as expiry approaches, and never above the strike.

The perpetual put (no expiry): above the exercise boundary S*, P(x) = (K − S*)(x/S*)^{−γ} with γ = 2r/σ², and S* = Kγ/(1+γ). Verified two ways (hitting-time Laplace transform; free-boundary ODE with smooth pasting: at the boundary, value matches intrinsic value and the derivative matches −1). Read the boundary formula: the higher the interest rate and the lower the volatility, the closer S* sits to K: high carry plus low volatility makes early exercise attractive. With r = 0 the perpetual put is never exercised (holding cash earns nothing, waiting is free).

Early exercise premium: P = p + e, where e(x,t) = Ẽ∫ e^{−r(u−t)} rK 1_{S_u < S_u*} du (Theorem 8.4.1, via local time and the dual predictable projection): the American put is the European put plus the interest income on the strike during all the times exercise would be optimal. The entire early-exercise decision is a dividend on the strike.

The free boundary problem: L(e^{−rt}P) = 0 in the continuation region, Dirichlet boundary data, and smooth pasting ∂P/∂x = −1 across S* (Prop 8.5.2, proved with distributional inequalities). Uniqueness (Theorem 8.5.5). The variational inequality characterisation (Jaillet-Lamberton-Lapeyre, Theorem 8.5.9) is the basis of stable numerical schemes: min{optionality left, exercise signal} at every point, which is the binomial Bellman recursion in continuous form.

The Barone-Adesi/Elliott approximation: the early-exercise premium ε(x,t) ≈ A(t)(x/S_t*)^{q(t)} with a three-equation iterative scheme (8.29)-(8.31); fast and accurate for practical maturities.

## Chapter 9: Bonds and Term Structure

Zero-coupon bonds: B(t,T) = E(exp{−∫_t^T r_u du} | F_t). The T-forward price F(t,T) = S_t^1/B(t,T). The forward measure Q_T: dQ_T/dP = (S_T^0)^{-1}/B(0,T); under Q_T, every T-maturity forward price is a martingale (Lemma 9.1.6). Futures price G(h,t,T) = E(h | F_t), a P-martingale (in the risk-neutral world); futures differ from forwards by the correlation between the payoff and the discount factor: F = G iff conditionally uncorrelated, and positive correlation gives G ≤ F. Marking to market is the economic origin of the wedge.

Change of numeraire (Theorem 9.3.3): for any numeraire Z, P_Z(A) = Z_0^{-1}∫_A Z_T/S_T^0 dP is the right measure for pricing claims in Z-units; self-financing is numeraire-invariant (Lemma 9.4.1), so hedging carries across measures unchanged (Theorem 9.4.3: hold Φ(h_1(t)) stock and KΦ(h_2(t)) bonds). Option pricing via two measures: V_0 = S_0^1 Φ(h_1) − K B(0,T) Φ(h_2), the El Karoui-Geman-Rochet structure, with each Φ computed under a different forward-type measure.

Term-structure models:

- Vasicek: OU short rate; Gaussian bond prices; the long rate R∞ = b − σ²/2a² independent of today's rate: a serious weakness (the long end does not respond to the level of rates).
- Hull-White: deterministic time-varying coefficients; B(t,T) = exp{−r_t C(t,T) − A(t,T)}; bond volatility σ(t)C(t,T); fits today's curve exactly, but keeps one-factor perfect correlation across maturities and Gaussian rates (negative rates possible).
- CIR: r_t as a sum of squares of n OU processes, dr = (a − br)dt + σ√r dW; non-negative for a ≥ σ²/2; Laplace transform of the transition law (Theorem 9.6.4) with the φ, ψ functions; chi-square transition density; bond options priced via two forward-type measures with the chi-square distribution function.
- HJM: forward rate f(t,T) = −∂_T log B(t,T), r_t = f(t,t). The drift condition (Theorem 9.7.3): no-arbitrage iff α(t,T) = σ(t,T)∫_t^T σ(t,u)du + σ(t,T)θ_t with θ independent of maturity. The market price of risk is common across maturities: the whole curve shares one risk premium. Volatility structure determines drift: exactly the Björk statement, proved here in full.
- Markov chain short rates (Elliott-Hunter-Jamieson): r_t = r·X_t with X_t a finite-state chain; bond price B = φ_t·X_t with dφ/dt = (diag r − A*)φ, φ_T = 1, solved by matrix exponentials: φ_t = e^{−B(T−t)}1. The attractive feature is self-calibration: filter the chain states from noisy observed yields with HMM methods (the authors' own research programme), turning term-structure estimation into a filtering problem.

## Chapter 10: Consumption-Investment (Karatzas-Lehoczky-Sethi-Shreve-Xu)

The complete-market Merton problem done with the martingale/duality method. n assets, uniform ellipticity (θ*σσ*θ ≥ ε|θ|²), unique market price of risk θ = σ^{-1}(μ − r1). Utility U strictly increasing, strictly concave, Inada conditions at 0 and ∞.

The deflator ξ_t = β_tΛ_t (discount factor times Girsanov density) converts everything into budget arithmetic: ξ_t X_t + ∫ξ_s c_s ds is a local martingale, and under the lower wealth bound a supermartingale, so E^θ(β_τ X_τ + ∫β c) ≤ x: the budget constraint says discounted terminal wealth plus discounted consumption, priced under the risk-neutral measure, cannot exceed initial capital (Theorem 10.2.4-10.2.5: conversely any consumption plan or terminal wealth satisfying the constraint can be financed by a strategy built from martingale representation).

Optimal consumption: c_1*(s) = I_1(s, G_1(x)ξ_s), where I is the inverse marginal utility and G_1 is the inverse of L_1(y) = E∫ξ_s I_1(s, yξ_s) ds. Terminal wealth: X_2(T) = I_2(T, G_2(x)ξ_T). The two problems combine by splitting the endowment x = x_1 + x_2 at G_1(x_1) = G_2(x_2), i.e. x_i = L_i(G(x)) (Theorem 10.5.1). Dual optimality: V'(x) = G(x), K'(y) = yL'(y): the marginal value of wealth equals the state-price level of the dual problem. Log and power utility solved in closed form (V = a log(x/a) + b for log); feedback-form closed solutions exist for constant coefficients. This is the complete-market version of Merton: with the martingale method there is no HJB equation, just inverse marginal utility times the state price: the optimal portfolio is the one that makes ξ times marginal utility a martingale.

## Chapter 11: Measures of Risk

VaR: VaR_α(X) = −q^α(X) with q^α the lower α-quantile. Properties: monotone, positively homogeneous, cash-invariant; Lipschitz in L∞. But VaR is not subadditive: two independent 0.8%-default loans each have VaR ≤ 0 at the 1% level individually, yet the diversified portfolio's VaR = 50,000; the 100-bond example flips the sign of VaR under diversification. VaR also says nothing about the severity of losses beyond the quantile. The failure of subadditivity means VaR can penalize diversification: it is a quantile, not a tail integral.

Coherent risk measures (Artzner-Delbaen-Eber-Heath): (i) X ≥ 0 → ρ(X) ≤ 0; (ii) cash-invariance ρ(X + k) = ρ(X) − k; (iii) positive homogeneity; (iv) subadditivity (equivalent to convexity given (iii)). The representation: ρ_P(X) = sup_{Q∈P} E_Q(−X) over a set of probability measures; every lower semi-continuous coherent risk measure has this form with a weak*-closed convex set of densities (Theorem 11.2.19, via Krein-Smulian and the bipolar theorem). The single extreme {P} is too tolerant; all Q too strict. The worst conditional expectation family P_k = {Q ≪ P : dQ/dP ≤ k}: for continuous F_X, ρ_{P_k}(X) = E_P(−X | X ≤ q^α(X)) ≥ VaR_α(X) with α = 1/k: the tail conditional expectation, which averages the tail rather than reading its edge.

TCE is not subadditive in general (jumps); the 150-client binomial example has TCE = 6.287 in the independent case versus 14.5 under dependence while VaR moves only from 5 to 6: tail-based measures see dependence that quantile-based measures miss. With continuous distributions WCE = TCE = CVaR.

Deviation measures (Rockafellar-Uryasev style): axioms D1-D4 (translation invariance, positive homogeneity, subadditivity, D(X) = 0 iff X constant); examples include standard deviation, one-sided deviations σ_+, σ_−. Theorem 11.3.4: R(X) = D(X) − E(X) is an expectation-bounded risk measure and conversely D(X) = R(X − E(X)): deviation measures and expectation-bounded risk measures are the same object in two coordinate systems. The lower-range example: D(X) = E(X) − ess inf X, with R(X) = ess sup(−X).

CVaR / expected shortfall done properly for general distributions: with U = {X ≤ q_α(X)}, CVaR_α(X) = −α^{-1}E(X 1_U) + q_α(X)(α − P(U)); equivalently the integral of quantiles, CVaR_α(X) = −(1/α)∫_0^α q_β(X)dβ (11.14). Theorem 11.3.7 proves coherence of CVaR in full, including subadditivity via the fractional indicator functions 1^α: the technical fix for atoms at the quantile. CVaR is continuous in α, which VaR is not.

### Hedging with shortfall risk (Föllmer-Leukert; Nakano)

The problem: super-replication may cost more capital than the investor will commit; accept a specified probability of shortfall and minimize cost, or fix capital and minimize shortfall risk.

Quantile hedging in a complete market: maximize the probability of the success set A(H,θ) = {V_T ≥ H} subject to V_0 ≤ V_0^*. The trick: the optimal success set A* is found by hedging the knockout option H 1_{A*}, and A* is identified by the Neyman-Pearson lemma: the most powerful test of P against P* (with dP*/dQ = H/E_Q(H)) rejects where the likelihood ratio dP/dQ exceeds a level aH. The optimal strategy replicates the claim only on the states most likely under the real-world measure: quantile hedging = hypothesis testing, where you deliberately fail to hedge the least likely tail states given your capital (Theorem 11.4.3).

Efficient hedging with coherent risk measures: minimize ρ((V_T − H) ∧ 0) over admissible strategies with V_0 ≤ V_0^*. Success ratio φ = 1_{V_T ≥ H} + (V_T/H)1_{V_T < H} (Definition 11.4.6): partial hedging measured fractionally. The optimal randomised test φ* ∈ R_0 = {φ ∈ [0,1] : sup_Q E_Q(φH) ≤ V_0^*} exists by weak* compactness (Prop 11.4.5), and the strategy comes from the optional decomposition theorem: the processes V_t = V_0 + ∫θ dS − C_t (supermartingale under every EMM) are exactly the optional decompositions, the incomplete-market replacement for the Doob-Meyer decomposition, with a non-predictable increasing cost process C. Theorem 11.4.8: the optimal strategy's shortfall risk equals ρ(−(1−φ*)H). In the Black-Scholes case with worst conditional expectation, the optimal test is a simple threshold on S_T: hedge the claim whenever S_T > c, and the capital saved by accepting the shortfall is computable explicitly.

The deep point: when arbitrage cannot pin a price (incomplete markets) or capital cannot buy full replication, pricing and hedging become constrained optimization under a risk measure, and the Neyman-Pearson geometry of statistical tests is the solution machine.

## Durable lessons

1. Arbitrage pins prices to points only in complete markets; everywhere else it pins them to intervals. Any price strictly inside the interval (π_b, π_s) is arbitrage-free. Real markets are incomplete (Theorem 4.6.1), so value judgments outside the band are always criteria, not arbitrage.
2. Option prices never depend on the drift μ of the underlying. Only volatility, rates, and carry enter. Any pricing model whose output depends on your directional forecast has mispriced the hedge.
3. Elasticity E_H = (S/H)Δ ≥ 1 for calls: options are leveraged stock, with σ_H = E_H σ_S and μ_H − r = E_H(μ_S − r) in both discrete and continuous time. Sizing options like stock without scaling by elasticity over-risks the account.
4. The Snell envelope is the complete theory of "when to act": stop at the first time continuation value equals immediate reward (τ* = min{Z = X}); the value process is the smallest dominating supermartingale; any other rule either leaves money on the table or stops into negative drift.
5. The American put's early-exercise premium is exactly the interest income on the strike during optimal-exercise periods: P = p + E∫e^{−ru} rK 1_{exercise optimal} du. Early exercise is a carry decision, not a view.
6. Perpetual put boundary S* = Kγ/(1+γ) with γ = 2r/σ²: high rates and low volatility pull the exercise boundary toward the strike; at r = 0 the American put is never exercised early.
7. A change of measure can only add drift, never volatility: Girsanov's theorem is why the risk-neutral measure is usable, why volatilities are measure-invariant, and why implied volatility is the one option-market number that survives measure change.
8. Barrier options are differences of European claims (reflection principle, in + out = vanilla). A stop-loss order is economically a short knock-out position: its risk is a hitting-time tail probability, computable as e^{−√(2λ)|a|}-type decay in volatility units.
9. The HJM drift condition: the market price of risk is the same for every maturity; the volatility structure of the curve determines its drift. A cross-maturity risk premium that varies by maturity is either data noise or an arbitrage violation.
10. VaR is not subadditive and can penalize diversification; CVaR (expected shortfall) is coherent, tail-sensitive, and continuous in the confidence level. For any risk budget that aggregates positions, use CVaR.
11. Deviation measures and expectation-bounded risk measures are two coordinate systems for one object: R(X) = D(X) − E(X) and D(X) = R(X − E(X)) (Theorem 11.3.4).
12. With limited capital, optimal hedging is a hypothesis test: quantile hedging replicates the claim on the highest-likelihood states and deliberately accepts shortfall in the least likely ones, chosen by the Neyman-Pearson likelihood ratio (Theorem 11.4.3). Capital allocation under risk constraints is formally the same mathematics as statistical testing.

## Connections to prior books

- Björk (arbitrage-theory-bjork): this is the discrete-time-then-continuous parallel of Björk's continuous-first treatment. Both Fundamental Theorems, Girsanov, BS, Greeks, barrier options via reflection, HJM's drift condition, forward measures, numeraire change, and Merton appear in both. Elliott-Kopp adds what Björk states without proof: the full separating-hyperplane and Kreps-Yan proof of the First Fundamental Theorem, the step-by-step conditional EMM construction, the splitting index, and the (d+1)^T theorem that completeness is exceptional. Björk in turn has more on the LIBOR market models, divisa, and the full fixed-income zoo; use Björk for breadth of instruments, Elliott-Kopp for depth of the discrete foundations.
- Tsay (analysis-financial-time-series-tsay): Chapter 11 is the mathematical corrective to Tsay's Chapter 7. Tsay builds the VaR toolbox (EWMA, EVT, POT, expected shortfall as a computation); Elliott-Kopp proves that VaR itself fails subadditivity and that CVaR is the coherent alternative, and supplies the exact CVaR integral representation Tsay uses for expected shortfall. Tsay's GARCH vol feeds the σ that appears in every formula here; his EVT tails are what shortfall-risk hedging deliberately leaves unhedged.
- Javaheri (volatility-arbitrage-javaheri): stochastic volatility makes markets incomplete, which is exactly the Elliott-Kopp Chapter 4 situation (interval of EMMs, prices only pinned by criteria). The risk-neutral variance swaps and filtered vol estimates Javaheri trades live inside the arbitrage interval this book defines. Chapter 11's efficient hedging is the principled version of "sell tail insurance for premium": the optimal test is a threshold rule on the underlying, i.e., a deliberately unhedged tail.
- Levy (computational-finance-levy): Levy's Part II implements Chapter 7 numerically (CRR lattices with node Greeks, BAW/Roll-Geske-Whaley American approximations) and Elliott-Kopp supplies the analytic backbone those algorithms approximate: the CRR limit theorem via the triangular-array CLT, the American put free-boundary problem and its variational inequality that the binomial Bellman recursion solves by monotone convergence. The Barone-Adesi/Elliott approximation here is the analytic parent of Levy's BAW implementation.
- Härdle (applied-quantitative-finance-hardle): Härdle's VaR chapters (delta-gamma, Cornish-Fisher, copulas) are the computational layer; Elliott-Kopp Chapter 11 is the axiomatic layer that says which of those numbers are even coherent. Härdle's Kalman-filtering state-space work connects to the Elliott-Hunter-Jamieson Markov-chain term-structure model here, which is explicitly designed to be calibrated by HMM filtering of noisy yields.
- The Quants (the-quants-patterson): Theorem 4.6.1 is the mathematical statement of the book's model-risk theme: complete markets are exceptional, so every real-world replication claim carries basis risk, and the 1987 portfolio-insurance failure is what a supermartingale hedge looks like when liquidity is the missing state variable. Quantile hedging formalizes how a desk under capital constraints chooses which tail to leave bare.
- Elliott Wave base (elliott-wave-balan): pricing-by-no-arbitrage gives the fair-value anchor (futures basis F = S e^{(r−q)(T−t)}) that wave counts float on; the Snell envelope is the formal version of "let the position tell you when to act" (stop when continuation value hits reward); hitting-time probabilities put a number on stop placement (a stop d volatility units away is a bet with computable ruin probability); and elasticity is the discipline for sizing leveraged instruments so that a wave-3 thrust does not force a wave-2-sized liquidation.

## Strategy / research candidates (weekly NQ practice)

1. Fair-value futures anchor: compute NQ fair value from cash index, cost of carry, and dividend yield; track the basis. Persistent deviation beyond the transaction-cost band is the threshold-cointegration signal Tsay's S&P futures-cash analysis measured; Elliott-Kopp supplies the no-arbitrage price the band must center on.
2. Elasticity-weighted position sizing: never size an NQ futures or options position by contract count alone; scale by the elasticity of the instrument (E = (S/V)Δ for options, or the leverage ratio for futures) so that a given percentage adverse move hits the account by a planned amount. This makes stop distance and position size one decision rather than two.
3. Snell-envelope stop logic: an exit rule should have the martingale property: exiting when continuation value equals realized reward is the smallest optimal time; any earlier exit destroys expectation, any later exit rides a supermartingale. Operationally, on the weekly chart: exits belong at levels where the wave count and the reward structure are exhausted together, not at fixed price increments.
4. Hitting-time stop sizing: a stop placed a volatility units away is exercised with probability computable from hitting-time theory (P ≈ e^{−2a·dist/σ²}-type decay in driftless units). Use implied or GARCH weekly σ to convert a desired stop-out probability into a stop distance; this replaces arbitrary point stops with probability-calibrated ones.
5. CVaR-based risk budget: aggregate the weekly trade risk with expected shortfall at a fixed tail level, not VaR, since VaR breaks subadditivity and can hide dependence between correlated positions (the 150-client example). One NQ position plus one correlated equity position is exactly the case where tail dependence is missed.
6. Carry-aware exercise logic: the perpetual-put boundary S* = Kγ/(1+γ) with γ = 2r/σ² says early exercise decisions are driven by the ratio of carry to variance squared. The same ratio governs when rolling a levered weekly position costs more in carry than the volatility it buys: in high-rate low-vol regimes, reduce levered exposure; in low-rate high-vol regimes, the option value of waiting is large.
7. Deliberate-tail capital allocation (quantile hedging): under a fixed risk budget you cannot hedge all states; the Neyman-Pearson structure says the optimal hedge covers the high-likelihood states and accepts shortfall in the least likely tail. Operationally: allocate capital so that normal weekly ranges are fully covered, and define in advance the (rare) scenario you will absorb with a predefined loss, rather than pretending all tails are covered.
8. Cross-maturity consistency check: when using futures at different expiries or the cash-futures pair, the HJM maturity-independent market price of risk implies risk premia should not vary by expiry after accounting for carry; a persistent expiry-specific premium is a candidate signal or a data artifact, worth testing before being trusted.
