# Chernozhukov et al 2018, double/debiased machine learning: causal inference with ML nuisance functions — methods paper notes

Sources: Chernozhukov, Chetverikov, Demirer, Duflo, Hansen, Newey & Robins 2018, "Double/Debiased Machine Learning for Treatment and Structural Parameters" (Econometrics Journal 21(1), C1-C68); arXiv 1608.00060v7 (fetched clean, 178,790 chars, full text studied). ACCESS FLAG: full text. Saved 12 Nov 2026. Wave 4 of the paper queue (methods, the causal-discipline layer).

## The problem (fetched)

Inference on a low-dimensional parameter of interest theta_0 in the presence of a high-dimensional or highly complex nuisance parameter eta_0. When eta_0 is estimated by ML methods (random forests, lasso, neural nets, boosting), two biases destroy the naive plug-in estimator: regularization bias (the ML estimator trades bias for variance by construction) and overfitting bias (the same observations used to fit the nuisance functions contaminate the estimating equation). Both make the naive estimator fail to be root-N consistent: the paper's Figure 1 shows a random-forest plug-in estimator badly biased and non-normal in a seemingly favorable simulation.

## The two remedies (fetched, the paper's core)

1. Neyman-orthogonal scores: use a score function psi(W; theta, eta) whose Gateaux derivative with respect to the nuisance parameter vanishes at the true values: partial_eta E_P psi(W; theta_0, eta_0)[eta - eta_0] = 0 (their Definition 2.1). The moment condition is locally insensitive to nuisance estimation error, so a noisy plug-in estimate of eta_0 does not strongly violate the moment condition. The paper devotes Section 2 to constructions: Neyman's original C(alpha) construction (his 1959/1979 orthogonal scores), the GMM construction with the optimal rotation matrix mu_0 = J_theta-beta J_beta-beta^{-1}, the concentrating-out approach, conditional moment restrictions, and influence-function adjustments. All roads lead to orthogonal scores; the choice affects efficiency, not validity.
2. Cross-fitting: K-fold random partition; for each fold k, estimate the nuisance functions eta on the complement of fold k, then solve the estimating equation on fold k only (their Definitions 3.1/3.2, DML1 and DML2). Sample splitting kills the overfitting term (their Figure 2 shows contrived-but-realistic overfitting that diverges without splitting and vanishes with it); swapping the roles and averaging (cross-fitting) regains full efficiency. Recommendation (their Remark 3.1): K = 4 or 5, DML2 (pooled Jacobian) preferred over DML1 (per-fold estimators averaged); both are implemented in the DoubleML package.

## The partially linear model worked example (fetched, Robinson 1988)

Y = D theta_0 + g_0(X) + U and D = m_0(X) + V, with theta_0 the treatment effect of interest and X high-dimensional controls. The naive regression of Y on D and an ML plug-in of g_0 diverges because its bias term involves (1/sqrt n) times sum of m_0(X_i)(g_0(X_i) - ghat_0(X_i)), non-centered at rate slower than root-n. The orthogonalized estimator partials X out of D as well: theta-check = (sum of Vhat_i D_i)^{-1} (1/n) sum of Vhat_i (Y_i - ghat_0(X_i)), with Vhat = D - mhat_0(X). The bias term is now the PRODUCT of the two nuisance estimation errors, upper-bounded at rate n^{-(phi_m + phi_g)}, vanishing even though each learner converges slower than root-n. The Donsker conditions of classical semiparametrics (bounded entropy of the function class) are exactly what high-dimensional ML violates, which is why sample splitting, not empirical-process assumptions, carries the proof.

## Models covered and guarantees (fetched)

The general theory (Section 3) gives approximate normality of the root-N-scaled estimator with estimable covariance, uniform over a wide class of data-generating processes. Applications worked through: the partially linear regression model, partially linear IV (endogenous treatment), the interactive model for ATE/ATT under unconfoundedness (propensity score plus conditional outcome regressions, the AIPW-type score, robust when either nuisance is consistent), and LATE in an IV potential-outcomes setting. The theory is deliberately learner-agnostic: any ML method with the right convergence rates qualifies.

## Empirical examples (fetched, Tables 1-3)

Three applications, each reported across five simple learners (lasso, regression tree, random forest, boosting, neural net) plus an Ensemble (CV-weighted average) and a Best (per-nuisance best predictor) column, with 100 sample splits summarized by the median method:
- Pennsylvania Reemployment Bonus experiment: ATE of the cash bonus on log unemployment duration about -0.075, significant at 5% under every learner, both in the partially linear and interactive models, 2-fold and 5-fold.
- 401(k) eligibility on net financial assets: the no-controls estimate of about 19,559 dollars attenuates to roughly 7,700-9,250 dollars under flexible DML control of income and job-related confounders, across all learners; the DML estimate of the LATE of actual 401(k) participation (eligibility as instrument) is uniformly positive and significant.
- Acemoglu-Johnson-Robinson institutions-and-growth IV: estimates attenuate modestly relative to the published baseline but remain qualitatively similar.
The paper's own headline lesson (Section 6.4): results are broadly consistent regardless of which ML method estimates the nuisance functions, exactly as the theory predicts if the orthogonality is doing its work. The choice of learner is a performance question, not an inference question.

## Programme connections

- The causal-discipline layer for the order-flow wave: "OFI predicts price impact" (Cont/Kukanov/Stoikov) is a linear associational claim; the DML question is whether the impact coefficient survives when the nuisance structure (volatility state, momentum, regime, calendar effects, all potentially high-dimensional and nonlinear) is flexibly controlled and orthogonalized away. Any future amendment proposing a causal claim about a feature must pass through this template, or state explicitly why it is settled for associational use only.
- Neyman orthogonality is the design pattern for the platform's feature-null tests: when a proposed platform feature is tested against a frozen baseline, the test statistic should be constructed so that its sensitivity to nuisance-estimated quantities vanishes to first order; the paper's construction sections are the recipe book. Cross-fitting is also the honest answer to any tuning-on-sample worry: nuisance tuning happens off-fold by construction.
- The weekly programme's sample size (roughly 300 points) is the binding honesty constraint: DML guarantees are asymptotic in N, and the paper's examples use thousands of observations. A weekly-data DML estimate would be underpowered; the method belongs on the platform's larger intraday/daily sample regimes if ever assembled, per the same sample-size honesty as the transfer-entropy note.
- Implementation: DoubleML (the authors' package) is sklearn-compatible and matches the programme's existing stack (sklearn, no pip installs, source-run only); the partially linear DML estimator is also implementable directly from Robinson's formula with the bundled learners, which is the honest fallback and a reasonable cross-check.
- The 401(k) example is the template honesty benchmark for the programme: a headline effect (19.5k) that halves under flexible confounding control (about 8k) is the canonical shape of an associational coefficient inflating a causal one; market-microstructure features with regime- and volatility-correlated confounders are the direct analogue (a feature that "works" only because it proxies the volatility state).
- Cross-links: Cont/Kukanov/Stoikov OFI and Cont/Degond/Xuan (the models whose coefficients this framework would put on causal footing), the validation spine (DML is the single-hypothesis discipline applied to effect estimation: one pre-registered target parameter, not a feature search; White/HTW-style deflation would apply if many features were DML-tested at once), Schreiber transfer entropy (the directed-association counterpart; TE measures information transfer, DML measures structural effect under confounding), and the regime wave (Hamilton/Markov states and breaks are themselves confounders that the nuisance functions must be allowed to absorb, which the interactive model handles by feeding regime indicators as controls).

# Faber et al 2021, WATCH: Wasserstein-based change detection on streaming data — methods paper notes

Sources: Faber, Corizzo, Sniezynski, Baron & Japkowicz, "WATCH: Wasserstein-based Change Detection on Streaming Data" (IEEE International Conference on Big Data 2021, DOI 10.1109/BigData52589.2021.9671962; arXiv 2201.07125, fetched clean, 47,672 chars, full text studied). ACCESS FLAG: full text. Authorship correction to the queue label: the queue's working title was the venue-year only; the five-author list and venue are now confirmed from the fetched text. Saved 12 Nov 2026. Wave 4 of the paper queue (methods). Companion note: methods-peyre-cuturi-2020-computational-ot.md (the theory behind the distance WATCH uses).

## The method (fetched)

- WATCH detects changes in streaming multivariate data by maintaining a reference buffer D of up to kappa recent points, processing non-overlapping mini-batches B of omega points, and comparing B to D with an approximate Wasserstein distance W_A.
- Approximation: W_A is the Sinkhorn-based approximation from the SAG method (computed via the R transport package through rpy2 in the reference implementation); approximate transport keeps each comparison tractable for streaming use.
- Adaptive threshold (their Eq 2): the alarm threshold adapts to the observed dispersion of distances, scaling with alpha times the maximum of recent within-reference distances, so a constant tuned threshold is not required; the buffer resets to the current batch after an alarm, keeping the reference window local.
- The design goal: multivariate change detection without an assumed parametric model, since distributional distance is agnostic to the data's generating family; the paper positions this against divergence- and density-based streaming detectors.

## Results (fetched)

- TCPD benchmark (42 datasets, 13 competitor methods from the TSB-UAD/TCPD literature): WATCH achieves the best multivariate F1 average (0.836 in best mode, versus BOCPD at 0.798 second) and the best F1 plus Cover averages in the univariate setting; rank plots use Holm's procedure to check that the F1 advantage is significant across the field.
- Five additional high-dimensional datasets were contributed (network security, sensor streams) to test scalability; the method's cost grows with buffer size and batch size, not with the number of dimensions directly, which is its claimed advantage over per-dimension detectors.
- Honest failure modes, stated by the authors themselves: run_log fails because one cumulative-dimension dominates the transport cost (a single monotonically drifting coordinate swamps the distance, masking real changes in the other dimensions); gas_sensor fails due to fractional-distance effects in the cost matrix. Both failures are structurally informative: W-distance change detection is strong when all dimensions are comparable-scale and weak when a single persistent-trend dimension dominates.

## The mode warning (fetched, programme-critical)

The headline results are for the best mode, i.e. parameters (kappa, omega, alpha) selected per dataset by a grid search maximising benchmark F1. The default-mode results are materially weaker (univariate F1 averages drop to roughly 0.540/0.570 across settings). The benchmark literature accepts per-dataset tuning as a comparison convention, but the paper's own numbers quantify the gap between tuned and untuned operation: a WATCH-style detector without dataset-specific parameter selection loses its leaderboard position.

## Programme connections

- WATCH is the applied consumer of the Peyre-Cuturi machinery (previous note): it turns the Wasserstein distance into a streaming change-point detector with a local, adaptive threshold. Reading order matters less than the pairing: the theory note explains what W_A measures and what its statistical guarantees are; this note shows the operational pattern (buffer, mini-batch, adaptive threshold, reset).
- Prospective use in the programme is descriptive and amendment-gated: a parameter-stability monitor for rule set T (e.g. is the joint distribution of the weekly vol8/momentum features drifting?) belongs to the same future amendment topic as the stage0f Bai-Perron audit and the stage0g regime filter, as a third descriptive view: BP sees coefficient breaks in a regression, the Markov filter sees recurring mean/variance regimes, WATCH sees distributional drift without any model at all. Its default-mode weakness is a reminder to pre-commit parameters in any amendment rather than grid-searching on programme data.
- The grid-search best-mode convention is exactly the programme's anti-pattern (the validation spine's full lesson: tuned-on-the-test-set results do not transfer). If WATCH-style detection is ever proposed in an amendment, the parameters must be pre-registered on external data or justified by first principles, and the run_log failure mode (a dominating cumulative dimension) is directly relevant: programme series with persistent trend components (momentum12, near-integrated per the stage0f audit) are precisely where transport-distance detection silently degrades. Detrending or differencing before comparison is mandatory, and the note records the reason.
- No rpy2/R dependency will be available: any implementation would need the POT/numpy Sinkhorn route (previous note's implementation plan), not the reference implementation's R transport backend.
- Cross-links: Peyre/Cuturi (the distance and its approximation), Bai-Perron and Hamilton 1989 (the model-based change views WATCH complements), stage0f/stage0g reports (the descriptive regime/stability substrate already built in the programme), the validation spine (best-mode versus default-mode is a live demonstration of selection effects), and Diebold-Inoue (a drifting near-integrated series is the exact run_log pathology to guard against).

# Peyre & Cuturi 2020, Computational Optimal Transport: Kantorovich, entropic regularization, Sinkhorn — methods paper notes

Sources: Peyre & Cuturi 2019/2020, "Computational Optimal Transport" (book manuscript, arXiv 1803.00567; published as Foundations and Trends in Machine Learning 11(5-6), 355-607). ACCESS FLAG: the arXiv PDF is 42 MB and exceeds the fetch toolkit's 25 MB cap (recorded honestly, not fetched); ar5iv is blocked and the book's website optimaltransport.github.io is a JS shell. The full LaTeX source was fetched instead (export.arxiv.org/e-print/1803.00567, 63 MB tar.gz via curl; note arxiv.org/e-print redirects and export.arxiv.org works), and all chapter .tex files were extracted and studied (Intro, theory, entropic, algorithmic foundations, variational, extensions, semi-discrete, dynamic, statistical, W1 chapters). Saved 12 Nov 2026. Wave 4 of the paper queue (methods).

## Monge-Kantorovich and the LP formulation (fetched)

The transport problem: move mass from distribution a (sources) to b (targets) at minimal total cost. Kantorovich's relaxation replaces a transport map with a coupling P (a joint distribution with marginals a and b), and the problem becomes the linear program: minimize <C, P> = sum of C_ij P_ij over the polytope U(a, b) of couplings, where C is the ground cost matrix. The mines-factories formulation is the historical anchor. Key structural facts: the LP always attains its optimum at an extreme point of the coupling polytope (Birkhoff-type tightness); for the assignment problem (uniform marginals) extreme points are permutation matrices, which recovers discrete matching (Prop on matching).

## Entropic regularization (fetched, the book's signature contribution)

Regularized problem: L_eps(a, b) = min over P in U(a,b) of <C, P> - eps * H(P), where H is the entropy. This strictly convex problem has a unique solution for any eps > 0: as eps to 0 it converges to the maximum-entropy optimal coupling; as eps to infinity it tends to the independent coupling a tensor b. The gravity-model interpretation in transportation science (flows proportional to source mass times destination demand times a distance-decay kernel) is the historical precursor, and the regularized problem interpolates between hard assignment and independent transport.

## Sinkhorn's algorithm (fetched, with derivation)

The unique solution has the form P = diag(u) K diag(v), with K = exp(-C/eps) (elementwise). The Lagrangian derivation in the book shows that the marginal constraints reduce to matrix scaling conditions; the resulting algorithm alternates the scaling updates u = a / (K v) and v = b / (K^T u), each a single matrix-vector product. Convergence is linear; the algorithm is parallelisable, GPU-friendly, and differentiable, which is why regularized OT became the workhorse in ML applications. The book develops the full algorithmic apparatus (convergence rates, log-domain stabilization for small eps, greedy and screened truncated variants).

## Wasserstein distances (fetched)

When the ground cost is a power of a distance, C = D^p, the optimal value defines the p-Wasserstein distance W_p(a, b) = MK_{D^p}(a,b)^{1/p}, and this is a true metric on distributions (triangle inequality included; the book proves the metric structure). W1 has its own chapter (duality, Beckmann flow formulation, Sliced W1). This is the property that makes OT suitable as a change-detection substrate: a distance between distributions that behaves geometrically even when the distributions have disjoint supports (unlike KL-type divergences, which blow up or stay undefined for non-overlapping supports).

## Statistical and computational chapters (fetched, at survey level)

The statistical chapter covers sample complexity of plug-in empirical OT estimators (concentration of W_p between empirical measures, dimension-dependent rates), the use of OT in generative model evaluation (Wasserstein GANs; the book's account of why the Wasserstein loss supports gradients even for disjoint supports), and Sinkhorn divergences as debiased alternatives. The extensions chapter covers unbalanced OT (relaxed marginal constraints for unequal masses), Gromov-Wasserstein (comparing distributions on different metric spaces), and semi-discrete settings (one marginal continuous, one discrete; the power-diagram structure of optimal cells).

## Programme connections

- The Cont/Degond/Xuan order-book dynamics framework note already records the optimal-transport bridge: their price-impact operator transports the empirical order-book distribution toward a target distribution at minimal cost, so the book is the mathematical reference for the exact object that framework defines. Any future implementation of book-state dynamics comparisons (e.g. how far the current book distribution sits from its pre-shock state) is a W_p computation, and the entropic machinery is the practical route at scale.
- POT (Python Optimal Transport) is the standard library and fits the source-run pattern (pure-python core with compiled accelerators; no pip installs, run from source like ruptures/nolitsa). The Sinkhorn core is also a few dozen lines of numpy, which is the honest fallback if the library is heavy.
- WATCH note (wave-4 companion): its change-point detector is literally an approximate-Wasserstein distance threshold on sliding buffers, i.e. the applied consumer of this book's machinery; reading both together gives the theory and the applied benchmark in one pass.
- Descriptive-use discipline: OT distances on programme data would be descriptive comparisons (book-state drift, distributional distance between regimes), not new edge tests. Per programme discipline, no forward-return test of any OT-derived statistic happens without a signed dated amendment; the holdout stays sealed.
- Cross-links: Cont/Kukanov/Stoikov OFI (order-flow imbalance is a linear functional of the book change; Wasserstein distance is its distributional generalisation), Cont/Degond/Xuan (the transport-based impact model, now with its mathematical foundation filled in), Faber et al WATCH (the applied change-point consumer), Kolm/Turiel/Westray (deep models on book states, where an OT distance is the interpretable alternative baseline), and the validation spine (any reported OT comparison is a statistic with a sample complexity that depends on dimension, per the statistical chapter; dimension-dependence is the honesty caveat for high-dimensional book data).

# Schreiber 2000, transfer entropy: measuring directional information transfer — methods paper notes

Sources: Schreiber 2000, "Measuring Information Transfer" (Physical Review Letters 85(2), 461-464), arXiv nlin/0001042 (fetched clean, 17,374 chars, full text studied). ACCESS FLAG: full text. Bibliographic correction recorded: the correct arXiv identifier is nlin/0001042; nlin/0001041 is an unrelated quantum-chaos paper (wrong fetch deleted). Saved 12 Nov 2026. Wave 4 of the paper queue (methods). Companion note: methods-theiler-1992-surrogate-data.md, which uses the same author's review systematization of surrogate testing.

## The definition (fetched, Eq 4 and surrounding text)

Transfer entropy from a source j to a target i, with prediction horizons k (target history length) and l (source history length), is

TE = sum p(i_{n+1}, i_n^(k), j_n^(l)) * log [ p(i_{n+1} | i_n^(k), j_n^(l)) / p(i_{n+1} | i_n^(k)) ]

i.e. the Kullback-Leibler entropy between the conditional distribution of the target's next state given its own k-history alone versus given its own history plus the source's l-history. It measures the deviation from the generalized Markov property p(i_{n+1} | i_n^(k)) = p(i_{n+1} | i_n^(k), j_n^(l)): if knowing the source adds nothing to predicting the target, TE is zero; otherwise it is positive and, crucially, asymmetric in general.

## Key properties (fetched)

- Directionality: unlike mutual information, which is symmetric, TE distinguishes driver from driven. The estimate is a functional of transition probabilities, not instantaneous values, so it captures the temporal structure of coupling.
- Relation to MI: mutual information detects shared information but cannot attribute direction; TE reduces to a conditional mutual information and answers "which process's history improves prediction of which process's future."
- Conditioning on common inputs: the definition extends to conditioning on additional processes Z, so common-drive confounds can be handled by conditioning rather than by hoping they are absent.
- Estimation: for continuous data, kernel estimation of the transition probabilities (Gaussian kernels with widths set by heuristics given in the paper); finite-alphabet data uses direct histogram estimates. The paper notes the estimator's bias grows with the number of dimensions (k + l + 1), so short histories and small alphabets are essential at modest sample sizes.
- Tent-map lattice results (fetched): in a coupled lattice of tent maps with unidirectional coupling, the TE estimate in the driven direction stays at the expected finite-sample noise floor while the driving direction shows large positive TE, and this holds across coupling regimes; time-delayed mutual information, in contrast, is symmetric and confounded, peaking for both directions.
- Heart-breath example (fetched): applying the statistic to heart-rate and breathing data shows heart-to-breath information transfer substantially stronger than breath-to-heart at some lags, a demonstration that the measure extracts direction from data where MI cannot.

## Programme connections

- The measure is the natural formalisation of a directional microstructure claim: "order flow imbalance drives price" is precisely a statement that TE from OFI to future price changes exceeds TE from price changes to future OFI (and that both exceed the common-history baseline). The Cont/Kukanov/Stoikov linear impact result is the linear special case; TE would test whether there is directed information beyond the linear component, with the Theiler/Schreiber-Schmitz null machinery as the control (the wave-4 companion note).
- Sample-size honesty: the programme's weekly series is roughly 300 points; the paper's own tent-map and heart-rate examples use tens of thousands of samples, and the estimator's bias grows with history lengths k and l. Any TE claim on weekly NQ data is underpowered by construction and should be treated as infeasible at present; the parked LOB platform (daily/intraday book data, if ever assembled) is the honest sample-size regime for this statistic.
- Implementation route: the measure is a few pages of numpy given kernel estimators; JIDT (Java Information Dynamics Toolkit, the standard reference implementation with GPU support) is the established off-the-shelf route and fits the programme's source-run pattern (Tseyuan; no pip installs). Gaussian-system approximations via local linearization are feasible with the existing scipy stack.
- Relation to Granger causality: TE with Gaussian variables and k = l = 1 reduces to Granger causality; TE is the nonlinear, information-theoretic generalisation, which is why it belongs to this wave's methods queue rather than a linear-causality note.
- Cross-links: Cont/Kukanov/Stoikov OFI (the linear directed claim TE would generalise), Cont/Degond/Xuan (the framework whose price-impact operator is the model-side object a nonzero TE would need to survive), Theiler 1992 (the surrogate nulls that calibrate any "significant" TE estimate), Easley/Lopez de Prado/O'Hara VPIN (a toxicity measure whose implicit claim, flow imbalance predicts adverse price movement, is a TE-type claim made concrete), and the validation spine (a TE estimate is a statistic like any other: pre-registered, single-shot, never re-tuned against the holdout).

# Theiler et al 1992, surrogate data: the null-hypothesis test behind N1-N2 — methods paper notes

Sources: Theiler, Eubank, Longtin & Galdrikian 1992, "Testing for nonlinearity in time series: the method of surrogate data" (Physica D 58, 77-94). ACCESS FLAG: the primary article is Elsevier-paywalled and was not fetched (scispace PDF is JS-gated, CiteSeerX legacy URL dead, ADS CAPTCHA-gated); the abstract was verified verbatim via a ScienceDirect search snippet. The methodology was studied in full from the authors' own systematization: Schreiber & Schmitz 2000, "Surrogate time series" (Physica D 142, 346-382; arXiv chao-dyn/9909037, 106,784 chars fetched clean), which restates and extends the Theiler framework (same TISEAN research line, Schreiber a co-developer). The N0-N3 null-hierarchy labels as commonly cited are from general knowledge, not from the fetched texts, and are flagged below. Saved 12 Nov 2026. Wave 4 of the paper queue (methods).

## The core idea (abstract verified verbatim, Theiler et al)

The method detects nonlinear structure in time series by comparing the value of a nonlinear statistic on the data against its distribution under a null hypothesis, realised by surrogate data: artificial sequences that match the original in all linear properties but contain no nonlinear structure by construction. A significant excess of the measured statistic over the surrogate distribution rejects the null of a linear Gaussian stochastic process (possibly measured through a static monotone nonlinearity), rather than "proving nonlinearity" in some open-ended sense.

## The machinery as systematized in Schreiber & Schmitz 2000 (fetched, verbatim where quoted)

- Constrained vs typical realisations: constrained surrogates (AAFT, iterative amplitude-adjusted) exactly preserve spectrum and amplitude distribution; typical realisations only realise the properties in expectation, which changes the null being tested (the paper stresses these are different tests and misidentifying the null invalidates the rank test).
- Rank-order test design: with M surrogates and a one-sided test at level alpha, reject only if the data's statistic exceeds all M surrogate values; M = 1/alpha - 1 surrogates give an exact rank test at alpha (95% needs 19 surrogates, 99% needs 99). The statistic's analytical distribution is never needed, which is the point.
- Fourier surrogate hierarchy: phase randomisation of the discrete Fourier transform preserves the periodogram, hence the autocorrelation, and yields Gaussian linear surrogates; a further rescaling step (AAFT) handles observed data measured through a static nonlinearity (non-Gaussian marginals). The paper's term for the family: "Gaussian linear process, possibly measured through a static monotone nonlinearity" as the standard intermediate null between independence and full nonlinear alternatives.
- AAFT flatness bias and the iterative fix (Schreiber & Schmitz's own PRL 77, 635, 1996): AAFT surrogates are too white on average; the iterative amplitude-adjusted Fourier transform alternates spectral and amplitude adjustments (their Eqs 11-13) until convergence.
- Endpoint mismatch: naive Fourier surrogates of short non-periodic segments introduce spurious periodicity (the jump artifact); remedies are end-matched sub-sequences and the jump/slip diagnostics (their Eqs 16-17, with the AR(2) coefficient-0.9001 worked example).
- The curse of accuracy (Kugiumtzis, cited in the review): before running the nonlinear test, test surrogates with a LINEAR statistic; if a linear statistic already discriminates data from surrogates, the null is misspecified and the nonlinear result is meaningless. Surrogate testing does not certify a specific nonlinear model, only the inadequacy of the null.
- Interpretation rule, verbatim: "there is no such thing in statistics as testing a result against surrogates. All we can do is test a null hypothesis." Non-rejection means one of three things: the null is true; the statistic lacks power against the actual alternative; the nonlinearity exists but lies outside the sampling coverage of the surrogate ensemble.
- Financial precedent, fetched in full: the review's BUND futures example (1500 daily returns, surrogates constrained on running mean and running variance, test statistic = time-reversal asymmetry) does NOT reject the null. The authors' reading: on realistic financial sample sizes, the linear null survives a purpose-built asymmetry test, an honesty benchmark for anyone tempted to see nonlinear structure in a price series.
- Implementation: TISEAN routines throughout; the same algorithms are ported in nolitsa (Python, already in the programme's source-run repository list).

## General-knowledge addendum (flagged, not from fetched text)

The N0-N3 ladder as usually cited (N0: i.i.d. with fixed amplitudes; N1: Gaussian linear; N2: Gaussian linear measured through a static monotone nonlinearity; N3: Gaussian linear measured through a monotone nonlinearity plus time-reversal preservation) is standard in the physiological-signal literature that grew out of Theiler et al; the fetched review describes the same construction sequence without the N-labels.

## Programme connections

- The Elliott null tests N1-N2 in the strategy programme's engine specification name this exact family: the engine's wave-count significance tests are surrogate-data tests in all but name. The principled null for an Elliott claim on NQ weekly bars is the rescaled-Gaussian-linear surrogate (AAFT or iterative AAFT), evaluated with a pre-registered statistic and a pre-registered M, not ad hoc wave-count comparisons.
- The circular-shift null already validated in the Amendment 4/5 power readout is the constrained-randomisation member of this family (permutation of a cyclic sequence preserving local autocorrelation); Theiler machinery is the upgrade path if that null is ever found too weak or too strong, but per programme discipline it is frozen as validated until an amendment says otherwise.
- The BUND example is the standing honesty benchmark: before claiming NQ weekly data reveal nonlinear (wave) structure beyond trend and momentum, the linear null must actually be rejected at the pre-registered level, and the Kugiumtzis linear-stat-first rule must pass first. If the BUND precedent repeats (no rejection), the honest conclusion is not "the waves are there but subtle", it is "the null stands at this sample size" (roughly 300 weekly bars, far below the 1500 daily bars the BUND study had).
- Cross-links: Schreiber 2000 transfer entropy (same author, same issue of Physica D; TE is the directed-information statistic this family of nulls can calibrate), Schreiber & Schmitz 2000 review as the canonical systematization, Hamilton 1989 and Bai-Perron (the alternative labels for low-frequency structure that the linear null cannot see; regime machines and breaks are exactly the "nonlinear structure" surrogates destroy), Diebold-Inoue (near-integrated series are where linear-null rejection is easiest to fake via endpoint artifacts), Kolm/Turiel/Westray (deep-OFI predictability claims on LOB data are exactly the kind of result a surrogate baseline must precede, per the curse-of-accuracy rule), and the validation spine (a surrogate test is a single pre-registered null comparison, not a specification search; it needs no multiple-testing deflation unless the statistic itself was selected).

# Market microstructure literature review notes — Aigbovo & Isibor (2017), "Market Microstructure: A Review of Literature", RJFSR Vol 2 No 2, pp 1-27

Source: uploaded paper "MarketMicrostructureAReviewofLiterature.pdf", 27 pages. Read in full November 2026.

## What it is

A lightweight conceptual and theoretical survey from University of Benin. It walks through definitions (O'Hara, Spulber, Madhavan, NBER), market architecture dimensions (continuity: call vs continuous; intermediation: quote-driven vs order-driven; automation: floor vs screen), then summarizes the model families. No new results, no data.

## Coverage and how it maps onto the existing stack

- Inventory models: Garman 1976 (gambler's ruin, spread as survival necessity), Amihud-Mendelson 1980 (monotone pricing around preferred inventory, serially correlated transaction prices still consistent with efficiency), Ho-Stoll 1981/1983 (inventory shifts quote levels, not spread width; spread compensates risk aversion; multi-dealer relative inventory determines interdealer trading), Stoll 1978.
- Empirical spread decomposition: Roll 1984 (effective spread from negative serial covariance, cov(Δp_t, p_{t-1}) = -s²/4), Stoll 1989 (π reversal probability and δ continuation fraction; order-processing, inventory, adverse-selection components), Hasbrouck 1988 (Bayesian Roll), Huang-Stoll 1997 (two-way and three-way decompositions off serial correlation in trade flows).
- Information and strategic models: Glosten-Milgrom 1985, Kyle 1985, Admati-Pfleiderer 1988 timing/clumping, plus a sketch of the Biais-Glosten-Spatt 2005 synthesis where δ (signal-to-noise weight in the informed trader's summary statistic θ = s - γσ²_ε L) nests Roll/Ho-Stoll (δ=0) and Kyle (k=0, c=0) as limiting cases.

Everything here is already covered at derivation level by the O'Hara notes and at empirical level by Hasbrouck, Harris, and the Biais-Glosten-Spatt survey notes. The paper's only additions are the definitional list of market architecture dimensions and a compact one-page summary table of which model produces which spread component.

## Verdict

Reference-grade summary, not a source of new mechanism knowledge. Useful as a quick index of the spread-decomposition lineage (Roll to Stoll to Huang-Stoll) if that empirical family ever becomes relevant to the programme. No strategy implications. No contradictions with the stack.

---

# Nonlinear Optimization with Financial Applications (Bartholomew-Biggs)

A hands-on optimization textbook built entirely around portfolio problems. The engine room of every quant workflow: how a Markowitz problem is actually solved once you write it down. The book runs two threads in parallel: the portfolio thread (risk minimization, return maximization, re-balancing, downside risk, trend channels) and the solver thread (bisection through interior point methods), each motivating the other.

## Part I: The portfolio problems and one-variable methods (Ch 1-3)

### Chapter 1: Portfolio optimization

- Expected return of a portfolio: r̄ᵀy where r̄ is the vector of mean asset returns and y the invested fractions. Risk: V = yᵀQy where Q is the variance-covariance matrix. The efficient frontier is the parabola traced by minimizing V for each target return Rₚ.
- Minrisk0: minimize yᵀQy subject to eᵀy = 1 (least-risky portfolio, no return target). Minrisk1/Minrisk1m: add the constraint r̄ᵀy = Rₚ, in equality or scaled-penalty form. Maxret1/Maxret1m: maximize return subject to a risk cap, in composite or quartic/absolute-value penalty form.
- Short-selling shows up as negative yᵢ. Risk can also be written as a least-squares quantity ‖Ay‖ with AᵀA = mQ, which is what later lets Gauss-Newton attack portfolio problems directly.
- Risk-Ret1: composite objective −R + ρV, where ρ is the risk-aversion dial that picks a point on the efficient frontier.

### Chapter 2: One-variable optimization

- Bisection needs a sign change in the derivative; iteration count K = (log₁₀(b−a)+s)/log₁₀2 is known in advance. Bracketing first, then secant, Newton (quadratic convergence when the second derivative is well behaved), and quadratic/cubic interpolation for the line search inside bigger methods.
- Safeguarded Newton: fall back to bisection when the Newton step leaves the bracket. This hybrid pattern (trust the fast method only when it behaves) recurs all the way to SQP and IPM.
- Maxret1m already shows multiple local minima: the answer depends on the starting point. This is the book's first warning about non-convexity, paid off fully in Chapter 23.

### Chapter 3: N-asset portfolio problems

- Constraint elimination: solve eᵀy = 1 by substituting y = α + Bx, reducing the dimension by one (or two with a return constraint). Gradients and Hessians derived in explicit form for every portfolio variant.
- Elimination is clean when constraints are linear and few; it breaks down for inequalities, which is what forces the constrained-method machinery of Chapters 12-20.

## Part II: Unconstrained methods (Ch 4-11)

### Chapter 4: Direct search

- Univariate search, Hooke-Jeeves, Nelder-Mead: derivative-free, robust, slow. Useful when gradients are noisy or unavailable, and as a baseline. Finite-difference gradients bridge to derivative methods; automatic differentiation is flagged as the better route.
- SAMPO test problems T1-T4 established: two Minrisk1m and two Maxret1m variants on the Real-20-5 dataset. All later method comparisons run on these.

### Chapter 5: Steepest descent

- Descent direction condition pᵀg < 0. With perfect line searches, successive steepest-descent directions are orthogonal, which causes the classic zigzag on elongated contours. Linear convergence with rate bounded by the Hessian condition number: K = (C−1)/(C+1).
- Weak (inexact) line searches: Wolfe conditions (descent angle, sufficient decrease, step bounded away from zero), Armijo and modified Armijo backtracking. D(s) ratio diagnostics. SD results: hundreds to thousands of iterations; fails outright on the poorly scaled T3 with ρ = 100.

### Chapter 6: Newton's method

- Quadratic model, Newton step −G⁻¹g, quadratic convergence near the solution: one iteration from 10⁻⁴ to 10⁻⁵ accuracy where steepest descent needs 700+.
- Cholesky factorization as both solver and positive-definiteness test; breakdown means the Hessian is not PD, which means the quadratic model has no minimum. Fixes: matrix modification (Gershgorin-style diagonal boost, 1.1 × row sum) and trust regions (solve (λI+G)p = −g with ‖p‖ ≤ Δ).
- Costs: second derivatives and O(n³/6) factorization per iteration. Indefinite Hessians produce ascent directions if used naively.

### Chapter 7: Quasi-Newton methods

- Quasi-Newton condition Hγ = δ (curvature pairs). DFP and BFGS updates; Dixon's theorem: with perfect line searches all quasi-Newton methods generate identical iterates. Finite termination on quadratics in n steps with Hₙ = A⁻¹. Superlinear convergence, O(n²) per iteration.
- SR1 rank-one update: satisfies the memory property, self-dual under the H↔H⁻¹ swap, but can lose positive definiteness. The B-form duality swaps DFP and BFGS via Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury.
- Practical verdict: BFGS with weak line search is the default unconstrained workhorse.

### Chapter 8: Conjugate gradients

- Conjugacy with respect to A; finite termination in n steps on quadratics; Fletcher-Reeves and Polak-Ribiere β formulas; restart cycles for general functions; n-step quadratic convergence (better than superlinear). Preconditioning fixes bad eigenvalue spreads.
- CG costs O(n) per iteration and needs no matrix storage: the method for very large problems. Truncated Newton uses CG inner iterations on the Newton system with tolerance vₖ = min{C‖g‖, k⁻¹}: Newton accuracy at a fraction of the Hessian cost.
- On the portfolio tests CG is the weakest of the three families, but the cost analysis flips for large n.

### Chapter 9: Restrictions on variables

- Penalty formulations in y directly (Minrisk1u/Maxret1u), or transformation: yᵢ = xᵢ² to exclude short-selling, yᵢ = y_L + (y_U−y_L)sin²xᵢ for two-sided bounds, with extra penalty terms as needed.
- T5a vs T5: forbidding short-selling raises risk from 0.0018 to 0.0038, roughly double. Constraints always cost something; the cost is measurable and worth knowing before imposing them.
- Newton-type methods handle the transformed problems best (NMw).

### Chapter 10: Larger portfolios

- Synth-400-n problems (T7/T8): flat optima converge fast because the objective is insensitive to some directions; QNw cost ~5n² per iteration, run-time crossover against direct-search methods around n = 200.
- Time-variation: optimal portfolios computed from Real-m-5 histories shift materially as the history lengthens (big shifts after day 70 in the example). The "optimal" portfolio is a function of the estimation window, not a fact.
- Performance of optimized portfolios out of sample fluctuates and can lose short-term. Re-balancing is only worth doing when the weight shift is substantial.

### Chapter 11: Data fitting

- Least-squares residuals f_k, Gauss-Newton with normal equations (JᵀJ)p = −Jᵀf and 2JᵀJ ≈ ∇²F; Levenberg-Marquardt for singular JᵀJ. Trend-line fitting for time series (z = 3.94 + 0.0339t on the example data) and a de-trended AR model.
- Single-stage nonlinear models fit by GN in 3 iterations where quasi-Newton needs 28: exploit sum-of-squares structure whenever it exists.
- Risk itself is a sum-of-squares (m+2 residual terms f_k), so portfolio problems are themselves least-squares problems and GN applies to them. GN fails when the Jacobian gains a zero column (T5a, x₃ ≈ 0): parameters the data does not identify.

## Part III: Constrained methods (Ch 12-20)

### Chapter 12: Equality constraints and KKT

- Lagrangian L = F − Σλᵢcᵢ; first-order conditions ∇F = Σλᵢ∇cᵢ; feasible directions satisfy zᵀ∇F = 0; second-order conditions on the constraint tangents via the Lagrangian Hessian.
- Lagrange multipliers are sensitivities: λᵢδ is the objective change from a constraint perturbation δ. The multiplier tells you what the constraint costs, which is the efficient frontier slope for a return constraint.
- Scaling constraints (by 1/Rₚ, 1/V_a) matters for well-balanced convergence.

### Chapter 13: Linear equality constraints

- EQP optimality system [[G, −Aᵀ], [−A, 0]] solved blockwise. Explicit solutions for Minrisk0 (y* = Q⁻¹e / (eᵀQ⁻¹e)) and Minrisk1 (α, β, γ, δ formulas) are the closed-form Markowitz solutions.
- Reduced gradients: null-space basis via orthogonal factorization, vertical (feasibility-restoring) and horizontal (optimality) steps. Projected gradient P = I − Aᵀ(AAᵀ)⁻¹A. Powell's modification ηₖ = (1−θ)γₖ + θBₖδₖ keeps PD-ness of the BFGS approximation under active-set changes.

### Chapter 14: Penalty functions

- P(x,r) = F + (1/r)Σcᵢ². Ill-conditioning as r → 0: start with moderate r₁ and shrink by β. Multiplier recovery from −2cᵢ/rₖ → λᵢ*. P-SUMT.
- Augmented Lagrangian M(x,v,r) = F + Σ vᵢcᵢ + (1/r)Σcᵢ² with multiplier update v ← v − (2/r)c: much faster constraint-norm decrease, no need to drive r to zero. AL-SUMT dominates plain penalty.
- Exact penalty functions (l₁, non-smooth; smooth versions need multiplier estimates and higher derivatives).

### Chapter 15: SQP

- WHP-SQP: QP subproblem with BFGS update on the Lagrangian Hessian. AL-SQP derived as a Newton step on the augmented Lagrangian (equations 15.3.13/15.3.14 form), cheap when m ≪ n. Results: T4 solved in 18/31 total calls vs 75/147 for SUMT; ~half the run-time.
- Maratos effect: line search rejects good steps near the solution because the merit function rises. Filter methods (Fletcher-Leyffer) fix this by accepting any step that improves either objective or constraint violation (dominance in (F,e)-space).

### Chapter 16: Further portfolio problems

- Transaction costs: effective holdings ȳᵢ = (1−cᵢ)yᵢ, risk V̄ = yᵀDQDy/(eᵀDy)². Minrisk3 counterintuitive result: y₂ rises as c₂ rises, to keep the effective exposure.
- Re-balancing with minimal change: penalize ‖x‖ (the trade vector). xᵀx = 0.0034 for minimal-change re-balancing vs 0.0108 for full re-optimization: most of the theoretical gain from a fraction of the trading.
- Sensitivity: how far can weights drift while keeping R̄ = 0.99Rₚ and V̄ = 1.01V*? Flat optima allow 10-20% changes in some assets. The optimum is a plateau, not a spike.

### Chapter 17: Inequality constraints

- KKT with complementarity λᵢcᵢ = 0, λᵢ ≥ 0; binding vs non-binding constraints; second-order conditions on the binding-constraint null space. Sector caps, Minrisk4/Maxret4 (all-inequality with cash allowed, V̄ = yᵀQy/(eᵀy)²).
- Squared slack variables cᵢ − wᵢ² = 0 convert inequalities to equalities; slack-variable-plus-bounds form is often better behaved numerically.
- The b-variants of the test problems (T11b-T14b) have multiple distinct solutions with the same risk but different returns: inequality-constrained portfolio problems are genuinely non-convex in practice.

### Chapter 18: Active-set methods for inequalities

- IQP: solve EQP on the current active set, add the most violated constraint (via 18.1.4), drop constraints with negative multipliers. Worked example; reduced gradients with changing active sets; simple bounds as a specialization (Z = identity columns).
- P-SUMT with min[0, cᵢ]² terms; AL-SUMT inequality form with the v-update rule 18.4.2; SQP with IQP subproblems. AL-SQP best overall, but each iteration hides multiple EQP solves; warm starting near the solution matters.

### Chapter 19: Barrier methods

- B(x,r) = F + rΣ1/cᵢ or F − rΣlog cᵢ; iterates stay strictly interior and feasible; multiplier estimates rₖ/cᵢ(xₖ) → λᵢ*. Feasible starting point required; line search must reject boundary crossings.
- B-SUMT is usually the least competitive SUMT variant, but the log barrier is the seed of modern interior point methods.

### Chapter 20: Interior point methods

- Slack variables plus log barrier give the perturbed KKT conditions wᵢλᵢ = r; Newton system (20.2.9)-(20.2.11) solves jointly for δx, δλ, δw. Step length limited to 0.9 × fraction-to-boundary; barrier parameter r reduced only when the error function τ improves; the v parameter plays the same role as in AL-SQP.
- Descent proof (exercise 20.3.4): (δx, δw) is a descent direction for the augmented Lagrangian when G̃ is PD and v = λ⁺.
- IPM beats B-SUMT and P-SUMT on T13b and is competitive with AL-SQP (T11b-T14b tables: IPM 33-42 iterations vs AL-SQP 8-47). IPM systems include all inequality constraints (larger); AL-SQP solves smaller active-set systems but may need several per iteration. The SQP-vs-IPM question is still open; Gould and Toint suggest they co-exist, since IP algorithms can solve the QP subproblems inside SQP.

## Part IV: Applications (Ch 21-23)

### Chapter 21: Minimax fitting and trend channels

- Minimax approximation min max|zᵢ − φ(x,tᵢ)| reformulated as smooth constrained problem: minimize x_{n+1} subject to x_{n+1} ≥ zᵢ − φ(x,tᵢ) ≥ −x_{n+1}. Solved by AL-SQP (6 iterations) or IPM (20).
- Minax trend line vs least-squares line on the share data: minimax z = 3.7 + 0.00286t vs least-squares z = 3.9 + 0.00105t. Minimax is pulled toward extreme points because it minimizes the worst discrepancy; least-squares minimizes overall error.
- Trend channels: find parallel support and resistance lines z = x₁t + x₂ and z = x₁t + x₃ enclosing the data, minimizing the squared width (x₃−x₂)² subject to x₁tᵢ + x₃ ≥ zᵢ ≥ x₁tᵢ + x₂. Feasible start from (0, z_min, z_max).
- Trading rule demonstrated: fit a channel on days 1-20, project forward; price stays inside for 7 days then breaks below support, a sell signal (and the subsequent history confirms). Channel on days 20-40 is downward; breakout through resistance at day 48 is a buy signal. Days 40-60 channel is upward: hold. This is a fully objective, optimization-based alternative to hand-drawn trendlines.

### Chapter 22: Re-balancing with transaction costs, downside risk, worst-case

- Full re-balancing formulation with separate buy variables x⁺ and sell variables x⁻ (both ≥ 0), costs C_T = c_b Σx⁺ + c_s Σx⁻, cash-drain constraint (1+c_b)eᵀx⁺ = (1−c_s)eᵀx⁻, and the risk objective in y = ŷ + x⁺ − x⁻ form. Gradients via the chain rule (g⁺ = V̄_y, g⁻ = −V̄_y).
- Surprising result: the optimal solution can involve simultaneously buying and selling the same asset (xᵢ > 0 and x_{n+i} > 0), because the cost structure makes wash trades optimal in an artificial case (target return below the minimum-risk return). Adding complementarity constraints xᵢx_{n+i} = 0 gives a different, worse portfolio in that case. Lesson: formulations that look obviously right can be wrong; check the economics.
- With realistic targets, the no-wash-trade property holds automatically. An optimally re-balanced portfolio differs from an optimal fresh one under the same costs, even on the same data.
- Downside risk: V_d = (1/m)Σ[min(0, R_j − Rₚ)]² counts only shortfalls below target. Minimizing V_d rather than V changes the optimal weights appreciably even when the V_d values themselves barely move (T1: y₁ 0.42 → 0.32, y₄ 0.2 → 0.26). Risk definition is a choice, and it moves the portfolio.
- Worst-case analysis: V_w = max_j |R_j − Rₚ|, formulated as minimax with an auxiliary variable z. Worst-case changes the optimal portfolio substantially (T1 short-selling solution y₁ ≈ 0.77, y₄ ≈ −0.4).
- Multi-scenario worst-case: minimize max(yᵀQ⁽¹⁾y, yᵀQ⁽²⁾y) with both short and long estimation histories. Lagrange multipliers reveal when a return constraint should be an inequality instead (a negative multiplier on r̄⁽¹⁾ᵀy = Rₚ says the objective improves by exceeding the target). Rustem and Howe's book is the reference for systematic worst-case design.

### Chapter 23: Global optimization

- No local conditions certify a global optimum. Multi-start wastes effort but works weakly; Rinnooy Kan and Timmer formalize it with cluster analysis (prune duplicate-converging starts) and Bayesian estimation W_t = W(N_s−1)/(N_s−W−2) of the expected number of minima: 5 minima in 30 searches predicts 6.3 total; finding no new ones by 100 searches predicts 5.3, so you can stop.
- DIRECT: trisect a hyperbox, evaluate at centers, subdivide the box with the best center per size class, subject to a Lipschitz-based potential-optimality test (23.3.1) and an improvement filter (23.3.2, ε ≈ 0.001). Function values only, so non-smooth problems are fine. No reliable stopping test; it converges in the limit.
- Portfolio example: (23.4.1) has two local minima near (0.65, 0.15) and (0.6, 0.55). QNw from (0.5, 0.5) stops at the inferior local optimum; from (0,0) it finds the global one. Ten random restarts find the global optimum 7 times (ρ = 0.1) and 5 of 12 times (ρ = 1, with two additional spurious stopping points). DIRECT finds the global optimum in about 20 iterations from anywhere in the box.
- Buy-in thresholds: each yᵢ = 0 or yᵢ ≥ y_min. Penalty function ψ(yᵢ) (zero outside the forbidden zone, ≤ 1 inside) added to the objective; the resulting function has many local minima, so DIRECT locates the region and a quasi-Newton pass refines to full accuracy. QNw alone stops at y₅ ≈ 0; DIRECT + QNw finds the better y₅ ≈ 0.05 solution with barely worse risk. The standard pattern: global method to locate, local method to polish.

## Appendix

SAMPO Fortran 90 codes available by FTP, plus the data files for every table in the text. References span Markowitz (1952) through Karmarkar, Byrd-Hribar-Nocedal and Forsgren-Gill interior point papers, Rinnooy Kan-Timmer global optimization, Rustem-Howe worst-case design, and two volumes of the author's own poetry.

## Durable lessons

1. Every "optimal portfolio" is the output of a numerical method run on estimated inputs. The optimizer's job is to make the solve reliable; the statistician's job is to make the inputs honest; confusing the two produces false confidence.
2. Convergence rates trade against per-iteration cost: steepest descent O(n) per iteration with linear convergence, Newton O(n³) with quadratic, BFGS O(n²) with superlinear, CG O(n) with n-step. The right method depends on n, on the available derivatives, and on how accurate the answer needs to be.
3. Newton's quadratic convergence is real but fragile: it needs a PD Hessian. Matrix modification and trust regions exist precisely to keep the quadratic model honest.
4. The quasi-Newton condition Hγ = δ plus symmetry is nearly the whole story; DFP vs BFGS is a second-order concern, and with perfect line searches the choice does not even matter (Dixon's theorem).
5. Constraints are never free. Forbidding short-selling roughly doubled risk in the example. Always measure the cost of a constraint (the Lagrange multiplier does exactly this) before imposing it.
6. Ill-conditioning is the recurring failure mode of penalty methods: as r → 0 the subproblem becomes un-solvable in finite precision. Augmented Lagrangians fix this by learning multipliers instead of forcing r down.
7. SQP and interior point are the two modern winners, and the open question of which is better dissolves once you see IPM solving the QP subproblems inside SQP.
8. The Maratos effect and filter methods: a single merit function can reject good steps. Filters trade objective against feasibility in two dimensions instead of blending them.
9. Risk definition is a modeling choice with real weight: variance, downside risk V_d, and worst-case V_w each produce materially different portfolios from the same data. Choose the measure that matches how you actually experience risk.
10. Optimization results are start-point dependent in non-convex problems, and portfolio problems are non-convex more often than the textbook geometry suggests (the b-variants, the buy-in threshold problem, (23.4.1)).
11. The professional pattern for non-convexity: multi-start (with Bayesian stopping) or DIRECT to locate the basin, then a fast local method to polish. Global to find, local to refine.
12. Flat optima are a gift: 10-20% weight changes often cost almost nothing in risk and return. Exploit this for low-turnover re-balancing; the minimal-change re-balancing solution achieved most of the benefit with about a third of the trading.
13. Trend channels from minimax fitting are a fully objective version of support/resistance lines, complete with a demonstrated breakout-trading rule. Minimax hugs the extremes; least-squares averages through them. Know which behavior you want.
14. Formulations that look obviously correct can be wrong: the re-balancing problem that optimally wash-trades (buys and sells the same asset) is the standing example. Always sanity-check a solution against the economics.
15. Estimation windows move the optimum: solutions computed from different history lengths shift materially. Before re-balancing, ask whether the input estimates changed or only the window.

## Connections to prior books

- Johnson (Algorithmic Trading and DMA) and Cartea (Algorithmic and H-F Trading): portfolio construction is the upstream consumer of everything they optimize. Cartea's execution algorithms assume you already decided the target portfolio; this book is how that target gets computed. Cartea's HJB solutions also need numerical solvers for their ODEs/PDEs, and the trust-region and Newton machinery here is what solves them.
- Elliott and Kopp (Mathematics of Financial Markets): their mean-variance and Markowitz chapters give the covariance algebra; this book supplies the solvers (Cholesky, EQP, KKT) that make those formulas computable at n > 3. The efficient frontier parabola of Chapter 1 is the same object theirs describes geometrically.
- Björk (Arbitrage Theory): Björk's optimal control chapters derive optimality conditions from first principles; this book is the static, finite-dimensional case of the same theory (gradient = 0, second-order conditions, KKT as the constrained analog of the HJB verification machinery).
- Tsay (Analysis of Financial Time Series): Chapter 11's trend fitting and de-trended AR models are Tsay's regression and ARIMA territory, and the Q matrix that drives every portfolio problem is estimated from exactly the kind of return series Tsay teaches you to model. Bad volatility modeling means a badly-scaled Q means a meaningless optimum.
- Levy (Computational Finance) and Härdle (Applied Quantitative Finance): both cover numerical methods and estimation; this book goes deeper on the optimization ladder itself (Cholesky factorization, line searches, quasi-Newton updates) and on least-squares/NLLS estimation, which is the estimation engine behind their calibration examples.
- Javaheri (Volatility Arbitrage): parameter estimation by optimization is the same Gauss-Newton/Levenberg-Marquardt machinery of Chapter 11 applied to volatility model calibration.
- Bouchaud and Hasbrouck: both stress that covariance and impact estimates carry error; this book shows exactly how that error propagates: an unstable Q produces shifting "optimal" weights (Chapter 10's time-variation results) and multipliers that misprice constraints.
- Williams (Master the Markets) and Balan (Elliott Wave): trend channels from minimax fitting are the objective, data-driven replacement for hand-drawn support/resistance and channel lines; Williams's resistance and support levels are informal versions of the constraint boundaries in (21.2.1)-(21.2.2).
- The Quants (Patterson): the over-fitting and multiple-testing pathologies in that history map directly onto this book's warnings: multiple local optima, start-point dependence, and optimized portfolios that underperform out of sample.

## Strategy / research candidates (weekly NQ practice)

1. Objective trend channels: replace hand-drawn Elliott channel lines with minimax-fitted channels (support/resistance pair minimizing width subject to enclosing the weekly closes). The book's own breakout rule (break below fitted support after a channel regime = exit/short signal; break above fitted resistance in a downtrend channel = entry signal) is directly testable on weekly NQ data.
2. Minimax vs least-squares trend sensitivity: minimax slopes hug the recent extremes, least-squares slopes average through them. Compare both fits on the same NQ weekly window; minimax's sensitivity to the last swing extreme makes it a natural companion to wave counting, where the most recent extreme is the reference point.
3. Downside risk as the position-sizing measure: V_d = (1/m)Σ[min(0, R_j − Rₚ)]² applied to the trade's weekly return history. Size positions to cap downside shortfall rather than variance; this matches how a weekly-chart trader actually experiences risk (drawdown below entry reference, not two-sided noise).
4. Worst-case channel width as stop placement: V_w logic (max historical deviation from trend) sets initial stop distance from the worst prior excursion within the current channel, rather than an arbitrary multiple.
5. Flat-optimum tolerance for entry zones: the sensitivity analysis (10-20% weight changes tolerable on flat optima) argues for entry zones rather than exact entry prices. If the objective barely changes across a range of positions, place orders within the zone and let fills come to you, which also reduces execution impact (Cartea connection).
6. Re-balance only on substantial drift: the minimal-change re-balancing results support a rule of adjusting an existing NQ position only when the wave count or trend-channel parameters have shifted materially, not on every weekly bar. Trading costs and impact dominate small improvements.
7. Multi-start discipline for any fitted parameter: whenever fitting channel slopes, Fibonacci retracement anchors, or volatility windows, run from multiple starting points and check whether the optimum is unique. If different starts find different optima, the signal is not robust enough to trade.
8. Window sensitivity testing: Chapter 10's result that optima shift materially with estimation-window length translates to a specific test: fit trend channels and volatility on 10, 20 and 40 weeks of data; if the trading signal flips between windows, treat it as no signal. This is a concrete defense against curve-fitting.
9. Worst-case across scenarios: minimize the max risk across two estimation windows (22.3.8) is a template for combining conflicting wave counts: take the position that is acceptable under both count scenarios rather than optimizing the single most likely one.
10. Lagrange multipliers as risk/return prices: when an optimizer enforces a risk cap or return target, read the multiplier. A near-zero multiplier means the constraint is not binding (relax or tighten freely); a large one means the frontier is steep there (small changes in target move the risk a lot).

# Cont, Cucuringu & Zhang (2023), "Cross-Impact of Order Flow Imbalance in Equity Markets" — order-flow-core paper 3

CATALOGUE RESOLUTION (recorded honestly): the SALIENT-derived queue listed this slot as "Cont/Xu 2025". Search on 12 Nov 2026 found no such paper; the Cont-line multi-level/cross-asset OFI paper is Cont/Cucuringu/Zhang, arXiv:2112.13213 (v1 Dec 2021, v4 Jun 2023; earlier titles "Price Impact of Order Flow Imbalance: Multi-level, Cross-asset and Forecasting" / "Cross-Impact of Order Flow Imbalance: Contemporaneous and Predictive"; first author Rama Cont; Chao Zhang corresponding). The multi-level OFI predecessor it builds on is Xu et al. [53] (contemporaneous multi-level impact, no cross-impact). "Verify before formal citation" applies, as with the earlier catalogue corrections (Admati-Pfleiderer, Stoll 1978, Roll 1984, Lee-Ready).

Source: arXiv:2112.13213v4. ACCESS: full text fetched to the extraction cap — abstract, introduction, main contributions, Section 2 (data, all variable definitions verbatim incl. the integrated-OFI construction, summary statistics, PCA variance table) retrieved verbatim; Sections 3-4's detailed result tables and Section 5 were not fully retrieved (headline results anchored in the abstract and contributions, flagged). Saved 12 Nov 2026. Wave 3 of the paper queue.

## What the paper asks

Two questions about OFI in a multi-asset setting: (1) does another stock's OFI help explain MY stock's contemporaneous price change (cross-impact)? (2) does lagged cross-asset OFI help FORECAST my stock's return? Prior literature (Benzaquen et al., Schneider-Tomas, Rosenbaum) found sizable cross-impact, but ignores the high correlation between cross-asset order flows — an overfitting risk the authors flag explicitly.

## The integrated OFI (verbatim construction, Section 2)

- Multi-level OFI: level-m order flows OF^{m,b}, OF^{m,a} defined by the same price/size change rules as CKS's e_n, summed over the interval; each level scaled by average depth across the top M=10 levels (ofi^m = OFI^m / QM).
- Key structural fact (fetched table): multi-level OFIs are strongly correlated (>75% across stocks); the FIRST principal component explains 89.06% of total variance (sd 6.12).
- Integrated OFI: ofi^I = w1ᵀ·ofi/‖w1‖₁ — the first PC of the 10-level OFI vector, normalized so the level weights sum to 1. First work to aggregate multi-level OFIs into a single variable; PCA weights computed from historical data.
- Data: LOBSTER Nasdaq ITCH, top-100 S&P 500 components, 2017-01-01..2019-12-31; returns are 1-minute mid-price log returns.

## Headline results (abstract + contributions, verbatim-anchored)

1. CONTEMPORANEOUS: integrated OFI explains price impact BETTER than best-level OFI; and once multi-level information is integrated, cross-impact terms add NO additional explanatory power vs a parsimonious sparse model without them (complementing Capponi & Cont 2020, who showed the same in-sample with the flow common factor controlled). The cross-correlation "found" by earlier studies is largely the common factor in order flows.
2. PREDICTIVE: lagged cross-asset OFI DOES improve forecasting of future returns — but only at short horizons (up to several minutes, 1-minute headline) and the predictability DECAYS RAPIDLY in time. (Section 4's R² and economic-gains tables were not fully retrieved; magnitudes flagged.)

## Programme connections

- ORDERFLOW platform: this is the pre-registered null shape for the platform's own cross-impact hypotheses on NQ/ES/YM/RTY futures: (a) build the integrated (PCA, depth-scaled) OFI before adding cross-asset terms, (b) expect contemporaneous cross-impact to vanish once the common factor is handled, (c) expect any true cross-predictability to be horizon-bounded and rapidly decaying — the platform's hypothesis templates must pre-commit horizons short enough for the effect to exist at all. The l1-normalization trick (weights sum to 1) keeps the integrated variable interpretable as "average effective queue change", which matters for feature-null tests (N1-N4).
- AVFI mirror: the 89%-first-PC fact is the intraday twin of the programme's own finding that smooth aggregate descriptors collapse to one effective factor (H5's slow-EMA artifact; sp_z/sp_z_clv correlation 0.934). When a feature family is internally near-collinear, the honest move is dimension reduction BEFORE testing, not 10 correlated tests — CCZ is the citation for that platform rule.
- Rapid decay is the cross-asset version of the CKS reading and of the programme's weekly nulls (H8/H9): flow information is a short-horizon phenomenon; nothing in this literature supports weekly-timing claims, which is why the parked platform (intraday, Tier-2 data) and never the weekly AVFI track is the right home for these hypotheses.
- Validation spine: the authors' overfitting warning about correlated cross-asset flows is exactly the PBO/DSR entry-ticket problem (Lesson: honest trial counts); a platform cross-impact search must register the family size before evaluating.
- Links: CKS notes (level-1 OFI definition this paper generalizes), Kolm/Turiel/Westray notes (deep-OFI features these authors also use), O'Hara/Harris notes (the common-factor result is the multi-asset face of Hasbrouck commonality), Bouchaud notes (cross-impact vs the square-root law's single-asset frame).

# Cont, Degond & Xuan, "A Mathematical Framework for Modelling Order Book Dynamics" — order-flow-core paper 5 (wave-3 addendum)

Source: arXiv:2302.01169 (v1, Feb 2023), fetched in full (104,019 extracted chars; all sections through the conclusions and the appendix-proof structure readable). Published version: SIAM Journal on Financial Mathematics, DOI 10.1137/22M1541538 (venue verified by search 12 Nov 2026; exact publication year not pinned, and the queue's "Cont-Xu 2025" label is not confirmed as a 2025 journal item — the label resolves to THIS paper by authorship: Rama Cont, Pierre Degond, Lifan XUAN, with "Xuan" apparently mis-catalogued as "Xu"). CATALOGUE CORRECTION: the wave-3 queue slot (c) labelled "Cont/Xu (2025) order-book dynamics framework" therefore resolves to TWO papers, both now studied — this framework paper (the label's literal match) and Cont/Cucuringu/Zhang cross-impact arXiv:2112.13213 (the earlier catalogue resolution, recorded in the wave-3 index entry). Saved 12 Nov 2026. Wave-3 addendum session.

## The framework in one sentence

Any limit-order-book model decomposes into exactly two independent ingredients — a stochastic order flow (a general spatial point process) and a deterministic market-clearing operator (a mass-transport map projecting arbitrary order configurations onto admissible books) — and the paper shows this decomposition carries through to the infinitesimal generator, unifying the existing LOB model zoo as special cases.

## The two ingredients (verbatim structure)

- Order flow: events at random times with marks M(t_k) in Z^d x Z^d perturbing the current book X(t_k-) into a transient configuration X(t_k-) + M(t_k); cancellations cannot exceed existing queue sizes (the process stays in E). "Order flow" is deliberately general — any point process (Poisson, Hawkes, state-dependent) plugs in.
- Clearing operator: a map C: E -> L whose invariant points are exactly the admissible books (X in L iff C(X) = X). The concrete instance is ORDER-MATCHING clearing (Def 2.2, four axioms: non-negative executed volume; equal total executed buy/sell volume; any matched buy priced above any matched sell; price priority on both sides), proved UNIQUE (Prop 2.3) via the g_X(k) = S_X(k) - B_X(k) monotone-difference construction with p_A/p_B inverse functions. Prop 2.4 works out the four elementary cases (limit buy, limit sell, cancel buy, cancel sell) in full — this is the matching-engine formalism: the same rules a CME-style engine implements, stated as operator algebra.
- Centred coordinates: the book re-centred at the mid-price (state space L_m). Clearing in centred coordinates is the composition of the order-matching operator with a centering operator J that shifts the coordinate system after each clearing (Prop 4.4) — with boundary orders dropping out of range, an honest bookkeeping detail real simulators must copy.

## Generator decomposition and unification

If both ingredients are Markovian, the LOB process is Markov and its infinitesimal generator factors as the composition of three simpler operators: the order-flow generator, the clearing operator (acting on measures), and the embedding of L into E. The paper's Section 5 demonstrates the framework's reach by embedding prior models inside it: queueing models (Cont-Stoikov-Talreja; Luckock; Daniels et al), the Markovian price-dynamics models of Cont-de Larrard, the queue-reactive model of Huang-Lehalle-Rosenbaum, SPDE density models (Cont-Mueller; Hambly-Kalsi-Newbury), and state-dependent Hawkes flows (Morariu-Patrichi-Pakkanen). Scaling limits (fluid/diffusion, laws of large numbers per Horst-Kreher/Horst-Paulsen) become properties of the flow component, independent of the clearing operator.

## Numerical illustration (fetched in full)

Test problem: conditional probability of an ask-price increase in 0.2s as a function of bid/ask depths, calibrated to Lobsterdata order-book records for Micron (MU), 2019-07-01. Two solution routes compared: Monte Carlo simulation vs direct resolution of the Kolmogorov backward equation. Fetched findings: the two agree closely at short horizons; MC is faster for a single horizon (the KBE run takes ~17 hours on their server) but KBE cost grows linearly with horizon extension while MC grows quadratically — the KBE wins for sequential evaluation of successive horizons. Honest model-vs-data gap: the conditional probability is smaller in the model than in the data, attributed by the authors to the same calibration simplifications, and the data show no significant dependence on BID depth (the ask depth is what matters at this horizon).

## Access-honesty notes

- Full text fetched and readable throughout, including the conclusion and appendix-proof structure; the framework is theoretical (measure-valued processes, generator algebra) — no estimation results, no trading-rule claims, and its one empirical exercise (MU calibration) is illustrative, not a performance study.
- The authors flag future work: viewing the clearing operator as an OPTIMAL TRANSPORT operator — the explicit bridge from this paper to the queue's Peyre/Cuturi OT tutorial (wave 4).

## Programme connections

- ORDERFLOW platform simulator architecture: this is the mathematical blueprint for the parked platform's simulation engine. The decomposition — stochastic flow module + deterministic matching module — is exactly how a clean LOB simulator should be built: swap flow models (Poisson / Hawkes / queue-reactive) without ever re-implementing the matching engine, which is fixed as the unique order-matching operator (Prop 2.3's four axioms are the acceptance test for any matching implementation; the catalog's pgaref/orderbook-class reference implementations are instances of this same operator).
- Simulator validation target: under any calibrated flow, CKS's linear-OFI relation and beta ~ c/depth scaling should EMERGE from the simulated book dynamics rather than being imposed — a falsifiable simulator-validation check tying this note to the CKS note; deviations would mean the flow model is wrong, not that impact is nonlinear.
- Centred coordinates = stationary book states: the mid-price re-centring trick is the feature-side twin of Kolm/Turiel/Westray's finding that models on stationary derived inputs (price-relative, not raw) outperform raw-state models; the platform's feature layer should follow the same normalization.
- MC-vs-KBE economics: the platform's compute budget for conditional-probability surfaces should use MC for one-shot questions and generator/KBE machinery for sequential monitoring — a direct operational rule from their measured runtimes.
- AVFI mirror: none at weekly horizon — this is intraday machinery, reinforcing the standing wave-3 synthesis that all flow/LOB hypotheses belong to the parked platform, not to the frozen weekly rule set T.
- Discipline: the framework itself makes no predictive claim and registers nothing; its function here is architecture, consistent with the programme rule that simulation is for understanding microstructure, never counted as evidence of edge (paper-trading-only discipline).
