# Cont, Kukanov & Stoikov (2014), "The Price Impact of Order Book Events" — order-flow-core paper 1

Source: Journal of Financial Econometrics 12(1), 47-88 (arXiv:1011.6402). ACCESS: full author-hosted texts fetched — the arXiv v3 PDF (sections 1-3 verbatim, incl. Tables 1-3) and the SSRN April-2012 manuscript (SSRN 1712822; sections 1-3.3 verbatim incl. the OFI-vs-trade-imbalance regressions). ADDENDUM 12 Nov 2026 (wave-3 addendum session): a complete re-fetch of arXiv:1011.6402 retrieved sections 4-5 and the conclusion IN FULL (62,923 chars, clean; Proposition 1, Tables 4-5, all scaling mechanics verbatim) — the earlier "section 4 partially retrieved" flag is retired. Saved 12 Nov 2026. Wave 3 of the paper queue (order-flow core). This is the foundational OFI paper for the ORDERFLOW platform's Phase 3 feature families.

## The contribution in one sentence

Over short intervals, mid-price changes are driven not by trade volume but by the net order flow imbalance (OFI) at the best bid/ask — a single one-parameter linear variable that aggregates limit orders, market orders AND cancellations, with a price impact coefficient inversely proportional to depth.

## The OFI construction (verbatim from the fetched text)

Between consecutive best-bid/ask observations (P^B, q^B, P^A, q^A), each event contributes a signed quantity e_n to the demand/supply balance:

e_n = I{P^B_n >= P^B_{n-1}} q^B_n − I{P^B_n <= P^B_{n-1}} q^B_{n-1} − I{P^A_n <= P^A_{n-1}} q^A_n + I{P^A_n >= P^A_{n-1}} q^A_{n-1}

and OFI over an interval is the sum of e_n over events in that interval. Key semantics (authors' own words): a market sell and a buy cancellation of the same size are EQUIVALENT — both remove size from the bid queue. OFI therefore measures queue-size dynamics, not trade labels; it needs NO trade classification (explicitly contrasted with trade imbalance TI, which "hinges on trade classification, which is known to be problematic for TAQ data").

## The stylized model (why linearity is structural, not fitted)

Assume depth D per price level and activity only at the touch: price changes are the queue-imbalance divided by depth, ∆P ≈ OFI/(2D) + ε (their Eqn 1). This is why:
- The estimated model is ∆P_k = β OFI_k + ε_k, with the quadratic term OFI|OFI| statistically insignificant (average t −0.32, significant in only 17% of samples) — convexity/concavity of instantaneous impact is REJECTED, unlike the trade-size literature's concave impact functions.
- The impact coefficient should scale as β_i = c/AD^λ with λ = 1: the data agree (λ̂ ≈ 1 across stocks; {λ=1} not rejected for 35/50; v2's depth convention gives ĉ ≈ 0.5, matching the stylized model). Price impact is inversely proportional to depth — observable quantities replace unobservable "information asymmetry" parameters in explaining intraday impact/volatility patterns.

## Headline empirical facts (fetched)

- Data: one month (April 2010) TAQ, 50 random S&P 500 stocks, 10-second aggregation, 273 half-hour subsamples per stock. NBBO quote updates outnumber trades roughly 40:1 — quotes, not trades, carry most of the information.
- Contemporaneous regression: average R² 65% (SLB 76%); β significant in ~98% of subsamples, intercept significant ~10% (≈ the Type-I rate).
- TAUTOLOGY CONTROL (important for our platform): OFI includes price-changing events, so part of the R² is mechanical. The authors re-ran excluding price-changing events: R² falls but stays 35-60%. High explanatory power is not just definition.
- OFI vs trade imbalance (SSRN version, verbatim): TI alone R² 32% vs OFI 65%; in the joint regression TI's t-statistic collapses to ~1.5 (significant in only 43-47% of samples) while OFI stays strongly significant — OFI absorbs the trade signal. Trade volume's famous concave "square-root" impact is, per the abstract, a noisy aggregation artifact: the linear-OFI model plus a scaling argument IMPLIES the apparent square-root relation between price changes and volume.
- Deeper book levels contribute only marginally at this horizon (appendix, fetched version) — the touch dominates short-horizon price formation.

## Access-honesty notes

- v3 (arXiv, 2011) and the SSRN April-2012 version differ in depth-variable conventions (v2 uses queue sizes adjacent to price changes; ĉ ≈ 0.5 in v2 vs lower values under arithmetic/geometric average depth — an explicit warning that the depth PROXY biases the impact constant by ~40%). Both retrieved texts cap mid-Table-3; section 4's full scaling derivation and the conclusion are not fully retrieved — flagged, not reconstructed.
- The paper is the empirical foundation later generalized by Xu et al. (multi-level OFI), Kolm/Turiel/Westray (deep OFI), and Cont/Cucuringu/Zhang (integrated/cross-impact OFI) — the other three Wave 3 papers.

## Addendum (12 Nov 2026): sections 4-5 and the conclusion, now fetched verbatim

- Section 4.1 trade-imbalance comparison, full Table 4: TI alone averages R² 32% vs OFI 65%; in the joint regression (8c) TI's average t-statistic falls by a factor of four (to ~1.16) and TI is significant in only 31% of subsamples while OFI stays significant in 95% — the authors' own numbered conclusions: (1) OFI explains price movements better than trade imbalance; (2) the effect of trade imbalance is adequately INCLUDED in OFI. Robustness on transaction prices in trade time (L = 2/5/10 trades, five stocks pooled): same ordering; TI insignificant jointly. An honest wrinkle: at TRADE-TIME sampling the impact of OFI becomes concave in some samples (quadratic term significant in ~half) — attributed to sampling bias (traders time orders to minimize impact), not to true nonlinearity; the effect vanishes at uniform 10-second sampling of mid-prices.
- Section 4.2 "Does volume move the prices?", full Proposition 1 (the square-root artifact, verbatim mechanics): under event-rate convergence, iid event contributions with finite variance, and iid trade sizes with mean m-phi, the CLT gives OFI(T) ≈ sigma*sqrt(N(T)) vs VOL(T) → m-phi*N(T), hence the noisy scaling relation OFI ≈ const*sqrt(VOL) — substituting into the linear model yields their Eq (14): delta-P ≈ beta_k*sqrt(VOL) + noise with a RANDOM slope beta_k ~ N(0, beta² sigma²/m²). The authors' explicit verdict on the square-root price-impact law: the slope's randomness "makes this model considerably less robust than (2) and we do not recommend to use it" — the empirical square-root law is a statistical artifact of aggregation even when prices are driven by OFI. If the iid assumptions fail, other exponents 0 < H < 1 are possible, matching the range observed in the literature.
- Empirics (Table 5): the local volume exponent H averages 0.18 (generally below 1/2); |OFI| explains |delta-P| at average R² 58% vs 23% for VOL^H; in the joint regression only |OFI| stays significant (volume significant in 67% of samples alone but OFI dominates jointly). The NUMBER OF TRADES (Jones-Kaul-Lipson) is significant stand-alone but insignificant added on top of both — a three-way elimination: order-flow imbalance absorbs trade imbalance AND volume AND trade counts.
- Depth regression (Table 3, full): log-linear depth elasticity lambda averages 0.98 (grand mean), with c ~ 0.45 — beta ≈ c/AD^lambda with lambda ≈ 1 confirmed as the cross-time scaling; intraday patterns follow: depth is ~2x lower near the open, impact ~2x higher than average and 5x the close, and the intraday volatility U-shape is reproduced by beta²*var(OFI) without any "information asymmetry" parameter (their Eq 7 decomposition, verbatim).
- Conclusion (verbatim anchors): "Trades seem to carry little to no information about price changes after the simultaneous order flow imbalance is taken into account"; the price-volume relation "simply captures the noisy scaling relation between these variables."

## Programme connections

- ORDERFLOW platform: OFI is Phase 3's primary feature family, and this paper supplies its exact Level-1-observable formula (the platform can compute OFI from BBO files alone, no message-level data needed for v1), the volume-clock-agnostic aggregation convention, and the pre-registered expectation of linearity — deviations from linearity in NQ flow become a falsifiable diagnostic, not a fitting opportunity.
- AVFI mirror: AVFI's flow input (volume weighted by range position) is a WEEKLY proxy for exactly the effort side of this paper's effort/queue ledger; CKS is the rigorous intraday version of "supply/demand imbalance moves prices" that VSA states narratively (Williams). The programme's null results for AVFI as a weekly TIMING signal are consistent with CKS: the OFI-price relation holds at SHORT horizons (seconds-minutes); nothing here licenses a weekly reversal-timing claim — if anything it predicts decay, which is what the H8/H9 nulls and CCZ's rapid-decay finding show.
- Stage 0d/0e caution: on NQ continuous-contract weekly bars, volume (and hence any OFI-proxy) is roll/expiry-contaminated; CKS's clean TAQ setting is unavailable at our horizon — a standing caveat for any weekly flow feature.
- Execution links: Cartea/Johnson — the β = c/depth relation is the scientific basis for the platform's intended "slow into adverse flow" execution heuristics; Hasbrouck's b/k decomposition (already in book notes) is the impact-decomposition frame this paper feeds; Bouchaud's square-root law notes are the object being explained away here as an aggregation artifact (one law, two readings — a live literature dispute to keep flagged, not resolved).
- Discipline links: the tautology control (exclude price-changing events) is a template for the platform's own feature-null tests (N1-N4): any feature mechanically entangled with its target must be shown to survive de-tangling before registration.

# Easley, López de Prado & O'Hara (2012), "Flow Toxicity and Liquidity in a High Frequency World" — order-flow-core paper 2

Source: Review of Financial Studies 25(5), 1457-1493. ACCESS: the author/conference-hosted February-2012 manuscript (NYU Stern PDF, "Forthcoming, RFS") fetched in full up to the extraction cap: title page, abstract, introduction and Sections 1-2.4 verbatim (PIN model, volume clock, volume bucketing, bulk volume classification, and the start of the VPIN construction). ADDENDUM 12 Nov 2026 (wave-3 addendum session): a complete re-fetch (105,318 chars, clean) retrieved Sections 3-6, the appendices (full VPIN pseudocode; Monte Carlo validation) and Tables 2-5 verbatim — the earlier "sections 3-6 not retrieved" flag is retired. COMPANION PAPER: the JPM article "The Volume Clock: Insights into the High Frequency Paradigm" (Easley/López de Prado/O'Hara, Journal of Portfolio Management, forthcoming May 2012; SSRN 2034858) fetched from a mirror whose PDF uses construction-ordered subset fonts, producing systematic glyph substitutions in the prose ("te argue" = "we argue", "gournal of cinance" = "Journal of Finance"); exhibit captions decode cleanly. HONESTY FLAG: the JPM prose is treated as partially unreliable — only passages whose meaning survives the substitution are used, and nothing from that mirror is quoted verbatim. Saved 12 Nov 2026. Wave 3 of the paper queue. Registered caution from the queue stands: VPIN is a CONTESTED proxy — the Andersen-Bondarenko critique literature (unfetched, general knowledge, flagged) argues VPIN's flash-crash predictive success is largely a mechanical function of volatility; treat as an open dispute, not settled science.

## The volume clock (the durable idea)

In high-frequency markets, trades are not equally spaced in clock time, and information relevant to a market maker arrives as volume bursts: "the more relevant a piece of news is, the more volume it attracts." Sampling in VOLUME time (one observation per fixed traded quantity V) instead of clock time: (a) weights each information event by the volume it attracts, (b) reduces volatility clustering and heteroscedasticity — "sampling by volume is a proxy for sampling by volatility" — yielding series closer to normal and closer to iid, which is exactly what downstream statistics want. Example given verbatim: E-mini S&P 500 at 200,000 contracts/bucket ≈ 9 buckets/day; a EUR/USD futures day advances ~4 buckets by the time the E-mini fills its first.

## The toxicity model (Section 1, verbatim)

The standard sequential-trade model (their own: Easley-Kiefer-O'Hara-Paperman 1996): information events with probability α, good/bad news split by δ, informed arrival rate µ, uninformed rate ε each side. Liquidity providers learn from order arrivals via Bayes; the spread Σ(t) is the sum of "probability a buy/sell is informed times expected loss to the informed." With symmetric news, Σ = αµ/(αµ + 2ε) · (S̄ − S), and PIN = αµ/(αµ + 2ε) is the fraction of orders that are informed — the key spread determinant. An unanticipated PIN increase = losses for providers who don't adjust.

## VPIN and bulk volume classification (Section 2, verbatim)

- VPIN replaces the Poisson MLE of PIN with a direct estimate in volume time: trades are treated as unit-size aggregations (5 contracts = 5 unit trades, putting intensity into the analysis), grouped into equal-volume buckets; order imbalance per bucket OI_τ = |V^B_τ − V^S_τ|, whose expectation ≈ αµ, while expected total volume ≈ (αµ + 2ε)-flavored — so the PIN ratio is estimable from bucket imbalances without fitting latent parameters.
- Bulk volume classification (BVC): classify FRACTIONS of volume, not discrete trade signs. Over (time or volume) bars, V^B_τ = Σ V_i·Z((P_i − P_{i−1})/σ_∆P) with Z the standard-normal CDF; sells are the complement. No price change splits a bar 50/50; large standardized changes push the split toward one side. Explicitly contrasted with Lee-Ready: discrete vs continuous classification; BVC is robust to order splitting, quote-flicker, and same-price trades that actually execute against both the bid and the offer (their May-2010 E-mini count: 2.65M quote changes/day from adds/cancels vs 0.79M from trades — the CKS 40:1 pattern again).
- Scale invariance (their Eqn 8): E[OI_τ] is invariant to volume rescaling — bucketing makes the measure intensity-based rather than activity-based.
- Conflict disclosure (verbatim from the title page): "VPIN is a trademark of Tudor Investment Corp. The authors have applied for a patent on VPIN and have a financial interest in it." Recorded here as a standing reason for the programme's contested-proxy caution.

## Addendum (12 Nov 2026): Sections 3-6, the appendices, and the JPM companion, now fetched

Sections 3-6 and Tables 2-5 were retrieved in full this session; the previous "not retrieved" limits are replaced by the following.

- Robustness (Section 3): VPIN's LEVEL is classification-dependent (trade-by-trade Lee-Ready, 1-min bars, 10-sec bars give different absolute values) but its RELATIVE position (CDF) is stable across schemes — on the flash crash day all three schemes cross the 0.9 CDF threshold at least two hours before the crash; Lee-Ready additionally collapses to implausibly low toxicity after the crash while prices rose 4%. Starting-point stability: 1,000 shifted trajectories have cross-sectional standard deviation 0.015 against a VPIN mean of ~0.23. Rule for the platform: use CDF(VPIN), never raw levels.
- Estimates (Section 4, Table 2 verbatim): E-mini S&P 500 Jan 2008-Jun 2011, V = 1/50 average daily volume, n = 50: mean VPIN 0.2251, StDev 0.0576, AR(1) 0.9958; WTI crude mean 0.2191. High AR(1) is the n-bucket averaging, not a unit root (memory exactly n buckets long; CDFs stable across the flash-crash split, Table 3). Highest E-mini reading in the sample: May 6 2010; second episode: the Fukushima night session of Mar 14 2011 (2.5% index loss on only 287,360 contracts — toxicity also occurs at LOW trade intensity).
- Predictive content (Section 5, verbatim numbers): correlation of log VPIN with the absolute return over the following volume bucket, (50,250) parameter combination ("one week of data"): 0.400 on 44,537 observations. Conditional probabilities (Table 4a/4b): bottom-quartile VPIN is followed by |return| < 0.25% over 90% of the time; returns > 1.5% NEVER follow very low VPIN; when |return| > 0.75% the immediately preceding VPIN was in its upper quartile 84% of the time — VPIN as an insurance metric against extreme moves. Persistently-high VPIN (Table 5): while VPIN remains in any 5%-tile of the upper quartile, an intermediate return > 0.75% occurs 51.84% of the time at (50,250) and 78.57% at the power-maximizing (10,350) — it takes PERSISTENT toxicity, not a single high reading, to reliably generate large moves.
- Honest counterexample (Section 5.3, verbatim): the June 8 2011 natural-gas "fat finger" crash — 8.1% drop and rebound at LOW toxicity; "not all volatility is due to toxicity." VPIN is NOT directional (footnote 20: "it only indicates toxicity-induced volatility without predicting the sign") and NOT a VIX substitute (footnote 32: complementary, different risk).
- Appendix: full VPIN pseudocode (volume bucketing algorithm; BVC formula with the standard-normal CDF); Monte Carlo validation at 112 parameter nodes x 100,000 simulations: accuracy ~1e-4, bias ~-1e-3 (underestimates true PIN slightly), justifying n = 50.
- JPM companion (partially-garbled mirror, meaning-verified): HFT is defined NOT by speed but by operating in a volume-clock/event-time paradigm; the subordinated-process lineage is traced with verbatim-quoted passages — Mandelbrot-Taylor 1967 (transaction clock; "price changes over a fixed number of transactions may have a Gaussian distribution... over a fixed time period may follow a stable Paretian"), Clark 1973 (volume clock), An-Geman 2000; Mandelbrot's own critique is quoted: recovering normality in "local time" is useless for prediction as long as local time itself is unpredictable — to which the authors answer that HFT market makers LIVE in volume time (inventory turned over per contracts traded, not per clock hours), so no translation back to clock time is needed. Three statistical advantages of volume-time sampling restated: removes most intraday seasonality; partial recovery of normality/iid (their Exhibit 2: E-mini price-change distribution sampled per-minute vs per-1/50-ADV, the volume-clock sample hugging the normal); addresses asynchronous transactions in cross-asset correlation estimation. Practitioner colour (meaning-verified only): GUI traders' round-number order sizes create a detectable human footprint that event-time algorithms can front-run; the LFT survival choices (adopt event time; monitor toxicity statistics; trade in volume bursts).

## What is NOT in these notes (remaining honest limits)

The Andersen-Bondarenko critique literature is still not fetched at all; the queue's "contested proxy, registered caution" label remains the programme's standing posture, now reinforced by the fetched material itself: VPIN's flash-crash evidence is insample narrative plus conditional probabilities on the same window used for the CDF calibration, and the toxicity-volatility link is correlational — the mechanical-volatility critique is not addressed within the paper.

## Programme connections

- ORDERFLOW platform: the volume clock is Tier-2 architecture, not decoration — ELO's own argument (volume-time sampling de-heteroskedasticizes the series) is the fix for the exact failure mode the programme already hit (Lesson 11/13: near-integrated smooth series and within-sample resampling). Volume-bar sampling is a prerequisite for any high-frequency bootstrap or correlation-style test in the platform, and BVC is the platform's default signing method when no aggressor flag exists (Lee-Ready flagged as the discrete, error-prone alternative; the platform's data dictionary must record which convention each feature used).
- O'Hara notes (this KB): the RFS paper is the empirical operationalization of the Glosten-Milgrom/Easley-O'Hara machinery whose derivations live in the Market Microstructure Theory notes — PIN's spread equation there, VPIN here. The two files cross-reference.
- AVFI mirror: AVFI normalizes volume by a slow EMA of volume — a crude volume-clock gesture at weekly scale; ELO makes the principle explicit. Caution: on weekly continuous-contract NQ bars the volume clock inherits the roll/expiry contamination (stage 0d/0e), and VPIN-style toxicity metrics need intraday bar data the weekly programme does not have — no weekly VPIN is proposed.
- Stoll/Madhavan notes: "effective minus realized = informed-flow tax" is the descriptive ancestor of "toxicity"; VPIN is its real-time estimator. Cartea/Johnson: VPIN as a market-maker risk throttle is the industrial version of U3's AVFI throttle — same defensive-insurance economics (and the same lesson the battery taught: insurance underperforms when the insured regime doesn't occur).
- Discipline: the authors' own patent/conflict disclosure is the reason the platform's hypothesis registry should prefer features with no vendor constituency, and the standing caution is an instance of the programme's "never accept a proxy's marketing at face value" rule.

# Kolm, Turiel & Westray (2023), "Deep Order Flow Imbalance: Extracting Alpha at Multiple Horizons from the Limit Order Book" — order-flow-core paper 4

Source: Mathematical Finance 33(4), 1044-1081 (SSRN 3900141, Aug 2021). ACCESS: FULL TEXT NOT FETCHABLE in this session — the SSRN page yielded only the abstract (verified verbatim) and the reference list; the Wiley text is paywalled; no author-hosted or arXiv preprint was found on 12 Nov 2026 (paper_fetch.py on a Python-enabled reset may retry SSRN's PDF, but no confidence is claimed). Everything below is anchored in the verified abstract (marked) or established general knowledge of this paper's methods (flagged). Saved 12 Nov 2026. Wave 3 of the paper queue.

## Verified abstract (verbatim claims)

- Task: deep learning for forecasting high-frequency returns at MULTIPLE horizons, 115 Nasdaq stocks, order book data at the most granular level.
- Core finding: while raw order-book states can be used as inputs, "state-of-the-art predictive accuracy" comes from "simpler 'off-the-shelf' artificial neural networks trained on STATIONARY INPUTS DERIVED from the order book" — models trained on ORDER FLOW (OFI-style features) "significantly outperform most models trained directly on order books".
- Cross-sectional regressions link the LSTM's forecasting performance to stock-level microstructure characteristics, "suggesting that 'information-rich' stocks can be predicted more accurately".
- The effective horizon of stock-specific forecasts "is approximately two average price changes" — predictability is horizon-bounded and short.

## Flagged context (general knowledge, not fetched)

- The OFI features follow the CKS/Xu multi-level OFI definitions (depth-scaled per level), the same inputs as the CCZ paper; the paper is one of the standard citations for "feature engineering beats raw-state deep learning" in LOB prediction.
- Architectures compared include (flagged) deep feedforward nets, LSTM, and variants of CNN/attention LOB models (DeepLOB-style baselines); the "off-the-shelf" point is architectural modesty, not architecture novelty.
- Performance is measured on high-frequency return prediction (R²-style and directional accuracy); "alpha" in the title is prediction, not net-of-cost tradability — the costs/latency question the ORDERFLOW platform must add itself.

## Programme connections

- ORDERFLOW platform: two platform rules come from this paper. (1) FEATURE SIDE: stationary derived inputs (order-flow imbalances, depth-scaled) are the default feature layer for any platform ML tier; raw book states are the ablation baseline, not the mainline — this matches the platform's L0-L6 ablation ladder design. (2) HORIZON SIDE: "≈ two average price changes" is a measured, market-calibrated statement of the decay horizon; the platform's triple-barrier labels and hypothesis horizons must be short relative to this, and any model claiming multi-minute alpha must be shown to beat the feature-based shallow baseline first. Qlib-style "optimization-first" ML culture (already flagged in the GitHub catalog) is exactly what this paper's baseline-first discipline guards against.
- "Information-rich stocks": the cross-sectional heterogeneity result means the platform should EXPECT feature predictive power to vary with instrument microstructure (spread, depth, trade intensity) and pre-register per-instrument expectations rather than pool — the same regime-conditional honesty as Wave 2 (state-dependent forecasting power).
- AVFI mirror: none at weekly horizon — this paper is additional evidence (with CKS and CCZ) that order-flow predictability lives at horizons the weekly programme cannot access. It strengthens the standing conclusion that the parked intraday platform, not the weekly AVFI track, is where flow hypotheses belong.
- Validation spine: deep-learning claims of alpha are the DSR/PBO papers' worst-case population (large trial counts, flexible specification); the platform must register model families and trial counts before any leaderboard is read, and PBO/CSCV only on raw per-period returns (Lesson 11/13 forbid smoothed-series versions).
- Links: CKS notes (the OFI definition everything here builds on), CCZ notes (the same features, linear-vs-ML cross-impact split), ELO notes (volume-clock stationarity as the sampling-side twin of "stationary inputs"), O'Hara notes (information-richness as an adverse-selection/conditioning variable), Javaheri/Tsay notes (the estimation machinery behind "off-the-shelf" nets' simpler cousins).

# Bai & Perron (1998; 2003), structural breaks: estimation, testing, and critical values — regime-machinery paper notes

Sources: Bai & Perron 1998, "Estimating and Testing Linear Models with Multiple Structural Changes" (Econometrica 66(1), 47-78); Bai & Perron 2003, "Critical Values for Multiple Structural Change Tests" (Econometrics Journal 6, 72-78). ACCESS FLAG: the 1998 Econometrica text is available only as a scanned image PDF (unreadable by our tooling). The framework was studied from two fully fetched author-hosted sources: (1) Bai & Perron 2003 (Bai's Columbia page), which restates the BP 1998 model, estimators, and test statistics and supplements the critical values; (2) Casini & Perron 2019, "Structural Breaks in Time Series" (Oxford Research Encyclopedia survey, Perron's BU page, 38 pages fetched in full), covering the sup-Wald/double-maximum/sequential machinery verbatim. 1998-specific results are flagged from general knowledge where used. Saved 12 Nov 2026. Wave 2 of the paper queue (regime machinery).

## The model and estimator (fetched, BP framework)

Partial structural change model with m breaks (m+1 regimes): y_t = x_t'beta + z_t'delta_j + u_t for t in segment j, where beta (on x_t) is NOT subject to change (estimated on the full sample) and delta_j (on z_t) switches; p = 0 gives the pure structural change model. Break dates (T1..Tm) are unknowns; estimates minimize the global sum of squared residuals over all partitions restricted by the trimming parameter epsilon = h/T, which imposes a minimal segment length h and requires breaks to be asymptotically distinct and bounded away from the sample boundaries. Breaks in the error variance are permitted at the same dates as the coefficient breaks. Computation: global SSR minimization over partitions via the dynamic programming algorithm of Bai & Perron 2003 (JAE); one-break-at-a-time estimation is consistent (Bai 1997b: the first fitted break converges to the break giving the greatest SSR reduction) but has different limit distributions unless a repartition step re-estimates each date conditional on neighbours.

## The test statistics (fetched, verbatim)

- supF(k): Wald/F test of 0 breaks vs k breaks, evaluated at the SSR-minimizing break dates (asymptotically equivalent to maximizing the Wald statistic and tractable); the limit is a functional of independent Wiener processes on the segments; HAC-robust covariance versions exist for serial correlation and heteroscedasticity.
- Double maximum tests, for when the number of breaks is not pre-specifiable: UDmax(M, q) = max over m = 1..M of the individual tests, and WDmax with weights equating marginal p-values across m. "The choice M = 5 should be sufficient for most applications" (critical values vary little beyond). Three reasons the survey gives for their usefulness: some multiple-break configurations are hard to detect with single-break tests (e.g. two breaks with regimes 1 and 3 identical); the non-monotonic power problem (tests lose power when the true number of breaks exceeds the number modeled; single-break tests against two-break alternatives per Vogelsang) is alleviated; power is nearly as high as knowing the true number of breaks (BP 2006 simulations).
- Sequential test of l vs l+1 breaks: test each segment of the l-break fit for one additional break; the selected date is the segment-minimizing one; reject if the best segment's SSR reduction is large enough. Applied from l = 0 upward until a non-rejection, this consistently estimates the number of breaks and beats information criteria (BP 2006); the survey's caution: "this sequential procedure should not be applied mechanically. In several cases, it stops too early. The recommendation is to first use a double maximum test to ascertain if any break is at all present," then start the sequence above zero.

## Critical values and trimming (the 2003 paper's purpose, fetched)

All BP test distributions depend on the trimming epsilon. The 1998 paper tabulated epsilon = 0.05 only; Bai & Perron 2000 showed 5% trimming causes substantial finite-sample size distortions when segment variances differ or errors are serially correlated (at T = 100, epsilon = 0.05 means estimating segment variances from ~5 observations, and HAC estimators on very short segments are highly imprecise). The 2003 paper supplies critical values for epsilon = 0.10, 0.15, 0.20, 0.25 via simulation (Wiener processes approximated by partial sums, 10,000 replications, supremum over partitions by dynamic programming) plus response surface regressions covering a wide parameter range. Rule: with heterogeneity across segments or serial correlation, trim 10-25%, not 5%.

## Further cautions from the survey (fetched, all programme-relevant)

- Strong serial correlation in regressors/errors distorts the asymptotic size; remedies: bootstrap, or fixed-bandwidth asymptotics (Kiefer-Vogelsang 2005; Cho-Vogelsang 2017) instead of standard HAC.
- Sup and Exp-Wald tests dominate Mean-type and LM-type tests (Kim-Perron approximate Bahadur efficiency: LM-based tests have zero asymptotic relative efficiency against Wald-based; "any test based on the LM statistic should be avoided").
- Endogenous regressors: "simply using least-squares is preferable over instrumental variable methods" for break testing.
- Confidence sets for break dates (Bai 1997a; inverted-LR; continuous-time HDR frameworks) still lack a single method with good coverage across all break sizes.
- No trimming-free test is recommended: statistics-literature tests allowing breaks at the sample boundary have poor finite-sample properties.

## Programme connections

- The programme's frozen rule set T and its descriptors (12-week momentum, vol8 factor) embed an implicit no-break assumption over 2000-2026. The honest Bai-Perron reading of that period is: known data-level breaks exist (the two roll splices pending the broker/CME export, checklist 093; the 2020 COVID week documented in repairs) and unknown parameter-level breaks are untested. Under programme discipline (holdout spent; battery never re-run; no test without a signed dated amendment), the framework's use here is prospective only: a Bai-Perron parameter-stability audit is a legitimate FUTURE amendment topic (e.g. "did the 12-week rule's edge break at any date?"), which would need supF/UDmax with epsilon >= 0.10, HAC or fixed-bandwidth handling for weekly autocorrelation, and pre-committed max M = 5 — and its result would be descriptive for rule set T, not a re-tuning license.
- Diebold-Inoue 2001 is the identification twin: rare breaks and near-persistent regimes produce the same apparent long memory; Bai-Perron machinery is how one tests the break reading of that ambiguity, with the survey's non-monotonic-power warning explaining why single-break tests (and CUSUMs, per the A1 vacuity lesson) are weak instruments on smooth weekly series.
- Platform prospective use: the ORDERFLOW platform should offer break-date estimation as a service (supF, UDmax first, then sequential; trimmed >= 0.10; deterministic dynamic programming; no LM-based variants), with the result-confidence caveat (wide confidence sets for small breaks) reported automatically. Any platform hypothesis about regime change must declare whether it means a recurring regime (Hamilton/Maheu-McCurdy machinery) or a one-time break (Bai-Perron machinery); the fork changes the estimator, the test, and the null.
- Cross-links: Hamilton 1989 (regimes vs breaks as competing labels for the same low-frequency variation; MS handles recurring, BP handles one-time), Maheu-McCurdy (duration dependence on the regime side), Diebold-Inoue (the confusion theorem binding the two literatures), Tsay (threshold cointegration and unit-root chapters: Perron 1989's classic — trend breaks masquerading as unit roots — is the integer-root ancestor of Diebold-Inoue's fractional version), Härdle (CUSUM regime monitoring as the online cousin of BP's retrospective testing), Bartholomew-Biggs (dynamic programming over partitions is a global-optimization problem; local search finds wrong break sets), and the validation spine (a break search over dates is a specification search: trial counts must be registered and DSR-style deflation applies if a platform user selects the best of many break models).

# Diebold & Inoue (2001), "Long Memory and Regime Switching" — regime-machinery paper notes

Source: Journal of Econometrics 105(1), 131-159. FULL TEXT fetched (author-hosted PDF from Diebold's UPenn page) and studied in full: all three definitions of long memory, both aggregation origins, all three propositions, the entire Monte Carlo section (all tables), and the concluding perspective. Saved 12 Nov 2026. Wave 2 of the paper queue (regime machinery).

## The claim

Structural change, and stochastic regime switching in particular, is easily confused with long memory (fractional integration I(d), 0 < d < 1), whenever only a SMALL amount of breakage occurs in the observed sample. The long-memory surveys of the 1990s (Robinson, Beran, Baillie) "do not so much as mention the issue." The paper provides theory (propositions) and 10,000-replication Monte Carlo evidence that the confusion is "not merely a theoretical curiosity."

## Definitions used (fetched)

- Long-lag autocorrelation: rho(tau) ~ c tau^{2d-1} as tau grows.
- Low-frequency spectrum: f(omega) ~ g omega^{-2d} as omega goes to 0.
- Variance of partial sums: var(S_T) = O(T^{2d+1}) — the definition the results are built on.
Contrast origins (fetched): Granger (1980) aggregation of AR(1)s with beta-distributed coefficients gives x ~ I(1 - q/2); Mandelbrot-Taqqu on/off aggregation with power-law duration tails converges to fractional Brownian motion; Parke (1999) error-duration model: output is the sum of still-alive shocks, long memory when durations have infinite variance. Both routes are aggregation-based and "not fully satisfying" (rigid assumptions; no evidence long memory is more prevalent in aggregated series).

## The three propositions (fetched, core theory)

1. Mixture / mean-plus-noise model: mu_t = mu_{t-1} + v_t, v_t = 0 with prob 1-p, N(0, sigma_w^2) with prob p. If p = O(T^{2d-2}), then var(S_T) = O(T^{2(d-1)+1}), i.e. partial sums of mu (and of y = mu + noise) grow at the I(d) rate. The trick is that the break probability falls with sample size, so realizations "tend to have just a few breaks" at any T.
2. STOPBREAK (Engle-Smith 1999): the standard STOPBREAK model (shocks have permanent effects proportional to their size) is I(1); with its smoothing parameter kappa shrinking at rate T^{-delta}, partial-sum variances match I(1-delta).
3. Markov switching (Hamilton 1989): with staying probabilities p00 = 1 - c0 T^{-delta0} and p11 = 1 - c1 T^{-delta1}, partial sums of y grow at the rate of I((1/2)max(min(delta0, delta1) - |delta0 - delta1|, 0)). The standard FIXED-parameter Markov-switching model is I(0) — but it "can nevertheless generate high persistence at short lags" (Timmermann 2000), and in finite samples near-unity staying probabilities look exactly like fractional integration.

## Monte Carlo evidence (fetched, selected verbatim numbers)

GPH log-periodogram estimation of d on sqrt(T) ordinates, 10,000 replications.
- Mean-plus-noise: empirical size of nominal 5% tests of d = 0 rises in both p and T; at p = 0.01, size is 0.749 at T = 300 and 1.000 by T = 2500. Mean d-hat climbs from 0.21 (p=0.01, T=100) to 0.95 (p=0.05, T=5000). Densities of d-hat are bimodal at small p: a mixture of the no-break density and the at-least-one-break density.
- Markov switching: with p00 = p11 = 0.999, rejection of d = 0 rises with T (0.196 at T=100 to 0.990 at T=5000 for the balanced case) — non-monotonic in T only eventually turning down, because the model is truly I(0) but with high short-lag persistence. The Ryden et al. (1998) finding that MS does a poor job of mimicking long memory is reconciled: their estimated transition matrices had diagonal elements well away from unity; only their model H (both diagonals near unity) is highly persistent.

## The honest perspective (fetched, and important)

The T-varying parameters are a thought experiment, "local to no breaks" in exact analogy to local-to-unity asymptotics: the interest is fixed-parameter, fixed-T processes that are truly I(0) or I(1) but APPEAR fractionally integrated in finite samples. And the concluding caution cuts both ways: "the temptation to jump to conclusions of 'structural change producing spurious inferences of long memory' should be resisted... 'structural change' and 'long memory' may be effectively different labels for the same phenomenon, in which case attempts to label one as 'true' and the other as 'spurious' may be of dubious value." Long memory may remain "a convenient shorthand description... very useful for tasks such as prediction" — with Clements-Krolzig (1998): fixed-coefficient ARs often OUTPERFORM Markov-switching models for forecasting even when the truth is Markov switching.

## Programme connections

- This is the paper behind programme Lesson 11 (and session 13's H10a verdict): the registered sp_z series at lag-1 autocorrelation 0.985 with effective n ~ 2 over 299 weeks is precisely the near-unity-staying-probability regime-switching configuration that mimics long memory and destroys within-sample resampling. Any future statistic computed on smooth weekly-derived series inherits this; persistence-preserving nulls or event-study machinery are the validated alternatives.
- The H5 parameter-scan lesson (the "plateau" at slow EMA settings was a slowness artifact) is Diebold-Inoue in action: parameters that only work at extreme persistence are fitting the appearance of structure that near-integrated processes generate for free.
- The dual honesty is now standing programme doctrine: even if the truth is regimes-with-rare-breaks, a well-specified I(d)-style shorthand may retain predictive value, and a fixed-coefficient rule (our rule set T, a 12-week momentum rule) can forecast as well as or better than an estimated switching model on T ~ 300 weekly bars. This is the evidence-based justification for the programme's deliberate simplicity — and for never upgrading to an estimated MS model without first proving it beats T out of sample (which would spend the holdout; hence the signed-amendment discipline).
- Vol-regime measurement caution: the vol8 factor's persistence could be genuine vol clustering, a break artifact, or fractional integration in volatility; the three are hard to distinguish at our sample size (Mikosch-Starica, cited here, made exactly this point for GARCH).
- Cross-links: Hamilton 1989 (the model analyzed), Maheu-McCurdy 2000 (duration dependence pushes persistence even higher — the bull/bear hazard structure compounds the confusion), Bai-Perron (the deterministic-break twin of the same identification problem), Lo 2002 (the estimation-side cousin: autocorrelation-corrected standard errors), Tsay (the ARFIMA/long-memory formalism this paper warns about misreading), Härdle (the R/S long-memory chapter measured H ~ 0.5 for stocks, 0.33-0.41 for spreads), Bouchaud (empirical autocorrelation magnitudes), and the validation spine (White/SPA: apparent persistence inflates apparent performance of slow variants; DSR/PBO: slow regime-riding variants are exactly the overfitting-prone family).

# Hamilton (1989), "A New Approach to the Economic Analysis of Nonstationary Time Series and the Business Cycle" — regime-machinery paper notes

Source: Econometrica 57(2), 357-384. ACCESS FLAG: the 1989 published text is only available as a scanned image PDF (unreadable by our tooling; two fetches confirmed image-only). These notes are built from two flagged layers: (1) FULL TEXT of Hamilton's own survey "Regime-Switching Models" (Palgrave Dictionary of Economics, May 2005, fetched from his UCSD page, 16 pages, all model construction and inference content below verbatim from it); (2) the 1989 paper's specific empirical results from established general knowledge, flagged inline. Saved 12 Nov 2026. Wave 2 of the paper queue (regime machinery).

## The idea the 1989 paper installed

Economic series occasionally exhibit dramatic breaks (crises, policy shifts) and behave very differently in downturns. The unsatisfactory pre-1989 practice: fit regime 1's AR up to a known date t0, regime 2's AR after it. Hamilton's argument (survey, verbatim): a deterministic break at t0 "is rather unsatisfactory as a probability law that could have generated the data. We surely would not want to maintain that the change... was a deterministic event that anyone would have been able to predict with certainty looking ahead." The fix: an encompassing model

  y_t = c_{s_t} + phi * y_{t-1} + eps_t,   eps_t ~ N(0, sigma^2)

where s_t is a latent two-state variable governed by a first-order Markov chain with transition probabilities pij = Pr(s_t = j | s_{t-1} = i). The break becomes a probabilistic event inside the model, with a probability law for when it happened and whether it reverses (p22 < 1 allows recovery; p22 = 1 would be a permanent break). For business cycles and financial crises "we know that the situation, though dramatic, is not permanent."

## The inference machinery (the Hamilton filter, survey verbatim)

Given only observed y, regime is inferred through filtered probabilities Pr(s_t = j | Omega_t; theta). The iteration: with prior state probabilities Pr(s_{t-1} = i | Omega_{t-1}) and regime densities eta_jt = f(y_t | s_t = j, Omega_{t-1}), the one-step conditional density is f(y_t | Omega_{t-1}) = sum_i sum_j pij * Pr(s_{t-1} = i) * eta_jt, and the updated filter is that same sum's (i,j) term normalized by the conditional density. Iterating t = 1..T yields the sample log-likelihood sum log f(y_t | Omega_{t-1}), maximized numerically over theta = (sigma, phi, c1, c2, p11, p22). Start-up: ergodic unconditional probabilities Pr(s0 = i) = (1 - pjj)/(2 - pii - pjj), or 1/2, or estimate the start itself. Smoothed probabilities Pr(s_t = j | Omega_T) (inference using the full sample) via Kim (1994). The vector generalization is matrix form: f(y_t | Omega_{t-1}) = 1'(P * f_{t|t-1}) with element-wise multiplication; N regimes and vector observations cost no change in logic.

Lineage (survey verbatim): no-AR version first analyzed by Lindgren (1978) and Baum et al. (1980); AR-augmented versions are the hidden Markov models of speech recognition (Poritz 1982, Juang-Rabiner 1985, Rabiner 1989); Markov-switching regressions introduced to econometrics by Goldfeld-Quandt (1973); likelihood first correctly calculated by Cosslett-Lee (1985); the filter-plus-MLE formulation is Hamilton (1989, 1994).

## The practical warnings (survey verbatim, all programme-relevant)

- Time-varying transition probabilities (Diebold-Lee-Weinbach 1994, Filardo 1994) exist, but "there are relatively few transitions among regimes, making it difficult to estimate such parameters accurately"; most applications use a time-invariant chain and N = 2 or 3 regimes.
- Testing N regimes against N+1 is non-standard: under the null some parameters are unidentified (p11 has no population limit), so the likelihood-ratio test has no usual chi-square limit; use Hansen (1992) or Garcia (1998) critical values, Hamilton's (1996) specification tests, Carrasco-Hu-Ploberger (2004), Bayesian marginal likelihoods (Chib 1998), or forecast comparison (Hamilton-Susmel 1994).
- GARCH + Markov switching is path-dependent (the conditional variance inherits the full state history). Solutions: Gray (1996) collapse of the conditional variance across states, or Haas-Mittnik-Paolella (2004) separate per-state GARCH processes, each keeping recursions a function of data only.
- Bayesian estimation: Gibbs sampler alternating theta | s and s | theta; the state draw is Albert-Chib (1993).

## The 1989 application (flagged, established general knowledge; not in the fetched survey)

Two-state switching-mean model of quarterly US real GNP growth (1951-1984 sample): an expansion state with positive mean growth and a recession state with negative mean; estimated staying probabilities around 0.75 for recessions and about 0.9 for expansions, implying expected durations of roughly a year or less for contractions and several years for expansions; downturns are sharp while recoveries are gradual (asymmetry the linear AR cannot represent); the filtered recession probabilities align with NBER chronology; and the model fits significantly better than a linear AR by likelihood. Verify against the full text if ever cited formally.

## Programme connections

- The AVFI weekly framework already runs crude regime descriptors (12-week momentum state, reversal_down state, vol8 throttle factor), all DETERMINISTIC functions of data, i.e. the pre-Hamiltonian regime assignment. Hamilton's contribution is the probabilistic version: filtered regime probabilities with honest uncertainty, which is what the ORDERFLOW platform's regime module should implement (weekly bars, 2-3 states, time-invariant chain, smoothed probabilities for reporting and filtered probabilities for decisions).
- The regime-count testing trap is a standing platform rule: nobody may claim "3 regimes fit better" with a naive LR test; the unidentified-parameter problem means regime-count claims need Hansen/Garcia critical values or Bayesian marginal likelihood, and small-sample weekly data (T ~ 300) makes regime-count estimation fragile, exactly the few-transitions warning.
- Cross-links within Wave 2: Diebold-Inoue 2001 (analyzes this exact model: near-unity staying probabilities generate apparent long memory and effective-sample-size collapse; our sp_z at lag-1 0.985 is the observation-side symptom), Maheu-McCurdy 2000 (the applied descendant adding duration dependence), Bai-Perron (the one-time-break alternative to recurring regimes).
- Links back: Tsay (TAR/STAR/Markov-switching chapters: threshold switching as the deterministic cousin, already studied), the Elliott Wave base (wave counting is informal, drawn regime assignment; the N1-N4 null battery is the discipline this machinery demands before any wave claim), H8/H9 (threshold-style top/bottom detectors are TAR cousins of the MS framework), Bouchaud (measured autocorrelation/persistence stylized facts that any regime model must reproduce), and the validation spine (a family of regime models searched over state counts is a multiple-testing problem: White/SPA discipline applies to the search over specifications too).

# Maheu & McCurdy (2000), "Identifying Bull and Bear Markets in Stock Returns" — regime-machinery paper notes

Source: Journal of Business & Economic Statistics 18(1), 100-112. ACCESS FLAG: the published JBES text is paywalled and no legitimate full-text copy was fetchable; the ABSTRACT was fetched and verified verbatim (RePEc). The framework was studied from the authors' own successor working papers, fetched in full from author/university sites: Maheu-McCurdy-Song, "Extracting Bull and Bear Markets from Stock Returns" (U Toronto WP, June 2009) and Maheu-McCurdy-Song, "Components of Bull and Bear Markets: Bull Corrections and Bear Rallies" (Feb 2012). 2000-specific results not in the abstract are flagged from established general knowledge. Saved 12 Nov 2026. Wave 2 of the paper queue (regime machinery).

## Verified abstract (verbatim content)

Markov-switching model incorporating duration dependence in BOTH the conditional mean and the conditional variance of stock returns; sorts returns into a high-return stable state (bull) and a low-return volatile state (bear); the filter identifies all major stock-market downturns in over 160 years of monthly data; bull markets have a declining hazard function although the best market gains come at the START of a bull market; volatility INCREASES with duration in bear markets; duration-dependent volatility captures volatility clustering.

## The method in the lineage (from the fetched successor papers, verbatim)

- Hamilton (1989): two-state Markov switching on quarterly US GNP growth. Durland-McCurdy (1994): transition probabilities as a function of DURATION in the state. Maheu-McCurdy (2000): duration-dependent transitions AND duration-dependent intra-state dynamics for returns and volatilities. Duration/hazard logic: the hazard is the probability of leaving the current regime given how long it has lasted; duration dependence makes regimes self-referential processes rather than memoryless coin flips.
- The ex-post dating critique (2009 paper, verbatim): traditional bull/bear identification sorts returns ex post from peaks and troughs (Gonzalez et al. 2005, Lunde-Timmermann 2004, Pagan-Sossounov 2003, Bry-Boschan business-cycle dating). Drawbacks: "a turning point can only be identified several observations after it occurs," and "these methods cannot be used for statistical inference on returns or for investment decisions which require more information from the return distribution." What is needed is a probability model of the full return distribution with latent regimes.
- The 4-state extension (2009/2012): two states per regime — bull and bull-correction inside the bull regime; bear and bear-rally inside the bear regime — allowing persistent intra-regime reversals (secondary trends) without exiting the regime. Identification: long-run mean negative in bear, positive in bull; overall mean positive. Intra- AND inter-regime transitions (a bull correction can revert to bull or become a bear). Moments coupled (mean and variance jointly regime-dependent) fits best. Bayesian estimation accounts for parameter and regime uncertainty and gives probability statements about future regimes and returns.
- Why two states fail at higher frequency (verbatim): a 2-state MS model on higher-frequency data "results in too many switches between the high and low return states... it is incapable of extracting the low frequency trends in the market." The correction/rally states absorb the secondary trends.

## 2000-specific results beyond the abstract (flagged, general knowledge)

Applied to monthly US stock index returns 1834-1996 era data; the bear state combines negative mean with high and duration-increasing variance; expected durations of regimes are endogenous outputs (the declining bull hazard means long-lived bulls are less likely to end than young ones); smoothed state probabilities align with the classical downturn chronology. Verify against the full text if ever cited formally.

## Programme connections

- The AVFI weekly framework's states (12-week momentum, reversal_down, vol8 throttle) are duration-FREE, deterministic regime descriptors — the special case Maheu-McCurdy generalizes. The 2012 paper's question "will this bull market correction become a bear market?" is structurally the same question the PGT terminal detector (Amendment 5, unsigned) asks about G2Top runs; the occupancy analysis there (n=11 runs against 299 weeks) is exactly the "few transitions make regime estimation fragile" reality these papers handle by using 160-year samples. Our 1364 weekly bars are marginal for duration-dependent estimation; that scarcity informed the H11a power verdict (only ~4% absolute effects detectable).
- Platform prospective use: the ORDERFLOW platform's regime module should be a duration-dependent MS model on weekly (or higher-frequency) returns with the 4-state decomposition, filtered probabilities for decisions and smoothed probabilities for reporting, Bayesian estimation for honest regime uncertainty, and the ex-post dating critique as the design rule: no dashboard may label a turning point with a rule that only works several observations later without saying so.
- The declining-hazard + best-gains-early result connects to the momentum literature's plain lesson the programme already encoded in rule set T: a young confirmed trend carries more expected drift than a mature one; if a future amendment ever adds trend-age conditioning, the hazard function is the correct formal object, not ad-hoc caps.
- Cross-links: Hamilton 1989 (the base filter and the two-state construction), Diebold-Inoue 2001 (duration dependence pushes persistence higher still, deepening the long-memory confusion), Bai-Perron (one-time breaks vs recurring regimes: the modelling fork any regime claim must choose), the Elliott Wave base (bull/bear/correction/rally is what wave counting attempts to label informally with zero probabilistic machinery; this is the formal, testable counterpart and the standard H3 nulls should reference), Bouchaud (volatility persistence stylized facts the duration-dependent variance must reproduce), and the validation spine (a 4-state Bayesian model on the platform is a trial generator: any reported regime-based edge must clear DSR/PBO discipline with the model-specification search honestly counted).

# The Quants (Scott Patterson) — study notes

Narrative history of the rise of quantitative hedge funds: the genealogy from Ed Thorp's card-counting and Kelly-criterion money management, through Black Monday 1987 and the fat-tails warning, to the four titans (Griffin, Muller, Asness, Weinstein), the August 2007 "quant meltdown," the 2008 crisis, and the post-mortem (Financial Modelers' Manifesto, Mandelbrot's vindication, Lo's adaptive markets). Where Dark Pools is the story of market *structure*, this is the story of market *models*: how they won, how they broke, and what that teaches about prediction limits.

## The storyline (chapter by chapter)

### Act 1: The godfather (Ch.1-3)
- **Ch.1 ALL IN**: opens at the February 2006 Wall Street Poker Night (St. Regis, invited by Peter Muller of Morgan Stanley's Process Driven Trading; Ken Griffin, Boaz Weinstein among the players) then rewinds. The grail of every quant: **alpha**, a systematic edge over the market. The culture: mathematics as "The Truth," a universal secret discoverable through numbers. Foreshadowing of hubris and 2007; the Isaac Newton epigraph theme (genius undone by the South Sea Bubble).
- **Ch.2 Ed Thorp, the godfather**: UCLA math professor, extends blackjack hi-lo card counting (with Claude Shannon) into a real edge; **Beat the Dealer** (1962). Core lesson: a small statistical edge, played many times, compounds into near-certainty (law of large numbers) — provided bets are sized right. Meets John Kelly Jr. → **Kelly criterion**: bet a fraction of capital proportional to your edge divided by the odds; never overbet, or ruin becomes certain even with a positive edge. Thorp then proves markets can be beaten the same way.
- **Ch.3 BEAT THE MARKET (1967)**: Thorp and Sheen Kassouf attack **warrants and convertible bonds**: price the embedded option instead of guessing direction; hedged positions that profit regardless of whether the stock rises or falls, a strategy conventional wisdom deemed impossible (the source cuts the sentence there, but the historical record: it produced 20%+ annual returns). Ideas inherited from Louis Bachelier (1900, random walk), Norbert Wiener (Brownian motion): price moves as a stochastic process. Key reconceptualization: **volatility is itself quantifiable and tradeable**. Thorp's option-pricing work pre-dates Black-Scholes (1973); Princeton-Newport Partners (with James Regan) becomes the prototype quant fund, and its stat-arb pairs-trading relative-value playbook founds statistical arbitrage.

### Act 2: The warning nobody priced (Ch.4)
- **Ch.4 THE VOLATILITY SMILE — Black Monday, October 19, 1987**: the Dow falls 508 points (-23%), the worst single day ever. At the Chicago Mercantile Exchange, $2 billion of S&P futures losses threaten to shut the clearinghouse; Leo Melamed calls Alan Greenspan; the Fed floods the system and the market recovers. The mechanism: **portfolio insurance** (Hayne Leland's brainchild), a Black-Scholes-based strategy mechanically *selling futures as prices fall* to "insure" a portfolio's value — a feedback loop that turned decline into cascade. Portfolio insurance was blamed for deepening the crash, and the crash cracked the credibility of efficient-market theory. **Benoit Mandelbrot's** critique became central: real price changes have **fat tails** and wilder swings than the Gaussian assumption; the models' tails were too thin. Option markets answered permanently: the **volatility smile/skew**, the crash's scar tissue — deep-out-of-the-money options have been priced with a fear premium ever since. Thorp survived (disciplined sizing); later he helped expose **Madoff's** fraud as impossible-to-be-real returns. Lesson of the act: models that ignore tail behavior are not wrong on average, they are wrong catastrophically.

### Act 3: Four of a kind (Ch.5-8)
- **Ch.5 FOUR OF A KIND** — the generation that industrialized Thorp's edge:
  - **Ken Griffin**: starts convertible arbitrage from a Harvard dorm (1987, installs a satellite dish for bond prices); funded advice from Thorp; founds **Citadel** (1990).
  - **Peter Muller**: BARRA factor-model alumnus; joins Morgan Stanley, builds **PDT** (Process Driven Trading, 1992-93), the "Midas"-touch internal fund.
  - **Cliff Asness**: PhD under **Eugene Fama** (efficient-markets high priest) at Chicago; finds **momentum** — past winners keep winning for months — which should not exist under EMH; joins Goldman's asset-management arm, founds **AQR** (1998) on value + momentum.
  - **Boaz Weinstein**: Deutsche Bank credit-derivatives star; chess master; later runs **Saba**.
  - Backdrop: **LTCM's 1998 collapse** (Nobel laureates, 25:1+ leverage, converging trades that didn't converge in time) as the standing warning every new fund claims to have absorbed.
- **Ch.6 THE WOLF — Aaron Brown**: quant trader who beat Liar's Poker-era traders with probability thinking (first real money as a poker player using odds); publicizes the culture war: **quant discipline vs. gut-trader instinct**. Meanwhile **Jim Simons' Renaissance Technologies** — Simons a mathematician (codebreaker background, Chern-Simons) — builds the **Medallion Fund** with speech-recognition/pattern-matching technology: models trained on data, not stories. Medallion becomes the greatest money machine in history (long-run net returns far above everyone else's).
- **Ch.7 THE MONEY GRID**: the scaling years. Citadel's launch (1989-90, ~$4.6M) and grind; **1994**: the Fed's rate hikes hit highly levered relative-value books everywhere (the "Great Bond Massacre"), Citadel loses big and responds with **client lockups and multi-strategy diversification** — the modern hedge fund format. PDT at "Midas" strength; Asness runs Global Alpha at Goldman; Weinstein's credit book grows inside Deutsche. Moral: leverage plus correlated positions means the cycle can hit everyone at once.
- **Ch.8 LIVING THE DREAM (2004-2007)**: hedge funds boom on cheap credit; everyone is borrowing. Thorp warns the trade has become **saturated**: too much money chasing the same anomalies shrinks the edge and raises the leverage needed to harvest it. Acquisitions absorb blowups: Amaranth (2006 natural-gas, $6B), Sowood (2007, absorbed by Citadel). Weinstein's Saba positions for the coming credit storm; the mortgage machine (CDOs) runs at full speed, with **David Li's Gaussian copula** supplying the fantasy that default correlations are stable and known — the same thin-tail error as 1987, now embedded in trillions of dollars of structured credit.

### Act 4: The August Factor (Ch.9-10)
- **Ch.9 (mid-2007)**: Aaron Brown joins **AQR as risk officer**; quant pioneers watch the mortgage machine's smoke with their own measures. Khandani-Lo-style anomaly collapse signals emerge. The stage: quant equity funds are quietly levered into *the same kind* of factor positions, funded in the credit markets that are now cracking.
- **Ch.10 THE AUGUST FACTOR — the quant meltdown, August 2007**: on August 6-9, quant funds across the board suffer unprecedented, simultaneous losses; not just in one market but across every strategy, everywhere. Deleveraging: a credit-constrained holder (or several) starts dumping positions; those sales push the very prices quant models say are mispriced further away; margin calls force more selling — a cascade through **common positions and common models**. Carry trades unwind; Goldman injects cash into Global Alpha; AQR, unwisely per the author, defends the positions and suffers more. The signature image: **"events that models predicted would occur once in 10,000 years happened every day, for three days."** In three weeks the anomaly is over and markets recover — but the shock proves quant strategies share hidden **commonality in liquidity**: one pocket of trouble in subprime can drain an unrelated equity-stat-arb pond. (This is the narrative twin of FPR Ch.9's funding-liquidity spirals and commonality-in-liquidity evidence.)

### Act 5: Crisis and post-mortem (Ch.11-14)
- **Ch.11 DOOMSDAY CLOCK (late 2007 - early 2008)**: opportunists buy cheap assets (E*Trade); Lo and Khandani formalize the August episode as systemic risk; quants are approached by wounded institutions (Lehman); a rogue-trader blowup at **Société Générale (Jérôme Kerviel, January 2008)** forces emergency unwinds that roil markets again.
- **Ch.12 A FLAW (2008-09)**: in crisis, everything quant goes wrong together: Citadel down ~55% (hit in all its strategies at once; client redemptions), AQR ~-46% (with Asness' bitterness at investors who said the models had failed); Greenspan's famous congressional testimony: **"I found a flaw… I've been very distressed by that fact"** — the admission that risk models built on decades of data missed what actually happens. Weinstein, after a huge blowout month (a ~$2B quarter loss on his watch), walks from Deutsche and rebuilds with Saba.
- **Ch.13 THE DEVIL'S WORK**: the intellectual reckoning. **Paul Wilmott and Emanuel Derman** publish the **Financial Modelers' Manifesto** with a modeler's **Hippocratic Oath** ("I will remember that I didn't make the markets… my job is to help them"; models are metaphors, not the thing itself; never confuse precision with accuracy). Jeremy Grantham, Paul Krugman and others attack EMH and the orthodoxy that markets price risk correctly; **Mandelbrot is vindicated**: fat tails, wild variation, dependence — the math was there since the 1960s. Thorp, consulted, warns of **leverage** as the recurring poison and designs new low-leverage strategies (System X).
- **Ch.14 DARK POOLS (2009 epilogue)**: the poker night reconvenes, subdued. Medallion recovers fast (its edge intact — still the exception); Renaissance's larger funds (RIEF) underperform, showing that capacity and crowding matter even for the best. The field pivots: **Andrew Lo's Adaptive Market Hypothesis** — markets evolve like ecosystems; edges are food sources that get eaten, and models must evolve or die (behavioral biases persist but get arbitraged only partially and slowly). Behavioral finance gains standing. Patterson's closing concerns: leveraged ETFs, HFT (the bridge to his next book, Dark Pools), and the next LTCM happening "in less than five minutes."

## The durable mechanics worth remembering

1. **Kelly criterion**: bet size ~ edge/odds; expected growth maximized at that fraction; **overbetting converts a positive edge into certain ruin**. The counterintuitive core: it caps your bet *below* what maximizes expected wealth, trading some return for survival. Every blowup in the book (LTCM, Citadel 2008, PDT-lite funds) is, at root, Kelly violation via leverage.
2. **An edge is an ecosystem variable, not a constant** (Lo): anomaly returns decay as money chases them (Thorp's saturation warning); edges must be refreshed. Even Renaissance's outsize returns coexist with its bigger funds' mediocrity — capacity constraints are real.
3. **Fat tails / non-Gaussian reality** (Mandelbrot): real returns have much fatter tails than the normal distribution; the 10,000-year flood happens regularly. Model error is concentrated exactly where sizing decisions matter most. The volatility smile is the market's own permanent monument to this fact (and to the 1987 feedback loop).
4. **Feedback loops**: portfolio insurance in 1987; deleveraging cascades in 2007-08; models that say "sell more as price falls" (or trigger margin calls that do so) manufacture their own hurricanes. A strategy is incomplete until you specify what happens when *everyone else* holds (or is forced out of) the same trade.
5. **Commonality in liquidity / crowded trades**: uniform models + leverage + shared prime brokers = hidden correlation that appears exactly when you don't want it (August 2007). Correlation of strategies is not the average of their pairwise correlations; it is conditional on the funding state (FPR Ch.9's liquidity spirals, in narrative form).
6. **Model epistemology** (Derman/Wilmott): a model is a metaphor with numbers; precision is not accuracy; uncertainty in inputs (volatility, correlation, liquidity) propagates to outputs. Never let the model's confidence become yours.
7. **EMH vs momentum**: Fama's own student finding momentum is the perfect emblem of the field's tension. The resolution offered by the book: markets are mostly efficient *relative to the arbitrage capital available* (limits to arbitrage again), and slow behavioral biases leave persistent, harvestable-but-shrinking inefficiencies.
8. **Ruin asymmetry**: losing 55% requires ~122% gain to recover; a drawdown has a permanent cost even in an eventual recovery. Risk management is not error-correcting, it is survival-preserving.

## Connections to earlier books

- **To the Elliott Wave base (Balan)**: the book is, read one way, a 300-page empirical argument for two things Elliott practitioners hold anyway: (a) markets have **regimes** in which statistical relationships that held for years simply stop holding (August 2007, 2008) — the quantitative mirror of Elliott's insistence that patterns transform at degree boundaries; (b) **prediction has hard limits** — even Medallion-scale machine learning does not repeal fat tails, and Mandelbrot's critique of Gaussian assumptions matches Elliott's own observation that crowd psychology alternates between measured advance and runaway extension. Where Balan prescribes wave counts, the quants prescribe statistical edges; both books prescribe the same medicine on position sizing: never bet what you cannot survive (Kelly; Elliott's own risk rules on failed counts).
- **To Harris / Hasbrouck / Biais-Glosten-Spatt / Foucault-Pagano-Röell**: the August 2007 chapter is the narrative embodiment of FPR Ch.9: **funding liquidity spirals** (losses → tighter margin → forced selling → more losses) and **commonality in liquidity** (unrelated strategies crash together because they share funding channels). Khandani-Lo's study of the 2007 anomaly collapse appears in both books. Portfolio insurance 1987 is the classic example of a trading strategy with negative externality that microstructure theory (Harris on order types, BGS on stability vs. other objectives) discusses formally: mechanical rules can destabilize the very prices they assume are exogenous.
- **To Bouchaud**: direct continuity — Mandelbrot's fat tails, the square-root impact of large liquidations (the 2007 unwind happened *because* liquidation impact pushed prices away from "fair value"), and the latent-liquidity view that big positions can only be exited at a cost that scales super-linearly with urgency. The August Factor is a live demonstration of Bouchaud's impact laws fed by funding constraints.
- **To Master the Markets (Williams/VSA)**: the quants are the modern "smart money" whose footprints VSA tries to read. The book supplies the *why* behind VSA's "effort vs. result": a quant unwinding a levered book is the effort; impact that overshoots "value" is the result — the exact conditions under which VSA reads absorption, climactic volume, and no-supply rallies. Weinstein's Saba and Griffin's Citadel are the professional syndicates Williams sketches in the abstract.
- **To Dark Pools (same author)**: overlapping cast (Weinstein, Griffin, the poker night) and a handoff: The Quants ends where Dark Pools begins — the machines that ran the models moved into the market-structure arms race (HFT), and Patterson's "next LTCM in five minutes" concern is realized in the Flash Crash anatomy covered in the Dark Pools notes.

## Strategy / research candidates (for Andrea's weekly NQ Elliott Wave practice)

1. **Kelly-informed sizing with a fat-tail haircut**: compute rough position sizes as a fraction of edge over risk, then **halve it** (fractional Kelly) because (a) edge estimates on weekly wave counts are uncertain, (b) fat tails mean the ruin boundary is closer than Gaussian math suggests. Rule of thumb to test: never risk more than ~1-2% per trade, sized down further when the count sits past a fourth-wave or extension where the invalidation is far away.
2. **Crowding and correlation regime checks on the weekly chart**: before acting on a wave count, ask what the *uniform-positioning* story is. When everyone can see the same obvious fifth-wave top, the risk of an August-2007-style cascade (everyone exiting through the same door) is highest. Practical signals to research: CFTC COT extremes in leveraged funds plus volatility-market stress (VIX term structure inversion) as a "funding fragile" flag that raises the priority of the bearish count.
3. **Model-breaking regime shifts as the Elliott thesis restated**: maintain a literal "regime notebook": list the relationships your analysis quietly assumes (e.g., NQ leading the Dow, breadth confirming, credit spreads benign). When one breaks — as factor relationships broke in August 2007 — treat it as evidence that the market is shifting degree or character, and reduce exposure until the new regime is legible. This is Elliott's "market exceeds prediction at turning points" made operational.
4. **Feedback-loop awareness in crash legs**: in a third-of-a-third or C-wave collapse, part of the move is mechanical (deleveraging, stop cascades), which is why such legs overshoot "fair value" and why their endings are recognizable by climactic volume — the VSA climax signal. Research: quantify post-climax retrace behavior on NQ weekly data as the model-legible signature of forced selling exhaustion.
5. **Drawdown arithmetic as a standing rule**: because a 55% Citadel-style drawdown takes ~122% to recover, predefine the equity stop for the whole research program (e.g., -20% study-stop on any model portfolio before it is abandoned/overhauled), mirroring how the surviving quants (Thorp, Brown) institutionalized their exits rather than negotiating them mid-crisis.
