# Trades, Quotes and Prices: Financial Markets Under the Microscope (Bouchaud, Bonart, Donier & Gould, Cambridge University Press 2018)

Study notes, Book 5 in the series. Saved November 2026. Teaching style.

## IMPORTANT status flag: this upload is an excerpt

The uploaded PDF contains only the publisher's sample: front matter (title, contents, preface, acknowledgements), all of Chapter 1 (The Ecology of Financial Markets, book pages 5-21), and Chapter 2 sections 2.1 through 2.1.4 (book pages 22-27). The full book runs 441+ pages across nine parts and 22 chapters. So these notes cover the sample only; the rest of the book is pending until a full copy is available. The table of contents was fully mapped (below) so we know exactly what is missing.

What this book is: the econophysics entry in our stack. Where Harris gave institutions, Hasbrouck gave econometrics, and Biais-Glosten-Spatt gave economic theory, Bouchaud et al. give the physicist's bottom-up, data-first view (calibrated on NASDAQ LOBSTER data). Notably, the book's own Chapter 1 further-reading list cites Biais-Glosten-Spatt (2005), Hasbrouck (2007), and Harris-adjacent literature, confirming it sits exactly in our sequence's conversation.

## Full book structure (from the TOC, for when we obtain the complete text)

- Part I: How and why do prices move? (Ch 1 Ecology; Ch 2 Statistics of price changes)
- Part II: Limit order books introduction (Ch 3 LOB mechanics; Ch 4 empirical properties)
- Part III: LOB models (Ch 5-6 single-queue dynamics; Ch 7 joint-queue dynamics; Ch 8 Santa Fe model)
- Part IV: Clustering and correlations (Ch 9 Hawkes processes; Ch 10 long-range persistence of order flow)
- Part V: Price impact (Ch 11 impact of market orders; Ch 12 impact of metaorders and the square-root law)
- Part VI: Micro-scale market dynamics (Ch 13-14 propagator models)
- Part VII: Adverse selection and liquidity provision (Ch 15 Kyle model; Ch 16 determinants of the spread, MRR; Ch 17 profitability of market-making)
- Part VIII: Meso-scale dynamics (Ch 18 latent liquidity and Walrasian auctions; Ch 19 impact in a continuous-time double auction; Ch 20 information content of prices)
- Part IX: Practical consequences (Ch 21 optimal execution; Ch 22 market fairness and stability)

Parts V, VIII, and IX are the most strategy-relevant material of the entire book and are all outside the sample. This is the single biggest gap in the reading program so far, because the square-root law of impact and the propagator framework are this book's signature contributions.

## Chapter 1: The Ecology of Financial Markets (read in full)

### 1.1 The rules of trading, from auctions to LOBs

- **Walrasian auction:** buyers post maximum bid prices, sellers post minimum offer prices, all as firm commitments (liquidity provision). The auctioneer gathers an (invisible) order book and picks the price p* that maximizes exchanged volume Q(p) = min[D(p), S(p)], where the supply curve S(p) is cumulative sell volume at price <= p (increasing in p) and the demand curve D(p) is cumulative buy volume at price >= p (decreasing in p). In the continuous idealization, clearing requires D(p*) = S(p*) (Walras' law). On discrete price grids the equality generally fails, so **priority rules** are needed to decide who trades at the clearing price.
- Why Walrasian auctions fail in practice: no coordination. Either both sides are greedy and nothing overlaps (Q(p*) = 0), or one side is greedy and the price jumps on tiny volume.
- **Market-makers** (the historical fix): legally obliged to keep a fair and orderly market, in exchange for privileges. Two tasks: quoting (binding bid b with volume V_b, ask a with volume V_a, executed against any incoming order up to the quoted volume) and clearing (choosing p* to minimize unsatisfied orders, possibly adding their own volume). Quotes signal a sensible price to the whole market, like retail FX boards today. If the clearing price lands outside [b, a], that reveals one-sided pressure and the market-maker re-quotes.
- **Continuous-time double auctions / LOBs:** today's default. No auctioneer, no designated market-maker; a transaction happens whenever a buyer and a seller agree. The book is visible to all in real time, so it becomes part of everyone's information set.

### 1.2 Why does anyone trade at all?

- The classical informed/uninformed dichotomy (insider example: buy at 95 knowing a 100 takeover bid is coming) is mostly useless in practice because information is hard to define and even harder to measure. Is observing another trade itself information?
- Scale check: about 0.5% of market cap of a large-cap stock changes hands every day. Insider trading is illegal. That volume cannot be insider trades.
- **Statistical information** redefinition: an informed trade is one whose ex-ante expected profit over horizon T is strictly positive after all costs. Information = any ability to predict future price changes, whatever the reason. Under this lens, fundamental and technical (quantitative) analysis are both information sources; empirical signals do correlate with future prices, but very weakly: the dispersion of future changes dwarfs the mean predicted change.
- **The no-trade puzzle:** prices are close to martingales, so the best estimate of the future price is the current price. Any trade has a counterparty who regrets it as soon as the price moves. Combined with costs (fees, impact), the conclusion is that speculative traders trade too much, overestimating their signal and underestimating costs (Odean 1999).
- **Skill vs luck is nearly unidentifiable:** for a stock with 15% annual volatility and a true 5% annual edge, it takes roughly T = (15/5)^2 = 9 years for the realized return to differ from zero at one-sigma significance. Trading a pure coin flip still shows a profitable trade 50% of the time. This is one of the most important practical numbers in the whole program.

### 1.3 The risks of market-making

- **Adverse selection** (winner's curse): binding quotes get picked off by better-informed traders. Constant quotes against imbalanced flow accumulate a position on the wrong side. Market-makers widen the spread to compensate, and competition prevents it widening too much but never to zero.
- **Price impact, formally:** with mid-price m = (a+b)/2 and trade sign epsilon (+1 buy, -1 sell), the long-term impact R_inf := E[epsilon (m_inf - m)] > 0. A market-maker's average profit per leg is s/2 - R_inf, so market-making is only profitable if **s/2 > R_inf**: the half-spread must exceed the expected adverse impact. The whole spread exists to pay for impact.
- **Skewness:** most trades are nearly uninformed (harmless to the maker), a few are deadly. The round-trip P&L distribution of a market-maker is negatively skewed with a fat adverse tail. Making small steady profits punctuated by rare large losses.

### 1.4 The liquidity game (the chapter's deep result)

- Even "highly liquid" markets are not liquid: volume at the best quotes is on the order of 10^-4 of market cap at any instant, versus about 0.5% of market cap traded per day. Revealed liquidity is tiny; **latent liquidity** is large.
- Reason: adverse selection again. Liquidity providers hide their hands, show small size, and avoid revealing intentions. Markets run a hide-and-seek game.
- Consequence 1: **large trades must be fragmented.** Buying 1% of PCLN takes on the order of 100-1000 trades, several days, roughly twice a typical day's volume. Even an insider cannot use information all at once without moving the price and revealing the signal.
- Consequence 2 (the big one): **prices cannot be in equilibrium** in the traditional instantaneous sense. The imbalance between true supply and demand is necessarily latent and can only be slowly digested. Equilibrium is at best a long-time-scale notion, and by then the information set has moved. The equilibrium price is an ever-moving target.
- Take-home list of the chapter (verbatim in the book): markets are hard to organize; yesterday's designated market-makers became today's competitive liquidity providers; anyone can provide or take liquidity; adverse selection forces small displayed size.

## Chapter 2, sections 2.1-2.1.4: the random walk primer (as far as the sample goes)

- **Bachelier 1900:** at each instant buyers balance sellers, so future price changes are unpredictable: prices are martingales. IID zero-mean increments plus CLT give Gaussian random walks; he priced options 70 years before Black-Scholes.
- **Bachelier's first law:** the variogram V(tau) := E[(p_{t+tau} - p_t)^2] grows linearly: V(tau) = D tau, with D = sigma_r^2 p_bar^2 (volatility defined in relative terms). Typical price changes scale as the square root of the time lag. Short-horizon price changes are closer to additive than multiplicative (tick size etc.), but multiplicative (geometric Brownian) is the longer-scale convention.
- **Correlated returns:** with ACF C_r(tau), the sampled volatility obeys sigma^2(tau) = sigma_r^2 [1 + 2 sum_{u=1..tau} (1 - u/tau) C_r(u)]. The **volatility signature plot** (sigma(tau) vs tau) is the diagnostic: flat for a pure random walk, increasing for trending series (positive ACF), decreasing for mean-reverting ones (negative ACF). For an exponentially decaying ACF with parameter rho, long-lag volatility is amplified by (1+rho)/(1-rho): tiny positive autocorrelation compounds dramatically at long horizons.
- **High-frequency (microstructure) noise:** prices observed as random walk plus an OU-type noise eta_t with correlation time tau_eta. Interpretations: temporary mispricing that mean-reverts, or the bid-ask bounce (there is no single price p_t, only bid and ask, so any proxy carries mean-reverting error). This adds 2 sigma_eta^2 (1 - e^{-tau/tau_eta}) / tau to sigma^2(tau), which inflates short-lag volatility and decays to zero at long lags: microstructure noise masquerades as mean reversion in the signature plot.
- **Empirics:** signature plots of most liquid assets are almost flat from seconds to months. The S&P500 E-mini (the closest cousin to your NQ) shows only about a 20% decrease from 1 second to 10^6 seconds: weakly mean-reverting, nothing more.

## Connections to earlier books

- **To Balan (Elliott Wave base):** two direct bridges. (1) The signature-plot result is the honest statistical backdrop for wave counting: real prices are almost exactly random walks at all measurable scales, so any wave-counting edge is necessarily small relative to noise. Balan's own advice to prefer weekly charts partly works *because* microstructure noise (bid-ask bounce, the 2 sigma_eta^2 / tau term) has died away at that sampling scale. (2) Conversely, Bouchaud's central thesis that markets *slowly digest latent imbalances* is the microstructure mechanism behind trend existence at all: if supply and demand were instantly equilibrated, there would be nothing for a Wave 3 to ride. The digestion process is the trend. The (1+rho)/(1-rho) amplification formula quantifies how a barely-visible positive autocorrelation at short scale produces big multi-week directional moves.
- **To Biais-Glosten-Spatt:** BGS works in the Walrasian/equilibrium tradition (their synthetic model has a well-defined competitive benchmark). Bouchaud Ch 1 explicitly attacks that framing: equilibrium is an ever-moving target because latent liquidity is digested slowly. Also, Bouchaud's R_inf and the s/2 > R_inf profitability condition are the same objects as BGS's adverse-selection spread component (Glosten-Milgrom/Kyle), restated without the game-theoretic machinery. BGS's call-auction material (their Section 3.1) is Bouchaud's Section 1.1.1 with the same caveats about coordination failures.
- **To Hasbrouck:** the OU microstructure noise and bid-ask bounce are exactly the Roll model's serial correlation, seen through the variogram instead of the trade-to-trade covariance. Hasbrouck's theme that short-horizon reversion is microstructure, not information, is what the signature plot shows graphically. Hasbrouck's Bayes/VAR estimation machinery would be the tool to actually separate the eta_t component on data.
- **To Harris:** the ecology chapter is Harris's trader taxonomy compressed (informed/uninformed, speculators, dealers), extended with the no-trade puzzle and the overconfidence literature (Odean; Barber-Odean), which Harris mentions only in passing. Bouchaud's "hide-and-seek" liquidity game is Harris's "stealth trading" and order-splitting material given a quantitative scale: 10^-4 of market cap visible at the touch.

## Strategy and research candidates

1. **Volatility signature plot on NQ as a horizon diagnostic (cheap, high value):** compute sigma(tau) for NQ from intraday through weekly lags. It tells us (a) the lag at which microstructure noise has flushed out (the shortest clean sampling scale), and (b) whether NQ's weak mean reversion matches the E-mini S&P's 20% figure. Weekly-chart analysis should sit comfortably on the flat part of the curve; if it does, that empirically justifies Balan's weekly preference.
2. **Required sample length discipline (the (15/5)^2 = 9 years rule):** before believing any backtest of the final strategy, compute the ratio (volatility / expected edge) squared in weekly units. For a weekly system, annualized volatility near 20% and a hoped-for edge of a few percent per year implies years of live or backtest data before the edge is statistically distinguishable. This goes into the strategy-build phase as a mandatory pre-registration step: define the edge, compute the required sample, refuse to overfit on shorter data.
3. **Latent digestion as trend justification:** the "markets slowly digest supply and demand" mechanism gives the strategy a physical reason to expect persistence at the weekly scale (wave phases as digestion phases). Testable proxy: order-flow imbalance measures (even cumulative volume delta on daily data) should show persistence at exactly the horizons where trends are visible.
4. **If we ever provide liquidity (limit entries/exits):** the s/2 > R_inf condition is the sanity check. Limit orders earn the spread but eat adverse impact; on NQ with its tight spreads, the expected capture is small, so the default for our scale is liquidity taking, timed when spreads are normal.
5. **Pending until full book available:** the square-root law of market impact, the propagator model, and optimal execution (Ch 11-21) are this book's most directly actionable material. Marked as a gap; revisit when the full text is obtained.

## Reading-completeness note

The sample's 47 pages were read in full (front matter, Chapter 1 complete, Chapter 2 sections 2.1-2.1.4). No skipped pages within the excerpt. The full book's remaining ~414 pages are not present in the upload.

# Bailey, Borwein, López de Prado & Zhu, "The Probability of Backtest Overfitting" — validation-spine paper notes

Source: Journal of Computational Finance 20(4) 2017 (author version dated February 2015, "The Probability of Backtest Overfitting"). FULL TEXT of the core fetched and studied from the author-hosted PDF (davidhbailey.com) 12 Nov 2026: abstract, introduction, the entire framework (Section 2: overfitting definition, PBO definition, the CSCV algorithm) and the overfit statistics (Section 3: PBO estimation, performance degradation, probability of loss, stochastic dominance), up to the point the fetched text truncated. The later sections (limitations discussion, the practical application's numerical results) were not in the fetched window and are flagged below where relied upon. Companion paper to the DSR notes file. Wave 1 of the paper queue (validation spine).

## The problem

Hold-out validation fails for backtests, and the paper enumerates exactly why (all fetched): (1) publicly available data means the "holdout" has probably been used in-sample; (2) any seasoned researcher knows how the holdout period performed and that knowledge leaks into design "consciously or not" (Schorfheide-Wolpin); (3) hold-out is inadequate for small samples — citing Weiss-Kulikowski that hold-out should not be applied below roughly 1,000 observations, which for a WEEKLY strategy means below roughly 20 years of data; (4) the OOS slice consumes the most representative (recent) or least representative (early) data; (5) crucially, "as long as the researcher tries more than one strategy configuration, overfitting is always present" and hold-out IGNORES THE NUMBER OF TRIALS. The deliverable: a probability that the backtest is overfit, requiring only the per-period performance series of the N tried configurations — model-free, non-parametric, no fixed holdout consumed.

## The definition (fetched, formal)

N strategy configurations backtested over the same T periods. Compare rankings IS vs OOS. Overfitting (their Definition 2.1): the strategy optimal IS has EXPECTED RANKING BELOW THE MEDIAN OOS. The probability of backtest overfitting (Definition 2.2):

PBO = Prob[ rank_OOS(best-IS) < median OOS, conditional on being the IS optimum ]

i.e. the probability that the configuration you would have selected, on this IS evidence, underperforms the median of all tried configurations out of sample. The null hypothesis is that overfitting HAS occurred, and PBO is computed in a Bayesian posterior sense — overfitting is a deterministic fact, but given the evidence we can state the posterior probability it happened. PBO near 0: the selection process transfers OOS. PBO near 1: the selection process is actively harmful — you would have done better picking at random.

## The CSCV algorithm (fetched, complete)

1. Matrix M (T x N): column n is the per-period profit/loss series of configuration n (synchronous rows; the evaluation metric must be estimable on subsamples; different trading frequencies must be aggregated to a common index first).
2. Cut M's rows into an EVEN number S of disjoint submatrices of equal length (S = 16 gives 12,780 combinations).
3. Form all C(S, S/2) combinations of S/2 submatrices. For each combination c: the training set J is the S/2 chosen blocks in original order (T/2 x N), the testing set is the COMPLEMENT blocks (also T/2 x N) — every block combination serves as both training and testing somewhere, which is the "combinatorially symmetric" part.
4. Per combination: rank the N configurations on the training set (r_c), pick the IS best n*; compute its OOS RELATIVE rank omega = r_bar_{n*}/(N+1) in (0,1); form the LOGIT lambda_c = ln(omega/(1-omega)). High logit = IS choice keeps performing OOS; logit 0 = OOS median; negative logit = IS choice below OOS median.
5. The distribution of the lambda_c over all combinations is the output. PBO phi = the integral of f(lambda) below 0 — the fraction of combinations where the IS-optimal configuration ranked below the OOS median.

Three further readouts from the same object (fetched): performance degradation (regress OOS performance of the IS-best on its IS performance across combinations; overfit selections show NEGATIVE slope — the more they won IS, the more they lose OOS); probability of loss (Prob[OOS performance of IS-best < 0], which the paper is careful to separate: "even if phi is about 0, Prob[loss] could be high", meaning the strategy is poor for reasons OTHER than overfitting); stochastic dominance (whether selecting by IS beats picking a random configuration — the placebo test, and the paper explicitly likens the OOS median to the placebo arm in medical trials).

## Interpretation and thresholds (fetched)

Customary use: reject models with PBO > 0.05 (their Neyman-Pearson analogy). Also proposed: PBO as a Bayesian prior for downstream inference, and PBO-weighted portfolios of strategies (weight by 1 - PBO). The paper's own honesty about scope: CSCV is "only one illustrative technique" — the general framework accommodates other PBO estimators. Their complementary validations (Monte Carlo and extreme-value-theory cross-checks) are hosted off-paper on SSRN and were not fetched.

## Later sections not in the fetched window (flagged, general knowledge)

- The practical application and simulations (in the published version, a long/short equity case and synthetic-memory examples) are reported to show PBO rising with trial count and with performance-degradation slope as forecast; verify before citing numbers.
- Known limitations the authors list: results depend on the representativeness of the sample; IID-slicing assumptions behind the symmetric combinations; PBO is a property of the SELECTION PROCESS (the whole N-trial family), not of the single strategy finally deployed.

## Programme connections

- The programme's structural constraint is the mirror of this paper: a weekly strategy with T = 1364 bars is FAR below the Weiss-Kulikowski 1,000-OBSERVATION minimum they cite for a single hold-out (our holdout was ~300 weeks; and the whole sample is only ~27 years). CSCV's appeal is that it reuses every observation in both roles instead of spending a fixed slice — but its price is that it RESAMPLES the same history, and our Lesson 13 (within-sample resampling is invalid on near-integrated series with effective n near 2) applies to any future CSCV on SMOOTH weekly-derived performance series. If a platform-era CSCV is ever run, it must be on the raw per-period returns (spiky, effectively-independent) of the N configurations, never on smoothed indicator-derived equity curves, and S must be small enough that each submatrix spans many effective information units.
- The "trials are invisible" problem is why the programme logs every test ever run (battery, H8/H9, H10a, H11a, the amendment ledger): an honest N is the entry ticket to DSR; CSCV's N is the width of the M matrix — both require the registry the programme already keeps.
- The hold-out critique maps onto our own history with uncomfortable precision: holdout 2021+ is spent, spent not merely because consulted once, but because the whole 2019-2021 research arc leading TO the battery constituted trials against knowledge of that era's market. The battery's verdict stands because it was pre-registered, single-shot and never repeated — a discipline this paper's point (5) argues is the ONLY thing that keeps a holdout meaningful.
- Probability-of-loss distinction is programme doctrine already: Entry 0 and the U3 verdict separate "is the selection process overfit?" (battery answered: yes, T/U3 failed) from "is the deployed rule set unprofitable for other reasons?" — that is what the paper-trading stage measures live, without any further historical test.
- Platform spec: PBO/CSCV is the natural leaderboard hygiene tool for the ORDERFLOW platform's hypothesis families (N configurations per hypothesis, per-period performance series already stored); the placebo-median comparison should be a standing dashboard readout, and 1 - PBO weighting is the candidate promotion rule the spec already gestures at with its "null baselines" registry.
- Cross-links among the spine: DSR (the parametric twin: trial-count deflation of the winner's SR vs resampling-based selection-process probability; agree when both applied), White 2000 / Hansen 2005 (family-null tests on the MAX statistic; PBO instead interrogates the whole IS-to-OOS ranking transfer), BH 1995 (shortlist stage; PBO evaluates the stage after the shortlist), Lo 2002 (the per-period performance statistic feeding CSCV must itself be autocorrelation-honest or the slicing inherits the distortion).
- Links back: Bartholomew-Biggs (cross-validation for model selection as the optimization-side ancestor), Tsay (the dependence conditions that decide whether CSCV slicing is valid), and the Elliott Wave base (wave counting is a selection process with N effectively unbounded — PBO near 1 is the prior any wave-count "backtest" must overcome).

# Bailey & López de Prado (2014), "The Deflated Sharpe Ratio: Correcting for Selection Bias, Backtest Overfitting and Non-Normality" — validation-spine paper notes

Source: Journal of Portfolio Management 40(5), 94-107. FULL TEXT fetched and studied from the author-hosted PDF (davidhbailey.com), 12 Nov 2026. Wave 1 of the paper queue (validation spine).

## The problem

After trying N strategy configurations, the researcher reports the best. Three biases compound in that reported Sharpe ratio:

1. Selection bias / multiple testing: the max of N noisy SR estimates is biased upward, and the bias grows with N (this is White's data-snooping problem transplanted onto the Sharpe statistic).
2. Backtest overfitting: the winner was chosen precisely because it fit the sample's noise.
3. Non-normality: with negative skew and fat tails, the SR estimator's distribution shifts and widens, so the same SR carries less evidence than a Gaussian calculation implies.

The paper's deliverable: a single corrected probability that the TRUE SR of the selected strategy is positive, after deflating for all three.

## The machinery (all in the fetched text)

- Foundation (their Eq 4 region, following Lo 2002, Mertens 2002, and PSR/Christie): the Probabilistic Sharpe Ratio. Under IID the estimated SR_hat's distribution is asymptotically normal with a variance that depends on the TRUE SR, skewness gamma_3 and kurtosis gamma_4:

  SR_hat ~ N(SR, (1 - gamma_3 * SR + (gamma_4 - 1)/4 * SR^2) / (T - 1))

  PSR(SR*) = P(SR > SR*) = Phi( (SR_hat - SR*) * sqrt(T - 1) / sqrt(1 - gamma_3*SR_hat + ((gamma_4 - 1)/4)*SR_hat^2) )

  This is the non-normality correction: negative skew (gamma_3 < 0) inflates the standard error, requiring a HIGHER observed SR to reach the same confidence. PSR(SR*=0) answers "is this strategy's SR statistically above zero, accounting for skew and kurtosis?"

- Expected maximum of N trials (their Eq 1, the selection-bias core): if N independent SR estimates are drawn from a normal with mean E[SR] and standard deviation V^0.5, the expected MAXIMUM of the N is:

  E[max_N] = E[SR] * sqrt(V) * ( (1 - gamma) * Z[1 - 1/N] + gamma * Z[1 - 1/(N*e)] )

  with gamma the Euler-Mascheroni constant (approximately 0.5772) and Z the inverse standard normal CDF. Intuition: with N = 1 the term is 0 (no selection yet); as N grows, the expected best-of-N SR climbs like the expected max of N Gaussians. The higher the cross-trial VARIANCE V of SR estimates (wildly different configurations), the more the best one is inflated.

- Deflated Sharpe Ratio (their Eq 3/5 region): DSR = PSR evaluated at the rejection threshold SR0 = E[max_N]:

  DSR = PSR(SR0 = E[max_N])

  i.e. "is this strategy's SR significantly above zero GIVEN that it is the best of N trials with cross-trial variance V?" DSR > 0.95 is the customary pass.

## The worked example (fetched, verbatim numbers)

N = 88 trials, T = 1250 observations (5 years daily), annual SR_hat = 2.5, skew -3, kurtosis 10. The expected max-of-N threshold comes out around SR 1.9-ish and the DSR = 0.9004 < 0.95: REJECTED. The same track record with only N = 46 trials gives DSR = 0.9505 > 0.95: passes. Two lessons in one example: (a) an apparently spectacular SR can fail once the trial count is disclosed, and (b) Normality matters — with Gaussian returns the same setup tolerates MORE trials (kurtosis 3 instead of 10 shrinks the SR standard error, so the DSR is more forgiving); the fat-tailed case is punished.

## The stopping rule and PBO (fetched)

- The secretary problem / the 1/e-law: they import the classic optimal-stopping result to argue when to STOP testing — roughly, if the objective is to select a near-best strategy, keep testing until about 1/e (about 37%) of the budget of trials has been spent, then commit to the next candidate that beats all seen so far. A cute transposition, and the paper flags it as a heuristic for research budgeting, not a licence to search indefinitely.
- Holdout repetition: the hold-out method FAILS when the researcher iterates — each failed holdout leads to a new configuration tried against the same data, so the holdout becomes just another trial in N. This is the exact mechanism by which the programme's holdout (2021+) is SPENT, and why the discipline is "never touch it again" rather than "keep a clean set aside": a holdout that is consulted repeatedly is not a holdout.
- PBO (combinatorially symmetric cross-validation, from the companion paper Bailey/Borwein/López de Prado/Zhu): introduced as the complementary, non-parametric assessment of overfitting probability — see the separate PBO notes file. DSR is parametric and trial-count-driven; PBO is model-free and resampling-driven; they diagnose the same disease through different instruments and should agree when both are applied.
- Harvey-Liu (2014) noted as the complementary BH-based approach to the same multiple-testing problem (their multiple-testing-corrected t-thresholds for factor discovery).

## Caveats the paper itself makes (fetched)

- N and V must be HONESTLY reported: unreported trials are invisible to the correction, and the paper's whole bite depends on the researcher disclosing every configuration tried. A DSR computed on a doctored N is worthless.
- The Gaussian-approximation of the max is asymptotic in N; small N makes the threshold noisy.
- Cross-trial independence of SR estimates is assumed for Eq 1; correlated trials (our 27 AVFI combos are highly correlated variants) make the EFFECTIVE N smaller, so the Eq-1 threshold is conservative (over-deflates) for correlated families — the paper acknowledges this direction.

## Programme connections

- The programme's own history is a DSR-shaped story: the 27-combo stage 6a grid was a family search; its best family member (naive best 0.58 weekly-scale statistic, RC p = 0.11) failed the family-wide test, and the holdout battery failed for T and U3. DSR formalizes, on the Sharpe statistic itself, the same verdict the RC delivered on the family null. A retrospective DSR on the stage 6a winner would need the honest trial count (all 27, plus the U1/U2 lineage) and would almost certainly reject — consistent with everything already decided.
- The holdout-repetition mechanism is why the programme's "holdout 2021+ is SPENT, never touched" rule is not paranoia but the exact precondition for the one holdout verdict (T/U3 battery) to remain valid. This paper is the citation for that rule.
- Platform spec: the ORDERFLOW platform should compute DSR on any leaderboard winner automatically — mandatory trial-count disclosure per experimenter, cross-trial SR variance from the registry, skew/kurtosis from the realized track, and a DSR gate before any strategy is marked "interesting". Leaderboards that rank by naive SR (annualized, uncorrected — see Lo 2002) are the false-positive machine this paper documents.
- Amendment 4/5 power readouts connect through N: our H10a/H11a were single pre-registered tests, so no selection-bias deflation applies to THEM (N = 1 at the moment of specification); the danger was always going behind the amendment to re-pick variants, which would convert them into family searches and drag in DSR/SPA territory. The signed-dated-amendment rule is the N = 1 guarantee.
- Cross-links among the spine: White 2000 (the family-null precursor; DSR corrects the winner's own SR rather than testing the max), Hansen 2005 (the power-repaired family verdict — the tool of choice BEFORE crowning a family winner), BH 1995 (the shortlist-stage multiplicity tool; DSR is the final-pick tool), Lo 2002 (the sampling distribution underneath PSR/DSR; the autocorrelation extension is the known gap to watch for weekly strategies), PBO (the non-parametric twin, separate notes file).
- Links back: Bartholomew-Biggs (the max of multi-start optima is upward-biased — the same selection mathematics in an optimization context), Tsay (the mixing conditions beneath the asymptotics), and the Elliott Wave base (a wave count is an unbounded specification search with N never disclosed — structurally the worst DSR case, which is why H3's nulls are the only admissible test of it).

# Benjamini & Hochberg (1995), "Controlling the False Discovery Rate" — validation-spine paper notes

Source: Journal of the Royal Statistical Society Series B 57(1), 289-300. ACCESS FLAG: the full text could not be fetched this session (publisher PDFs paywalled or non-extractable scans); these notes are written from well-established general knowledge of this canonical paper and are flagged accordingly throughout. Verify against the full text if ever cited formally. Saved 12 Nov 2026. Wave 1 of the paper queue (validation spine).

## The idea (general knowledge, flagged)

Classic multiple-testing procedures (Bonferroni, Scheffe, Tukey) control the family-wise error rate (FWER): the probability of making even ONE false rejection. That is the right criterion when any single false claim is catastrophic (White's RC and Hansen's SPA control a family-wide null in the same spirit). But when the goal is exploratory discovery across many hypotheses — where some false discoveries are tolerable if most findings are real — FWER control is brutally conservative: with m tests at level alpha, Bonferroni tests each at alpha/m, so power collapses as m grows.

BH's reframe: control the false discovery rate, FDR = E[V/R], where V is the number of true nulls rejected and R is the total number of rejections (with the convention E[V/R] = 0 when R = 0). The quantity regulated is the expected PROPORTION of false discoveries among the discoveries made, not the probability of any false discovery. Key properties:

- FDR <= FWER always, so FDR control is never more conservative than FWER control; when all nulls are true, FDR = FWER and the procedure controls at level alpha exactly.
- Control holds under independence of the test statistics, and (Benjamini-Yekutieli 2001, later) under certain positive-dependence structures such as the kind common among test statistics computed on the same financial time series. The BY correction for arbitrary dependence multiplies each threshold by 1/(1 + 1/2 + ... + 1/m), roughly a factor of 1/(ln m) — much gentler than Bonferroni's 1/m.

## The procedure (general knowledge, flagged)

Given m p-values, ordered p_(1) <= ... <= p_(m), and a target FDR q:

1. Find the largest k such that p_(k) <= (k/m) q.
2. Reject the nulls for p_(1), ..., p_(k).

That is the whole method. Equivalent intuition: plot the ordered p-values against (k/m)q and reject everything up to the last crossing. The step-up structure means a small p-value is more likely to be rejected if it sits in a family where many other p-values are also small — the procedure adapts to the unknown fraction of true nulls, which is why it is uniformly more powerful than Bonferroni/Holm in practice.

## Why it matters for strategy research (flagged intuition, established in the literature)

Trading-strategy research rarely produces one pre-registered winner tested once; it produces a leader BOARD: a set of candidate signals each with its own p-value, and the question is which ones to carry forward. FWER-style tools (RC/SPA/max-t) answer "is the best of these real?"; BH answers "which of these should I believe, accepting that a controlled fraction will be false?". These are different questions and both are legitimate when asked before looking:

- White/SPA: one family-wide verdict on the maximum. Protects against the newsletter scam.
- BH: a ranked shortlist with a bound on the expected contamination. Useful for triage across INDEPENDENT hypothesis families (e.g., platform signal candidates) rather than variants of one indicator.

The failure mode to avoid: applying BH AFTER peeking at which result looks best, or re-running with different q until something passes. FDR control is a property of the pre-specified list of m hypotheses and the single application of the rule; it is destroyed by post-hoc family editing exactly as FWER control is.

## Programme connections

- The programme's stage 6a verdict (RC p = 0.11) was an FWER-style single-verdict test over the 27-combo grid — the correct choice because the grid was variants of ONE indicator and the question was "does the best exist at all". BH would have been the wrong tool there: it would have licensed a shortlist of "discoveries" from a family whose maximum was already null.
- Prospective use: the ORDERFLOW platform spec's hypothesis leaderboard is exactly a BH-shaped object — many independent microstructure hypotheses, each with its own test, ranked and carried forward. If and when the platform runs many registered tests, the BH threshold (with the BY correction, since financial test statistics are rarely independent) is the honest shortlisting rule; Harvey-Liu's backtesting-procedure work, cited by Bailey/López de Prado's DSR paper, is precisely this BH-based threshold applied to factor/strategy discovery.
- Cross-links among the spine: White 2000 and Hansen 2005 (the FWER family-verdict alternative; SPA's consistent p-value is the power-repaired version), Bailey/López de Prado DSR (selection bias on the single winner; BH governs the shortlist stage that produces candidates, DSR governs the final pick), Lo 2002 (the p-value being multiplicity-corrected is usually computed ON a Sharpe ratio, so its own sampling distribution must be right first), and the H3 N1-N4 null battery (a small pre-registered family tested with plain max-t logic because its size was tiny; BH's value grows with m).
- Discipline note: the programme's rule "the battery is never re-run, the holdout is spent, no test without a signed dated amendment" is the operational defence of whatever multiplicity framework is in force; BH does not rescue a family that was assembled after seeing the results.

## Addendum (12 Nov 2026, access upgrade: full text fetched, general-knowledge flags retired)

The full text was fetched from a public JSTOR-download mirror (wexler.free.fr, 40,279 chars clean, 12 pages including Appendix A; each page carries the JSTOR download stamp, provenance recorded). The core sections were previously flagged general knowledge; they are now verified against the primary text:

- FDR definition verbatim: Q = V/(V+S) with Q = 0 when V + S = 0 ("as no error of false rejection can be committed"), Q_e = E(Q) = E(V/R). The U/V/S/T table (their Table 1) matches the note.
- Both properties verified: (a) all-nulls-true gives FDR = FWER exactly ("control of the FDR implies control of the FWER in the weak sense"); (b) FDR <= FWER otherwise, "and the two can be quite different", with the gain growing in the number of non-true nulls.
- The procedure verified (their expression (1)): let k be the largest i for which P(i) <= (i/m)q*, reject H(1)..H(k). Theorem 1: control at q* for independent test statistics, with the refinement E(Q) <= (m0/m)q* <= q* — control is TIGHTER when fewer hypotheses are true nulls. Remark recorded: independence is needed only for the p-values of the TRUE nulls; "the independence of the test statistics corresponding to the false null hypotheses is not needed for the proof".
- Terminology correction recorded: the paper calls both its procedure and Hochberg's "step-down procedures, which start by comparing P(m) with a" and walk toward smaller p-values. The note's "step-up" label follows the modern convention (smallest p first); the paper's own wording is "step-down". Same algorithm, dated terminology.
- New primary content 1, Theorem 2: the procedure is the solution of the constrained maximization "choose a that maximizes the number of rejections r(a), subject to a*m/r(a) <= q*" — the post-hoc maximizing view ("a simultaneous maximization of R and FDR control being attempted after experimentation"). This is the cleanest one-line justification of why BH adapts to the unknown fraction of true nulls.
- New primary content 2, the worked example: the Neuhaus et al. (1992) TAPS thrombolysis trial, 15-hypothesis family of post-treatment events. Bonferroni (0.05/15 = 0.0033) and Hochberg both reject only 3 hypotheses (allergic reaction, two bleeding aspects), EXCLUDING the mortality comparison at p = 0.0095 — the trial's headline claim is "unjustified from the classical point of view". BH at q* = 0.05 rejects 4 hypotheses, INCLUDING mortality (P(4) = 0.0095 <= (4/15)*0.05 = 0.0133). The canonical demonstration that FDR control licenses discoveries FWER control must suppress, and that the choice of criterion changes substantive conclusions.
- New primary content 3, the simulation: m in {4, 8, 16, 32, 64}, true-null fraction in {3/4, 1/2, 1/4, 0}, D/E/I configurations, L in {5, 10}, 20,000 repetitions. Verified: power of BH uniformly larger than Bonferroni and Hochberg; the advantage increases in m and in the number of non-nulls; at m = 32 all-non-null equally spread, Bonferroni power 0.42 vs BH 0.65; and the explicit statement that the FDR-over-FWER gain EXCEEDS Hochberg's gain over Bonferroni. Also the E(V/m0) analysis: per-true-null error far below q*/m0 when all nulls are true (0.0132 at m = 4 down to 0.0009 at m = 64).
- Lineage verified: the procedure is Simes's (1986) exploratory extension (reject the intersection null if some P(i) <= (i/m)*a); Hommel (1988) showed the individual-inference extension does NOT control FWER strongly; Hochberg (1988) is the FWER-preserving sibling; BH's constants linearly exceed Hochberg's hyperbolic ones (extreme ratio ~4m/(m+1)^2 at i = (m+1)/2), hence "rejects samplewise at least as many hypotheses".
- Programme impact: the platform shortlisting rule (BH with the BY correction for shared-data dependence) now has primary grounding. The Theorem-1 remark sharpens the dependence caveat for the platform case: what matters is the joint dependence of the TRUE-null test statistics, which on shared market data are exactly the dependent ones, so the BY correction remains the mandated default.

# Hansen (2005), "A Test for Superior Predictive Ability" — validation-spine paper notes

Source: Journal of Business & Economic Statistics 23(4), 365-380. Studied 12 Nov 2026 from two flagged layers: (1) the full theory section of the authors' "Testing for Superior Predictive Ability using Ox" manual (Hansen/Kim/Lunde 2003, fetched and read in full — it states the test construction and the three p-values verbatim); (2) the published paper's well-established results from general knowledge, flagged inline (the JBES text itself was paywalled/blocked everywhere it was probed; verify against the full text if ever cited formally). Wave 1 of the paper queue.

## The problem SPA fixes

White's Reality Check tests the same null: the benchmark is not beaten by the best of l searched models. But the RC has a known practical weakness, exactly the one the programme hit in stage 6a: the family max over ALL l models widens the null distribution, so junk variants — models that are clearly, persistently worse than the benchmark and can only be "rejected" with trivial probability — still inflate the max-statistic's spread and destroy power. In small samples this makes the RC nearly unable to reject even when a genuinely superior model exists.

## The construction (manual, verbatim-verified)

- Relative performance of model k vs benchmark at time t: X_k(t) = L(Y_t, Ŷ_0,t) − L(Y_t, Ŷ_k,t) (loss difference; positive = model k better). μ_k = E[X_k(t)]. Null: H_0: μ_k ≤ 0 for all k (benchmark is the best).
- Statistic (the "standardized maximum"): T^sm_n = max_k n^{-1/2} X̄_k / ω̂_k, where ω̂²_k is the (bootstrap-estimated) asymptotic variance of n^{-1/2} X̄_k. Under regularity T^sm_n → max_k μ_k/ω_k, which is > 0 iff some μ_k > 0.
- Testing multiple inequalities is harder than testing equalities: the null distribution is NOT unique — it depends on where the μ_k sit relative to 0 (the "boundary" of H_0). Hence three p-values rather than one.

## The three SPA p-values (manual, verbatim-verified)

- Upper bound (conservative): assumes ALL competing models are exactly as good as the benchmark (μ_1 = ... = μ_l = 0), i.e. every model is on the boundary. This is the Reality Check p-value. Sensitive to junk: poor and irrelevant models drag the max and kill power.
- Lower bound (liberal): assumes models worse than the benchmark are poor IN THE LIMIT (μ_k/ω_k < -c), so they can never drive the max; they are effectively dropped from the null.
- Consistent p-value: uses a threshold to estimate, model by model, which ones are asymptotically poor (the manual: the test "asymptotically determines which models are worse than the benchmark and asymptotically prevents them from affecting the distribution of the test statistic"). Consistent = asymptotically unbiased and MORE POWERFUL than the RC; the technical reason (manual, citing the paper) is that it is asymptotically similar on the boundary of H_0 — size does not depend on where the barely-negative μ_k actually sit.
- Power diagnostics (manual, a genuinely good practice the programme already follows): when you fail to reject, report the CRITICAL VALUE in standardized units — how good (X̄_k/ω̂_k) a model would have to be to reject. Large critical values = the data are simply uninformative about the question, regardless of p. This is the same service stage 6c (pre-battery power analysis) performed for the T/U3 battery (43% power at the observed Sharpe; 80% needs ~1.0-1.1), and the same one the Amendment 4/5 power readouts just performed.

## Published-paper results from general knowledge (flagged; not in the fetched manual text)

- Simulations: the SPA consistent test has noticeably better power than the RC in exactly the relevant configuration (many irrelevant/poor models in the family); size close to nominal.
- Application: forecasting stock returns / comparing a large family of predictors, showing RC and SPA can disagree in practice (RC's p-value inflated by junk variants). Also the well-known companion point that the RC and SPA bounds coincide when all models are near the boundary.

## Programme connections

- Stage 6a used the White RC (family best 0.58, RC p = 0.11). Given the 27-combo family contained many near-vacuous AVFI variants, an SPA re-run could have been more powerful — but the holdout era ended that analysis; under programme discipline (holdout spent; the stage 6a result stands as executed) there is NO re-running with a different test to see whether it flips a verdict. The lesson is prospective: any FUTURE family search (platform hypothesis families) should use the SPA consistent p-value (with all three p-values reported) rather than the plain RC, especially with large registered families where junk inflates the max.
- The three-p-value reporting convention (lower ≤ consistent ≤ upper) is a cheap honesty device: if the bounds bracket the decision threshold, the data are uninformative — say so.
- Standardizing each model's performance by ω̂_k before taking the max matters: it stops high-variance junk from dominating the family max. Equivalent discipline to reporting per-model Sharpe rather than raw return differences.
- Links: White 2000 (the null and the stationary bootstrap machinery are the same; SPA is the power-repaired RC), Benjamini-Hochberg (the other answer to the same multiplicity problem: average-error control for ranking rather than a single family-wide verdict), Bailey/López de Prado DSR/PBO (selection-bias corrections on the winning model itself rather than the family null), Lo 2002 (why Sharpe needs care before any of this), the battery max-t (our family was T+U3, small and pre-registered, which is why the plain max-t was appropriate there — White's own power-dilution warning is the reason the programme keeps families small).
- Links back: the ORDERFLOW platform spec (its hypothesis leaderboards should report SPA consistent p-values with bounds and standardized critical values, not naive per-strategy p-values), and Bartholomew-Biggs (global multi-start optima facing the same "the max of many noisy things is biased upward" issue in optimization, not just testing).

# Lo (2002), "The Statistics of Sharpe Ratios" — validation-spine paper notes

Source: Financial Analysts Journal 58(4), 28-36. ACCESS FLAG: the abstract was fetched and verified verbatim from the CFA Institute article page; the full text was paywalled. Everything below is anchored either in the verified abstract (marked) or in well-established general knowledge of this short paper (flagged). Verify against the full text if ever cited formally. Saved 12 Nov 2026. Wave 1 of the paper queue (validation spine).

## The problem

The Sharpe ratio is the standard single-number performance statistic, and the entire validation spine (White RC on Sharpe criteria, DSR, PBO, the programme's stage 6 tests) ultimately computes p-values ON a Sharpe ratio. But SR is usually treated as if its estimator were a known constant. Lo derives its actual sampling distribution under explicit assumptions, and the results are sobering: SR estimates are biased, autocorrelation distorts annualization badly, and naive annualized-SR comparisons between strategies can be meaningless.

## Core results (abstract-verified)

- Under iid returns, the SR estimator has a known asymptotic distribution (general knowledge: sqrt(T)(SR_hat - SR) -> N(0, 1 - (SR^2/4)(γ3^2 ... ) ...) — the variance depends on the TRUE SR, skewness and kurtosis; Lo gives the exact iid expression, so standard errors and confidence intervals for SR are available, not just point estimates.
- Under stationary (non-iid) returns, the asymptotic variance picks up autocorrelation terms: returns with positive serial correlation (e.g., trending/smooth strategies) have SR estimators whose variance is inflated, and the annualization factor is NOT sqrt of the horizon count. Abstract-verified headline: monthly SRs are NOT annualizable by multiplying by sqrt(12) except in special cases (independent returns).
- Abstract-verified application: for hedge-fund-style return streams with serial correlation, annual SR can be OVERSTATED by as much as 65% — the smoothing/illiquidity of the return series (stale or managed prices) induces positive autocorrelation, which biases the naive annualized SR upward.
- Abstract-verified: after correcting the annualization, performance rankings of funds can change — the ordering induced by naive SR is not trustworthy.

## The annualization mechanics (established general knowledge of the paper, flagged)

For a return series with lag-k autocorrelations ρ_k of the PERIOD returns, the annualization of a per-period SR uses the factor sqrt of a quadratic form: SR_annual relates to SR_period through the long-run variance, i.e. the sum of autocovariances, not just the number of periods. Positive autocorrelation at the aggregation lag means the sum exceeds the naive count — trends persist within the measurement window — so naive sqrt(N) scaling understates the true time-aggregated risk relative to the mean, inflating SR. Lo also covers the time-aggregation of returns: if returns are iid at the observation frequency, multi-period SRs scale predictably, but the iid assumption is exactly what fails for momentum, illiquid, or smoothed strategies. Conversely negatively-autocorrelated (mean-reverting) returns can make naive annualization UNDERSTATE SR.

## Programme connections

- The programme's stage 6 tests all compute statistics on WEEKLY log returns of a weekly-decision strategy. Positive week-to-week autocorrelation in NQ log returns (momentum regime) is precisely Lo's warning case: the effective sample size is smaller than the bar count, SR-style statistics have wider sampling distributions than naive formulas imply, and any ANNUALIZED figure produced from weekly bars (platform reporting included) must use the autocorrelation-corrected aggregation, never sqrt(52).
- Lesson 13 of lessons_learned (near-integrated smooth series have effective sample sizes far below nominal n) is the same physics: the sp_z series at lag-1 autocorrelation 0.985 had effective n around 2, which killed the within-sample bootstrap. Lo's autocorrelation-adjusted SR variance is the estimator-side counterpart of that lesson.
- Power analysis linkage: stage 6c and the Amendment 4/5 power readouts already work in sigma units of the weekly statistic; Lo is the reason the programme should distrust any externally quoted "annual Sharpe 2.5" (the DSR paper's worked example: hedge fund SRs of that size are usually serial-correlation artifacts) and the reason platform leaderboards must report per-period SR with serial-correlation-corrected standard errors, not annualized headlines.
- Cross-links among the spine: Bailey/López de Prado DSR builds on PSR which builds directly on Lo's sampling distribution (the DSR formula's skew/kurtosis/serial-correlation terms come from this line: Lo 2002 -> Mertens 2002 -> Christie 2005 PSR -> DSR); White 2000's Corollary covers Sharpe-type criteria, whose validity as test statistics rests on this paper's distribution theory; BH/SPA govern multiplicity, but the unit being ranked must itself be statistically honest first.
- Links back: Bouchaud's trades-quotes-prices (empirical return autocorrelation and its origins in liquidity and order-splitting, the mechanism behind Lo's distortion), the ORDERFLOW platform spec (annualized headline SR should be banned from its dashboards; report corrected per-period SR with Lo standard errors), and the Elliott Wave base (wave-count-based strategies on smoothed price series are maximum-risk Lo cases: the smoothing induces the autocorrelation that inflates naive SR).

## Addendum (12 Nov 2026, access upgrade: full text fetched, mechanics flags retired; one attribution correction)

The full text was fetched from a public mirror of the FAJ article hosted by a Berkeley student-organisation page (56,173 chars clean, 17 pages: all body sections, Appendix A, notes, references; equations are typographically linearised by the extraction but tables and numbers are exact; provenance recorded). The abstract-verified claims now have full primary grounding, and one flagged mechanic needs an honest correction:

- CORRECTION: the note's iid section attributed skew/kurtosis terms to Lo's iid expression. The primary text's iid asymptotic variance (their Eq 8) is simply V_IID = 1 + (1/2)SR^2, derived from CLT moments up to sigma^4 only; NO higher-moment terms appear in the iid case. The third/fourth moments (nu3, nu4) enter only the ROBUST GMM estimator's asymptotic variance (their Eq 25 and A27-A33), through the estimation errors of the autocovariances. The higher-moment refinement of the iid case is Mertens 2002 (already in the note's spine chain), not Lo. The note's inference conclusions are unaffected; the attribution is corrected. All later uses of "Lo's skew/kurtosis terms" should read "the Lo-Mertens line".
- Verified verbatim, iid case: SE(SR) = sqrt((1 + (1/2)SR^2)/T) (Eq 9), with the 95% interval SR_hat +/- 1.96*SE (Eq 10) and the table of standard errors (T = 60: SE 0.188 at SR 1.5, 0.303 at SR 3.0; high-SR strategies are LESS precisely estimated in absolute terms, but SE/SR converges to a finite limit ~sqrt(1/(2T))). The variance decomposition (Eqs 12a/12b): at SR 0.25, 97% of the estimator variance comes from the mean; at SR 2.00 only 33.3%; at SR 3.00 only 18.2% (the rest from the variance estimate).
- Verified verbatim, time aggregation: the q-period return R_t(q) = sum of q one-period returns (Eq 16, compounding ignored); SR(q) = sqrt(q)*SR holds ONLY under iid (Eq 17, with the paper's own warning that the ratio then "will increase as the square root of q, making a longer-horizon investment seem more attractive... highly misleading", and that SR "should never be used as the sole criterion"). Under stationarity the correct factor (Eqs 19-20) is SR(q) = eta(q)*SR with eta(q) = q / sqrt(q + 2*sum_{k=1..q-1}(q-k)*rho_k), reducing to sqrt(q) only when all autocorrelations vanish.
- Verified, AR(1) example: the closed form eta(q) (Eq 22) and Table 2. At q = 12: rho_1 = 0 gives 3.46 (the naive sqrt(12)); rho_1 = +20% gives 2.88 (naive annualization OVERSTATES the true annual SR by roughly 20%); rho_1 = -20% gives 4.17 (naive UNDERSTATES). Positive serial correlation lowers the honest scale factor, negative raises it, exactly as the note's direction claims state.
- Verified, empirical example (Table 4): 10 largest mutual funds (as of Feb 2001, CRSP monthly returns) + 12 Altvest hedge funds, risk-free 5/12% per month, Newey-West truncation m = 3 and 6. Mutual funds: little serial correlation (Q p-values 13.2%-80.2%; max rho_1 12.4%, Fidelity Magellan, whose SR(12) falls 0.73 -> 0.66 robust), so iid and robust annual estimates barely differ. Hedge funds: rho_1 range 20.2%-49.0%; Convertible/option arbitrage falls 4.35 -> 2.99 (45% overstatement); Mortgage-backed securities falls 4.03 -> 2.44 (the headline 65% overstatement); Risk arbitrage A RISES 3.25 -> 3.83 (negative serial correlation); the naive-annualization rank ordering changes to 3, 2, 5, 7, 1, 6, 8, 4, 10, 9, 12, 11 once serial correlation is handled. Robust annual SEs 0.25-1.16; 95% intervals generally exclude zero.
- Verified, the estimator-selection rule: the robust (GMM) estimator carries extra estimation error from the autocovariances (their Eq 25-27 and Table 3): even at q = 2 its asymptotic variance is 33% above the iid estimator at SR 2.0; at q = 12 and SR 1.0 it is 334% of the iid variance. Lo's own rule: use the robust estimator ONLY when the Ljung-Box Q-statistic (Eq 28, chi-squared q-1) rejects the no-serial-correlation null; otherwise the iid formula is the efficient choice. This is the primary-text licence for the programme's practice: weekly NQ log returns are near-iid (stage 0f lag-1 autocorrelation -0.011), so per-period SR standard errors on weekly bars legitimately use the iid formula, and the annualization ban is now anchored to the eta(q) formula: an honest q = 52 conversion would need autocovariance estimates at lags 1-51, which n ~ 300 weekly bars cannot support, so platform reporting stays per-period.

# White (2000), "A Reality Check for Data Snooping" — validation-spine paper notes

Source: Econometrica 68(5), September 2000, 1097-1126. Full core text studied from the author-hosted PDF (sections 1-2.3 verbatim: motivation, framework, all propositions, the Monte Carlo and Bootstrap Reality Check constructions, extensions to Sharpe-ratio-type criteria; the simulation section and the Brock-Lakonishok-LeBaron application are covered from the paper's well-established published results, flagged below where unverified against the fetched text). Saved 12 Nov 2026. Wave 1 of the paper queue (validation spine).

## The problem

Data snooping: using the same data more than once for inference or model selection. With a single time series, searching "long and hard enough" will always surface a model that looks good by chance. White's analogy: flipping many coins produces an all-heads coin; the newsletter scam (halve the mailing list each week, survivors see a perfect record) is data snooping done deliberately. The problem is not the search itself — search is unavoidable — it is that the sampling distribution of the BEST model found by the search is not the sampling distribution of a single pre-specified model. Classic multiple-comparison fixes (Bonferroni, union-intersection, Westfall-Young) were "not designed for the sheer numbers of candidate patterns generated by data mining" (Hand). White's deliverable: an asymptotically valid p-value for the null that the best of l searched models has NO predictive superiority over a benchmark, regardless of how many models were tried and how correlated their performances are.

## Framework

- l models plus a benchmark (index 0), evaluated over prediction periods R..T (n = T − R + 1), horizon τ. Per-period relative performance f_k,t+τ = f_k(Z_t+τ, θ̂_t) — e.g. negative MSE loss difference (Ex 2.1), log-return difference of a trading strategy vs a benchmark signal function with positions in {−1,0,+1} and fixed a-priori parameters (Ex 2.2, explicitly covering buy-and-hold as S_0 = 1), or predictive log-likelihood difference (Ex 2.3). Models need not be nested; trading-rule parameters may be set a priori (Diebold-Mariano framework), which is exactly how the programme's frozen rule sets behave.
- Null: H_o: max_k E[f_k] ≤ 0, an intersection of l one-sided hypotheses. The least favorable element is E[f_k] = 0 for all k — enforce that and the distribution of the best model's statistic is known asymptotically (inverting a confidence interval for max_k E[f_k]).
- Key asymptotic fact (West 1996 assumptions, strong-mixing data): n^{1/2}(f̄ − E f*) → N(0, V), so n^{1/2} max_k (f̄_k − E f_k) → max over a vector of correlated normals Z ~ N(0, V). The l×l correlation structure V is the whole difficulty: model performances are typically highly correlated (variants of the same indicator), and an analytic extreme-value distribution is infeasible.

## The two Reality Checks

1. Monte Carlo Reality Check: estimate V consistently (Politis-Romano block resampling/subsampling estimator for dependent data), draw Z_i ~ N(0, V̂) many times, p = fraction of max_k Z_i,k ≥ V_l = max_k n^{1/2} f̄_k. Recursive implementation exploits the Cholesky of V̂: adding model l to the family costs O(l), not a recomputation (storage grows with l²).
2. Bootstrap Reality Check (the standard form): resample the per-period relative performances f_k,t+τ with the Politis-Romano stationary bootstrap (geometric block lengths, mean 1/q, wrap-around — the SAME stationary bootstrap the programme's stage2/stage6 machinery uses, mean block 13). Statistic per replicate: V*_l = max_k n^{1/2}(f̄*_k − f̄_k). p-value = frac(V*_l ≥ V_l). Theorem 2.3: the conditional bootstrap law converges to the null law; the fitted parameters θ̂_t do NOT need to be re-estimated under resampling (subject to n/R conditions or the law of the iterated logarithm; with rolling/finite windows the estimation aspect "disappears"). Corollaries extend this to min (for testing the worst) and to singular V.

## Extensions that matter for the programme

- Corollary 2.6-2.8: the criterion can be any continuously differentiable function g of a vector of averages, explicitly including "the prediction sample Sharpe ratio for evaluating investment strategies". So the RC applies directly to Sharpe-based searches (our stage 6a grid family) with the delta method carrying the asymptotics.
- Hold-out logic: applying an in-sample estimate to a test set is covered by part (i) of the theory — the RC is valid on a single hold-out split too, which is what a one-shot battery does.
- Power (Prop 2.5): under a fixed alternative the statistic diverges at rate n^{1/2} — consistent; the test has power against the best model being genuinely superior, and the level can be driven to zero while power → 1.

## Well-established results not in the fetched text (flagged, from general knowledge of the published paper — verify against full text if ever cited formally)

- Simulation section: the RC controls size well, but including many clearly poor models in the family dilutes power (the max over a larger family widens the null) — the practical lesson: pre-screen the family on a criterion that does not use the evaluation data, or accept power loss. Naive single-model p-values over-reject massively.
- Brock-Lakonishok-LeBaron application (26 technical trading rules on the Dow, 1897-1996, with Sullivan/Timmermann/White): several rules show striking naive p-values, but the RC p-value for the best rule is roughly 0.12 — after accounting for the search, no rule beats the benchmark. (This is the canonical demonstration that the programme's discipline mirrors: the naive best Sharpe is not evidence.)

## Programme connections

- Stage 6a executed exactly this: the 27-combo AVFI grid family failed the White reality check (family best 0.58, RC p = 0.11) — the same structure as the BL result above: best naive performance, RC p ~ 0.11, nothing survives. The stage 6a implementation and this paper's construction are the same object.
- The stationary bootstrap with mean block 13 that the whole programme uses is P&R 1994 as specified here; White 2000 is its licence in the model-selection context.
- The family definition matters: our H8/H9 max-t and battery max-t are RC-type maxima over REGISTERED families; White's warning about power dilution is the reason the programme pre-registers small families instead of searching wide.
- Links forward: Hansen 2005 SPA (fixes the RC's poor-power-under-irrelevant-models problem), BH 1995 (the average-error alternative to simultaneous control, relevant when ranking rather than declaring one winner), Bailey/López de Prado DSR (the Sharpe-specific selection-bias correction), Lo 2002 (why Sharpe needs care before any of this).
- Links back: the Elliott Wave base (wave counts are the ultimate specification search; H3's N1-N4 null tests are the RC philosophy applied to pattern claims), Bartholomew-Biggs (multi-start optima = spurious maxima without out-of-sample validation), Tsay (the mixing/asymptotics assumptions), and the ORDERFLOW platform spec (its pre-registered hypothesis registry with mandatory null baselines is White's discipline operationalized).
