# Inside Volatility Arbitrage: The Secrets of Skewness — Alireza Javaheri (Wiley, 2005)

- source: "Inside Volatility Arbitrage: The Secrets of Skewness, Alireza Javaheri (Wiley, 2005), upload 15"
- status: COMPLETE (all 3 chapters + references skimmed)
- saved: Nov 2026

This book is the author's PhD-dissertation-based program for volatility modeling. Its three questions: (1) The Volatility Problem — which process actually generates returns and smiles; (2) The Inference Problem — how to estimate any parametric stochastic-volatility (SV) model's parameters from stock time series via filtering; (3) The Consistency Problem — do options markets and stock time series agree, and if not, can you trade the difference? The whole book builds toward one empirical conclusion: options markets imply systematically stronger negative skewness and kurtosis than stock time series do, and the trade that exploits this (buy OTM calls, sell OTM puts) is really insurance selling — steady premiums, rare disasters.

## Chapter 1: The Volatility Problem

### Baseline: GBM, Black-Scholes, and the smile
- Stock process dS = μS dt + σS dB; log-normal returns. Historic volatility = annualized standard deviation of log returns (×√252); Parkinson's high-low range estimator is more statistically efficient (Feller-distributed range).
- Black-Scholes PDE derived two ways: hedged-portfolio replication (complete market, unique risk-neutral measure) and Feynman-Kac/martingale pricing. Put-call parity, covered calls, CRR binomial trees (u = e^{σ√Δt}, d = 1/u, risk-neutral p) as the discrete equivalent.
- Implied volatility is the σ that inverts BS to a market price. Post-1987, equity indices show a persistent negative skew (OTM puts priced at higher implied vol than ATM), nonexistent before the crash. Skew flattens with maturity. Fat tails ("leptokurticity") accompany it.

### Candidate processes for the smile
1. Jump diffusion (Merton, Kou): add Poisson jumps dq with intensity λ to GBM. Total default case (jump size −100%, k = −1) gives Var(S_t) ≈ S₀²(σ² + λ)t and, elegantly, λ = credit spread C, so σ̃² = σ² + C: jump risk and credit risk are the same object priced as volatility. BS PDE holds with "risky rate" r + λ.
2. Level-dependent volatility: σ(S,t) as deterministic function of spot. CEV: σ = C·S^γ (γ < 0 gives the leverage effect — negative return-volatility correlation). BCG derive σ(S,t) from firm structure: equity is a call option on firm assets (Merton 1974), so equity volatility inherits an implicit level dependence; eliminating assets yields a nonlinear PDE for σ.
3. Local volatility (Dupire, Derman-Kani): nonparametric. Breeden-Litzenberger: the risk-neutral density is p(K,T) = e^{rT} ∂²C/∂K² — the option strip IS the market's distribution. Dupire inversion: σ²(K,T) = [∂V/∂T + rK ∂V/∂K] / [½K² ∂²V/∂K²]. Gatheral's identity: local variance = E[v_T | S_T = K] — the risk-neutral conditional expectation of instantaneous variance given the final price. Derman-Kani do the same on implied trees with Arrow-Debreu prices.
   - Critical weakness: local vol is an ill-posed inversion, extremely sensitive to input noise (negative probabilities/variances appear); calibration is unstable. Traders use continual recalibration (CR) rather than the single calibration (SC) the theory assumes, which destroys the original no-arbitrage property for path-dependent claims: barrier options (strongly path-dependent) are badly priced, bets (single-date) fine.
4. Stochastic volatility (SV): give variance v_t its own SDE. Heston/steat: square-root (CIR) dv = (ω − θv)dt + ξ√v dZ. GARCH(1,1) diffusion limit (Nelson): dv = (ω − θv)dt + ξ v dZ. NGARCH adds a leverage parameter c, producing the ρ correlation with ρ = −2c/√(κ − 1 + 4c²).
   - Subtle point: discrete GARCH has ONE noise source yet its continuous limit has TWO independent Brownian motions (via Donsker: B and B² − 1 are uncorrelated). Discretizing the two-factor limit again does NOT return the original GARCH — different parameterizations converge to one-factor or two-factor limits (Corradi). You must know which object you calibrated.
5. Incomplete markets: with a second (imperfectly correlated) noise source, risk-neutrality alone no longer prices the claim. Enter the market price of volatility risk: λ = ρλ_e + √(1−ρ²)λ*, where λ_e is the stock's market price of risk and λ* prices the risk of the volatility-hedged portfolio. Risk-neutral variance drift is b(v) − λa(v). Under Girsanov, ω, ξ, ρ are shared between the real and risk-neutral measures; only the drift θ shifts. This is the load-bearing fact for Chapter 3.

### Pricing machinery for SV
- Two-factor PDE (Ito in S and v) priced by finite differences or two-factor Monte Carlo.
- Lewis's generalized Fourier transform: transform the PDE in x = ln S; each payoff has a known transform (call: −K^{ik+1}/(k² − ik) in the strip k_i > 1); the "fundamental transform" Ĥ(k,v,τ) with Ĥ(·,0) = 1 multiplies the payoff transform. For Heston, Ĥ is analytic in k — hence fast, robust pricing. This is the method of choice.
- Mixing solution (Hull-White zero correlation; Romano-Touzi general): conditional on the variance path, the problem collapses to Black-Scholes with effective spot S^eff = S₀e^{Y_T} and effective variance ν^eff = (1/T)∫(1−ρ²)σ²dt; price = E[f*(S^eff, ν^eff)] over the variance process only — a ONE-factor Monte Carlo.
- Long-maturity asymptotics: eigenvalue/eigenfunction decomposition of the transform operator; the saddle k₀ lies on the imaginary axis ("ridge property").
- Vol-of-vol series expansion: C = c_BS + ξ(…) + ξ²(…) — fast but breaks down for ξ > 0.40 and maturities around 1 year. Key comparative statics: ξ generates leptokurticity and raises far-OTM prices; ρ < 0 makes the true call CHEAPER than BS for OTM strikes (this is the skew).
6. Pure-jump models: Variance-Gamma (VG, Madan-Carr-Chang) — Brownian motion evaluated at a gamma-distributed random time; closed-form return density via modified Bessel functions; VGSA adds a CIR "arrival rate" for clustering; VGG uses a gamma arrival rate. Characteristic functions available for Fourier pricing.

## Chapter 2: The Inference Problem

### The two estimation universes
- Cross-sectional (options at one date) estimates the RISK-NEUTRAL parameter set Ψ by least squares over strikes; time-series (stock history) estimates the STATISTICAL Ψ by maximum likelihood. Girsanov says ξ and ρ must agree between them if the diffusion model is right and data are sufficient. This is a falsifiable prediction.
- Practical detail: for daily data, drift barely matters (Δt = o(√Δt)); using μ or r − q in estimation is indistinguishable. Bakshi-Cao-Chen: bounded risk aversion implies θ ≈ θ^(r) too.
- LSE minimization via Powell's direction-set method (gradient-free, quadratically convergent via conjugate directions) — chosen because jump-likelihoods are non-differentiable.
- MLE justification: KL distance minimization; ML estimators are consistent and hit the Cramér-Rao bound (inverse Fisher information) asymptotically.

### Filtering as the estimation engine
- Since SV has TWO noise sources, the likelihood has no closed form (unlike GARCH, where the variance is an exact function of past observations). You must filter: p(x_k|z_{1:k}) via Chapman-Kolmogorov time update + Bayes measurement update.
- EKF: first-order linearization (Jacobian matrices A, W, H, U; Kalman gain K = P⁻Hᵀ(HP⁻Hᵀ + URUᵀ)⁻¹). UKF: unscented transformation with 2n_a+1 sigma points through the TRUE nonlinear function, accurate to third order. Kushner's NLF: Gauss-Hermite quadrature, M^N points (expensive).
- Kalman gain interpretations: conditional Gaussian mean update; least-squares regression slope S_xz S_zz⁻¹; the K that minimizes posterior error covariance.
- Joint filtering: concatenate state and parameters into one augmented vector (vs dual filtering); demonstrated by recovering π ≈ 3.14159 from data.
- State-space choice for Heston matters enormously: naive representations are UNOBSERVABLE (observability matrix [H; HA; …; HA^{n−1}] not full rank) — ω, θ converge, ξ, ρ don't. The fix (method of choice): a ONE-dimensional state where the variance equation absorbs the observed return via ρξ ln(S_k/S_{k−1}) and the orthogonal noise Z̃ = (Z − ρB)/√(1−ρ²). Observable, fast, stable.
- THE core difficulty: volatility is literally the noise of the observation process. Access to v_k comes only through the −½v_kΔt Itô term, which is dominated by the √(v_kΔt) noise term because Δt = o(√Δt) at daily frequency. Result: ω, θ (drift parameters, entering the observation's drift) estimate well from ~5000 daily points; ξ and ρ (entering the "noise of the noise") have FLAT likelihoods and need 100k–500k+ points. Rewriting in daily parameters does not escape this — it is a low signal-to-noise ratio, not a unit problem.
- Crucial partial rescue: the PRODUCT ρξ is well identified even when each separately is not (it enters the drift term at the same level). ρξ determines the skewness; ξ alone determines the kurtosis. So time-series skewness estimates are trustworthy; kurtosis estimates are not.
- Particle filtering: importance sampling with recursive weights w_k = w_{k−1}·p(z|x)p(x|x_prev)/q(x|…), resampling to prevent weight degeneracy, Metropolis-Hastings step against sample impoverishment (marginal benefit). EPF/UPF use a Kalman proposal that incorporates the latest observation. Likelihood = total normalized weight per step; maximize over Ψ.
- Empirical verdict across filters: PFs beat Gaussian filters on error (UPF best), and all filters beat none — but NO filter solves the (ξ,ρ) identification problem at daily frequency. With annual steps (Δt = 1, 200 points) EPF recovers parameters well — but that is 200 years of data, and Girsanov itself fails at that step size. High-frequency data genuinely helps: likelihood scales as 1/Δt, so sampling 1000× per day over the same calendar period adds real information.
- Diagnostics: MPE/RMSE of residuals; χ² normality test on residuals; Box-Ljung test on residual autocorrelation; variograms (flat at 1 for iid, linear b/2 for Brownian motion).
- Model identification on real SPX data (1996–2001): the 3/2 model (p = 3/2) beats Heston (p = 1/2) and GARCH (p = 1) on filtering error, in line with Engle & Ishida. Filtering can thus discriminate models, not just estimate parameters.
- Bayesian alternative (MCMC): Gibbs sampler (iterate known conditionals; justified by Hammersley-Clifford + ergodic averaging), Metropolis-Hastings (accept/reject with ratio α; reversibility proof; simulating from the prior makes the ratio a pure likelihood ratio), blocking for speed. FMW apply this to Heston; they too find inefficiency — agreeing with the classical results. The book's stance: classical (frequentist) filtering is the method of choice; Bayesian machinery is retained for MH resampling steps.
- Characteristic-function filtering (Bates) works only for exponentially affine processes; Dragulescu-Yakovenko give a semianalytic Heston likelihood. Particle filtering's advantage is full generality (VGSA/VGG have no affine transform).
- Jumps (Bates model: Heston + fixed-fraction Poisson jumps of size j): generic PF handles the jump indicator naturally. Striking result: jump parameters (λ, j) are estimated ACCURATELY from just 245 daily points (λ̂ = 2.65 vs 2.52, ĵ = 0.20 vs 0.20) while diffusion parameters from the same series remain hopeless. Jumps enter the observation's drift, not its noise.
- VGSA/VGG: same pattern — VG parameters (σ, θ, ν) affecting the observation drift estimate well; arrival-rate parameters (κ, η, λ) affecting its noise estimate poorly. Different parameter sets generating nearly indistinguishable price paths is the signature of weak identification.
- Recapitulation: choose the model CATEGORY (diffusion vs jumps vs pure-jump) by financial argument, not residual comparison; AIC-type criteria work only asymptotically. Always run a simulation back-test and report the sampling distribution of each estimator.

## Chapter 3: The Consistency Problem

### The test
- Estimate Ψ from one-year SPX options (cross-sectional, risk-neutral) and from 12 years of daily SPX data (filtered MLE, statistical). If Girsanov holds, (ξ, ρ) must match. They don't: options imply ξ ≈ 0.42, ρ ≈ −0.67; time series imply ξ ≈ 0.10, ρ ≈ −0.13. Options markets price in stronger negative skewness and kurtosis than the stock history contains. Robust to MC step size, bid/ask mid vs bid, estimated vs fixed v₀, strike set, and recalibration date.
- The mismatch is not an estimation artifact of the time series alone: it is exactly what the flat-likelihood analysis predicts — but the cross-sectional values sit far above even the upper tail of the time-series sampling distribution.

### Peso theory / crash-o-phobia
- The time series is ONE realized path; if the true process contains rare large downward jumps, a 12-year sample may contain none, while options ALWAYS price the possibility (Friedman's peso: Mexican interest rates priced a devaluation that then happened). Adding jumps to the cross-sectional calibration lowers the implied (ξ, ρ) — but the fitted jump is λ ≈ 0.009 with j ≈ 0.91: options expect a rare, near-total crash. The gap survives. Consistent with the smile's birth in 1987: options markets "learned" crashes exist; stock paths since then often haven't shown one.

### The trades
- Skewness trade (options' skew too negative): buy 10% OTM calls, sell 10% OTM puts, delta-hedged, vega-weighted (ratio V_C/V_P). This is SELLING INSURANCE: steady premium collection, large losses on sudden moves — in either direction, since large upward moves also produce realized third/fourth moments.
- Kurtosis trade (kurtosis overvalued): sell ATM and far-OTM options, buy close-OTM.
- Exact refinement: Carr-Madan replication — E[f(S_T)] = f(F) + e^{rT}∫₀^F f''(K)P dK + e^{rT}∫_F^∞ f''(K)C dK — lets you trade any payoff shape, including pure third-moment payoffs, at higher transaction cost.
- Mirror trades (options underprice skew/kurtosis after a crash) = buying insurance: start with a loss, hope for a crash. Unattractive since post-crash crash probabilities are genuinely reduced.
- Worked SPX example (Apr–Jun 2002): final PnL ≈ +$1.85 after a mid-trade jump nearly wiped out the premium — a "hardly" profitable trade.
- 40 systematic three-month SPX trades (2002–2003): LOSS if executed at bid/ask, PROFIT if executed at mid. The bid-ask spread (~$1 on 10% OTM SPX options) is the same order as the edge. Verdict: no decisive proof of options/stock inconsistency; the trade PnL is the model-free empirical measure of it.

### Which names offer the trade (the book's most practical section)
Two conditions create a skewness-trade opportunity — weak historic volatility-of-volatility (SPX: high implied ξ vs modest historic ξ) or weak historic correlation (GW, a "penny" stock that revives — becomes MORE volatile as it rises, so historic skew is low or even positive while options price negative skew: trade exists but crash risk is real).
Neither condition holds when the historic process itself is violently negatively skewed: MSFT (high ξ, strong negative ρ — equity gets riskier as it falls, justified skew, no trade) and NDX (Figures 3.18–3.19: high ξ, strong negative correlation; the historic smile MATCHES the options smile — no skewness-trade opportunity on Nasdaq in the 2002–2003 window). Index skew trades are "safest" for crash diversification reasons, but the NDX's own crash-heavy history means its options skew is largely earned.

### Extensions
- Non-Gaussian models (VGG): no Girsanov, so parameters cannot be compared across measures; compare MOMENTS instead, or use the p/q density ratio to solve for the utility-optimal payoff φ(S) = (U′)⁻¹(λe^{−rT} q/p) (Carr-Madan optimal positioning). VGSA keeps a conditionally Gaussian arrival rate so λ IS comparable — and in some periods (mid-2000) the risk-neutral λ (16.5) dwarfs the statistical one (2.8–4.4): a potential kurtosis-trade signal.
- FX: smiles nearly symmetric (ρ ≈ 0), so skewness trades are hard, but high implied ξ supports kurtosis trades. EUR/USD 2004: cross-sectional ξ = 0.45 vs time-series ξ = 0.11.
- Fixed income: the short rate is unobservable but equals the initial yield-curve slope in an extended-Vasicek setup; stochastic variance breaks closed-form bond-option prices but two-factor MC works; multiple swap tenors per option maturity add a cross-sectional dimension.

### Word of caution
The skewness trade is not an arbitrage. Models are approximations of a mutating market; detecting statistical inconsistency licenses a risk premium position, not a riskless profit.

## Durable lessons

1. The option strip IS the risk-neutral distribution (Breeden-Litzenberger: p = e^{rT}∂²C/∂K²). Any claim about "what the market expects" can be read directly from option prices without a model.
2. Dupire/local volatility is an ill-posed inversion: it fits today's surface exactly but is unstable and implies future smiles that flatten; continual recalibration silently abandons the no-arbitrage property it was built on. Path-dependent claims (barriers!) are where local-vol calibration error concentrates.
3. Stochastic volatility makes markets incomplete; the market price of volatility risk λ = ρλ_e + √(1−ρ²)λ* is the price of that incompleteness. Girsanov implies ω, ξ, ρ are measure-invariant; only variance-drift θ shifts.
4. ξ (vol-of-vol) creates kurtosis and lifts far-OTM prices; ρ < 0 creates skew and lowers OTM call prices relative to BS. Skewness is governed by the product ρξ, kurtosis by ξ alone.
5. Volatility is the noise of the price process. Parameters entering an observation's DRIFT (ω, θ; jump λ, j; VG σ, θ, ν) are estimable from thousands of points; parameters entering its NOISE (ξ, ρ; arrival κ, η, λ) have flat likelihoods and need hundreds of thousands. No filter fixes this — it is a signal-to-noise property, not an algorithm deficiency.
6. The product ρξ is far better identified than ξ or ρ separately. Trust time-series skewness estimates; distrust time-series kurtosis estimates.
7. Higher sampling frequency over the SAME calendar period adds real information (likelihood scales as 1/Δt); more data at the same daily frequency over longer histories is the second-best, and 200 years is not available.
8. On real data the 3/2 model beats Heston (and GARCH) on filtering error (Engle-Ishida agreement). Model identification via filtering is feasible — but choose the model CATEGORY (diffusion vs jump vs pure-jump) on financial grounds, since residual comparisons across categories are unfair and AIC is only asymptotic.
9. Cross-sectional options parameters systematically exceed time-series ones in (ξ, |ρ|): options price in crash risk the historical path may not contain (peso theory / crash-o-phobia, born 1987). Adding jumps to calibration confirms it: the fitted jump is rare and near-total (λ ≈ 0.01, j ≈ 0.9).
10. The skewness trade (buy OTM call, sell OTM put, delta-hedged) is insurance selling: regular small profits, occasional large losses; a large UP move can also hurt. Its PnL is a model-free measure of options/stock inconsistency — and on SPX 2002–2003 that measure was ≈ 0 after transaction costs.
11. A skewness-trade opportunity requires weak historic ξ (SPX-type) or weak historic ρ (GW-type). If both are strong — MSFT, and crucially NDX — the historic skew justifies the implied skew and there is NO trade.
12. Bid-ask spreads (~$1 on 10% OTM SPX options) are the same order of magnitude as the skew edge; execution at mid vs bid/ask flips the average sign of the strategy's PnL.
13. Discrete GARCH and continuous SV are different objects: one noise source vs two in the limit, and discretizing the limit does not return the original. Calibration must be performed on exactly the process that will be used.
14. Particle filters dominate Gaussian filters for error and handle non-Gaussian (jump, pure-jump) models, but only pay at larger time steps; at daily frequency their advantage is real but does not rescue (ξ, ρ) identification. Bayesian MCMC (Gibbs/MH) confirms the same inefficiencies — the difficulty is informational, not methodological.
15. Even where parameters cannot be compared across measures (non-Gaussian), the statistical density p and risk-neutral density q jointly define the optimal derivative payoff via φ = (U′)⁻¹(λe^{−rT} q/p): the p/q ratio is the trade.

## Connections to prior books

- Björk (Arbitrage Theory): the entire pricing half rests on Björk's machinery — risk-neutral valuation, Girsanov and market price of risk (Björk Ch. 5, 15), incomplete markets and multiple equivalent martingale measures (Ch. 15, 17), Feynman-Kac, and the change-of-numeraire discipline. Javaheri supplies the empirical falsification of a complete-markets assumption Björk flagged: the smile exists precisely because single-factor lognormal pricing fails. Björk's fixed-income block (extended Vasicek, HJM) reappears in Javaheri's fixed-income consistency section with stochastic variance added.
- Bouchaud (Trades, Quotes, Prices): Javaheri's leptokurticity, vol clustering, and the need for jump/pure-jump processes are the time-series face of the fat tails Bouchaud documents from micro data; both conclude Gaussian/continuous models understate extreme events.
- The Quants: the 1987-crash origin of the skew and "crash-o-phobia" link directly to the crash-machine history there; both books treat option-market prices as embodying learned disaster memory.
- Hasbrouck (Empirical Market Microstructure): the filtering framework — latent state, observation equation, Bayesian updating, Gibbs/MCMC — is the same state-space machinery Hasbrouck uses for microstructure noise; Javaheri's observability analysis (rank of [H; HA; …]) is a rigorous version of Hasbrouck's identification concerns.
- Elliott Wave (Balan): the leverage effect (negative return-volatility correlation) and vol clustering give a quantitative backbone to wave-count practice: volatility expands in third/fifth waves and contracts in complexes. Javaheri's NDX finding says Nasdaq's realized skew is genuinely violent — consistent with the Nasdaq's crash-prone wave history (2000–2002) in which collapse phases dominate.
- Foucault (Market Liquidity): local-vol instability and recalibration echo Foucault's theme that dealer pricing responds to inventory and adverse selection; both warn that today's fitted surface does not describe tomorrow's dynamics.

## Strategy / research candidates (weekly NQ practice)

1. NDX-specific non-edge: Javaheri's own NDX figures (2002–2003) show historic and options-implied smiles agreeing — no skewness trade on Nasdaq then. For NQ analysis: do NOT assume index options overprice crash risk on Nasdaq; the index's realized path is genuinely negatively skewed. Any options-based signal on NDX must first pass this consistency check.
2. Parkinson weekly estimator: use high-low range rather than close-close returns for weekly volatility measurement — more efficient, and directly supported by Javaheri's Ch. 1. Cheap upgrade to any NQ vol-regime tracking.
3. Vol-regime model: a mean-reverting variance process (CIR-type, ω/θ long-run variance, θ reversion speed) is the principled version of "vol clustering" reading on weekly charts. Estimate ω and θ only — they are the estimable parameters — and treat ξ, ρ as unidentifiable from weekly data.
4. Skew-as-signal: since ρξ (not ξ, ρ separately) is the robustly estimable statistic, build any skew/kurtosis diagnostics on the PRODUCT. A weekly NQ log could track rolling realized skewness (third moment of weekly returns) as the trustworthy moment.
5. Peso-aware backtesting: any backtest over a period without a crash systematically understates crash probability; weight crash scenarios explicitly. This is the statistical justification for scenario-based position sizing rather than purely historical VaR.
6. Insurance-selling topology: Javaheri's PnL decomposition (premium collected + hedge PnL + interest − jump losses) is the exact shape of selling naked OTM premium — and of ANY strategy with steady small gains and rare large losses (including over-leveraged trend-following). Audit any NQ strategy for this payoff profile and cap the disaster leg deliberately.
7. Execution-cost realism: the SPX skew trade's edge was smaller than its bid-ask spread. For any NQ idea, compute whether the edge survives one round trip of the spread; if the edge and the spread are the same order, the edge is not tradable.
8. Crash-o-phobia as sentiment gauge: options-implied skew steepening without a corresponding move in realized skew is a fear gauge (the market pricing a crash its own path hasn't shown). If NQ options data were ever added to the toolkit, implied-skew-minus-realized-skew would be a ready-made regime indicator.
9. Frequency trade-off in research design: parameter estimation on weekly bars (52/yr) is hopeless for vol-of-vol; any statistical claim from weekly NQ data must be a claim about drift-level quantities (long-run variance, reversion speed, mean) or use far longer histories / higher-frequency data.
10. Model-class discipline: before fitting anything to NQ, decide the category (diffusion vs jump) on financial grounds — weekly Nasdaq returns contain jumps (gap opens, earnings, macro shocks); jump parameters are estimable from short samples even where diffusion parameters are not.

# Jim Forte — Anatomy of a Trading Range (MTA Journal, Summer-Fall 1994, pp. 47-58)

Source: journal article, 12 pages, full text studied 18 Nov 2026 (third upload batch). Wyckoff-method layer, applied to trading-range (TR) analysis. Bibliography: Hutson/Weis/Schroeder "Charting the Market" (TASC 1990); Pruden & Fraser Wyckoff Seminars (GGU 1992/93); Stock Market Institute (SMI) literature. Charts via Telescan 3.0.

## Core content

The three Wyckoff laws as Forte states them:
1. Supply and Demand: demand > supply -> price rises; supply > demand -> price falls.
2. Cause and Effect: effect proportional to cause; the force of accumulation/distribution inside a TR is the cause that unfolds in the move out; point-and-figure chart counts measure the cause and project the effect.
3. Effort vs Result: divergence between volume and price progress (spread, relative price progress, volume) reveals which side is dominant.

Stated objective: enter in anticipation of the move out of a TR with reward/risk of at least 3 to 1. TR = equilibrium zone where professionals run accumulation or distribution campaigns; that campaign is the "cause".

## Accumulation Schematic 1, Phases A-E (with numbered points)

Phase A (stopping the decline): PS (Preliminary Support) — pronounced buying after a prolonged downmove, volume/spread widen; SC (Selling Climax) — widening spread and panicky public selling absorbed by larger professional interests near the bottom; AR (Automatic Rally) — selling exhausted, rally fueled by short covering, AR high helps define the TR top; ST (Secondary Test) — revisits the SC area on significantly diminished volume and spread; SC/ST lows plus AR high set the initial TR boundaries. Forte's honesty note: Phase A can end without climactic action, but dramatic selling "generally clear[s] out all the sellers" and is better for a sustained markup. Reaccumulation caveat: a TR within an upmove has no PS/SC/ST; its Phase A looks like distribution's Phase A; Phases B-E analyzed the same.

Phase B (equilibrium, mixed clues): early = wide erratic swings, greater volume; late = diminishing volume as professionals absorb the last supply. Support/resistance lines (A/B/C/D) contain price and frame the coming tests.

Phase C (THE TEST — the decisive phase): either the stock starts leaving the TR with higher tops/bottoms, or a spring/shakeout breaks previous support. The shakeout is preferred by Wyckoff logic: it cleans out weak holders and creates a false directional impression. SPRING-VOLUME TRICHOTOMY (Forte's exact, encodable rule): supply surfacing on the break of support is (a) very light / low volume -> way clear for a sustained advance, (b) heavy -> usually renewed decline, (c) moderate -> more testing of support, proceed with caution. The spring also supplies dominant interests with additional stock from weak holders at low prices. Prescribed entries: point 8 (the shakeout itself, first prescribed long); point 10 (the test of the spring, better: low-volume pullback with a specific danger point at 8).

Phase D (markup begins): a consistent pattern of SOS's (advances on widening spread + increasing volume) and LPS's (reactions on smaller spread + diminished volume). If the pattern does not occur, do NOT add; consider closing the original position until evidence is conclusive. SOS brings JAC (Jump Across the Creek, a deliberate break of the creek resistance on good spread/volume). Entries/adds at point 12 (BU: back up to the creek edge / LPS after the JAC) and point 14 (more important support/resistance point). A series of SOS's and LPS's = evidence the bottom is in and markup has begun.

Phase E (stock leaves the TR): demand in control, setbacks short-lived; position management only.

The Creek: wavy resistance line across rally peaks inside the TR; minor and major creek lines must be crossed on the way up.

P&F projections: counts taken from points 8, 10, 12, 14 project the extent of the move, set reward/risk before entry, and later target close/adjustment areas.

Forte's falsification honesty (rare and valuable): the presence of an SC does not confirm accumulation; it may be accumulation, redistribution, or nothing; schematics are one idealized model with many variations.

## Distribution Schematics 2 and 3, Phases A-E

Inverse logic; "jumping the creek" replaced by "falling through the ice". Claimed asymmetry (encodable, unverified): distribution is generally accomplished in a SHORTER time period than accumulation.

Phase A: PSY (Preliminary Supply) — selling provides pronounced resistance after an upmove; BC (Buying Climax) — urgent public buying filled by professionals near the top; AR (Automatic Reaction) — its low helps define the TR bottom; ST of the BC on diminished volume. Redistribution = TR within a larger downmove; Phase B-E analysis unchanged.

Phase C: SOW (Sign of Weakness) — increased spread and volume downward that breaks the Phase B standoff (Schematic 2, point 10); may or may not fall through the Ice; the rally back to point 11 (LPSY, Last Point of Supply) is unconvincing, narrow-spread, light or heavy volume (weak demand or substantial supply). First short opportunity at point 11; better on the rally testing 11 (more evidence, tighter danger point). Schematic 3 alternative: UTAD (Upthrust After Distribution) — pronounced move up through the TR highs (point 11) that returns into the TR. Forte's UTAD signature (encodable): new highs on increasing volume with RELATIVE NARROWING spread, then return to the average level of closes of the TR = lack of solid demand = distribution, not accumulation. UTAD also strips weak shorts to dominant players and distributes to less-informed buyers before the decline. Third variation (no schematic): upthrust with quick fall to TR middle where the TR is too wide to be full distribution and concentrated selling is absent except late in the TR.

The Ice: wavy support line under reaction lows; a break through the Ice is usually followed by attempts to get back above; failure to regain it may mean "drowning".

UT vs UTAD temporal distinction (Pruden's slides state it explicitly; Forte implies it): UT = minor penetration of highs overcome by supply EARLIER in the TR; UTAD = the definitive terminal test LATE in the TR.

Phase D: evidence of supply dominance increases via the Ice break or further SOW after an upthrust; shorts added at points 13, 15, 17 (LPSY rallies); pyramid only if initial positions are in profit. Phase E: markdown, supply in control, rallies feeble.

## Real-world examples (annotated, Telescan)

Long-term accumulation (Phase C = light-volume break of TR lows then SOS on dramatically increased volume); two intermediate reaccumulations (springs/shake-outs on very light volume, no new supply, demand dominance off the spring); two distributions (PSY/BC on failing volume; SOW on increased volume; Ice breaks; the MDT chart shows the UTAD following the right side of the TR, then breakdown on significantly higher volume).

## Programme mapping (descriptive/encodable only; any evaluation needs a new dated signed amendment)

- Encodable event set for the parked ORDERFLOW platform's Addendum A2 Wyckoff state machine: every event above (PS/SC/AR/ST/spring/creek/JAC/SOS/LPS/BU and the distribution mirrors) has an explicit price-volume definition Forte gives in words; each becomes a parameterized detector.
- Spring-volume trichotomy: a THREE-WAY pre-declared regime for the A2 spring detector (light/hold, heavy/reject, moderate/wait) rather than a binary spring flag; maps to Phase 5 hypothesis H-W1's refinement and to order-flow confirmation testing (does absorption at the breach change the trichotomy outcome?). Tier 1 (OHLCV + proxy volume) testable on decades of daily/weekly data.
- UT vs UTAD (early vs late TR) + the relative-narrowing-spread-with-rising-volume signature: parameterizable UTAD detector distinct from generic upthrusts; maps to H-W2.
- Distribution shorter than accumulation: cheap falsifiable Tier-1 claim (duration comparison across detected TRs, with the detector-lag caveat).
- P&F count as the parameterized estimator of Law 2 (cause and effect): the count is the cause; the realized move is the effect; regression with block-bootstrap CIs per Addendum A2's Law-2 test.
- Entry ladders 8/10/12/14 (long) and 11/13/15/17 (short): pre-registered add/exit ladders with the 3:1 reward/risk gate; pyramiding only in profit.
- Forte's own disclaimer (SC != accumulation) is the null-model discipline the A2 engine needs: event presence never confirms structure; only the full causal state sequence does.

Links: Pruden's 1997 workshop (the Nine Buying/Selling Tests are the Phase C->D admission checklist; same SMI source), Williams/VSA (effort vs result instrumented), the Balan Elliott base (TRs as correction structures; distribution/accumulation as macro context), O'Hara and the microstructure stack (absorption = informed-flow mechanics behind "professional interests"), and the ORDERFLOW platform spec (A2 event detectors, A6 Tier-1/Tier-2 two-tier design).

# Hank Pruden — The Wyckoff Method (TAG XIX Las Vegas 1997 workshop slides, 31 pp)

Source: conference workshop slide deck, "The Wyckoff Method: Profit and Survival Techniques used by Legendary Operators", TAG XIX, Las Vegas, 1997. Full text (all 31 slides) studied 18 Nov 2026. Sourced from the Stock Market Institute (SMI, founded 1931) and the MTA Journal Summer-Fall 1994. Pruden: Golden Gate University, executive director of the Institute for Technical Market Analysis, editor of the MTA Journal.

## Core content

Three essential factors of the method: (1) volume / tick-volume intensity, (2) the relationship between price movement and volume, (3) the time required for movements to run their course.

The Three Wyckoff Laws (Exhibit 4): Supply and Demand ("when demand is greater than supply, price will rise and when supply is greater than demand, price will fall", SMI source); Cause and Effect; Effort vs Result.

Primary market phases (Exhibit 3, SMI source): Accumulation, Markup, Re-Accumulation ("momentary stopping or pausing points in the up-trend"), Distribution, Re-distribution ("momentary stopping or pausing points in the down-trend"), Markdown. Idealized Cycle exhibit.

Schematic 1 (Accumulation, numbered): 1 PS, 2 SC, 3 AR, 4/5 STs, 6 Creek, 7/8 Springs/Shakeouts/Tests, 9 JUMP (Jump Across the Creek, deliberate, on good spread and volume = SOS), 10/12 SOS, 11/13 LPS + BU (Back Up to the Edge of the Creek). Spring-volume trichotomy identical to Forte's (light -> way clear for advance; heavy -> renewed decline; moderate -> more testing, caution). Note: a series of SOS's and LPS's = bottom in place, markup begun; build/pyramid at those points.

Distribution schematics 2/3 (Exhibit 17): PSY, BC, AR, ST, UT (minor penetration of prior highs/BC area immediately overcome by supply; occurs EARLIER in the TR, Schematic 3 point 7), SOW, the ICE, LPSY, UTAD (upthrust after distribution, LATER stages of the TR, point 11 — the explicit UT vs UTAD temporal distinction, matching Forte). UTAD signature: light volume with no follow-through, or heavy volume with a breakdown back into the middle of the old TR = distribution evidence.

Wyckoff technique exhibits: phases of testing; the readiness to move; the stopping of a prior trend (SMI/J. Philip Fund Management sources). Chart examples (Dow Jones TTSplice daily charts): Cocoa (CC), US 30Y T-Bonds (US), Japanese Yen (JYC), Gold RTH (GC); equities: Acclaim Entertainment (AKLM), AMD, Micron (MU), Medtronic (MDT). EasyLanguage code offered (not included in the upload).

## THE NINE BUYING TESTS (Exhibit 21, SMI source; applied to an average or a stock after decline)

1. Downside objective accomplished — determined from the Figure (P&F) chart.
2. Activity bullish: volume increases on rallies and decreases on reactions — Vertical (bar) chart.
3. Preliminary support — Vertical and Figure.
4. Average/stock stronger than the market (more responsive on rallies, more resistant to reactions) — Vertical chart.
5. Downward stride broken: supply line penetrated — Vertical or Figure.
6. Higher supports (daily lows) — Vertical or Figure.
7. Higher tops (daily highs rising) — Vertical or Figure.
8. Base forming (horizontal price line) — Figure chart.
9. Estimated profit at least three times the indicated risk — Figure chart for the profit objective; vertical chart for stop-order placement.

OCR honesty flag: the "Determined From" column interleaves in the OCR; rows 3-5's exact chart assignment is approximate (recorded in printed order); tests 1, 2, 8, 9 are unambiguous.

## THE NINE SELLING TESTS (Exhibit 22, SMI source; applied after an advance)

1. Upside objective accomplished — Figure chart.
2. Activity bearish: volume decreases on rallies and increases on reactions — Vertical chart.
3. Preliminary supply — Vertical and Figure (approx per OCR).
4. Average/stock weaker than the market (responsive on reactions, sluggish on rallies) — Vertical chart.
5. Upward stride broken — the slide prints "supply line penetrated", an apparent original typo for the demand line; flagged, not corrected silently.
6. Lower tops (daily highs falling) — Vertical or Figure.
7. Lower supports (daily lows falling) — Vertical or Figure.
8. Crown forming (lateral movement) — Figure chart.
9. Estimated profit at least three times the indicated risk — Figure chart for the profit objective; vertical chart for stop placement.

Observation: test 9 in both lists is the 3:1 profit-to-risk rule — the earliest explicit reward/risk gate in the whole collection, and the same 3:1 minimum Forte states as the campaign objective.

## Quality verdict and programme mapping (descriptive/encodable only; any evaluation needs a new dated signed amendment)

Quality: pre-scientific-era workshop slides; zero statistics, no nulls, no error accounting; but the Nine Tests are the OLDEST PUBLISHED QUANTIFIABLE WYCKOFF CHECKLIST — each test is a boolean with an explicit chart source, which makes the whole checklist encodable as fixed-threshold features on daily/weekly bars.

- Tests 2 (volume up on rallies / down on reactions, and its bearish mirror) = a signed volume-asymmetry statistic; the direct ancestor of the VSA effort-vs-result metrics already noted from Williams; Tier-1 proxy-flow testable (Addendum A6).
- Tests 4 (relative strength vs the market) = a cross-sectional RS feature.
- Tests 5-7 (stride broken, higher/lower tops and supports) = causal pivot/slope features; the repaint discipline (ts_extreme vs ts_confirmed) from the platform's A1 primitives applies directly.
- Tests 1, 8, 9 (and the profit objectives) need point-and-figure constructions: box size and reversal are explicit parameters whose grid counts toward trials.
- Placement in the parked platform's A2 engine: the Nine Tests are the natural Phase C->D admission checklist — a fixed, pre-registered checklist version of the Wyckoff state machine, with the strict-rule vs soft-scoring comparison the addendum requires.
- The 3:1 rule maps onto the programme's pre-registered reward/risk discipline (barrier heights vs stop placement), and onto nothing in the weekly SMH track, which refuses direction and has no entries to gate.
- UT vs UTAD temporal distinction (early vs late TR) cross-confirms Forte's note; both sources SMI-derived, so this is convergence of one lineage, not independent confirmation.

Links: Forte 1994 (same SMI lineage, the prose definitions for every event), Williams/VSA (effort vs result instrumented; no-demand/no-supply tests), the Balan Elliott base (correction/TR context), O'Hara (absorption as informed-flow mechanics), and the ORDERFLOW platform spec (Addendum A2, Phase 5 H-W family).
